Cloud-Native .NET

Modern .NET, GitHub and Azure for enterprise apps. A self-study course for senior .NET developers who built their career on .NET Framework, IIS, Remote Desktop and log files, and now want the modern stack: .NET 10, Aspire, GitHub Actions, Azure Container Apps, OpenTelemetry and the reliability habits that come with them.

📅 Written in October 2026 for .NET 10 (LTS), C# 14, Aspire 13 and the Azure services of that time. Facts marked 🔧 change often; check the linked docs.

What "cloud-native .NET" means here

You already know how to build good software. What changed is where it runs and how you know it works.

You know this This course gets you to
ASP.NET MVC / Web API 2 on IIS ASP.NET Core 10 minimal APIs in containers on Azure Container Apps
web.config and passwords in config Environment configuration, managed identities and Key Vault
Thursday-night deploys over RDP CI on every pull request, CD on every merge, with OIDC and no stored secrets
Windows Services, SQL Agent jobs, MSMQ Background workers, Azure Service Bus, the outbox pattern
Log files on three servers OpenTelemetry traces, metrics and logs, queried with KQL
"The site is up" SLIs, SLOs, error budgets and actionable alerts
Clicking in the portal Bicep in git, linted in CI

How the guide works

You join Ledgerly, a small fintech building a portfolio tracker, as a senior .NET developer. Each chapter opens with a problem the team hits, then explains the concepts, shows real code from a working sample, and ends with traps, copy-ready helpers and an optional exercise.

The sample

The repository contains Ledgerly, a runnable .NET 10 system the chapters quote line by line:

Part What it is
sample/src API, background worker, fake market-data vendor, migration runner, shared core, service defaults and the Aspire AppHost
sample/tests 15 unit tests and 8 integration tests against a real SQL Server
sample/infra Bicep for Azure Container Apps, Azure SQL, Service Bus, Azure Managed Redis, Key Vault, Container Registry and Application Insights
.github/workflows CI (build, test, container images, Bicep lint), CodeQL, and a deploy workflow that stays off until you connect your own Azure subscription

Browse it on GitHub.

Tools you need

Tool Why Required?
.NET 10 SDK Build and run everything Yes
Git and a GitHub account Chapters 8, 9 and 12 Yes
An editor: Visual Studio 2026, VS Code with C# Dev Kit, or Rider Writing code Yes
Docker Desktop or Podman Aspire runs SQL Server, Redis and the Service Bus emulator as containers For running the whole system locally
Aspire CLI aspire run (or run the AppHost project with dotnet run) Recommended

GitHub Copilot Chapter 20 Optional

The whole course can be read without an Azure subscription. Everything up to the deploy runs on your laptop.

Start → Chapter 1: From legacy to cloud-native

Chapter 1: From legacy to cloud-native

⏱ About 25 min · You'll learn: what "cloud-native .NET" actually changes for a senior developer, the Ledgerly architecture you will build in this course, and how the 21 chapters fit together.

📖 Story: The whiteboard with no servers on it

It is your first Monday at Ledgerly. Last month you were still at Nordbank Insurance, where "production" meant six Windows Server VMs behind a hardware load balancer, a shared drive full of web.config transforms, and a Thursday-night deployment window where you copied files over Remote Desktop. When something broke, you opened three RDP sessions and searched C:\inetpub\logs and a folder of NLog text files until you found the stack trace.

Maya Okafor, Ledgerly's tech lead, hands you a coffee and walks you to a whiteboard. It shows a few boxes: an API, a worker, a database, a queue, a cache. There are no server names on it, no IP addresses, and no drive letters. "Three rules," she says. "One: there are no servers you can log in to. Two: logs are queries, not files. Three: deploys are boring. They happen on every merge, many times a day, and nobody stays late for them."

You open a new notebook and write a title on the first page: Habits I brought with me. Over the next weeks you will look at each habit honestly, keep the good ones and replace the rest. Maya smiles when she sees it. "Good. Most of your habits are right. The places where they live have changed."

Numbers in the Ledgerly story (team size, timelines, traffic, latency, costs) are illustrative. They make the story concrete; they are not benchmarks.

🏚️ Old way → 🚀 New way

What you did before What you do now Why it changed
.NET Framework 4.x on Windows Server and IIS .NET 10 (LTS) on Linux containers in Azure Container Apps Cross-platform runtime, much faster, a new major version every November, and no OS to patch yourself
Deploy by RDP, Web Deploy or xcopy on a Thursday night Every merge to main builds, tests and deploys through GitHub Actions Small, frequent, automated deploys are safer than big, rare, manual ones
web.config transforms with passwords in them Environment variables, Key Vault references and managed identities (no passwords at all) Secrets in files leak; identities can't be copied into a chat message
Text log files on each server, read with RDP and Notepad++ Structured logs, traces and metrics through OpenTelemetry, queried with KQL With many short-lived replicas there is no "server" to log in to, and you need to follow one request across services
Windows Services and SQL Agent jobs for background work Background services, queues (Azure Service Bus) and Container Apps jobs Work survives restarts, scales with load and retries on its own
Servers sized for peak load, running all year Autoscaling from zero (or one) replica to many, based on traffic or queue length You pay for what you use, and a traffic spike doesn't need a purchase order
Infrastructure created by hand in a console or by a ticket to the ops team Infrastructure as code (Bicep) reviewed in pull requests Reproducible environments, and the history of every change is in git
"It works on my machine" One command starts the whole system locally (Aspire), and CI proves it on a clean machine New team members are productive on day one, and environment drift shows up early

💡 Most of your instincts (clean design, careful SQL, defensive code) still apply. What changed is where the code runs, how it gets there and how you see what it is doing.

🧠 What "cloud-native" means for a .NET developer

"Cloud-native" is used for marketing, so here is a practical definition you can work with. An application is cloud-native when it is designed for an environment where machines are disposable, failures are normal and change is constant. In practice that means a handful of properties:

Property What it means in practice Where you learn it
Disposable processes Any replica can be killed at any time (scale-in, new revision, host maintenance). Startup is fast, shutdown is graceful, and no state lives on local disk. Chapters 7, 14
Configuration from the environment The same container image runs in every environment. Only configuration differs, and secrets are not in config at all. Chapters 3, 13
Everything as code Application, infrastructure, pipelines, policies and dashboards live in git and change through pull requests. Chapters 8, 9, 11
Automated delivery A merge triggers build, test, image, deploy, migration and smoke test with no manual steps. Chapters 9, 12
Designed for failure Dependencies will be slow or down. Retries, timeouts, circuit breakers and graceful degradation are part of the design. Chapter 14
Observable by default Every request produces traces, metrics and structured logs that you can query across all services. Chapters 15, 16
Elastic Capacity follows demand automatically, including scaling to zero where it makes sense. Chapters 10, 18
Identity instead of secrets Services prove who they are with managed identities and tokens, not with passwords in connection strings. Chapter 13

None of these properties needs Kubernetes, microservices or a specific vendor. Ledgerly is a small system on purpose: one API, one worker, one fake vendor service and one migration job. That is enough to practice every property above without the complexity of fifty services.

💡 Cloud-native is a set of habits, not a technology list. You can build a cloud-native monolith and a very fragile set of microservices.

🧠 What changed in .NET while you were busy

If your last big project was on .NET Framework 4.8, the gap is large but not mysterious. Microsoft rebuilt .NET as a cross-platform, open-source runtime (.NET Core, now just ".NET"), and all new investment goes there.

Topic Fact Why it matters for you
Current version .NET 10 is the current LTS release, supported until 2028-11-14 🔧 This course targets net10.0 everywhere
Older modern versions .NET 8 and .NET 9 both reach end of support on 2026-11-10 🔧 If you start something new, don't start on 8 or 9
Release rhythm A new major version every November. Even numbers are LTS (3 years), odd numbers are STS (2 years) 🔧 Plan one upgrade per year or one every two years, as normal maintenance
.NET Framework 4.8 and 4.8.1 are Windows components with no announced end date, but get no new features 🔧 Your old apps are not on fire, but new work belongs on .NET 10
Language C# 14 ships with .NET 10 Records, pattern matching, primary constructors and collection expressions change how idiomatic code looks (chapter 2)

Sources: .NET support policy and .NET Framework lifecycle.

The other big shift is the hosting model. ASP.NET on .NET Framework was tied to IIS and System.Web. ASP.NET Core runs on its own web server (Kestrel) inside your process. IIS can still front it on Windows, but in a container it is just a process listening on port 8080. That one change explains a lot of this course: once your app is a self-contained process, it can run in a container, on Linux, anywhere.

The hosting model, then and now

Concern IIS + .NET Framework Container + .NET 10
Web server IIS + System.Web pipeline Kestrel inside your process
Process lifetime App pool recycles on a schedule or memory limit The platform starts and stops replicas, you handle SIGTERM gracefully
Configuration web.config + transforms appsettings.json + environment variables + Key Vault
Health "Is the app pool running?" /alive and /health endpoints that the platform probes
Scaling Add a VM to the load balancer Change maxReplicas or add a scale rule
Logs Files on the server's disk OpenTelemetry to Application Insights

🧠 Ledgerly: the product and the team

Ledgerly is a small fintech start-up of about 25 people. It is building a personal investment portfolio tracker, also called Ledgerly. Users can:

  • create portfolios and record buys and sells,
  • import broker CSV statements (the slow, bursty part),
  • see their holdings and a live valuation based on market prices from a third-party vendor,
  • set price alerts and get notified.

Version 1 launches as a public beta in about 12 weeks. There is no legacy code: you start greenfield, which is exactly why the team wants to do it "the modern way" from day one.

Your teammates

Name Role What they will push you on
Maya Okafor Tech lead / architect "What happens when it fails?" She writes the ADRs and pairs with you often.
Tomás Varga Platform / DevOps engineer "If it's not in git, it doesn't exist." He owns GitHub Actions, Bicep and the Azure subscription.
Priya Raman Site reliability engineer Observability, SLOs, on-call. She hates alerts that wake people up for nothing.
Jonas Berg Security engineer Managed identities, no secrets in config, least privilege, supply chain. Friendly but strict.
Elena Rossi Product manager "What does the user see?" and "What does it cost?" She owns the beta launch.
Leo Park Frontend developer He will build the React app later, so he cares about API contracts, OpenAPI, CORS and error shapes.

And you: the senior .NET developer with nine years of IIS, WCF and MVC behind you, and a notebook of habits to examine.

🗺️ The target architecture

This is what you will have running in Azure by chapter 12. Every box is real code or real Bicep in the sample.

flowchart LR
    User["Browser or<br/>React app"] -- "HTTPS + JWT" --> Api
    subgraph ACA["Azure Container Apps environment"]
        Api["api<br/>(Container App)"]
        Worker["worker<br/>(Container App)"]
        Market["marketdata<br/>(internal Container App)"]
        Job["job-migrations<br/>(Container Apps job)"]
    end
    Api -- "EF Core" --> Sql[("Azure SQL<br/>Database")]
    Api -- "prices" --> Market
    Api -. "optional L2 cache" .-> Redis[("Azure Managed<br/>Redis")]
    Worker -- "outbox relay<br/>and imports" --> Bus["Azure Service Bus<br/>queue: imports"]
    Worker -- "EF Core" --> Sql
    Worker -- "prices" --> Market
    Job -- "schema changes" --> Sql
    ACA -- "OpenTelemetry" --> AI["Application Insights<br/>+ Log Analytics"]
    KV["Azure Key Vault"] -. "secret references" .-> Api

And this is how a change gets from your laptop to that environment:

flowchart LR
    Dev["You<br/>(Aspire locally)"] -- "push branch" --> PR["Pull request<br/>(review + checks)"]
    PR -- "merge" --> CI["GitHub Actions: ci<br/>build, test, images, Bicep lint"]
    CI -- "green" --> CD["GitHub Actions: deploy<br/>OIDC sign-in, no secrets"]
    CD -- "push images" --> ACR["Azure Container<br/>Registry"]
    CD -- "Bicep" --> Azure["Azure resources"]
    CD -- "run" --> Mig["Migration job"]
    Mig -- "success" --> Rev["New revisions of<br/>api, worker, marketdata"]
    Rev -- "telemetry" --> Obs["Application Insights<br/>dashboards and alerts"]

Who does what

Piece Responsibility Old-world equivalent
Ledgerly.Api HTTP API for portfolios, transactions, valuation, imports, alerts Your ASP.NET Web API 2 site on IIS
Ledgerly.Worker Processes imports from the queue, relays the outbox, evaluates price alerts A Windows Service plus a couple of SQL Agent jobs
Ledgerly.MarketData A fake market-data vendor with "chaos" switches to make it slow or failing The flaky third-party SOAP service everyone complained about
Ledgerly.MigrationService Applies EF Core migrations, then exits The DBA running a script on Thursday night
Ledgerly.AppHost Starts everything locally with Aspire: SQL Server, Redis, the Service Bus emulator and all services A 14-step wiki page called "Developer setup"
Ledgerly.ServiceDefaults Shared OpenTelemetry, health checks, resilience and service discovery Copy-pasted Global.asax and logging config

💻 In Ledgerly: the whole system in one file

You don't need to understand this file yet (chapter 6 explains it line by line). Just notice that the entire local topology, including containers for SQL Server, Redis and a Service Bus emulator, is a C# file you can read and review.

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.AppHost/AppHost.cs

var builder = DistributedApplication.CreateBuilder(args);

// ── Infrastructure (containers locally; real Azure services in the cloud, see infra/) ──────────
var sql = builder.AddSqlServer("sql")
    .WithLifetime(ContainerLifetime.Persistent)   // keep the container (and data) between runs
    .WithDataVolume();
var db = sql.AddDatabase("ledgerlydb");

var cache = builder.AddRedis("cache");

var messaging = builder.AddAzureServiceBus("messaging").RunAsEmulator();
messaging.AddServiceBusQueue("imports");
// ...

Compare that with the wiki page you used to maintain about which SQL instance, which MSMQ queue and which IIS site bindings a new developer needed.

🧠 The stack you will learn, and why each piece is there

Layer Choice in this course Why this one Chapter
Runtime and language .NET 10 (LTS), C# 14, ASP.NET Core 10, EF Core 10 Current LTS, longest support window 2, 4, 5
Local orchestration Aspire (AppHost, service defaults, dashboard) 🔧 One command starts the system, with telemetry from the first run 6
Source control and collaboration GitHub: pull requests, rulesets, CODEOWNERS, Dependabot, CodeQL, GitHub Copilot Where the team works, reviews and automates 8, 20
CI/CD GitHub Actions with OIDC to Azure No stored cloud secrets, build once and deploy the same image 9, 12
Compute Azure Container Apps Containers and scale-to-zero without running Kubernetes yourself 10
Data Azure SQL Database (serverless) Your SQL Server skills transfer directly 5, 10
Messaging Azure Service Bus Durable queues with dead-lettering and duplicate detection 7
Caching HybridCache, optionally backed by Azure Managed Redis 🔧 Fast reads with stampede protection 18
Secrets and identity Microsoft Entra ID, managed identities, Azure Key Vault No passwords to leak or rotate 13
Infrastructure Bicep Readable, Azure-native infrastructure as code 11
Observability OpenTelemetry, Application Insights, Azure Monitor, KQL One standard for traces, metrics and logs, and one place to query them 15, 16

A note on two names that may surprise you:

  • Aspire used to be called ".NET Aspire". The product is now just "Aspire" 🔧.
  • Azure Managed Redis is the Redis service for new work. Azure Cache for Redis is being retired in stages 🔧 (chapter 10 has the dates).

🧠 Your habits notebook

Maya was right: most of your habits are good. The notebook is a tool for sorting them. Here is the first page, as you write it on day one:

Habit Keep, change or drop? Where it is examined
"I want to see the logs" Change. You still want evidence, but you'll get it from queries over structured telemetry, not files 15, 16
"Test it in staging before Thursday" Change. Test on every pull request, deploy small changes often, and roll back fast 9, 12, 17
"The DBA runs the migration script" Change. Migrations are a pipeline step with a job that must succeed before the new code starts 5, 12
"Put the password in web.config and encrypt the section" Drop. Use managed identities, so there is no password 13
"Retry in a loop with Thread.Sleep" Change. Use standard resilience handlers with jitter, timeouts and circuit breakers 14
"Careful SQL and indexes" Keep. Azure SQL is still SQL Server underneath, and EF Core still needs your judgment 5, 18
"Read the code before you approve it" Keep. It matters even more when an AI wrote part of it 8, 20

💡 The goal is not to forget what you know. It is to move each habit to the place where it works in the cloud.

⚠️ Common traps

Trap What happens Do this instead
"Lift and shift" thinking: one container that behaves like a VM You write logs to local disk, keep session state in memory and SSH in to debug. The first scale-in or restart loses data Treat replicas as disposable from day one: state in the database or cache, telemetry out of the process
Starting with microservices Ten services, ten pipelines and distributed transactions for a product with no users yet Start with a few deployable units with clear jobs (like Ledgerly) and split only when there's a real reason
Learning Kubernetes first Weeks on cluster plumbing before shipping any feature Use a managed platform (Container Apps) and learn the concepts (replicas, probes, revisions) that transfer anywhere
Copying old patterns into new code async void, .Result, static HttpClient hacks and string-concatenated SQL show up in a modern codebase Use chapter 2 as a translation guide and let analyzers enforce it (chapter 3)
Treating "the cloud" as one big decision Analysis paralysis over every service Use the decision tables in chapter 10, and accept that most choices can be changed later through infrastructure as code
Trusting version numbers from memory Last year's blog post says a feature is preview, or a service is current, when it changed Check the 🔧 items against the linked docs before you rely on them

🧰 Helpers

Get the sample on your machine:

git clone https://github.com/vygantas-meskauskis/cloud-native-dotnet.git
cd cloud-native-dotnet/sample
dotnet --list-sdks        # you need a 10.0.x SDK
dotnet build Ledgerly.slnx

What to install before chapter 6:

Tool Why
.NET 10 SDK Build and run everything
Docker Desktop or Podman Aspire runs SQL Server, Redis and the Service Bus emulator as containers
Aspire CLI 🔧 aspire run starts the whole system (installation is covered in chapter 6)
Git + a GitHub account Chapters 8 and 9
An IDE: Visual Studio 2026, VS Code with C# Dev Kit, or Rider 🔧 Any of them works with the sample
An Azure subscription (later) Only from chapter 11 on. 💰 Most resources in the sample are serverless or have free monthly grants, but not all

Make it permanent: keep your own "Habits I brought with me" page. The toolkit has an old-to-new cheat sheet that you can extend as you go. See the toolkit and the glossary for any term you don't recognize.

🧪 Try it yourself (optional, ~15 min)

  1. Clone the repository and build the sample: bash cd cloud-native-dotnet/sample dotnet build Ledgerly.slnx
  2. Look at the solution layout. Open Ledgerly.slnx in your editor and match each project to the "Who does what" table above.
  3. Open src/Ledgerly.AppHost/AppHost.cs and find, without reading the docs, the line that tells the API to wait for the migrations to finish. (Hint: WaitForCompletion.)
  4. Run the unit tests (they don't need a database): bash dotnet test --solution Ledgerly.slnx Observe: without a container runtime or the LEDGERLY_TEST_SQL variable, the integration tests are skipped, not failed. Chapter 17 explains why.
  5. Write the first page of your own habits notebook: three habits you suspect will change, and one you are sure you'll keep.

✅ Recap

  1. Cloud-native means disposable processes, configuration from the environment, everything as code, automated delivery, designed for failure and observable by default.
  2. Ledgerly is a small, realistic system (API, worker, fake vendor, migration job) that you will build, ship and run on .NET 10, GitHub and Azure.
  3. Your experience still counts. The course moves each habit to where it works today.

Next → Chapter 2: Modern .NET in one sitting

Chapter 2: Modern .NET in one sitting

⏱ About 30 min · You'll learn: how the hosting model, DI and configuration replaced Global.asax and web.config, which C# features you will meet in every Ledgerly file, and which small modern APIs (TimeProvider, Guid.CreateVersion7(), [LoggerMessage]) replace old habits.

📖 Story: "This reads like C# 5"

Your first pull request at Ledgerly is small: a service that values a portfolio. You wrote it the way you wrote hundreds of classes at Nordbank Insurance. A constructor that assigns six private fields. A new HttpClient() inside a using block. DateTime.Now for the timestamp. A .Result on one call because the method above it wasn't async. A log line built with string.Format. And a null check at the top of every method.

Maya's review has one comment at the top, and it is friendly: "This works, and the logic is right. But it reads like C# 5. Let's pair for an hour and I'll show you what changed. Most of it is less code, not more." Below that are twelve inline suggestions, and each one deletes lines.

You open your notebook, Habits I brought with me, and write a new heading: Things the compiler and the framework now do for me. This chapter is that hour with Maya. It is a translation guide, not a tutorial: you already know how to program, so the focus is on what moved and why.

🏚️ Old way → 🚀 New way

What you did before What you do now Why it changed
Global.asax Application_Start, HTTP modules and handlers in IIS Program.cs with WebApplication.CreateBuilder, middleware and endpoints One explicit startup file you can read top to bottom, with no IIS pipeline hidden behind it
Unity, Autofac or Ninject wired up by hand Built-in dependency injection (builder.Services) Every framework component already uses it, so your code plugs into the same container
ConfigurationManager.AppSettings["Key"] and web.config transforms IConfiguration from layered providers, bound to options classes The same build runs everywhere, and environment variables override files without transforms
new HttpClient() per call, or one static instance IHttpClientFactory with typed clients Handler lifetimes and resilience are managed for you, configured once at registration
.Result, .Wait() and async void mixed with sync code async all the way, with a CancellationToken passed down No thread-pool starvation under load, and work stops when the caller gives up
Null checks at the top of every method Nullable reference types plus required members The compiler tells you where nulls can flow, before the code runs
DateTime.Now and Guid.NewGuid() in domain code TimeProvider and Guid.CreateVersion7() Time becomes testable, and IDs sort by creation time
log.InfoFormat("Order {0} placed", id) into a text file Structured logging with [LoggerMessage] source generation Placeholders become queryable fields, and disabled log levels cost nothing

💡 The biggest change isn't a language feature. It's that the framework owns startup, DI, configuration and logging, and your code joins that model instead of building its own.

🧠 The hosting model: from IIS to a process you own

On .NET Framework, IIS owned your process. It loaded System.Web, ran Global.asax, pushed the request through HTTP modules and finally reached your controller. You configured half of the behavior in IIS Manager and the other half in web.config.

In ASP.NET Core, your app is the process. The web server, Kestrel, runs inside it. In a Linux container, that process listens on port 8080 by default 🔧 (it used to be 80; set it with ASPNETCORE_HTTP_PORTS). IIS can still sit in front on Windows, but nothing in Ledgerly needs it.

Translation table

.NET Framework concept .NET 10 equivalent Notes
Global.asax Application_Start Top of Program.cs, before builder.Build() Register services and read configuration here
HTTP modules (IHttpModule) Middleware (app.UseXyz()) Order in Program.cs is the order requests flow through
HTTP handlers and controllers Endpoints: minimal APIs or controllers Ledgerly uses minimal APIs with route groups (chapter 4)
HttpContext.Current (static) HttpContext passed or injected where needed No static ambient context, which also makes code testable
Windows Service with OnStart/OnStop BackgroundService hosted by the generic host Same host, same DI, same logging as the web app (chapter 7)
App pool recycle The platform starts and stops replicas, and you shut down gracefully HostOptions.ShutdownTimeout defaults to 30 s 🔧
Console app with a Main that news everything up Host.CreateApplicationBuilder(args) You get configuration, DI and logging even in a console tool

There are two builders, and both come from the same generic host (docs): WebApplication.CreateBuilder(args) for anything that serves HTTP (the API, the worker and the market-data service, even if only for health endpoints), and Host.CreateApplicationBuilder(args) for processes with no HTTP surface (the migration service).

💻 In Ledgerly: the API's composition root

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Api/Program.cs

var builder = WebApplication.CreateBuilder(args);

// ── Cross-cutting: OpenTelemetry, health checks, resilience, service discovery ────────────────
builder.AddServiceDefaults();

// ── Data: Aspire client integration = DbContext pooling + retries + health check + tracing ──────
builder.AddSqlServerDbContext<LedgerlyDbContext>("ledgerlydb");
// ...
var app = builder.Build();

app.UseExceptionHandler();
app.UseStatusCodePages();
app.UseCors(ApiPolicies.Spa);
app.UseAuthentication();
app.UseAuthorization();
app.UseRateLimiter();
// ...
app.MapPortfolioEndpoints();
app.MapImportEndpoints();
app.MapAlertEndpoints();
app.MapDefaultEndpoints();

app.Run();

Read it as two phases:

  1. Before Build(): you describe services. Nothing runs yet. This is where Application_Start code goes.
  2. After Build(): you describe the request pipeline. Each Use... line is a middleware, in order. The Map... lines attach endpoints. app.Run() starts Kestrel and blocks until shutdown.

Notice the file is short. Cross-cutting concerns (OpenTelemetry, health checks, retries) come from one call, AddServiceDefaults(), which lives in a shared project (chapter 6). That's the modern answer to the Global.asax you used to copy from project to project.

The migration runner shows the same model without HTTP:

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.MigrationService/Program.cs

var builder = Host.CreateApplicationBuilder(args);

builder.AddServiceDefaults();
builder.AddSqlServerDbContext<LedgerlyDbContext>("ledgerlydb");
builder.Services.Configure<AppDatabaseUserOptions>(builder.Configuration.GetSection(AppDatabaseUserOptions.Section));
builder.Services.AddHostedService<Migrator>();

builder.Build().Run();

Same configuration, same DI, same telemetry, no web server. Chapter 5 explains what Migrator does.

💡 If you can find it in Program.cs, it happens. If it isn't there, it doesn't. There is no hidden IIS configuration anymore.

🗺️ How a request flows through the API

This is the middleware order from Program.cs, drawn as the path one request takes:

flowchart LR
    Client["Client<br/>browser or SPA"] -- "HTTPS" --> Kestrel["Kestrel<br/>inside the process"]
    Kestrel --> Ex["UseExceptionHandler<br/>errors become ProblemDetails"]
    Ex --> Status["UseStatusCodePages<br/>bare 4xx get a body"]
    Status --> Cors["UseCors<br/>SPA origins only"]
    Cors --> AuthN["UseAuthentication<br/>JWT bearer"]
    AuthN --> AuthZ["UseAuthorization<br/>RequireAuthorization"]
    AuthZ --> Rate["UseRateLimiter<br/>per-user policies"]
    Rate --> Endpoint["Endpoint handler<br/>services injected"]
    Endpoint -- "DbContext" --> Db[("SQL")]

Compare it with the IIS pipeline you knew: the same idea (a chain of steps around your code), but you can see every step, and you choose the order.

🧠 DI, configuration, options and HttpClient

Dependency injection is built in

You don't choose a container anymore, unless you have a strong reason. builder.Services is an IServiceCollection, and every framework feature registers itself there. Three lifetimes cover nearly everything:

Lifetime Meaning Ledgerly example
Singleton One instance for the process TimeProvider.System
Scoped One instance per request (or per scope you create) LedgerlyDbContext, ValuationService
Transient New instance every time Rarely needed in Ledgerly

The one trap that bites experienced developers: a singleton (like a BackgroundService) must not hold a scoped service such as a DbContext. Create a scope instead. AlertEvaluator takes an IServiceScopeFactory for exactly this reason (chapter 7).

Configuration is layered, not transformed

WebApplication.CreateBuilder reads configuration from several providers, and later ones override earlier ones. A key such as MarketData:BaseUrl in JSON becomes MarketData__BaseUrl as an environment variable (double underscore instead of a colon). That's how the same container image gets different settings in every environment, with no transforms and no rebuild.

flowchart LR
    A["appsettings.json<br/>defaults in git"] --> B["appsettings.Development.json<br/>local overrides"]
    B --> C["User secrets<br/>Development only"]
    C --> D["Environment variables<br/>set by Aspire or Container Apps"]
    D --> E["Command line"]
    E --> Config["IConfiguration<br/>last value wins"]
    Config --> Opt["IOptions of T<br/>typed settings"]
    Config --> Cs["GetConnectionString<br/>ConnectionStrings__ledgerlydb"]

Locally, Aspire injects connection strings as environment variables (chapter 6). In Azure, Bicep sets them on the Container App (chapter 11). Secrets don't go in any of these files at all: chapter 13 replaces them with managed identities.

Options instead of magic strings

The options pattern binds a configuration section to a class once, and code receives a typed object. No more int.Parse(ConfigurationManager.AppSettings["AlertInterval"]) scattered across the codebase.

IHttpClientFactory and typed clients

You probably remember the advice to "make HttpClient static" and then the DNS problems that came with it. The factory manages the underlying handlers for you. A typed client is a class that takes an HttpClient in its constructor, and the registration decides its base address and behavior.

💻 In Ledgerly: the worker's registrations

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Worker/Program.cs

builder.Services.AddHttpClient<IMarketDataClient, MarketDataClient>(client =>
    client.BaseAddress = new Uri(builder.Configuration["MarketData:BaseUrl"] ?? "https+http://marketdata"));

builder.Services.AddSingleton(TimeProvider.System);
builder.Services.Configure<AlertOptions>(builder.Configuration.GetSection("Alerts"));

builder.Services.AddHostedService<OutboxRelay>();
builder.Services.AddHostedService<ImportProcessor>();
builder.Services.AddHostedService<AlertEvaluator>();

// Give in-flight messages time to finish when Container Apps stops a replica (SIGTERM).
builder.Services.Configure<HostOptions>(o => o.ShutdownTimeout = TimeSpan.FromSeconds(25));
Line Old-world equivalent
AddHttpClient<IMarketDataClient, MarketDataClient> A static HttpClient plus a hand-written retry loop
"https+http://marketdata" A hard-coded URL per environment in web.config. Here service discovery resolves a logical name (chapter 6)
Configure<AlertOptions>(...GetSection("Alerts")) AppSettings["AlertIntervalSeconds"] parsed by hand
AddHostedService<...> Three Windows Services, each with its own installer
ShutdownTimeout = 25 s Nothing: IIS just recycled and in-flight work was lost

The typed client itself is now almost nothing but HTTP:

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Core/Market/MarketDataClient.cs

/// <summary>
/// Typed HttpClient for the market-data service. Base address, retries, timeouts and the circuit breaker
/// are configured at registration time (see ServiceDefaults), not here. This class just speaks HTTP.
/// </summary>
public sealed class MarketDataClient(HttpClient http) : IMarketDataClient
{
    public async Task<IReadOnlyDictionary<string, Quote>> GetQuotesAsync(IReadOnlyCollection<string> symbols, CancellationToken cancellationToken)
    {
        // ...
        var query = Uri.EscapeDataString(string.Join(',', symbols));
        var quotes = await http.GetFromJsonAsync<Quote[]>($"/prices?symbols={query}", cancellationToken) ?? [];
        return quotes.ToDictionary(q => q.Symbol, StringComparer.OrdinalIgnoreCase);
    }
}

Retries, timeouts and the circuit breaker are not in this class. They are added to every HttpClient by ConfigureHttpClientDefaults in the service defaults, using the standard resilience handler (chapter 14).

💡 Configuration says what and where. DI decides who gets it. Your classes just declare what they need in a constructor.

🧠 Async all the way, cancellation and nullability

Async is the default, not an optimization

At Nordbank, async was something you added to a hot path. In ASP.NET Core, the whole stack is async: EF Core, HttpClient, Service Bus, file uploads. Blocking on async code with .Result or .Wait() ties up a thread-pool thread while it waits, and under load that's how you get a service that is "up" but doesn't answer.

Old habit Modern rule
.Result or .Wait() on a Task await it, and make the caller async too
async void methods async Task, except for real event handlers
ConfigureAwait(false) everywhere Not needed in ASP.NET Core app code. The sample turns off CA2007 for this reason
No way to stop work when the client leaves Accept a CancellationToken and pass it to every async call
Thread.Sleep in a polling loop PeriodicTimer or Task.Delay with a token

CancellationToken: the parameter you'll type a thousand times

In a minimal API handler, a CancellationToken parameter is bound to the request: if the client disconnects, the token fires. In a BackgroundService, the token passed to ExecuteAsync fires on shutdown (SIGTERM from Container Apps). Pass it down to EF Core, HttpClient and the Service Bus SDK, and your app stops cleanly instead of finishing work nobody is waiting for.

💻 In Ledgerly: a loop that stops when the host stops

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Worker/AlertEvaluator.cs

public sealed partial class AlertEvaluator(
    IServiceScopeFactory scopes, IMarketDataClient marketData, IOptions<AlertOptions> options, TimeProvider clock,
    ILogger<AlertEvaluator> logger) : BackgroundService
{
    protected override async Task ExecuteAsync(CancellationToken stoppingToken)
    {
        using var timer = new PeriodicTimer(TimeSpan.FromSeconds(options.Value.IntervalSeconds), clock);
        while (await timer.WaitForNextTickAsync(stoppingToken))
        {
            try
            {
                await EvaluateAsync(stoppingToken);
            }
            catch (Exception ex) when (ex is not OperationCanceledException)
            {
                // One bad tick (market data down, DB failover) must not kill the loop.
                LogEvaluationFailed(logger, ex);
            }
        }
    }
    // ...
}

Five modern ideas in twenty lines: a primary constructor, an options object, a TimeProvider-driven PeriodicTimer, a token that ends the loop on shutdown, and an exception filter that lets cancellation through while it logs everything else.

Nullable reference types

Every Ledgerly project has <Nullable>enable</Nullable> (chapter 3). string means "never null" and string? means "might be null". The compiler warns when you dereference something that might be null, and with warnings as errors, that warning stops the build.

Before After
if (name == null) throw new ArgumentNullException(...) in every method string name in the signature, and the compiler checks the callers
Comments like "may return null" Task<PortfolioValuation?> in the return type
Guard analyzers such as CA1062 Turned off in the sample's .editorconfig, because "nullable reference types already cover argument null checks"

💡 Nullable annotations are documentation that the compiler enforces. Treat a ? like a contract, not decoration.

🧠 The C# you will see in every Ledgerly file

net10.0 projects use C# 14 by default (what's new). You skipped a lot of versions. Here are the features that change how everyday code looks, with the version that introduced them and where they show up in Ledgerly.

Feature Since What it replaces Where in Ledgerly
Records C# 9 Hand-written DTOs with Equals/GetHashCode Request and response types, Holding, Quote
init-only setters, target-typed new() C# 9 Mutable DTOs, repeated type names Entities such as Portfolio
Top-level statements C# 9 class Program { static void Main } Every Program.cs
File-scoped namespaces C# 10 One extra level of indentation in every file Every file, enforced by .editorconfig
global using, implicit usings C# 10 15 using lines at the top of each file <ImplicitUsings>enable</ImplicitUsings>
Raw string literals """...""" C# 11 Escaped SQL and JSON strings The SQL in EnsureAppUserAsync
required members C# 11 Constructors with ten parameters, or forgotten properties Portfolio.OwnerId, Transaction.Symbol
List and relational patterns C# 9 to 11 if chains with && and || file.Length is 0 or > MaxFileBytes
Primary constructors C# 12 Constructor plus private readonly fields Services, typed clients, DbContext
Collection expressions [...] and spread .. C# 12 new List<T>(), ToArray(), Concat [] defaults, [.. portfolio.Transactions, transaction]
System.Threading.Lock C# 13 lock on a private object ChaosState in the fake market-data service

Source for the versions: C# version history, C# 12 and C# 13.

💻 In Ledgerly: records, primary constructors and collection expressions

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Core/Domain/Holdings.cs

namespace Ledgerly.Core.Domain;

public sealed record Holding(string Symbol, decimal Quantity, decimal AverageCost)
{
    public decimal CostBasis => Quantity * AverageCost;
}

public sealed class HoldingsException(string message) : Exception(message);

A positional record gives you a constructor, init-only properties, value equality and a readable ToString() in one line. The exception is a whole class in one line too: the primary constructor passes message to the base class.

One detail that surprises people: in a class with a primary constructor, the parameters are captured, not turned into properties. Only records generate public properties. So MarketDataClient(HttpClient http) keeps http private, which is what you want.

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Core/Domain/Portfolio.cs

public sealed class Portfolio
{
    public Guid Id { get; init; } = Guid.CreateVersion7();
    public required string OwnerId { get; init; }
    public required string Name { get; set; }
    public string BaseCurrency { get; init; } = "EUR";
    // ...
    public List<Transaction> Transactions { get; init; } = [];
}

required means an object initializer that forgets OwnerId doesn't compile. = [] is a collection expression: the compiler picks the right construction for List<Transaction>.

💻 In Ledgerly: patterns and raw strings

Pattern matching replaces many small if chains. From the import endpoint:

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Api/Endpoints/ImportEndpoints.cs

        if (file.Length is 0 or > MaxFileBytes)
        {
            return TypedResults.ValidationProblem(new Dictionary<string, string[]> { ["file"] = [$"File must be between 1 byte and {MaxFileBytes} bytes."] });
        }

And a property pattern that checks two optional settings and extracts them in one expression, from the migrator:

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.MigrationService/Program.cs

            if (appUser.Value is { Name: { Length: > 0 } name, ClientId: { Length: > 0 } clientId })
            {
                await strategy.ExecuteAsync(async () => await EnsureAppUserAsync(db, name, Guid.Parse(clientId), stoppingToken));
                LogAppUserEnsured(logger, name);
            }

The same file builds a SQL batch with a raw string literal, so the T-SQL reads like T-SQL:

        var sql = $"""
            IF NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM sys.database_principals WHERE name = N'{user.Replace("'", "''", StringComparison.Ordinal)}')
                CREATE USER [{user}] WITH SID = {sid}, TYPE = E;
            ALTER ROLE db_datareader ADD MEMBER [{user}];
            ALTER ROLE db_datawriter ADD MEMBER [{user}];
            """;

Two C# 14 features worth knowing

The sample doesn't use these yet, but you will see them in new code. Both are from the C# 14 notes.

// illustrative, not in the sample
public sealed class Position
{
    // `field` is the compiler-generated backing field: no private _symbol needed.
    public required string Symbol
    {
        get;
        set => field = value.Trim().ToUpperInvariant();
    }
}

public static class EnumerableExtensions
{
    // Extension members: properties (and static members) on types you don't own.
    extension<T>(IEnumerable<T> source)
    {
        public bool IsEmpty => !source.Any();
    }
}

Use field when a property needs one small rule, and extension members when you want items.IsEmpty instead of items.IsEmpty(). If the logic grows beyond a line, make it a method or a value object instead.

💡 Modern C# mostly removes ceremony. If a feature makes the line shorter and the intent clearer, use it. If it only makes the line shorter, don't.

🧠 Small APIs that replace big habits

TimeProvider: time as a dependency

DateTime.Now in domain code makes tests depend on the clock. TimeProvider is an abstract class in the base library. Production registers TimeProvider.System, and tests pass a FakeTimeProvider that you can move forward.

📄 sample/tests/Ledgerly.UnitTests/PriceTests.cs

        // TimeProvider makes "time" a dependency you can control in tests. No more DateTime.Now in domain code.
        var clock = new FakeTimeProvider(new DateTimeOffset(2026, 10, 28, 12, 0, 0, TimeSpan.Zero));
        var generator = new PriceGenerator(clock);

        var first = generator.GetQuote("aapl");
        clock.Advance(TimeSpan.FromSeconds(20));
        var sameMinute = generator.GetQuote("AAPL");
        clock.Advance(TimeSpan.FromHours(3));
        var later = generator.GetQuote("AAPL");

No Thread.Sleep, no flaky test that fails at midnight.

Guid.CreateVersion7(): IDs that sort

Every Ledgerly entity gets its ID in C#, not from the database: Guid.CreateVersion7(). A version 7 GUID starts with a timestamp, so new IDs are roughly in creation order. You get IDs you can generate before saving (useful for outbox messages and Location headers) without the random insert pattern that made you avoid GUID keys in SQL Server.

There is one sharp edge with client-generated keys in EF Core, and you will hit it in chapter 5.

[LoggerMessage]: structured logs, generated

Old habit: log.InfoFormat("Price for {0} unavailable", symbol). That produces a string, and strings are hard to query. Modern logging uses message templates with named placeholders, and the [LoggerMessage] source generator writes the fast code for you (docs).

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Api/Services/ValuationService.cs

public sealed partial class ValuationService(
    LedgerlyDbContext db, IMarketDataClient marketData, HybridCache cache, TimeProvider clock, ILogger<ValuationService> logger)
{
    // ...
    [LoggerMessage(Level = LogLevel.Warning, Message = "Price for {Symbol} unavailable ({Reason}); returning partial valuation")]
    private static partial void LogPriceUnavailable(ILogger logger, string symbol, string reason);
}
What you get Why it matters
Symbol and Reason become separate, queryable fields In chapter 16 you filter on them with KQL instead of grepping text
No allocations when the level is disabled Debug logging in hot paths stops costing you
Compile-time checks A method parameter that isn't referenced in the template is flagged (SYSLIB1015), and with warnings as errors, that's a broken build

The class must be partial so the generator can add the method body. Chapter 15 goes deeper.

What's gone, and where it went

Some things you relied on simply don't exist in .NET 10. This list is from experience, not an exhaustive compatibility matrix. Before you plan a real port of a Nordbank-style app, check the docs for each technology.

What you used In .NET 10 Where Ledgerly does it instead
System.Web: HttpContext.Current, HTTP modules, Global.asax Not part of ASP.NET Core Middleware and endpoints in Program.cs
WCF services (server side) Not part of .NET 10. Check the docs for current options before porting a WCF service HTTP APIs with OpenAPI for request/response, Azure Service Bus for messages
AppDomains for isolation and unloading You can't create extra AppDomains for isolation Separate processes or containers
.NET Remoting Not available HTTP APIs or messaging
web.config for app settings Only relevant if you host behind IIS appsettings.json, environment variables, Key Vault (chapter 13)
Windows-only assumptions (registry, event log, C:\ paths) The app runs on Linux in a container Configuration, OpenTelemetry, and no local disk state

.NET Framework 4.8 and 4.8.1 have no announced end date 🔧: they are supported as long as they're installed on a supported Windows version. They just aren't where new features land (lifecycle). Your old apps are fine where they are. New work, like Ledgerly, belongs on .NET 10, which is supported until 2028-11-14 🔧 (support policy).

💡 Most "missing" features were ways to share one big process. In the cloud you share nothing in-process: you split by process, and talk over HTTP or messages.

⚠️ Common traps

Trap What happens Do this instead
Injecting a DbContext into a BackgroundService constructor A scoped service is captured by a singleton. You get stale tracking and threading bugs, or a startup error from scope validation Inject IServiceScopeFactory and create a scope per unit of work, as AlertEvaluator does
.Result "just this once" in a request path Thread-pool starvation under load, and the service hangs while health checks still pass Make the call chain async. Analyzers and code review catch the rest
Forgetting to pass the CancellationToken Shutdown waits for work nobody needs, and requests keep running after the client left Accept CancellationToken ct in every async method and pass it on
Reading IConfiguration["Some:Key"] all over the code Typos fail at runtime, and there's no single place that lists the settings Bind a section to an options class once, inject IOptions<T>
new HttpClient() per call Socket and DNS problems under load, and no retries or timeouts Typed clients via AddHttpClient<TClient, TImpl> and the service defaults
DateTime.Now in domain logic Tests that depend on the time of day, and local-time bugs on servers in UTC TimeProvider (inject it) and DateTimeOffset
String-interpolated log messages Every value is baked into one string, so nothing is queryable Message templates, ideally [LoggerMessage]

🧰 Helpers

Your translation checklist for code review (copy it into your notes):

  • [ ] No .Result, .Wait() or async void outside event handlers
  • [ ] Every async method takes and passes a CancellationToken
  • [ ] Services get dependencies through a (primary) constructor, never new or a static
  • [ ] Settings come from an options class, not raw IConfiguration lookups in business code
  • [ ] Outbound HTTP uses a typed client registered with AddHttpClient
  • [ ] Time comes from TimeProvider, IDs from Guid.CreateVersion7()
  • [ ] Logs use templates with named placeholders, and hot paths use [LoggerMessage]
  • [ ] DTOs are records, required members are marked required, and nullability is honest

A minimal modern host, if you want to see the bare shape without Ledgerly around it:

// illustrative, not in the sample
var builder = Host.CreateApplicationBuilder(args);
builder.Services.AddSingleton(TimeProvider.System);
builder.Services.AddHostedService<Heartbeat>();
builder.Build().Run();

internal sealed partial class Heartbeat(TimeProvider clock, ILogger<Heartbeat> logger) : BackgroundService
{
    protected override async Task ExecuteAsync(CancellationToken stoppingToken)
    {
        using var timer = new PeriodicTimer(TimeSpan.FromSeconds(5), clock);
        while (await timer.WaitForNextTickAsync(stoppingToken))
        {
            LogTick(logger, clock.GetUtcNow());
        }
    }

    [LoggerMessage(Level = LogLevel.Information, Message = "Tick at {Now}")]
    private static partial void LogTick(ILogger logger, DateTimeOffset now);
}

Make it permanent: don't rely on memory. Chapter 3 turns most of this checklist into build rules: nullable enabled everywhere, file-scoped namespaces and primary constructors in .editorconfig, analyzers at latest-recommended, and warnings as errors. The toolkit's cheat-sheet-old-to-new.md has the old-to-new tables from this chapter on one page (see the toolkit), and the glossary explains terms like generic host and typed client.

🧪 Try it yourself (optional, ~20 min)

  1. Read the composition root and map it to your old world: bash cd sample Open src/Ledgerly.Api/Program.cs. For each line before builder.Build(), write down what you would have done in Global.asax or web.config. For each Use... line after it, name the IIS module or web.config section it replaces.
  2. Find the modern C# features from the table in the real code. Open src/Ledgerly.Api/Endpoints/PortfolioEndpoints.cs and find a record with validation attributes, a collection expression with a spread, and a handler that takes a CancellationToken.
  3. Try the C# 14 features in a file-based app. In a scratch folder outside sample/ (so the sample's strict build settings don't apply), create scratch.cs with the Position class and the extension block from this chapter, plus these top-level lines at the top of the file: csharp // illustrative, not in the sample var p = new Position { Symbol = " vwce " }; Console.WriteLine(p.Symbol); int[] none = []; Console.WriteLine(none.IsEmpty); Console.WriteLine(Guid.CreateVersion7()); Then run it: bash dotnet run scratch.cs Observe: VWCE, True, and a GUID. Run it twice and compare the first characters of the two GUIDs: version 7 GUIDs created later sort later.
  4. Build the sample and check that it's clean: bash cd sample dotnet build Ledgerly.slnx

✅ Recap

  1. Your app is now the process: Program.cs builds the host (services first, then the middleware pipeline), and DI, configuration and logging are built in.
  2. Async with cancellation, nullable reference types, records, primary constructors and collection expressions are the everyday shape of modern C#, and they mostly remove code.
  3. Small APIs retire old habits: typed HttpClients, options classes, TimeProvider, Guid.CreateVersion7() and [LoggerMessage].

Next → Chapter 3: A solution set up like a pro

Chapter 3: A solution set up like a pro

⏱ About 30 min · You'll learn: how Ledgerly's repo-wide files (Ledgerly.slnx, Directory.Build.props, Directory.Packages.props, global.json, nuget.config, .editorconfig, local tools) make a fresh clone build the same way everywhere, and why a vulnerable package you never referenced can fail the build.

📖 Story: "Clone it and build it, or it's broken"

On your second day, Tomás hands you a brand-new laptop that has nothing on it but the .NET 10 SDK, Git and Docker. "Ledgerly has one rule," he says. "Clone it and build it on a fresh laptop in one command, or it's broken. No wiki page with twelve setup steps. No 'works on my machine.' If you need a step, it goes in the repo."

You think of Nordbank. The build server had a hand-installed SDK nobody dared to upgrade. Each project had its own packages.config with slightly different versions of Newtonsoft.Json. The analyzers were "on" in one project and silently off in the others. Getting a new developer productive took two days and a senior engineer.

You clone, cd sample, run dotnet build Ledgerly.slnx, and it's green. Then Tomás tells you about the morning it wasn't: nobody had touched a single package, and the build went red with NU1903. A package Ledgerly never referenced directly had a known high-severity vulnerability. "That's the setup working," he says. "I'd rather the build tell me than an auditor." You write in your habits notebook: Build settings are code. They live in the repo, and they fail loudly.

🏚️ Old way → 🚀 New way

What you did before What you do now Why it changed
A .sln file full of GUIDs that merges badly Ledgerly.slnx: short, readable XML Starting with .NET 10, slnx is the default format 🔧, and diffs make sense
The same settings copy-pasted into every .csproj One Directory.Build.props at the root Every project inherits nullable, analyzers and warnings as errors, and none can drift
packages.config or versions in every project file Central Package Management in Directory.Packages.props One version per package for the whole solution, upgraded in one place
Whatever SDK the build server happened to have global.json pins the SDK band Your laptop, CI and teammates use the same compiler
Machine-wide NuGet feeds nobody remembers adding nuget.config with <clear /> and package source mapping Restores only from the sources the repo names, which blocks dependency confusion
Style rules in a wiki, enforced in code review .editorconfig plus analyzers that run on build The build rejects style violations, so reviews talk about design
"Install dotnet-ef globally, version whatever" .config/dotnet-tools.json and dotnet tool restore Tool versions are pinned in git like packages
Security scans once a quarter NuGet audit on every restore, with warnings as errors A vulnerable transitive package fails the build the day it's disclosed

💡 A pro setup isn't about clever MSBuild. It's about making the right thing the default, so nobody has to remember it.

🧠 The solution layout and .slnx

Ledgerly has nine projects. The layout is deliberately boring: src/ for things that ship, tests/ for things that verify them, and a handful of repo-wide files at the root.

sample/
├── Ledgerly.slnx
├── Directory.Build.props        compiler and quality settings for every project
├── Directory.Packages.props     every NuGet version, in one place
├── global.json                  SDK pin and test runner
├── nuget.config                 package sources
├── .editorconfig                code style and analyzer severities
├── .config/dotnet-tools.json    local tools (dotnet-ef)
├── src/
│   ├── Ledgerly.AppHost/            Aspire orchestration (chapter 6)
│   ├── Ledgerly.ServiceDefaults/    telemetry, health, resilience
│   ├── Ledgerly.Core/               domain, DbContext, migrations, contracts
│   ├── Ledgerly.Api/                minimal API (chapter 4)
│   ├── Ledgerly.Worker/             background processing (chapter 7)
│   ├── Ledgerly.MarketData/         fake price service
│   └── Ledgerly.MigrationService/   runs migrations, then exits (chapter 5)
└── tests/
    ├── Ledgerly.UnitTests/
    └── Ledgerly.IntegrationTests/

💻 In Ledgerly: the whole solution file

📄 sample/Ledgerly.slnx

<Solution>
  <Folder Name="/src/">
    <Project Path="src/Ledgerly.AppHost/Ledgerly.AppHost.csproj" />
    <Project Path="src/Ledgerly.ServiceDefaults/Ledgerly.ServiceDefaults.csproj" />
    <Project Path="src/Ledgerly.Core/Ledgerly.Core.csproj" />
    <Project Path="src/Ledgerly.Api/Ledgerly.Api.csproj" />
    <Project Path="src/Ledgerly.Worker/Ledgerly.Worker.csproj" />
    <Project Path="src/Ledgerly.MarketData/Ledgerly.MarketData.csproj" />
    <Project Path="src/Ledgerly.MigrationService/Ledgerly.MigrationService.csproj" />
  </Folder>
  <Folder Name="/tests/">
    <Project Path="tests/Ledgerly.UnitTests/Ledgerly.UnitTests.csproj" />
    <Project Path="tests/Ledgerly.IntegrationTests/Ledgerly.IntegrationTests.csproj" />
  </Folder>
  <Folder Name="/Solution Items/">
    <File Path=".editorconfig" />
    <File Path="Directory.Build.props" />
    <File Path="Directory.Packages.props" />
    <File Path="global.json" />
    <File Path="nuget.config" />
  </Folder>
</Solution>

That's the entire file. No project-type GUIDs, no per-configuration platform matrix, no GlobalSection blocks. When two people add a project on the same day, Git can merge it. dotnet new sln creates .slnx by default from .NET 10 🔧, and Visual Studio 2026 opens it (SDK what's new). To convert an existing .sln, the SDK has a dotnet sln migrate command 🔧 (check the docs for the exact syntax on your SDK).

Project references: who may depend on whom

The layout also encodes the architecture. Ledgerly.Core has no web dependencies, so the API, the worker and the migration runner can all share the domain model and the DbContext.

flowchart TD
    AppHost["Ledgerly.AppHost<br/>Aspire orchestration"]
    Api["Ledgerly.Api"]
    Worker["Ledgerly.Worker"]
    Market["Ledgerly.MarketData"]
    Mig["Ledgerly.MigrationService"]
    Defaults["Ledgerly.ServiceDefaults<br/>telemetry, health, resilience"]
    Core["Ledgerly.Core<br/>domain, DbContext, contracts"]
    Unit["Ledgerly.UnitTests"]
    Int["Ledgerly.IntegrationTests"]
    AppHost --> Api
    AppHost --> Worker
    AppHost --> Market
    AppHost --> Mig
    Api --> Defaults
    Api --> Core
    Worker --> Defaults
    Worker --> Core
    Mig --> Defaults
    Mig --> Core
    Market --> Defaults
    Unit --> Core
    Unit --> Market
    Int --> Api
Rule the graph enforces Why it matters
Nothing references Ledgerly.Api except the AppHost and the integration tests The API is an edge, not a library. Shared logic goes to Core
Ledgerly.MarketData doesn't reference Core It plays an external vendor, so it may share nothing but HTTP
Every service references ServiceDefaults Telemetry, health checks and resilience are identical everywhere (chapter 6)

💡 A project reference is an architectural decision. If a new arrow in this graph surprises you, it should surprise the reviewer too.

🧠 Directory.Build.props: one place for compiler and quality settings

MSBuild automatically imports Directory.Build.props from the project folder upward, and the first one it finds wins (docs). Put one at the solution root and every project gets its settings. You never see <Nullable> or <TargetFramework> in a Ledgerly .csproj.

💻 In Ledgerly: the quality gates

📄 sample/Directory.Build.props

<Project>
  <!-- Applies to every project under this folder. One place for compiler and quality settings. -->
  <PropertyGroup>
    <TargetFramework>net10.0</TargetFramework>
    <LangVersion>latest</LangVersion>
    <Nullable>enable</Nullable>
    <ImplicitUsings>enable</ImplicitUsings>

    <!-- Quality gates: warnings fail the build, analyzers and code style run in every build (also in CI). -->
    <TreatWarningsAsErrors>true</TreatWarningsAsErrors>
    <AnalysisLevel>latest-recommended</AnalysisLevel>
    <EnforceCodeStyleInBuild>true</EnforceCodeStyleInBuild>
    <!-- Needed so IDE0005 (unused usings) runs on build. CS1591 = "missing XML comment", not wanted for apps. -->
    <GenerateDocumentationFile>true</GenerateDocumentationFile>
    <NoWarn>$(NoWarn);CS1591</NoWarn>

    <!-- Reproducible, CI-friendly builds. -->
    <Deterministic>true</Deterministic>
    <ContinuousIntegrationBuild Condition="'$(GITHUB_ACTIONS)' == 'true'">true</ContinuousIntegrationBuild>
    <RestorePackagesWithLockFile>false</RestorePackagesWithLockFile>
  </PropertyGroup>
</Project>
Setting What it does Old-world equivalent
TargetFramework net10.0 Every project targets .NET 10 LTS A different TargetFrameworkVersion in each project
Nullable enable Nullable reference types everywhere (chapter 2) Null checks by hand
ImplicitUsings enable Common namespaces are imported globally 15 using lines per file
TreatWarningsAsErrors Every warning, including analyzer and NuGet audit warnings, fails the build A warnings list that grew to 400 and was ignored
AnalysisLevel latest-recommended Turns on the recommended set of .NET analyzer rules for the latest SDK FxCop, if someone remembered to install it
EnforceCodeStyleInBuild Runs the IDE code-style rules (IDExxxx) during dotnet build, not just in the editor Style rules that only showed up as squiggles
GenerateDocumentationFile Produces the XML doc file. Also required for IDE0005 to run on build Nothing
ContinuousIntegrationBuild Normalizes paths in builds on GitHub Actions Build-server-specific hacks

Analyzers ship with the SDK and are on by default for .NET 5 and later, but only a few rules are warnings out of the box (docs). latest-recommended raises a curated set to warnings, and TreatWarningsAsErrors turns those into errors. If you ever need CA rules to warn without blocking, CodeAnalysisTreatWarningsAsErrors=false exempts them. Ledgerly doesn't use it.

The IDE0005 lesson

When the team first set this up, the .editorconfig said unused usings (IDE0005) should be a warning. In the editor, they were. On dotnet build, nothing happened. The rule depends on information the compiler only produces when it generates the XML documentation file, so IDE0005 only runs on build with GenerateDocumentationFile=true.

Turning that on fixed IDE0005 and broke everything else: now every public type without an XML comment raised CS1591, "missing XML comment", and with warnings as errors, that's an error. Ledgerly is an app, not a library, so the fix was <NoWarn>$(NoWarn);CS1591</NoWarn>. The comment in the file records why, so the next person doesn't "clean it up."

💡 Every line in Directory.Build.props that isn't obvious gets a comment. Build settings are code that people will be tempted to delete.

🧠 Central Package Management, transitive pinning and NuGet audit

With Central Package Management (CPM), Directory.Packages.props holds every package version, and project files only name the package (docs). Upgrading EF Core is now a one-line change, and two projects can't end up on different versions by accident.

💻 In Ledgerly: one file for every version

📄 sample/Directory.Packages.props

<Project>
  <!-- Central Package Management: every NuGet version lives here, projects only name the package. -->
  <PropertyGroup>
    <ManagePackageVersionsCentrally>true</ManagePackageVersionsCentrally>
    <!-- Lets a PackageVersion below also override a TRANSITIVE dependency (used for security patches). -->
    <CentralPackageTransitivePinningEnabled>true</CentralPackageTransitivePinningEnabled>
  </PropertyGroup>

  <ItemGroup Label="Security pins for transitive dependencies (NuGet audit, NU1902/NU1903)">
    <PackageVersion Include="System.Security.Cryptography.Pkcs" Version="10.0.14" />
  </ItemGroup>
  <!-- ... -->
  <ItemGroup Label="ASP.NET Core and EF Core">
    <PackageVersion Include="Microsoft.AspNetCore.Authentication.JwtBearer" Version="10.0.14" />
    <PackageVersion Include="Microsoft.AspNetCore.OpenApi" Version="10.0.14" />
    <PackageVersion Include="Microsoft.EntityFrameworkCore.SqlServer" Version="10.0.14" />
    <PackageVersion Include="Microsoft.EntityFrameworkCore.Design" Version="10.0.14" />
    <!-- ... -->
  </ItemGroup>
  <!-- ... -->
</Project>

And the project side, with no versions at all:

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Core/Ledgerly.Core.csproj

  <ItemGroup>
    <PackageReference Include="Microsoft.EntityFrameworkCore.SqlServer" />
    <PackageReference Include="Microsoft.EntityFrameworkCore.Design" PrivateAssets="all" />
  </ItemGroup>

PrivateAssets="all" keeps the EF design-time package (used by dotnet ef) from flowing to projects that reference Core. The ItemGroup labels group packages by purpose, which makes upgrade pull requests easier to review: everything Aspire moves together, and everything ASP.NET Core and EF Core moves together.

Two CPM features Ledgerly doesn't need, but you should know exist:

<!-- illustrative, not in the sample -->
<!-- In one .csproj: an approved exception to the central version -->
<PackageReference Include="Some.Package" VersionOverride="2.0.0" />

<!-- In Directory.Packages.props: added to every project, e.g. an analyzer package -->
<GlobalPackageReference Include="Some.Analyzers" Version="1.0.0" />

Only the nearest Directory.Packages.props applies to a project. If you nest one deeper in the tree, it replaces the root one for that subtree instead of adding to it.

The day NuGet audit failed the build

Here is what Tomás meant. During restore, NuGet checks your packages, including transitive ones (the packages your packages depend on), against known vulnerabilities and reports them as warnings. One morning a transitive dependency, System.Security.Cryptography.Pkcs (versions 9.0.11 and 10.0.12 were in the graph), was flagged: NU1902 (moderate) and NU1903 (high). With TreatWarningsAsErrors, the build failed.

Nobody at Ledgerly had referenced that package. It came in underneath other packages. Without CPM, the usual fix is to add a direct PackageReference to a patched version in every affected project, and to remember to remove it later. With CPM, CentralPackageTransitivePinningEnabled lets a PackageVersion entry override a transitive dependency too. One line in Directory.Packages.props, under a label that says why it's there, and the whole graph moves to the patched version.

flowchart TD
    Restore["dotnet restore"] --> Graph["Resolve package graph<br/>direct and transitive"]
    Graph --> Audit{"NuGet audit<br/>known vulnerabilities?"}
    Audit -- "no" --> Build["Compile, analyzers, code style"]
    Audit -- "yes" --> Warn["NU1902 moderate<br/>NU1903 high"]
    Warn --> Twae["TreatWarningsAsErrors<br/>build fails"]
    Twae --> Pin["Pin patched version<br/>in Directory.Packages.props"]
    Pin --> Transitive["Transitive pinning<br/>overrides the old version"]
    Transitive --> Restore
    Build --> Green["Green build"]
Without the setup With the setup
The vulnerable package ships, and an auditor or attacker finds it months later The first build after disclosure fails, with the package name and advisory
The fix is a direct reference copied into several projects The fix is one pinned line, labeled with its reason
Nobody remembers why that odd reference is there The ItemGroup label documents it, and Dependabot proposes upgrades later (chapter 9)

🔧 NuGet audit has settings for severity levels and audit modes. Ledgerly uses the defaults, and this course doesn't depend on any specific value. Check the docs before you tune them. To see what audit sees, list vulnerable packages yourself (the command is in Try it).

💡 A red build caused by a vulnerability is a feature. Pin the fix centrally, label it, and let your dependency bot remove the pin when upstream catches up.

🧠 global.json and nuget.config: same SDK, same sources

Pinning the SDK

📄 sample/global.json

{
  "sdk": {
    "version": "10.0.100",
    "rollForward": "latestFeature"
  },
  "test": {
    "runner": "Microsoft.Testing.Platform"
  }
}

version sets the minimum SDK and rollForward decides how far the CLI may move from it (docs). latestFeature means "any .NET 10 SDK at or above 10.0.100, preferring the newest installed feature band." That's a pragmatic middle: everyone builds with .NET 10, but you don't have to install one exact patch to work.

rollForward value Behavior in short When to use it
latestFeature Newest installed feature band and patch of the same major and minor Ledgerly's choice for app repos
latestPatch Same feature band, newest patch You want tighter control and install SDKs deliberately
disable Exactly the version in the file Rarely: reproducing a specific compiler bug
latestMajor Whatever newest SDK is installed Almost never in a team repo

The test section is new in the .NET 10 SDK. It switches dotnet test to Microsoft.Testing.Platform, which xUnit v3 test projects need. Without it, dotnet test on the .NET 10 SDK failed with "Testing with VSTest target is no longer supported by Microsoft.Testing.Platform on .NET 10 SDK." Chapter 17 tells that story in full.

CI uses the same file: the actions/setup-dotnet step reads sample/global.json through its global-json-file input, so the build server can't drift from your laptop (chapter 8).

Locking down package sources

📄 sample/nuget.config

<?xml version="1.0" encoding="utf-8"?>
<configuration>
  <!-- Only nuget.org, no machine-wide feeds leaking in. Package source mapping blocks dependency-confusion attacks. -->
  <packageSources>
    <clear />
    <add key="nuget.org" value="https://api.nuget.org/v3/index.json" />
  </packageSources>
  <packageSourceMapping>
    <packageSource key="nuget.org">
      <package pattern="*" />
    </packageSource>
  </packageSourceMapping>
</configuration>

Jonas from security cares about this file more than any other in the repo:

  • <clear /> throws away every feed inherited from user-level or machine-level NuGet config. On a laptop that once had a corporate feed configured, restore results no longer depend on that history.
  • Package source mapping says which source each package ID may come from. Here it's simple, everything from nuget.org. The value shows up the day you add a private feed: map your internal prefix (say, Ledgerly.*) to the private feed, and an attacker who publishes a package with the same name on a public feed can't sneak in. That attack is called dependency confusion.

💡 global.json decides which compiler builds your code, and nuget.config decides where the code you didn't write comes from. Both belong in git.

🧠 Style, analyzers and local tools

.editorconfig: decisions, not opinions

The .editorconfig at the root sets formatting, naming and analyzer severities for every file. Because EnforceCodeStyleInBuild is on, rules set to warning are checked during build, and with warnings as errors, they block it.

📄 sample/.editorconfig

[*.cs]
# Namespaces and usings
csharp_style_namespace_declarations = file_scoped:warning
dotnet_sort_system_directives_first = true

# Prefer modern syntax
csharp_style_var_for_built_in_types = true:suggestion
csharp_style_var_when_type_is_apparent = true:suggestion
csharp_prefer_simple_using_statement = true:suggestion
csharp_style_prefer_primary_constructors = true:suggestion
dotnet_style_prefer_collection_expression = when_types_loosely_match:suggestion
# ...
# Analyzer tuning (each line is a deliberate team decision)
dotnet_diagnostic.CA1848.severity = suggestion   # LoggerMessage delegates: we use [LoggerMessage] where it matters
dotnet_diagnostic.CA1515.severity = none         # 'make types internal' is noisy for app code
dotnet_diagnostic.CA1062.severity = none         # nullable reference types already cover argument null checks
dotnet_diagnostic.CA2007.severity = none         # ConfigureAwait(false) is not needed in ASP.NET Core apps
dotnet_diagnostic.CA1716.severity = none
dotnet_diagnostic.IDE0005.severity = warning     # remove unnecessary usings

[**/Migrations/*.cs]
generated_code = true
dotnet_diagnostic.IDE0005.severity = none

[tests/**.cs]
dotnet_diagnostic.CA1707.severity = none         # underscores in test method names are fine
dotnet_diagnostic.CA2000.severity = none

Notice the pattern. Each rule that's turned off has a reason in a comment, so turning a rule off is a reviewed team decision, not a quiet #pragma. Generated code (EF migrations) is marked as generated, so it doesn't fight the style rules. Tests get their own section, because Stale_version_gets_409_conflict is a better test name than StaleVersionGets409Conflict.

Severity Effect in the editor Effect on dotnet build here
none Hidden Nothing
suggestion Dots under the code, with a quick fix Nothing
warning Green squiggle Error, because of TreatWarningsAsErrors
error Red squiggle Error

Suggestions are the gentle nudge for modern syntax (primary constructors, collection expressions). Warnings are the rules the team agreed are non-negotiable.

dotnet format

dotnet format applies .editorconfig style and analyzer fixes to the code, and dotnet format --verify-no-changes fails if anything would change (docs). Ledgerly's CI doesn't run it as a separate step, because EnforceCodeStyleInBuild already fails the build on the rules that matter. Run it locally to fix a batch of style issues in one go.

Local tools: pinned like packages

dotnet ef is a .NET tool. Installing it globally means every developer has a different version, and some have none. A tool manifest pins it in the repo.

📄 sample/.config/dotnet-tools.json

{
  "version": 1,
  "isRoot": true,
  "tools": {
    "dotnet-ef": {
      "version": "10.0.14",
      "commands": [
        "dotnet-ef"
      ],
      "rollForward": false
    }
  }
}

dotnet tool restore installs exactly these versions for this repo, and dotnet ef then works inside sample/. The tool version matches the EF Core package version in Directory.Packages.props (10.0.14 🔧), which avoids "tools version is older than runtime" warnings. Keep them in sync when you upgrade.

For a one-off tool you don't want in the manifest, the .NET 10 SDK can run a tool without installing it: dnx <tool> or dotnet tool exec 🔧, for example dnx dotnet-counters when you need runtime counters once.

💡 If a command is part of the workflow, its tool belongs in the manifest. If it's a one-off, use dnx and leave no trace.

🗺️ A fresh clone, end to end

This is Tomás's "one command" rule as a flow. Every box is driven by a file in the repo, not by the machine.

flowchart TD
    Clone["git clone<br/>cd sample"] --> Sdk["global.json<br/>selects a .NET 10 SDK"]
    Sdk --> Sources["nuget.config<br/>nuget.org only"]
    Sources --> Versions["Directory.Packages.props<br/>one version per package"]
    Versions --> Audit["NuGet audit<br/>direct and transitive"]
    Audit --> Props["Directory.Build.props<br/>net10.0, nullable, analyzers"]
    Props --> Style[".editorconfig<br/>style and rule severities"]
    Style --> Compile["Compile all 9 projects"]
    Compile -- "any warning" --> Red["Build fails<br/>fix before merge"]
    Compile -- "clean" --> Green["Green build<br/>same as CI"]

The CI workflow runs exactly the same steps. Its comment says it plainly: "Warnings are errors, analyzers + code style run, NuGet audit fails on vulnerable packages." There is no separate "CI configuration" for quality, which is why a green build on your laptop predicts a green build in CI (chapter 8).

⚠️ Common traps

Trap What happens Do this instead
Adding Version="..." to a PackageReference in a project With CPM on, restore reports an error instead of quietly using two versions Add or change the PackageVersion in Directory.Packages.props. Use VersionOverride only for a reviewed exception
Fixing a vulnerable transitive package with direct references in each project Pins scatter, nobody removes them, and versions drift Use CentralPackageTransitivePinningEnabled and one labeled pin
Setting IDE0005 to warning and expecting the build to enforce it It only shows in the editor Turn on GenerateDocumentationFile (and suppress CS1591 for apps)
Silencing a rule with #pragma warning disable in code The decision is invisible and spreads by copy-paste Change severity in .editorconfig with a comment, or fix the code. Keep #pragma for narrow, commented cases (the migrator's raw SQL is one)
Nesting a second Directory.Packages.props or Directory.Build.props The nearest one wins, and the root settings silently stop applying to that subtree Keep one of each at the root, or import the parent explicitly
Installing dotnet-ef globally Version mismatches between developers and CI Use the tool manifest and dotnet tool restore
A nuget.config without <clear /> Machine-level feeds join the restore, and results differ per laptop <clear />, then list exactly the sources you need, with source mapping

🧰 Helpers

A starter Directory.Build.props for a new app repo:

<!-- illustrative, not in the sample -->
<Project>
  <PropertyGroup>
    <TargetFramework>net10.0</TargetFramework>
    <Nullable>enable</Nullable>
    <ImplicitUsings>enable</ImplicitUsings>
    <TreatWarningsAsErrors>true</TreatWarningsAsErrors>
    <AnalysisLevel>latest-recommended</AnalysisLevel>
    <EnforceCodeStyleInBuild>true</EnforceCodeStyleInBuild>
    <GenerateDocumentationFile>true</GenerateDocumentationFile>
    <NoWarn>$(NoWarn);CS1591</NoWarn>
  </PropertyGroup>
</Project>

Commands you'll use for repo hygiene (run from sample/):

Task Command
Which SDK does this folder use? dotnet --version
Build everything with all gates dotnet build Ledgerly.slnx
Restore pinned local tools dotnet tool restore
Show vulnerable packages, including transitive dotnet list package --vulnerable --include-transitive
Fix style issues in bulk dotnet format
Check style without changing files dotnet format --verify-no-changes

Checklist for a new repo:

  • [ ] .slnx solution, src/ and tests/ folders
  • [ ] Directory.Build.props with nullable, analyzers, code style in build, warnings as errors
  • [ ] Directory.Packages.props with CPM and transitive pinning enabled
  • [ ] global.json with an SDK version and rollForward
  • [ ] nuget.config with <clear /> and package source mapping
  • [ ] .editorconfig with commented severity decisions
  • [ ] .config/dotnet-tools.json for every tool the workflow needs
  • [ ] CI runs the same dotnet build you run locally

Make it permanent: copy these files from sample/ into your next repo on day one, before the first feature, because tightening rules later means fixing hundreds of warnings at once. Add a Dependabot config (chapter 9) so pinned versions don't rot. The toolkit has ready-to-copy Directory.Build.props, Directory.Packages.props, .editorconfig and dependabot.yml files, and cheat-sheet-old-to-new.md repeats the command table (see the toolkit). Terms like CPM and transitive dependency are in the glossary.

🧪 Try it yourself (optional, ~20 min)

  1. Check which SDK the repo selects, and build: bash cd sample dotnet --version dotnet build Ledgerly.slnx The version should be a 10.0 SDK at or above 10.0.100, and the build should end with 0 warnings and 0 errors.
  2. Watch a quality gate work. Open src/Ledgerly.Core/Domain/Holdings.cs and add an unused using System.Text; below the namespace line. Build again: bash dotnet build Ledgerly.slnx You should get error IDE0005 (unnecessary using). It's a warning in .editorconfig, and warnings are errors here. Remove the line.
