  3. Restore the pinned tools and check the EF tool version: bash dotnet tool restore dotnet ef --version It should report 10.0.14, the version in .config/dotnet-tools.json.
  4. See what NuGet audit sees: bash dotnet list package --vulnerable --include-transitive With the Pkcs pin in place, you should see no vulnerable packages, unless something new was disclosed since this was written 🔧. If something shows up, you know what to do.
  5. Open Ledgerly.slnx and an old .sln from one of your own projects side by side. Count the lines. Write the difference in your habits notebook.

✅ Recap

  1. Repo-wide files (Directory.Build.props, Directory.Packages.props, global.json, nuget.config, .editorconfig, the tool manifest) make every clone build the same way on any machine and in CI.
  2. Warnings as errors plus latest-recommended analyzers and code style in build turn team agreements into rules the compiler enforces, with every exception commented.
  3. CPM with transitive pinning lets NuGet audit fail the build on a vulnerable package you never referenced, and lets you fix it with one labeled line.

Next → Chapter 4: Designing the API

Chapter 4: Designing the API

⏱ About 35 min · You'll learn: how to build a clear HTTP contract with minimal APIs, route groups and TypedResults, how validation, ProblemDetails and OpenAPI 3.1 come almost for free in ASP.NET Core 10, and how CORS, rate limiting, concurrency, async imports and feature flags each map to a status code the frontend can rely on.

📖 Story: Leo needs a contract

Leo, the frontend developer, sits down next to you with a coffee and a list. "I'm starting the React app next sprint. I don't need your code. I need a contract. What are the URLs? What do I send? What comes back when it works, and, more important, what comes back when it doesn't? If one endpoint returns { error: "..." }, another returns plain text and a third returns an HTML error page, I'll write three error handlers and hate you."

At Nordbank, your contract was a Word document with screenshots, and it was out of date the week after it was written. Errors were whatever the controller happened to throw. Leo wants something different: a machine-readable document generated from the code, one error format for everything, and status codes that mean the same thing on every endpoint.

You open your habits notebook and write: The API is a product. Its users are developers, and status codes are part of the UI. Maya adds one rule before you start: "Every response the client can get must be visible in the handler's signature. If it isn't in the type, it doesn't exist."

🏚️ Old way → 🚀 New way

What you did before What you do now Why it changed
Web API controllers with attribute routing, one class per resource Minimal APIs: route groups and static handler methods Less ceremony, and shared prefixes, tags and policies are set once per group
IHttpActionResult and return Ok(x) with no compile-time contract TypedResults and Results<Ok<T>, NotFound, ...> The possible responses are in the method signature, so OpenAPI and tests can see them
if (!ModelState.IsValid) in every action AddValidation() with DataAnnotations on records One registration validates every handler's input and returns a standard 400
Custom error JSON per team, or HTML error pages ProblemDetails (application/problem+json, RFC 9457) everywhere One error shape for every failure, including unhandled exceptions
A Word document or Swashbuckle bolted on Built-in OpenAPI 3.1 at /openapi/v1.json, Scalar as a dev UI The contract is generated from the code, so it can't drift
No protection against a client hammering one endpoint Built-in rate limiting with per-user partitions and 429 Expensive endpoints stay usable for everyone
"Last write wins" on updates Optimistic concurrency with a version and 409 Conflict Two users editing the same thing can't silently overwrite each other
Long uploads processed inside the request 202 Accepted plus a status URL, work done by the worker Requests stay fast, and the heavy work survives a restart

💡 A good API contract is mostly about the unhappy paths. The happy path is easy to agree on.

🧠 Minimal APIs, route groups and typed results

Minimal APIs map a route straight to a method. There's no controller base class and no attribute routing. What you lose in convention you gain in clarity: every endpoint and every policy on it is visible in one place (docs).

A route group applies a prefix, OpenAPI tags, authorization and filters to every endpoint in it. Ledgerly keeps each group in its own file, as an extension method that Program.cs calls.

💻 In Ledgerly: the portfolio routes

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Api/Endpoints/PortfolioEndpoints.cs

public static class PortfolioEndpoints
{
    public static IEndpointRouteBuilder MapPortfolioEndpoints(this IEndpointRouteBuilder app)
    {
        var group = app.MapGroup("/api/portfolios")
            .WithTags("Portfolios")
            .RequireAuthorization();

        group.MapGet("/", ListPortfolios);
        group.MapPost("/", CreatePortfolio);
        group.MapGet("/{id:guid}", GetPortfolio).WithName("GetPortfolio");
        group.MapPut("/{id:guid}", RenamePortfolio);
        group.MapPost("/{id:guid}/transactions", RecordTransaction);
        group.MapGet("/{id:guid}/valuation", GetValuation);

        return app;
    }
    // ...
}

You can read the whole resource from this block, like a table of contents. RequireAuthorization() on the group means no endpoint in it can be forgotten. {id:guid} is a route constraint: /api/portfolios/abc doesn't match, so it returns 404 without reaching your code.

Handlers are plain static methods. Parameters are bound by type and source: route values, the JSON body, ClaimsPrincipal for the user, services from DI and the request's CancellationToken.

    private static async Task<Results<Ok<PortfolioDetails>, NotFound>> GetPortfolio(
        Guid id, ClaimsPrincipal user, LedgerlyDbContext db, CancellationToken ct)
    {
        var ownerId = user.GetOwnerId();
        var portfolio = await db.Portfolios.AsNoTracking()
            .Include(p => p.Transactions)
            .SingleOrDefaultAsync(p => p.Id == id && p.OwnerId == ownerId, ct);

        if (portfolio is null)
        {
            return TypedResults.NotFound();
        }
        // ...
    }

Why Results<...> matters

The return type Results<Ok<PortfolioDetails>, NotFound> is Maya's rule in code. The compiler only allows those two outcomes, OpenAPI documents both automatically, and a unit test can assert on the result type without an HTTP server. Compare it with the old IHttpActionResult, which could be anything at all.

Return type Meaning for Leo
Ok<PortfolioSummary[]> Always 200 with an array
CreatedAtRoute<PortfolioSummary> 201 with a Location header pointing at GetPortfolio
Results<Ok<PortfolioDetails>, NotFound> 200, or 404 if it doesn't exist or isn't yours
Results<Ok<PortfolioSummary>, NotFound, ProblemHttpResult> 200, 404, or a ProblemDetails error (409 here)
Results<Created<TransactionResponse>, NotFound, ValidationProblem, ProblemHttpResult> 201, 404, 400 with field errors, or 409

Notice the ownership check: p.Id == id && p.OwnerId == ownerId. Another user's portfolio returns 404, not 403, so the API doesn't even confirm that the ID exists. An integration test (Users_cannot_see_each_others_portfolios) guards this.

The owner ID comes from the token, through one small extension method:

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Api/ApiConventions.cs

    /// <summary>
    /// Stable user id. Entra ID puts the object id in "oid"; dev tokens from `dotnet user-jwts` use "sub"
    /// (mapped to NameIdentifier by the JWT handler).
    /// </summary>
    public static string GetOwnerId(this ClaimsPrincipal user) =>
        user.FindFirstValue("oid")
        ?? user.FindFirstValue("http://schemas.microsoft.com/identity/claims/objectidentifier")
        ?? user.FindFirstValue(ClaimTypes.NameIdentifier)
        ?? user.FindFirstValue("sub")
        ?? throw new InvalidOperationException("Authenticated user has no id claim.");

The same code works with local dev tokens and with Entra ID tokens in Azure. Chapter 13 covers the identity side.

💡 If a status code isn't in the handler's return type, the client should never see it, except for the cross-cutting ones (401, 429, 500) that middleware owns.

🧠 Validation and ProblemDetails: one error shape for everything

Validation with AddValidation()

In ASP.NET Core 10, one line turns on validation for minimal APIs: builder.Services.AddValidation(). A source generator finds the types your handlers take, validates DataAnnotations attributes (and IValidatableObject), and returns 400 with a validation ProblemDetails when something fails. Records are supported, and you can opt an endpoint out with .DisableValidation() (release notes). The validation APIs now live in the Microsoft.Extensions.Validation namespace, so you can reuse them outside ASP.NET Core.

💻 In Ledgerly: requests are records with attributes

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Api/Endpoints/PortfolioEndpoints.cs

public sealed record CreatePortfolioRequest(
    [Required, StringLength(100, MinimumLength = 1)] string Name,
    [RegularExpression("^[A-Z]{3}$")] string? BaseCurrency);

public sealed record RenamePortfolioRequest(
    [Required, StringLength(100, MinimumLength = 1)] string Name,
    [Required] string Version);
// ...
public sealed record RecordTransactionRequest(
    [Required, StringLength(12, MinimumLength = 1)] string Symbol,
    TransactionType Type,
    [Range(typeof(decimal), "0.000001", "1000000000")] decimal Quantity,
    [Range(typeof(decimal), "0.000001", "1000000000")] decimal Price,
    DateOnly TradeDate);

The handler never sees an invalid CreatePortfolioRequest. There's no ModelState check, because validation runs before the handler.

Attribute validation covers the shape of input. Business rules still live in the domain. "You cannot sell what you do not hold" needs the portfolio's history, so RecordTransaction checks it and returns the same 400 format by hand:

        try
        {
            // Domain rule: you cannot sell what you do not hold (at that date).
            HoldingCalculator.Calculate([.. portfolio.Transactions, transaction]);
        }
        catch (HoldingsException ex)
        {
            return TypedResults.ValidationProblem(new Dictionary<string, string[]> { ["quantity"] = [ex.Message] });
        }

For Leo, both kinds of failure look identical: a 400 with an errors object keyed by field name. He writes one function that shows field errors next to inputs.

ProblemDetails for every other error

Three registrations in Program.cs give you RFC 9457 application/problem+json responses everywhere (docs):

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Api/Program.cs

// ── API surface: validation, ProblemDetails, OpenAPI, JSON ─────────────────────────────────────
builder.Services.AddValidation();
builder.Services.AddProblemDetails();
builder.Services.AddOpenApi();
builder.Services.ConfigureHttpJsonOptions(o => o.SerializerOptions.Converters.Add(new JsonStringEnumConverter()));
// ...
app.UseExceptionHandler();
app.UseStatusCodePages();
Registration What it covers
AddProblemDetails() Registers the service that writes ProblemDetails bodies
UseExceptionHandler() An unhandled exception becomes a 500 ProblemDetails, with no stack trace leaked to the client
UseStatusCodePages() A bare 401 or 404 with no body gets a ProblemDetails body too
JsonStringEnumConverter Enums travel as "Buy" and "Sell", not 0 and 1, which is friendlier for a TypeScript client

The integration tests pin this down, so nobody can break it by accident:

📄 sample/tests/Ledgerly.IntegrationTests/PortfolioApiTests.cs

    [Fact]
    public async Task Invalid_input_returns_problem_details()
    {
        var client = ClientFor("alice");

        var response = await client.PostAsJsonAsync("/api/portfolios", new { name = "", baseCurrency = "euro" }, Ct);

        Assert.Equal(HttpStatusCode.BadRequest, response.StatusCode);
        Assert.Equal("application/problem+json", response.Content.Headers.ContentType?.MediaType);
    }

💡 Errors are part of the contract. One format (ProblemDetails), one place to produce it (the framework), and a test that asserts the content type.

🗺️ The status code map Leo gets

Every Ledgerly endpoint follows the same map. Leo prints it and pins it above his desk.

flowchart TD
    Req["Request"] --> Auth{"Valid bearer token?"}
    Auth -- "no" --> R401["401 Unauthorized"]
    Auth -- "yes" --> Rate{"Within rate limit?<br/>imports only"}
    Rate -- "no" --> R429["429 Too Many Requests"]
    Rate -- "yes" --> Flag{"Feature enabled?<br/>alerts only"}
    Flag -- "no" --> R404a["404 Not Found"]
    Flag -- "yes" --> Valid{"Input valid?"}
    Valid -- "no" --> R400["400 validation ProblemDetails"]
    Valid -- "yes" --> Own{"Exists and yours?"}
    Own -- "no" --> R404b["404 Not Found"]
    Own -- "yes" --> Ver{"Version current?"}
    Ver -- "no" --> R409["409 Conflict"]
    Ver -- "yes" --> R2xx["200 OK, 201 Created<br/>or 202 Accepted"]

Anything unexpected becomes a 500 ProblemDetails from the exception handler. Chapter 14 covers the other kind of failure, when a dependency rather than your code is the problem.

🧠 OpenAPI 3.1, Scalar and the versioning decision

The contract, generated

builder.Services.AddOpenApi() plus app.MapOpenApi() serves an OpenAPI document at /openapi/v1.json, and .NET 10 generates OpenAPI 3.1 by default (docs). Your Results<...> return types become the documented responses, the route group's WithTags becomes the grouping, and XML doc comments flow into descriptions. That's one more reason chapter 3 turned on GenerateDocumentationFile: a /// <summary> on a handler like RenamePortfolio ("Optimistic concurrency: the client sends back the Version it read; a stale version gets 409.") shows up in the docs Leo reads.

Swashbuckle is no longer in the project templates 🔧. For a browsable UI, Ledgerly uses Scalar, a third-party package that renders the document at /scalar.

💻 In Ledgerly: docs in development only

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Api/Program.cs

if (app.Environment.IsDevelopment())
{
    app.MapOpenApi();                 // /openapi/v1.json
    app.MapScalarApiReference();      // /scalar  (interactive API explorer)
}

The interactive explorer stays off in production: there's no reason to hand an attacker a map with a "try it" button. Leo doesn't need production to get the contract. He can generate a TypeScript client from the JSON on a dev machine, or the team can generate the document at build time with Microsoft.Extensions.ApiDescription.Server and commit it.

Option Pros Cons
Serve /openapi/v1.json and /scalar in Development only (Ledgerly) Zero setup, always current Clients need a running dev instance
Generate the document at build time Reviewable in pull requests, contract diffs are visible One more package and build step
Serve docs in production behind auth Partners can explore the live API Extra surface to protect

Versioning: a decision, not a package

Leo asks, "Is it /api/v1/portfolios?" It isn't, and that's deliberate. ASP.NET Core has no built-in API versioning. The common choice is the .NET Foundation package Asp.Versioning.Http (10.2.3 🔧) for minimal APIs.

Ledgerly's only client is its own SPA, deployed from the same repo. The team decided to not version yet, keep changes backward compatible (add fields, never rename or remove them), and add Asp.Versioning.Http the day a third party depends on the API. The sample uses no versioning package. Maya asks you to write that down as an architecture decision record using the toolkit's ADR template, so the "why" survives the people who made it.

💡 Versioning has a cost. Pay it when you have consumers you can't redeploy, not before.

🧠 Protecting the API: CORS and rate limiting

CORS for the SPA

Leo's React dev server runs at http://localhost:5173, and the API runs on a different origin. Browsers block that kind of cross-origin call unless the API says the origin is allowed. Ledgerly reads the allowed origins from configuration, so each environment can list its own front end.

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Api/Program.cs

// ── Ready for a React SPA later: allowed origins come from configuration ────────────────────────
var spaOrigins = builder.Configuration.GetSection("Cors:AllowedOrigins").Get<string[]>() ?? [];
builder.Services.AddCors(o => o.AddPolicy(ApiPolicies.Spa, p => p.WithOrigins(spaOrigins).AllowAnyHeader().AllowAnyMethod()));

appsettings.json has an empty list, and appsettings.Development.json adds http://localhost:5173. An empty list by default means "no browser origin is allowed" until someone decides otherwise, which is the safe default. In Azure, the origin comes from an environment variable (Cors__AllowedOrigins__0) set by Bicep (chapter 11).

CORS is a browser rule, not a security boundary for the API. A script or curl ignores it. Authentication (the bearer token) is what protects the data.

Rate limiting the expensive endpoint

Importing a broker CSV is the most expensive thing a user can do. Ledgerly limits it per user with the built-in rate-limiting middleware (docs).

// ── Protection: per-user rate limit for the expensive import endpoint ──────────────────────────
builder.Services.AddRateLimiter(options =>
{
    options.RejectionStatusCode = StatusCodes.Status429TooManyRequests;
    options.AddPolicy(ApiPolicies.Imports, http => RateLimitPartition.GetFixedWindowLimiter(
        partitionKey: http.User.Identity?.Name ?? http.Connection.RemoteIpAddress?.ToString() ?? "anonymous",
        factory: _ => new FixedWindowRateLimiterOptions { PermitLimit = 5, Window = TimeSpan.FromMinutes(1) }));
});

Three decisions are hiding in those lines:

Decision Why
RejectionStatusCode = 429 The default is 503 Service Unavailable. A 503 tells clients and monitors that the server is in trouble, which triggers retries and alerts. 429 says "you, slow down."
Partition by user name, then IP, then "anonymous" Each user gets their own five imports per minute. One noisy user can't use up everyone's budget
Fixed window, 5 per minute The simplest of the four built-in algorithms (fixed window, sliding window, token bucket, concurrency). Good enough for an upload endpoint

The policy is attached to just the import endpoint, with .RequireRateLimiting(ApiPolicies.Imports). Policy names live in ApiConventions.cs as constants ("imports", "spa"), so a typo is a compile error, not a policy that silently doesn't apply.

💡 Always set RejectionStatusCode explicitly. A rate limiter that answers 503 makes your own monitoring think the service is down.

🧠 Concurrency, async imports and feature flags

Three endpoints show three more contract patterns Leo will meet.

409 Conflict: optimistic concurrency

Every portfolio response includes a Version string: the database's rowversion, Base64-encoded. To rename, the client sends that version back. If someone else changed the portfolio in between, the update affects zero rows, EF Core throws, and the API returns 409.

        db.Entry(portfolio).Property(p => p.RowVersion).OriginalValue = Convert.FromBase64String(request.Version);
        portfolio.Name = request.Name.Trim();
        portfolio.UpdatedAt = DateTimeOffset.UtcNow;

        try
        {
            await db.SaveChangesAsync(ct);
        }
        catch (DbUpdateConcurrencyException)
        {
            return TypedResults.Problem("Portfolio was changed by someone else. Reload and try again.", statusCode: StatusCodes.Status409Conflict);
        }

For Leo, the rule is simple: on 409, reload and show the user the current data. Chapter 5 explains the database side, and why recording a trade uses the same mechanism to stop a double "sell all."

202 Accepted: work that takes a while

A CSV import can take seconds or minutes. Holding an HTTP request open that long is fragile: a deployment or a scale-in kills it halfway. Instead, the API stores the file, queues the work and answers immediately.

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Api/Endpoints/ImportEndpoints.cs

        group.MapPost("/portfolios/{id:guid}/imports", StartImport)
            .RequireRateLimiting(ApiPolicies.Imports)
            // Bearer-token APIs are not vulnerable to CSRF (browsers never attach the token automatically).
            .DisableAntiforgery();
        // ...
    private static async Task<Results<AcceptedAtRoute<ImportStatusResponse>, NotFound, ValidationProblem>> StartImport(
        Guid id, IFormFile file, ClaimsPrincipal user, LedgerlyDbContext db, CancellationToken ct)
    {
        if (file.Length is 0 or > MaxFileBytes)
        {
            return TypedResults.ValidationProblem(new Dictionary<string, string[]> { ["file"] = [$"File must be between 1 byte and {MaxFileBytes} bytes."] });
        }
        // ...
        db.Imports.Add(import);
        db.EnqueueOutboxMessage(Queues.Imports, new ImportRequested(import.Id, id));
        await db.SaveChangesAsync(ct); // one transaction: both rows or neither

        return TypedResults.AcceptedAtRoute(ToResponse(import), "GetImport", new { importId = import.Id });
    }
Detail Why
IFormFile parameter Minimal APIs bind a multipart upload directly
MaxFileBytes = 1_000_000 Rejects empty and oversized files with the standard 400
.DisableAntiforgery() Opts the upload out of antiforgery checks. As the comment says, browsers never attach a bearer token automatically, so CSRF doesn't apply
Import row and outbox message in one SaveChangesAsync Both rows commit or neither does. Chapter 7 explains the outbox
AcceptedAtRoute(..., "GetImport", ...) 202 with a Location header pointing at /api/imports/{importId} 
sequenceDiagram
    participant SPA as "React SPA"
    participant Api as "Ledgerly.Api"
    participant Sql as "Azure SQL"
    participant Worker as "Ledgerly.Worker"
    participant Bus as "Service Bus"
    SPA->>Api: POST /api/portfolios/id/imports with CSV
    Api->>Sql: Insert Import and outbox row in one transaction
    Api-->>SPA: 202 Accepted with Location /api/imports/importId
    Worker->>Sql: Read pending outbox row
    Worker->>Bus: Send ImportRequested
    Bus->>Worker: Deliver ImportRequested
    Worker->>Sql: Save trades and set status Completed
    SPA->>Api: GET /api/imports/importId
    Api-->>SPA: 200 OK with status and row counts

Leo polls the Location URL every couple of seconds until the status is Completed or Failed. Later the team may push a notification instead, but the contract stays the same.

404 when a feature is off

Price alerts are new, and Elena wants to turn them off without a deployment if something goes wrong. Ledgerly uses Microsoft.FeatureManagement.AspNetCore (4.8.0 🔧), with flags read from the FeatureManagement configuration section. appsettings.json has "PriceAlerts": true.

For MVC, the library has a [FeatureGate] attribute that returns 404 when a feature is off. Minimal APIs have no attribute, so Ledgerly wrote a small endpoint filter that does the same for the whole group:

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Api/Endpoints/AlertEndpoints.cs

        var alerts = app.MapGroup("/api/alerts")
            .WithTags("Alerts")
            .RequireAuthorization()
            .AddEndpointFilter(RequireFeature(Features.PriceAlerts));
        // ...
    /// <summary>Feature flag gate: when the flag is off the endpoints behave as if they do not exist.</summary>
    private static Func<EndpointFilterInvocationContext, EndpointFilterDelegate, ValueTask<object?>> RequireFeature(string feature) =>
        async (context, next) =>
        {
            var features = context.HttpContext.RequestServices.GetRequiredService<IFeatureManager>();
            return await features.IsEnabledAsync(feature) ? await next(context) : TypedResults.NotFound();
        };

404 is the honest answer: with the flag off, the feature doesn't exist. Leo's app hides the alerts menu on 404. Chapter 19 uses flags like this one as kill switches during an incident.

💡 Every pattern in this section is a status code with a documented client reaction: 409 means reload, 202 means poll, 404 on a flagged feature means hide it.

⚠️ Common traps

Trap What happens Do this instead
Returning IResult or Task<IResult> from handlers OpenAPI can't see the responses, and nothing stops a handler from returning anything Use TypedResults and Results<...> so the contract is in the signature
Leaving the rate limiter's rejection code at the default Clients get 503, retry harder, and your alerts say the service is down Set RejectionStatusCode = StatusCodes.Status429TooManyRequests
Returning 403 for another user's resource You confirm the ID exists, which leaks information Filter by owner in the query and return 404
WithOrigins("*") or AllowAnyOrigin() "just for now" Any website can call your API from a user's browser Read allowed origins from configuration, empty by default
Mapping Scalar or OpenAPI in every environment A browsable attack map in production Map them inside IsDevelopment(), or protect them
Doing the CSV import inside the request Timeouts, half-finished imports when a replica stops, and angry retries 202 plus a status URL, with the work done by the worker via the outbox
UseCors after UseAuthorization, or UseRateLimiter before authentication Preflight requests fail, or per-user partitions see no user Keep the order from Program.cs: exception handler, status pages, CORS, authentication, authorization, rate limiter

🧰 Helpers

A route group template to copy for a new resource:

// illustrative, not in the sample
public static class WatchlistEndpoints
{
    public static IEndpointRouteBuilder MapWatchlistEndpoints(this IEndpointRouteBuilder app)
    {
        var group = app.MapGroup("/api/watchlists").WithTags("Watchlists").RequireAuthorization();
        group.MapGet("/{id:guid}", GetWatchlist).WithName("GetWatchlist");
        return app;
    }

    private static async Task<Results<Ok<WatchlistResponse>, NotFound>> GetWatchlist(
        Guid id, ClaimsPrincipal user, LedgerlyDbContext db, CancellationToken ct)
    {
        // Filter by owner in the query, return NotFound otherwise.
        await Task.CompletedTask;
        return TypedResults.NotFound();
    }
}

public sealed record WatchlistResponse(Guid Id, string Name);

Status codes and what the client should do (share this with your frontend team):

Code When Ledgerly returns it Client reaction
200 / 201 Success, with Location on 201 Show the result
202 Import accepted Poll the Location URL
400 Validation or business rule failed Show errors next to the fields
401 Missing or expired token Sign in again
404 Not found, not yours, or feature off Show "not found" or hide the feature
409 Stale version Reload, then let the user retry
429 Rate limit hit Wait, then retry, and tell the user
500 Bug or unexpected failure Generic error, and report it

API review checklist:

  • [ ] Every handler returns TypedResults with a Results<...> signature
  • [ ] Request records carry DataAnnotations, and business rules return ValidationProblem
  • [ ] Every error is ProblemDetails (application/problem+json)
  • [ ] Ownership is enforced in the query, so other users' data returns 404
  • [ ] Expensive endpoints have a rate-limit policy, and the rejection code is 429
  • [ ] Long work returns 202 with a status URL
  • [ ] Updates to shared data carry a version and can return 409

Make it permanent: add the checklist to your pull request template (the toolkit has pr-review-checklist.md and pull_request_template.md, see the toolkit), and back each rule with an integration test like Invalid_input_returns_problem_details and Stale_version_gets_409_conflict. Record the versioning decision with adr-template.md. Terms like ProblemDetails and route group are in the glossary.

🧪 Try it yourself (optional, ~30 min)

You need a container runtime (Docker Desktop or Podman) for Aspire.

  1. Start everything: bash cd sample aspire run (Without the Aspire CLI: dotnet run --project src/Ledgerly.AppHost.) Open the dashboard link from the console, and find the API's HTTPS URL on the resources page.
  2. Browse the contract: open <api-url>/scalar and <api-url>/openapi/v1.json. Find the responses listed for PUT /api/portfolios/{id} and match them to the Results<...> type in PortfolioEndpoints.cs.
  3. Create a dev token in a second terminal: bash cd sample dotnet user-jwts create --project src/Ledgerly.Api Paste the token into src/Ledgerly.Api/Ledgerly.Api.http (the @token line) and set @api to the API URL from the dashboard.
  4. Send the requests in the .http file from Visual Studio, Rider or VS Code with an HTTP client extension: create a portfolio (201 with a Location header), then send it again with an empty name (400, application/problem+json).
  5. Trigger a 409: get the portfolio, copy its version, and send the same rename twice with that version: ```http # illustrative, not in the sample PUT {{api}}/api/portfolios/ Authorization: Bearer {{token}} Content-Type: application/json

{ "name": "Renamed", "version": "" } `` The first returns 200, the second 409. 6. Trigger a 429: send the "Import a broker CSV" request from the.httpfile six times within a minute. The first five return 202 (or 404 if you didn't replace the portfolio ID), the sixth returns 429. 7. Turn the feature off: stop the app, set"PriceAlerts": falseinsrc/Ledgerly.Api/appsettings.json, run it again, and callPOST /api/alerts. You get 404. Set it back totrue`.

✅ Recap

  1. Minimal APIs with route groups and TypedResults put the contract in the code: every possible response is in the handler's Results<...> type, and OpenAPI 3.1 documents it.
  2. AddValidation(), ProblemDetails and a fixed status code map (400, 401, 404, 409, 429, 202) give the frontend one error shape and predictable reactions.
  3. Protection and evolution are explicit decisions: CORS from configuration, rate limiting with 429 instead of the default 503, Scalar only in development, feature flags as 404, and no versioning until a real consumer needs it.

Next → Chapter 5: Data: EF Core and Azure SQL

Chapter 5: Data: EF Core and Azure SQL

⏱ About 35 min · You'll learn: how Ledgerly wires EF Core 10 through Aspire, how a rowversion turns a double "sell all" into a 409, the EF trap with client-generated keys that the team really hit, why migrations run as their own short-lived process, and how Azure SQL serverless with Entra-only auth removes both idle cost and passwords.

📖 Story: "We sold twice?"

Elena forwards a support ticket with the subject line in capitals. A beta tester had Ledgerly open on her phone and her laptop, tapped Sell All on one, got impatient and clicked it on the other. Her portfolio now shows minus 40 shares of VWCE. "Two clicks at once and we sold twice?" Elena asks. "How is that even possible? We check that you own the shares before selling."

You know exactly how. Both requests read the portfolio at the same moment. Both saw 40 shares. Both passed the check. Both inserted a sell. Each request was correct on its own, and together they were wrong. At Nordbank you would have fixed it with a stored procedure and WITH (UPDLOCK), or by accepting "last write wins" and hoping nobody noticed.

Maya has a different plan: let the database tell you when someone else got there first, and turn that into a clear answer for the second request. In your habits notebook, under Data, you write: Never trust a check you did before someone else's write. This chapter is the fix, plus everything around it: the DbContext, migrations as a deploy step, the EF Core trap that cost the team an afternoon, and the Azure SQL setup that pauses when nobody is using it.

🏚️ Old way → 🚀 New way

What you did before What you do now Why it changed
EDMX designer or hand-written ADO.NET, stored procedures for writes EF Core 10 code-first model, configured in OnModelCreating The model, the schema and the migrations live in one codebase and one pull request
Pessimistic locks (UPDLOCK) or "last write wins" Optimistic concurrency with a rowversion column, conflicts become 409 No locks held across requests, and the loser gets a clear answer instead of silent data loss
IDENTITY integer keys from the database Guid.CreateVersion7() keys generated in C# IDs exist before the insert (for outbox messages and Location headers) and still sort by time
A DBA runs a script by hand, or Database.Migrate() at startup Migrations run in a separate process that runs to completion before the apps start Ten replicas don't race to change the schema, and a failed migration stops the deployment
Hand-rolled retry loops, or none An execution strategy that retries transient faults A cloud database that fails over or wakes from pause is a normal event, not an outage
A SQL Server VM running 24/7 Azure SQL serverless that auto-pauses, on the free offer 💰 You pay for compute when it's used, and a beta with little traffic can cost very little
SQL logins with passwords in web.config Microsoft Entra-only authentication with managed identities There's no password to leak, rotate or paste into Slack

💡 Most of what changed is about time and concurrency: many replicas, many requests and a database that can briefly disappear. The SQL itself is the part you already know.

🧠 The DbContext, the model and the indexes

Registering the DbContext through Aspire

In the old world, you'd pass a connection string name to a DbContext constructor and hope web.config had it. In Ledgerly, the API, the worker and the migration service each register the same DbContext with one line:

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Api/Program.cs

// ── Data: Aspire client integration = DbContext pooling + retries + health check + tracing ──────
builder.AddSqlServerDbContext<LedgerlyDbContext>("ledgerlydb");

AddSqlServerDbContext comes from the Aspire client integration Aspire.Microsoft.EntityFrameworkCore.SqlServer. It reads the connection string named ledgerlydb (locally from Aspire, in Azure from an environment variable), and it adds retries, a health check and telemetry 🔧. You get a retrying execution strategy without writing EnableRetryOnFailure() yourself, and every query shows up as a span in the traces (chapter 15).

The model, in one place

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Core/Data/LedgerlyDbContext.cs

public sealed class LedgerlyDbContext(DbContextOptions<LedgerlyDbContext> options) : DbContext(options)
{
    public DbSet<Portfolio> Portfolios => Set<Portfolio>();
    public DbSet<Transaction> Transactions => Set<Transaction>();
    // ...
    protected override void OnModelCreating(ModelBuilder modelBuilder)
    {
        modelBuilder.Entity<Portfolio>(e =>
        {
            e.Property(p => p.OwnerId).HasMaxLength(128);
            e.Property(p => p.Name).HasMaxLength(100);
            e.Property(p => p.BaseCurrency).HasMaxLength(3).IsFixedLength();
            e.Property(p => p.RowVersion).IsRowVersion();
            e.HasIndex(p => p.OwnerId);
            e.HasMany(p => p.Transactions).WithOne().HasForeignKey(t => t.PortfolioId);
        });

        modelBuilder.Entity<Transaction>(e =>
        {
            e.Property(t => t.Symbol).HasMaxLength(12);
            e.Property(t => t.Type).HasConversion<string>().HasMaxLength(4);
            e.Property(t => t.Quantity).HasPrecision(18, 6);
            e.Property(t => t.Price).HasPrecision(18, 6);
            e.Property(t => t.ExternalRef).HasMaxLength(64);
            e.HasIndex(t => new { t.PortfolioId, t.ExternalRef })
                .IsUnique()
                .HasFilter("[ExternalRef] IS NOT NULL");
        });
        // ...
    }
}

The domain classes stay free of EF attributes (they live in Ledgerly.Core/Domain). Everything about storage is here, in fluent configuration a DBA can review:

Configuration What it becomes in SQL Why
HasMaxLength(128), IsFixedLength() nvarchar(128), nchar(3) No accidental nvarchar(max) columns
HasPrecision(18, 6) decimal(18,6) Money and quantities never use floating point
HasConversion<string>() nvarchar(4) holding Buy or Sell Readable data, and reordering the enum can't corrupt rows
IsRowVersion() rowversion The concurrency token, next section
HasIndex(p => p.OwnerId) A nonclustered index Every query filters by owner

A filtered unique index for idempotent imports

The last index deserves a second look. An imported broker row carries an ExternalRef (the broker's own reference, like BRK-1001), and the domain comment says why it matters: "Unique per portfolio, so a re-imported file cannot double-count." Trades typed in by hand have no reference, so the index only applies where one exists. EF Core turns HasFilter into exactly that in the migration:

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Core/Data/Migrations/20261028152017_Initial.cs

            migrationBuilder.CreateIndex(
                name: "IX_Transactions_PortfolioId_ExternalRef",
                table: "Transactions",
                columns: new[] { "PortfolioId", "ExternalRef" },
                unique: true,
                filter: "[ExternalRef] IS NOT NULL");

If a user uploads the same CSV twice, the database rejects the duplicates, however the code above it behaves. Chapter 7 shows how the worker uses this for idempotent imports.

💡 Put invariants in the database when you can. Code checks can race, but a unique index can't.

🧠 Optimistic concurrency: how one "sell all" loses

The rowversion column

SQL Server's rowversion is a value the database changes on every update of the row. EF Core includes the value it originally read in the WHERE clause of each UPDATE. If another write got there first, the row no longer matches, zero rows are affected, and EF Core throws DbUpdateConcurrencyException (docs).

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Core/Domain/Portfolio.cs

    // Touched on every change (including new trades) so the rowversion check below protects the whole aggregate.
    public DateTimeOffset UpdatedAt { get; set; } = DateTimeOffset.UtcNow;

    // SQL Server rowversion: EF Core uses it for optimistic concurrency.
    public byte[] RowVersion { get; set; } = [];

Here's the subtle part. A new trade is an INSERT into Transactions. Nothing about the Portfolios row changes, so on its own a rowversion on Portfolios would protect nothing. That's why recording a trade also touches UpdatedAt on the portfolio. Now every trade is also an UPDATE of the portfolio row, guarded by its rowversion, in the same transaction.

💻 In Ledgerly: recording a trade

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Api/Endpoints/PortfolioEndpoints.cs

        // EF trap: the Id is already set (Guid v7, generated in C#). Adding via portfolio.Transactions would make EF
        // assume the row EXISTS and send an UPDATE (0 rows -> DbUpdateConcurrencyException). Add it explicitly.
        db.Transactions.Add(transaction);
        portfolio.UpdatedAt = DateTimeOffset.UtcNow; // bumps rowversion: two parallel "sell all" requests cannot both win

        try
        {
            await db.SaveChangesAsync(ct);
        }
        catch (DbUpdateConcurrencyException)
        {
            return TypedResults.Problem("Portfolio changed while recording the trade. Retry the request.", statusCode: StatusCodes.Status409Conflict);
        }

Here is the race from Elena's ticket, with the fix in place:

sequenceDiagram
    participant Phone as "Phone"
    participant Laptop as "Laptop"
    participant Api as "Ledgerly.Api"
    participant Sql as "Azure SQL"
    Phone->>Api: POST sell 40 VWCE
    Laptop->>Api: POST sell 40 VWCE
    Api->>Sql: Read portfolio, RowVersion v1, holds 40
    Api->>Sql: Read portfolio, RowVersion v1, holds 40
    Note over Api: Both requests pass the holdings check
    Api->>Sql: Insert trade and update portfolio where RowVersion is v1
    Sql-->>Api: 1 row, commit, RowVersion is now v2
    Api-->>Phone: 201 Created
    Api->>Sql: Insert trade and update portfolio where RowVersion is v1
    Sql-->>Api: 0 rows, EF rolls back the transaction
    Api-->>Laptop: 409 Conflict, retry

When the laptop retries, it reads the new state (0 shares), and the holdings check rejects the sell with a 400. The user sees "you don't hold these shares", which is the truth.

Two flavors of the same mechanism

Ledgerly uses the rowversion in two ways, and it helps to name them:

Scenario Where the expected version comes from Endpoint
Stale client: the user edited an old copy The client sends back the Version it read (Base64 of the rowversion) PUT /api/portfolios/{id} (rename) sets OriginalValue from the request
Racing requests: two writes in flight at once The version EF read at the start of this request POST .../transactions relies on the tracked value

Both end in DbUpdateConcurrencyException and a 409 ProblemDetails, the contract from chapter 4. If you ever need to show the user what changed, entry.GetDatabaseValues() gives you the current row.

💡 Optimistic concurrency doesn't prevent the race. It makes sure exactly one side wins and the other side knows it lost.

🧠 The EF trap you will hit: client keys added through a navigation

This one is a real story from building Ledgerly, and it will probably happen to you.

Every entity gets its ID in C#: public Guid Id { get; init; } = Guid.CreateVersion7();. The first version of RecordTransaction added the new trade the natural object-oriented way:

// illustrative, not in the sample: the version that failed
portfolio.Transactions.Add(transaction);
portfolio.UpdatedAt = DateTimeOffset.UtcNow;
await db.SaveChangesAsync(ct);

Unit tests were green. Then the integration test against a real SQL Server failed: the buy returned 409, on a portfolio nobody else was touching.

What happened: when EF Core finds a new object through a navigation of a tracked entity, it has to guess whether that object is new or already in the database. For a key that EF expects to generate, a key that's already set means "this row exists." So EF tracked the transaction as an existing row and sent an UPDATE Transactions ... WHERE Id = @id. No such row existed, the update affected 0 rows, and EF reported that the same way it reports a concurrency conflict: DbUpdateConcurrencyException. The endpoint caught it and returned 409, so the bug looked like a concurrency problem when it was really a tracking problem.

flowchart TD
    New["new Transaction<br/>Id = Guid.CreateVersion7"] --> How{"How is it added?"}
    How -- "portfolio.Transactions.Add" --> Guess["EF discovers it via navigation<br/>key already set"]
    Guess --> Existing["Tracked as an existing row"]
    Existing --> Update["UPDATE Transactions<br/>WHERE Id = new id"]
    Update --> Zero["0 rows affected"]
    Zero --> Ex["DbUpdateConcurrencyException<br/>API returns 409"]
    How -- "db.Transactions.Add" --> Added["Tracked as Added"]
    Added --> Insert["INSERT Transactions"]
    Insert --> Ok["201 Created"]

The fix is one line: add the entity through its DbSet, which tells EF explicitly that it's new. The comment in PortfolioEndpoints.cs (in the excerpt above) records why, so nobody "simplifies" it back.

Option Effect Ledgerly's choice
db.Transactions.Add(transaction) Explicitly Added, so EF sends an INSERT ✅ Used, with a comment
Configure the key as never generated by EF EF stops treating a set key as "exists" (check the docs for how this changes graph handling) Not used
Let the database generate keys No client-side IDs, so you lose IDs before insert Not used: the outbox and Location headers need them

If you want to try the second option, it looks like this:

// illustrative, not in the sample
modelBuilder.Entity<Transaction>().Property(t => t.Id).ValueGeneratedNever();

The bigger lesson is about tests. Only a test that talked to a real SQL Server could catch this, because the in-memory unit tests never sent SQL. Chapter 17 builds those tests with Testcontainers.

💡 With client-generated keys, add new entities through their DbSet. And when a 409 shows up with no second user in sight, suspect tracking before concurrency.

🧠 Migrations as a deploy step

Adding a migration

Migrations live in Ledgerly.Core/Data/Migrations, next to the model. The dotnet ef tool comes from the repo's tool manifest (chapter 3), so everyone uses the same version:

cd sample
dotnet tool restore
dotnet ef migrations add <Name> --project src/Ledgerly.Core --output-dir Data/Migrations

Adding a migration doesn't need a database. The tool builds the DbContext through a design-time factory with a placeholder connection string:

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Core/Data/DesignTimeDbContextFactory.cs

/// <summary>
/// Used only by the `dotnet ef` tool at design time (adding migrations, building bundles).
/// The connection string is a placeholder: no database is contacted when adding a migration.
/// </summary>
public sealed class DesignTimeDbContextFactory : IDesignTimeDbContextFactory<LedgerlyDbContext>

Review the generated migration like any other code. The Initial migration shows the rowversion column exactly as configured: RowVersion = table.Column<byte[]>(type: "rowversion", rowVersion: true, nullable: false).

Why not Database.Migrate() at startup?

It's tempting: the app starts, migrates and serves. With one instance on one server, it even works. In the cloud, the API might start as ten replicas at once, and the docs advise against migrating at startup in multi-instance production (docs). Even though EF9 and later take a migration lock, you still have problems: every replica needs schema-change rights, a slow migration delays every replica's readiness, and a failed migration leaves you with half-started apps.

Ledgerly runs migrations in their own process, Ledgerly.MigrationService, which migrates and then exits. The apps don't start until it has finished successfully.

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.MigrationService/Program.cs

            // The execution strategy retries transient failures (e.g. Azure SQL serverless waking up).
            var strategy = db.Database.CreateExecutionStrategy();
            await strategy.ExecuteAsync(async () => await db.Database.MigrateAsync(stoppingToken));
            LogMigrated(logger);
            // ...
        catch (Exception ex)
        {
            activity?.AddException(ex);
            activity?.SetStatus(ActivityStatusCode.Error);
            LogMigrationFailed(logger, ex);
            Environment.ExitCode = 1; // a failed job execution blocks the deployment pipeline
        }

        lifetime.StopApplication();

Three details matter:

  1. The execution strategy. A retrying strategy can't safely replay work it didn't start, so work you run yourself must go through CreateExecutionStrategy().ExecuteAsync(...) (docs). Here it covers the migration and the database-user step, so a paused Azure SQL database that takes a moment to resume isn't a failed deployment.
  2. The exit code. On failure, the process exits with code 1. Locally, Aspire shows the resource as failed. In Azure, the migration runs as a Container Apps job, and a failed job execution stops the pipeline before new app revisions roll out (chapter 12).
  3. StopApplication(): success or failure, the process ends. A migration runner that keeps running is a bug.

💻 In Ledgerly: the apps wait for completion

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.AppHost/AppHost.cs

var migrations = builder.AddProject<Projects.Ledgerly_MigrationService>("migrations")
    .WithReference(db)
    .WaitFor(db);

builder.AddProject<Projects.Ledgerly_Api>("api")
    .WithReference(db)
    .WithReference(cache)
    .WithReference(marketData)
    .WaitFor(cache)
    .WaitForCompletion(migrations)
    .WithHttpHealthCheck("/health")
    .WithExternalHttpEndpoints();

WaitFor(db) means "start when the database is healthy." WaitForCompletion(migrations) means "start only after the migrator has exited successfully." The worker has the same line. Locally and in Azure, the order is the same:

flowchart LR
    Sql["SQL database<br/>container or Azure SQL"] -- "healthy" --> Mig["Migration service<br/>run to completion"]
    Mig -- "exit code 0" --> Api["Ledgerly.Api"]
    Mig -- "exit code 0" --> Worker["Ledgerly.Worker"]
    Mig -- "exit code 1" --> Stop["Apps not started<br/>deployment stops"]

Other ways to ship migrations

Approach How When it fits
Migration service (Ledgerly) A small app that calls MigrateAsync, run as a job You want the same path locally and in Azure, plus extra steps like creating the DB user
Migration bundle dotnet ef migrations bundle --self-contained -r linux-x64 -o efbundle, then run ./efbundle --connection "<cs>" A single executable in CI, with no SDK or source needed
Idempotent SQL script dotnet ef migrations script --idempotent -o migrate.sql A DBA must review and run the SQL

Whatever you choose, add dotnet ef migrations has-pending-model-changes to CI. It fails when someone changed the model and forgot to add a migration.

💡 A schema change is a deployment step with its own success or failure, not a side effect of an app starting up.

🧠 Azure SQL: serverless, free to start, and no passwords

Serverless and auto-pause

For the beta, Ledgerly uses an Azure SQL serverless database in the General Purpose tier (docs). Compute scales between a minimum (as low as 0.5 vCore) and a maximum, and you're billed per vCore-second of use 💰. When nobody connects for the auto-pause delay (default 60 minutes, minimum 15, -1 disables it), the database pauses and compute billing stops. The first connection after a pause triggers a resume with a short delay, which is why every data access in Ledgerly goes through a retrying execution strategy.

On top of that, the Azure SQL free offer 🔧 gives each database, per month, 100,000 vCore-seconds, 32 GB of data and 32 GB of backup storage, for up to 10 serverless databases per subscription (docs). When the monthly allowance is used up, the database either auto-pauses until next month or continues and bills the overage, depending on a setting 💰.

📄 sample/infra/modules/sql.bicep

    administrators: {
      administratorType: 'ActiveDirectory'
      principalType: 'Application'
      login: adminIdentityName
      sid: adminIdentityClientId // 🔧 for a managed identity, the Learn Bicep sample uses the client id
      tenantId: subscription().tenantId
      azureADOnlyAuthentication: true
    }
// ...
  sku: {
    name: 'GP_S_Gen5_2'
    tier: 'GeneralPurpose'
    family: 'Gen5'
    capacity: 2
  }
  properties: {
    autoPauseDelay: 60
    minCapacity: json('0.5')
    useFreeLimit: useFreeLimit // 💰 100,000 vCore-seconds per month, then pause until next month
    freeLimitExhaustionBehavior: useFreeLimit ? 'AutoPause' : null
  }
Setting Value Effect
GP_S_Gen5_2 General Purpose, serverless, up to 2 vCores Small, and scales down when idle
minCapacity 0.5 The lowest compute while running
autoPauseDelay 60 minutes Pauses after an idle hour
useFreeLimit and AutoPause on exhaustion On by default A runaway month pauses instead of producing a surprise bill 💰

For a production system with steady traffic, auto-pause's resume delay may be unacceptable. Chapter 10 compares the options.

Entra-only authentication

azureADOnlyAuthentication: true disables SQL authentication on the server. There are no SQL logins and no passwords: only Microsoft Entra identities can connect (docs). That's Jonas's favorite line in the whole infrastructure.

The apps connect with their managed identity. This is the connection string the Bicep sets on the Container Apps:

📄 sample/infra/modules/apps.bicep

var sqlConnection = 'Server=tcp:${sqlServerFqdn},1433;Database=${databaseName};Authentication=Active Directory Managed Identity;User Id=${identityClientId};Encrypt=True;Connect Timeout=60'
Part Meaning
Authentication=Active Directory Managed Identity Microsoft.Data.SqlClient gets a token for the app's managed identity, with no secret (docs)
User Id=<client id> Which user-assigned identity to use
Connect Timeout=60 Gives a paused database time to resume on the first connection
No Password There's nothing to leak

You may see Authentication=Active Directory Default in tutorials. It tries a chain of credentials, which is convenient on a laptop, but the docs note it can be slow and isn't recommended for production. Use the explicit managed identity form in Azure.

Who creates the database user?

An identity that can sign in still needs a user in the database with the right roles. Ledgerly uses two identities: the migration job runs as the SQL admin identity, and the apps run as a separate identity with read and write rights only. After migrating, the migration service creates the app's database user idempotently (EnsureAppUserAsync, with CREATE USER ... WITH SID = ..., TYPE = E) when the Database:AppUser settings are present. Locally those settings aren't set, so the step is skipped. Chapter 11 explains why it uses a SID instead of FROM EXTERNAL PROVIDER, and chapter 13 covers the identities themselves.

💡 The cheapest database is the one that's paused, and the safest password is the one that doesn't exist. Azure SQL serverless with Entra-only auth gives you both.

⚠️ Common traps

Trap What happens Do this instead
Adding a new entity with a client-generated key through a navigation EF assumes the row exists, sends an UPDATE, affects 0 rows and throws DbUpdateConcurrencyException Add it via its DbSet (db.Transactions.Add), and keep the comment that explains why
A rowversion on the parent, but child inserts don't touch the parent Two requests both insert children, and nothing conflicts Touch the parent (UpdatedAt) in the same SaveChanges, so the parent's rowversion guards the aggregate
Database.Migrate() in Program.cs of every app Replicas race, every app needs DDL rights, and a failed migration half-starts the system A run-to-completion migration process, with the apps waiting for its success
Your own transaction or multi-step work with a retrying strategy An exception explaining that the strategy doesn't support user-initiated transactions, or a half-retried operation Wrap the whole unit in db.Database.CreateExecutionStrategy().ExecuteAsync(...)
No retries against serverless Azure SQL The first request after an idle hour fails while the database resumes Use the Aspire integration (retries included) and a generous connect timeout
Authentication=Active Directory Default in production Slow connections while it walks the credential chain Active Directory Managed Identity with User Id=<client id>
Testing data access only with in-memory fakes Tracking and SQL-translation bugs like the one in this chapter reach production Integration tests against a real SQL Server (chapter 17)

🧰 Helpers

EF Core commands (from sample/, after dotnet tool restore):

Task Command
Add a migration dotnet ef migrations add <Name> --project src/Ledgerly.Core --output-dir Data/Migrations
List migrations dotnet ef migrations list --project src/Ledgerly.Core (connects to the database to show which are applied)
Check for a forgotten migration dotnet ef migrations has-pending-model-changes --project src/Ledgerly.Core
SQL script for review dotnet ef migrations script --idempotent --project src/Ledgerly.Core -o migrate.sql
Self-contained bundle dotnet ef migrations bundle --self-contained -r linux-x64 -o efbundle --project src/Ledgerly.Core

A concurrency-safe write, the pattern from this chapter in one place:

// illustrative, not in the sample
var order = await db.Orders.SingleOrDefaultAsync(o => o.Id == id && o.OwnerId == ownerId, ct);
if (order is null)
{
    return TypedResults.NotFound();
}

db.Entry(order).Property(o => o.RowVersion).OriginalValue = Convert.FromBase64String(request.Version);
db.OrderLines.Add(new OrderLine { OrderId = order.Id, Sku = request.Sku }); // via the DbSet, not order.Lines
order.UpdatedAt = clock.GetUtcNow();                                         // touch the parent

try
{
    await db.SaveChangesAsync(ct);
}
catch (DbUpdateConcurrencyException)
{
    return TypedResults.Problem("Changed by someone else. Reload and try again.", statusCode: StatusCodes.Status409Conflict);
}

Data checklist for a new service:

  • [ ] DbContext registered via the Aspire integration (retries, health check, tracing)
  • [ ] Explicit lengths and precision for every string and decimal
  • [ ] Business invariants backed by unique (or filtered unique) indexes
  • [ ] A rowversion on every aggregate that can be edited concurrently, with 409 on conflict
  • [ ] New child entities added via their DbSet
  • [ ] Migrations run as a separate step, and the apps wait for it
  • [ ] has-pending-model-changes in CI
  • [ ] Entra-only auth and a managed identity connection string in Azure

Make it permanent: add dotnet ef migrations has-pending-model-changes as a CI step, and keep at least one integration test that records a trade against real SQL (chapter 17), because that's the test that caught the trap. The toolkit's production-readiness-checklist.md repeats the key ones (migrations as a separate step, managed identities for Azure SQL, 409 on conflicts) (see the toolkit). Terms like rowversion, execution strategy and auto-pause are in the glossary.

🧪 Try it yourself (optional, ~25 min)

None of these steps need a running database, except step 5.

  1. Restore the EF tool and look at the migrations: bash cd sample dotnet tool restore dotnet ef --version Open src/Ledgerly.Core/Data/Migrations/20261028152017_Initial.cs and find the rowversion column and the filtered index IX_Transactions_PortfolioId_ExternalRef.
  2. Generate the SQL a DBA would review: bash dotnet ef migrations script --idempotent --project src/Ledgerly.Core -o migrate.sql Open migrate.sql. Find CREATE UNIQUE INDEX ... WHERE [ExternalRef] IS NOT NULL and the IF NOT EXISTS checks that make the script safe to run twice. Delete the file when you're done.
  3. Catch a forgotten migration. Add a property to Portfolio in src/Ledgerly.Core/Domain/Portfolio.cs, for example public string? Notes { get; set; }, then run: bash dotnet ef migrations has-pending-model-changes --project src/Ledgerly.Core It reports pending changes (a CI step would fail here). Remove the property and run it again: no changes.
  4. Read the trap: open src/Ledgerly.Api/Endpoints/PortfolioEndpoints.cs, find the "EF trap" comment in RecordTransaction, and follow the sequence diagram above against the code.
  5. Optional, needs Docker: reproduce the trap. Change db.Transactions.Add(transaction); to portfolio.Transactions.Add(transaction); and run the tests: bash dotnet test --solution Ledgerly.slnx The integration tests that record a trade fail, because the setup buy returns 409 instead of 201. Revert the line and run the tests again: all green.

✅ Recap

  1. EF Core 10, registered through Aspire's AddSqlServerDbContext, gives you a configured model, retries, health checks and tracing, and invariants like duplicate imports belong in (filtered) unique indexes.
  2. A rowversion plus a touched parent turns racing writes into exactly one success and one 409, and client-generated keys must be added via their DbSet to avoid a fake concurrency error.
  3. Migrations run as a separate process that the apps wait for, against an Azure SQL serverless database that auto-pauses, uses the free offer, and only accepts Entra identities.

Next → Chapter 6: Local development with Aspire

Chapter 6: Local development with Aspire

⏱ About 30 min · You'll learn: how one C# file (the AppHost) starts SQL Server, Redis, the Service Bus emulator and every Ledgerly service, how connection strings and service addresses reach your code as environment variables, and what the Aspire dashboard and service defaults give you from the first run.

📖 Story: The 14-step wiki page

Your import feature needs everything at once: SQL Server for the data, Redis for the cache, a Service Bus queue for the work, the fake market-data vendor for prices, the API, the worker, and the migrations in front of all of them. At Nordbank Insurance this was the moment you opened the wiki page called "Developer setup". It had 14 steps. Step 4 was "Ask the DBA for a login on DEVSQL02", the shared dev server. Step 9 was "Enable MSMQ in Windows Features and create the private queues by hand". Step 12 said "If the service doesn't start, ask Lars". New joiners lost two days to it, and because everyone shared one SQL Server, one person's half-finished migration broke everybody's afternoon.

Tomás Varga sees you open a blank text file to start your own list. "Don't," he says. "Clone the repo, run aspire run, and the whole system is up. If it takes more than that, it's a bug, and we fix it in the repo, not on a wiki." Maya Okafor pulls up a chair. "And once it's up, you'll see every log, trace and metric from every service in one place. Then we can talk about what happens when the market-data vendor falls over."

In your notebook, under Habits I brought with me, you write: "The dev environment is a document." Then you add a question mark.

🏚️ Old way → 🚀 New way

What you did before What you do now Why it changed
A wiki page with 14 manual setup steps An AppHost project in the repo, started with aspire run Code is reviewed and versioned, so it can't drift from reality the way a wiki does
A shared dev SQL Server that everyone migrated against A SQL Server container per developer, with a data volume that survives restarts Your experiments don't break your teammates, and a reset is one command
MSMQ enabled by hand in Windows Features The Azure Service Bus emulator as a container, with the queue declared in C# Same SDK and same queue semantics as production, no machine setup
Connection strings copied into web.config on every machine Aspire injects connection strings and service addresses as environment variables Nothing to copy or commit, and the same code reads Azure settings later
Starting four Visual Studio instances in the right order WaitFor and WaitForCompletion express the start order in code The migrations finish before the API starts, every time
Reading NLog files in three folders The Aspire dashboard: console logs, structured logs, traces and metrics for every service One request is one trace across the API, the database and the vendor
Copy-pasted logging and retry setup in each project One AddServiceDefaults() call from a shared project OpenTelemetry, health checks, resilience and service discovery are the same everywhere

🧠 What Aspire is, and what it is not

Aspire is a set of tools for describing, running and observing a distributed application. It started as a .NET-only stack and, since version 13, also hosts JavaScript, Python, Java and Rust apps. You will use three parts every day:

Part What it is In Ledgerly
AppHost A small .NET project that describes your resources (containers, projects, cloud services) and how they connect, in C# src/Ledgerly.AppHost
Service defaults A shared project with one extension method that adds OpenTelemetry, health checks, resilience and service discovery src/Ledgerly.ServiceDefaults
Aspire dashboard A web UI with resources, console logs, structured logs, traces and metrics Opens when you run aspire run

A few facts changed recently, so check them before you rely on them (all 🔧):

  • The product used to be called ".NET Aspire". The ".NET" prefix was dropped with Aspire 13 (November 2025), and the docs moved to aspire.dev.
  • Aspire 13 requires the .NET 10 SDK. The current release line is 13.6, and the sample uses packages and the AppHost SDK at 13.6.4.
  • The old dotnet workload install aspire is gone. You install the Aspire CLI instead.
# Windows (PowerShell)
irm https://aspire.dev/install.ps1 | iex

# macOS or Linux
curl -sSL https://aspire.dev/install.sh | bash

# Alternatives: winget install Microsoft.Aspire   or   dotnet tool install -g Aspire.Cli

Source: Install the Aspire CLI.

You also need a container runtime: Docker Desktop or Podman. Aspire runs SQL Server, Redis and the Service Bus emulator as containers. Without a runtime, your projects can start, but their dependencies can't.

Local orchestration first, deployment optional

Aspire can do more than run things on your laptop. aspire publish emits deployment artifacts such as Bicep, and aspire deploy is now the recommended way to deploy an Aspire app to Azure 🔧 (Deploy with Aspire). The Azure Developer CLI (azd) still works, but it is no longer the recommended default for Aspire.

This course uses Aspire for local development only and deploys with hand-written Bicep (chapter 11). That is a teaching choice, not a verdict on aspire deploy:

Use aspire deploy when Use hand-written Bicep (this course) when
You want the fastest path from AppHost to Azure Container Apps You want to see and review every Azure resource, role assignment and setting
The team is small and the generated defaults fit A platform team owns infrastructure separately from application code
You are prototyping or running a demo environment A security reviewer needs every line explained, or you need settings the generator doesn't cover

💡 The AppHost describes your system for development. It is not your production configuration, even though it can generate one.

🧠 The AppHost, line by line

The AppHost is an ordinary .NET project with a special SDK. The version lives in the Sdk attribute, and the four service projects are plain project references:

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.AppHost/Ledgerly.AppHost.csproj

<Project Sdk="Aspire.AppHost.Sdk/13.6.4">
  <!-- ... -->
  <ItemGroup>
    <ProjectReference Include="..\Ledgerly.Api\Ledgerly.Api.csproj" />
    <ProjectReference Include="..\Ledgerly.Worker\Ledgerly.Worker.csproj" />
    <ProjectReference Include="..\Ledgerly.MarketData\Ledgerly.MarketData.csproj" />
    <ProjectReference Include="..\Ledgerly.MigrationService\Ledgerly.MigrationService.csproj" />
  </ItemGroup>

  <ItemGroup>
    <PackageReference Include="Aspire.Hosting.SqlServer" />
    <PackageReference Include="Aspire.Hosting.Redis" />
    <PackageReference Include="Aspire.Hosting.Azure.ServiceBus" />
  </ItemGroup>
</Project>

The Aspire.Hosting.* packages are hosting integrations: they teach the AppHost how to run a resource. The services use separate client integrations (for example Aspire.Microsoft.EntityFrameworkCore.SqlServer) to consume it. The versions come from Directory.Packages.props (chapter 3).

💻 In Ledgerly: the infrastructure

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.AppHost/AppHost.cs

// The whole Ledgerly system, described in C#. `aspire run` (or F5 on this project) starts everything.
var builder = DistributedApplication.CreateBuilder(args);

// ── Infrastructure (containers locally; real Azure services in the cloud, see infra/) ──────────
var sql = builder.AddSqlServer("sql")
    .WithLifetime(ContainerLifetime.Persistent)   // keep the container (and data) between runs
    .WithDataVolume();
var db = sql.AddDatabase("ledgerlydb");

var cache = builder.AddRedis("cache");

var messaging = builder.AddAzureServiceBus("messaging").RunAsEmulator();
messaging.AddServiceBusQueue("imports");
Line What it does Why it matters
AddSqlServer("sql") Runs a SQL Server container named sql No shared dev server, no install, and no password written in the repo
.WithLifetime(ContainerLifetime.Persistent) Keeps the container running after you stop the AppHost, and reuses it next time SQL Server is slow to start. The second aspire run is much faster
.WithDataVolume() Stores the database files in a named container volume Your data survives even if the container is recreated. Delete the volume to reset
sql.AddDatabase("ledgerlydb") Declares a database on that server ledgerlydb is the name the services ask for, here and in Azure
AddRedis("cache") Runs a Redis container The API uses it as the shared second level of HybridCache (chapter 18)
AddAzureServiceBus("messaging").RunAsEmulator() Declares a Service Bus namespace and, locally, runs the official emulator container instead Same SDK and same peek-lock semantics as production (chapter 7)
messaging.AddServiceBusQueue("imports") Declares the imports queue The queue is code. Nobody creates it by hand

The Service Bus emulator is honest about what it is. It is a Linux container for local development and testing only, it brings its own SQL backing store, and it has limits 🔧: 50 entities, 100 MB per entity, no Entra ID authentication (it uses a connection string with UseDevelopmentEmulator=true) and no persistence, so messages are lost when it restarts (Service Bus emulator overview).

Notice the asymmetry: SQL Server is persistent, the queue is not. If you stop the AppHost while messages are still waiting in imports, they are gone and those imports stay Pending. That is fine for development, and a good reminder that the emulator proves your code paths, not your durability.

💻 In Ledgerly: the services

// ── Services ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
var marketData = builder.AddProject<Projects.Ledgerly_MarketData>("marketdata")
    .WithHttpHealthCheck("/health");

var migrations = builder.AddProject<Projects.Ledgerly_MigrationService>("migrations")
    .WithReference(db)
    .WaitFor(db);

builder.AddProject<Projects.Ledgerly_Api>("api")
    .WithReference(db)
    .WithReference(cache)
    .WithReference(marketData)
    .WaitFor(cache)
    .WaitForCompletion(migrations)
    .WithHttpHealthCheck("/health")
    .WithExternalHttpEndpoints();

builder.AddProject<Projects.Ledgerly_Worker>("worker")
    .WithReference(db)
    .WithReference(messaging)
    .WithReference(marketData)
    .WaitFor(messaging)
    .WaitForCompletion(migrations)
    .WithHttpHealthCheck("/health");

builder.Build().Run();
Call What it means
AddProject<Projects.Ledgerly_Api>("api") Projects.Ledgerly_Api is generated from the project reference, so a renamed or missing project is a compile error, not a runtime surprise
.WithReference(db) Give this project the connection information for ledgerlydb
.WithReference(marketData) Give this project the address of marketdata, for service discovery
.WaitFor(cache) Don't start until the dependency is running and reports healthy
.WaitForCompletion(migrations) Don't start until the migrations process has exited successfully
.WithHttpHealthCheck("/health") The AppHost polls this endpoint. The dashboard shows the result, and anything that waits for this resource waits for "healthy"
.WithExternalHttpEndpoints() Marks the API's endpoints as public. It matters when Aspire generates deployment artifacts. Locally everything is on localhost anyway

Two details are worth a second look.

First, the reference graph is your architecture. The API references the cache but not Service Bus. The worker references Service Bus but not the cache. That is not an accident: the API never talks to the queue directly; it writes an outbox row in SQL, and the worker publishes it (chapter 7). Anyone reviewing a pull request that adds .WithReference(messaging) to the API should ask why.

Second, WaitForCompletion turns the migration service into a gate. The migrator is a run-to-completion process. If it fails, it sets a non-zero exit code, and the API and worker never start:

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.MigrationService/Program.cs

        catch (Exception ex)
        {
            activity?.AddException(ex);
            activity?.SetStatus(ActivityStatusCode.Error);
            LogMigrationFailed(logger, ex);
            Environment.ExitCode = 1; // a failed job execution blocks the deployment pipeline
        }

        lifetime.StopApplication();

This is the same contract the Container Apps job follows in Azure (chapter 12): new code never runs against an old schema. You practice the production deployment order every time you press F5.

💡 WaitFor means "is it up?". WaitForCompletion means "did it finish, and did it succeed?". Use the second one for anything that is supposed to exit.

This is AppHost.cs drawn as a picture. Solid arrows are references (configuration flows along them). Dotted arrows are start-order gates.

    end

🧠 How configuration flows: environment variables, not files

WithReference doesn't do anything magical. When the AppHost starts a project, it sets environment variables on that process. Your code reads them through the normal .NET configuration system, where a double underscore __ in a variable name means : in a configuration key. So ConnectionStrings__ledgerlydb is the same key as "ConnectionStrings": { "ledgerlydb": ... } in appsettings.json.

AppHost line What the service receives Code that reads it
.WithReference(db) ConnectionStrings__ledgerlydb builder.AddSqlServerDbContext<LedgerlyDbContext>("ledgerlydb")
.WithReference(cache) ConnectionStrings__cache GetConnectionString("cache"), then AddRedisDistributedCache("cache")
.WithReference(messaging) ConnectionStrings__messaging, an emulator connection string with UseDevelopmentEmulator=true builder.AddAzureServiceBusClient("messaging")
.WithReference(marketData) Endpoint variables named like services__marketdata__https__0 Service discovery resolves https+http://marketdata
Every project OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT, OTEL_SERVICE_NAME and related OTLP settings ConfigureOpenTelemetry() in service defaults

You don't need to memorize the exact names. Click a resource in the dashboard and its environment variables are listed there, which is the fastest way to answer "what does this service actually see?".

The address https+http://marketdata reads as: "find the service called marketdata, prefer its HTTPS endpoint, fall back to HTTP". The handler that resolves it is added once, in service defaults, for every HttpClient in the process.

💻 In Ledgerly: the same code reads Azure settings

The services never mention containers, ports or passwords. They ask for names:

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Api/Program.cs

// ── Data: Aspire client integration = DbContext pooling + retries + health check + tracing ──────
builder.AddSqlServerDbContext<LedgerlyDbContext>("ledgerlydb");
// ...
// ── Outbound HTTP: typed client. Resilience + discovery come from ConfigureHttpClientDefaults. ───
builder.Services.AddHttpClient<IMarketDataClient, MarketDataClient>(client =>
    client.BaseAddress = new Uri(builder.Configuration["MarketData:BaseUrl"] ?? "https+http://marketdata"));

In Azure there is no AppHost. Bicep sets the same variable names on the Container App instead:

📄 sample/infra/modules/apps.bicep

          env: concat(commonEnv, [
            { name: 'OTEL_SERVICE_NAME', value: 'worker' }
            { name: 'ConnectionStrings__ledgerlydb', value: sqlConnection }
            { name: 'ConnectionStrings__messaging', value: serviceBusFqns } // a namespace, not a key: Entra auth
            { name: 'MarketData__BaseUrl', value: marketDataUrl }
          ])
Setting Locally (AppHost) In Azure (Bicep)
ledgerlydb Connection string to the local container, supplied by the AppHost Azure SQL with Authentication=Active Directory Managed Identity, no password (chapter 13)
messaging Emulator connection string The namespace host name only. The sample's comment says it: "a namespace, not a key: Entra auth"
Market data address Service discovery from Aspire's endpoint variables MarketData__BaseUrl, the internal HTTPS address of the marketdata Container App
Telemetry OTLP to the Aspire dashboard APPLICATIONINSIGHTS_CONNECTION_STRING to Application Insights (chapter 16)

💡 Code asks for names. Environments supply values. That is how the same container image runs on your laptop and in production.

🗺️ What happens when you type aspire run

sequenceDiagram
    participant You
    participant CLI as Aspire CLI and AppHost
    participant Runtime as Container runtime
    participant Mig as migrations
    participant Svc as api and worker
    participant Dash as Aspire dashboard
    You->>CLI: aspire run
    CLI->>Dash: start dashboard and OTLP receiver
    CLI->>Runtime: start or reuse sql, cache, messaging
    Runtime-->>CLI: containers healthy
    CLI->>Mig: start with ConnectionStrings__ledgerlydb
    Mig->>Runtime: apply EF Core migrations
    Mig-->>CLI: exit code 0
    CLI->>Svc: start with connection strings and service addresses
    Svc->>Dash: logs, traces and metrics over OTLP
    CLI-->>You: dashboard URL with login token

🧠 The dashboard: your new log folder

At Nordbank, "looking at the logs" meant RDP, a file share and Notepad++. The Aspire dashboard is the local version of what Application Insights will give you in Azure, and it receives the same OpenTelemetry data.

View What you see Old-world equivalent Use it when
Resources Every container and project, its state, health, endpoints and environment variables Services console plus IIS Manager Something didn't start, or you want a service's URL
Console logs Raw stdout and stderr per resource Tailing a log file A process crashes on startup before telemetry is wired
Structured logs OpenTelemetry log records with their properties, filterable by resource, level and value grep over three servers "Show me every warning for import X"
Traces One request as a tree of spans across services, with timings and errors Nothing; you correlated IDs by hand "Why was this valuation slow?"
Metrics Counters and histograms, including ASP.NET Core's built-in http.server.request.duration and Ledgerly's own Ledgerly meter Performance Monitor "Is the import duration growing?"

Two things to know 🔧:

  • Aspire 13.6 added SQLite-backed dashboard history (What's new in Aspire 13.6).
  • The dashboard is also available as a standalone container image, which is handy when you want an OTLP viewer without an AppHost.

The data on the dashboard is only as good as what your services emit. That is the job of service defaults.

🧠 Service defaults: one line of cross-cutting code

Every Ledgerly service starts with builder.AddServiceDefaults(). The method lives in a shared project that started from the Aspire template and was then adapted.

💻 In Ledgerly: what one line adds

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.ServiceDefaults/Extensions.cs

    public static TBuilder AddServiceDefaults<TBuilder>(this TBuilder builder) where TBuilder : IHostApplicationBuilder
    {
        builder.ConfigureOpenTelemetry();
        builder.AddDefaultHealthChecks();

        builder.Services.AddServiceDiscovery();

        builder.Services.ConfigureHttpClientDefaults(http =>
        {
            // Retries with jittered back-off, per-attempt + total timeouts, circuit breaker, rate limiter.
            http.AddStandardResilienceHandler();

            // Resolves "https+http://marketdata" to a real address (Aspire locally, config or DNS in Azure).
            http.AddServiceDiscovery();
        });

        return builder;
    }
Piece What it gives you Where you go deeper
ConfigureOpenTelemetry() Logs, metrics and traces for ASP.NET Core, HttpClient, the runtime and Ledgerly's own ActivitySource and Meter. Export goes to OTLP when OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT is set (locally) and to Azure Monitor when APPLICATIONINSIGHTS_CONNECTION_STRING is set (in Azure) Chapter 15
AddDefaultHealthChecks() A self check tagged live. Aspire client integrations add their own checks (database, Redis, Service Bus) Chapter 14
AddServiceDiscovery() Logical names such as marketdata become real addresses This chapter
AddStandardResilienceHandler() For every HttpClient: rate limiter, total timeout (30 s), retries (3, exponential back-off with jitter), circuit breaker, attempt timeout (10 s) 🔧 Chapter 14

The tracing setup also filters out the health endpoints, because probes call them every few seconds and would drown the real traffic:

                .AddAspNetCoreInstrumentation(options =>
                    // Probes hit these every few seconds: keep them out of traces.
                    options.Filter = context =>
                        !context.Request.Path.StartsWithSegments(ReadinessPath)
                        && !context.Request.Path.StartsWithSegments(LivenessPath))
