One deliberate difference from the template: the template maps /health and /alive only in Development. Ledgerly maps them in every environment, because Container Apps probes need them, and they return a status without details:

    public static WebApplication MapDefaultEndpoints(this WebApplication app)
    {
        // Readiness: all checks (including DB, Redis, Service Bus registered by Aspire integrations).
        app.MapHealthChecks(ReadinessPath).DisableHttpMetrics();

        // Liveness: only checks tagged "live".
        app.MapHealthChecks(LivenessPath, new HealthCheckOptions
        {
            Predicate = r => r.Tags.Contains("live"),
        }).DisableHttpMetrics();

        return app;
    }

💻 In Ledgerly: the chaos switches

Ledgerly.MarketData is the fake vendor. It starts from the Chaos section in its appsettings.json (failure rate 0, latency 0), and in Development it exposes an endpoint that changes those values at runtime:

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.MarketData/Program.cs

// Chaos controls. Only exposed in Development: never ship a "break me" button to production.
if (app.Environment.IsDevelopment())
{
    app.MapGet("/chaos", (ChaosState chaos) => chaos.Current);
    app.MapPost("/chaos", (ChaosOptions options, ChaosState chaos) =>
    {
        chaos.Set(options);
        return Results.Ok(chaos.Current);
    });
}

ChaosState.Set clamps the values (failure rate between 0 and 1, latency up to 60,000 ms). With chaos on, the /prices endpoint adds latency and returns 503 for a share of requests. Because the API's HttpClient has the standard resilience handler, you can watch the retries happen in the Traces view. Priya will use this switch in chapter 14 to prove the API degrades gracefully instead of failing.

💡 Service defaults make "observable, resilient and discoverable" the default state of a new service instead of a task someone has to remember.

⚠️ Common traps

Trap What happens Do this instead
No container runtime running Containers never start, and the API and worker wait forever behind them Start Docker Desktop or Podman first, then check the Resources view for the stuck resource
Editing a migration that the persistent SQL container already applied The database keeps the old schema, and you get errors that don't match the code Add a new migration instead. To start clean, stop the AppHost and delete the SQL container and its data volume
Hard-coding https://localhost:7180 in another service It works on your machine, breaks for a teammate whose port differs, and breaks in Azure Use a reference plus a logical name (https+http://marketdata) or a configuration key such as MarketData:BaseUrl
Using WaitFor(migrations) instead of WaitForCompletion(migrations) The API starts while the migration is still running and hits missing tables Use WaitForCompletion for anything that is supposed to exit
Tagging a database check as live One database blip fails liveness, and the platform restarts every replica Keep only self in liveness. Dependencies belong in readiness (chapter 14)
Treating the emulator as production-like You assume messages survive restarts and that Entra ID auth works, and both assumptions fail Use the emulator for code paths. Prove durability and identity in Azure (chapters 12 and 13)
Putting real cloud secrets in the AppHost "just for local" They end up in git history Let the AppHost supply local connection strings, and keep cloud credentials out of the repo entirely (chapter 13)

🧰 Helpers

Install and run:

# once: install the Aspire CLI (see the PowerShell variant above)
curl -sSL https://aspire.dev/install.sh | bash

# every day
cd sample
aspire run                                   # finds the AppHost through aspire.config.json
dotnet run --project src/Ledgerly.AppHost    # same thing without the CLI

Checklist: adding a new service to the system

  1. Reference Ledgerly.ServiceDefaults, call builder.AddServiceDefaults() and app.MapDefaultEndpoints().
  2. Add the project to AppHost.cs with AddProject<Projects.X>("name").
  3. Add .WithReference(...) for each dependency and .WaitFor(...) or .WaitForCompletion(...) for each one that must be ready first.
  4. Add .WithHttpHealthCheck("/health").
  5. In the service, read connection strings and addresses by name only.
// illustrative, not in the sample: a hypothetical notifications service
builder.AddProject<Projects.Ledgerly_Notifications>("notifications")
    .WithReference(db)
    .WaitForCompletion(migrations)
    .WithHttpHealthCheck("/health");

Reset local data: stop the AppHost, then remove the SQL Server container and its volume with your container tool (docker ps -a, docker volume ls, then docker rm and docker volume rm, or the Podman equivalents). The next aspire run creates a fresh database and the migrator rebuilds the schema.

Make it permanent:

  • Replace the "Developer setup" wiki with three lines in the README: install the .NET 10 SDK, install a container runtime and the Aspire CLI, run aspire run. Anything else is a bug to fix in the AppHost.
  • The AppHost is part of Ledgerly.slnx, so CI builds it on every pull request (chapter 9). A broken AppHost can't be merged.
  • Copy the toolkit's ServiceDefaults.Extensions.cs into new solutions so every service starts observable (see the toolkit).
  • In chapter 17 you'll see how the Aspire testing builder starts the whole AppHost inside a test. Terms you don't recognize are in the glossary.

🧪 Try it yourself (optional, ~25 min)

  1. Start Docker Desktop or Podman, install the Aspire CLI, then run: bash cd sample aspire run Open the dashboard link the CLI prints.
  2. In Resources, watch the order: sql, cache and messaging start first, migrations runs and finishes, and only then do api and worker start. That is WaitForCompletion at work.
  3. Click api and look at its environment variables. Find ConnectionStrings__ledgerlydb and the services__marketdata__... entries.
  4. Create a development token and copy it: bash dotnet user-jwts create --project src/Ledgerly.Api
  5. Open the API's URL from the dashboard and add /scalar (or use src/Ledgerly.Api/Ledgerly.Api.http in your IDE). Create a portfolio, record a buy, then request /api/portfolios/{id}/valuation.
  6. In Traces, open the valuation request. Find the span for the call from api to marketdata.
  7. Turn on chaos. Take the marketdata URL from the dashboard and send: bash curl -X POST https://localhost:<marketdata-port>/chaos -H "Content-Type: application/json" -d '{"failureRate":0.7,"latencyMs":500}' Quotes are cached for 30 seconds, so wait a moment (or value a portfolio with a different symbol), then request the valuation again. Observe the retry spans in Traces, and isComplete: false when retries run out. Set both values back to 0 when you are done.
  8. Stop the AppHost with Ctrl+C and run aspire run again. The SQL container is reused and your portfolio is still there.

✅ Recap

  1. The AppHost describes the whole local system in C#: containers, projects, references and start order, so aspire run replaces the setup wiki.
  2. References become environment variables, and services read connection strings and addresses by name, which is why the same code runs against containers locally and Azure services in production.
  3. Service defaults and the dashboard make every service observable, healthy-checked and resilient from its first run.

Next → Chapter 7: Background work and messaging

Chapter 7: Background work and messaging

⏱ About 35 min · You'll learn: how to move slow work out of the HTTP request with hosted services and an Azure Service Bus queue, how the transactional outbox and an idempotent consumer make "at-least-once" safe, what peek-lock, dead-lettering and duplicate detection do for you, and how a worker shuts down without losing work.

📖 Story: The import that timed out

Elena Rossi forwards a support ticket on Monday morning. A beta user tried to import five years of broker statements, a file of about 15,000 rows (numbers in this story are illustrative). The first prototype parsed the CSV inside the HTTP request, the request ran past the 30-second timeout, and the browser showed an error. So the user clicked Upload again. And again. "What does the user see?" Elena asks. "Right now: an error, and then three copies of some of their trades."

Maya Okafor draws two boxes on the whiteboard, "API" and "worker", and a pipe between them. "The API accepts the file and answers in milliseconds. The worker does the heavy lifting. Now tell me what happens when it fails halfway." You start to answer, and she keeps going: "When the worker crashes after writing half the trades. When the queue delivers the same message twice. When the platform stops the worker in the middle of a file. Every one of those happens in the cloud, and every one has to be boring."

At Nordbank Insurance, background work meant a Windows Service with a System.Timers.Timer polling a "jobs" table, plus a SQL Agent job at 02:00 that nobody dared to touch. In your Habits I brought with me notebook, you write: "Background work runs on one special server that never restarts." Then you cross out "never".

🏚️ Old way → 🚀 New way

What you did before What you do now Why it changed
Long work inside the HTTP request, with a bigger timeout The API stores the request and returns 202 Accepted, and a worker processes it Users get an answer immediately, and the slow part can retry without the user
A Windows Service with a timer on one server, plus a SQL Agent job at 02:00 BackgroundService classes in a worker container with PeriodicTimer, or a scheduled Container Apps job Same hosting, DI, configuration and telemetry as the API, and it lives in the repo
MSMQ on the same box, or a "jobs" table polled every few seconds An Azure Service Bus queue with peek-lock, retries and a dead-letter queue Failed messages are retried and then parked for a human, not lost or retried forever
"Save to the database, then send the message" in two steps A transactional outbox: the business row and the message row in one transaction No more "saved but message lost" or "message sent but save rolled back"
Hoping a message is processed only once An idempotent consumer: a ProcessedMessages table and a unique index At-least-once delivery is safe when processing twice changes nothing
Stopping the service and hoping the current job finished Graceful shutdown: stop taking new work, finish in-flight work within a timeout Containers are stopped all the time (scale-in, new revisions), so stopping must be safe

🧠 Hosted services: the Windows Service, rebuilt

💡 A BackgroundService is a Windows Service that shares hosting, DI, logging and telemetry with the rest of your app, and that can be stopped at any time.

.NET's generic host runs hosted services (IHostedService) next to everything else in the process. BackgroundService is the convenient base class: you override ExecuteAsync, and the host starts it, passes it a CancellationToken, and signals that token when the process must stop. Ledgerly's worker is three of them in one process:

💻 In Ledgerly: the worker's composition root

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Worker/Program.cs

var builder = WebApplication.CreateBuilder(args);

builder.AddServiceDefaults();
builder.AddSqlServerDbContext<LedgerlyDbContext>("ledgerlydb");
builder.AddAzureServiceBusClient("messaging");
// ...
builder.Services.AddHostedService<OutboxRelay>();
builder.Services.AddHostedService<ImportProcessor>();
builder.Services.AddHostedService<AlertEvaluator>();

// Give in-flight messages time to finish when Container Apps stops a replica (SIGTERM).
builder.Services.Configure<HostOptions>(o => o.ShutdownTimeout = TimeSpan.FromSeconds(25));

var app = builder.Build();
app.MapDefaultEndpoints();
app.Run();

Notice WebApplication, not Host: the worker has no API, but it exposes /health and /alive for Container Apps probes (chapter 14). AddAzureServiceBusClient("messaging") is the Aspire client integration. It registers one ServiceBusClient as a singleton, which is what the SDK wants: clients, senders and processors are thread-safe and meant to be reused. Compared with the Windows Service, the biggest change is scaling: there can be more than one replica, so you must design for "more than one".

💻 In Ledgerly: a periodic job with PeriodicTimer

AlertEvaluator checks price alerts every 30 seconds (AlertOptions.IntervalSeconds defaults to 30). It is the replacement for the SQL Agent job:

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Worker/AlertEvaluator.cs

/// <summary>
/// Periodically checks active price alerts against live quotes.
/// Run the worker with ONE replica (or move this to a scheduled Container Apps job) so alerts are not evaluated twice.
/// </summary>
public sealed partial class AlertEvaluator(
    IServiceScopeFactory scopes, IMarketDataClient marketData, IOptions<AlertOptions> options, TimeProvider clock,
    ILogger<AlertEvaluator> logger) : BackgroundService
{
    protected override async Task ExecuteAsync(CancellationToken stoppingToken)
    {
        using var timer = new PeriodicTimer(TimeSpan.FromSeconds(options.Value.IntervalSeconds), clock);
        while (await timer.WaitForNextTickAsync(stoppingToken))
        {
            try
            {
                await EvaluateAsync(stoppingToken);
            }
            catch (Exception ex) when (ex is not OperationCanceledException)
            {
                // One bad tick (market data down, DB failover) must not kill the loop.
                LogEvaluationFailed(logger, ex);
            }
        }
    }

Four habits are packed into those lines:

  1. PeriodicTimer instead of System.Timers.Timer. You await the next tick, so a slow tick can't overlap the next one, and cancellation ends the loop cleanly.
  2. TimeProvider clock is injected, so tests can control time instead of waiting 30 seconds (chapter 17).
  3. A new DI scope per tick (scopes.CreateAsyncScope() inside EvaluateAsync). A hosted service is a singleton, and a DbContext must not live that long.
  4. Catch everything except cancellation. An exception that escapes ExecuteAsync ends the loop for good (and by default the host stops too), and one failed market-data call must not end alert checking.

Two replicas, two evaluations

Read the comment again: a timer loop runs in every replica, so two replicas can trigger the same alert at nearly the same moment and create two notifications. The Windows Service never had this problem because there was only one of it. In Azure, the worker has minReplicas: 1 (so the timers always run) and scales out on queue length, which is exactly when a second AlertEvaluator appears. You have three options:

Option How Trade-off
Keep one replica maxReplicas: 1 for the worker Simple, but imports can't scale out
Split the periodic job out A Container Apps job with a Schedule (cron) trigger Runs once per schedule wherever it lands, and finishes. Cron can't express "every 30 seconds"
Make the job safe to run twice A concurrency token on the alert, or a unique constraint on the notification The most robust choice, at the price of more code

Container Apps jobs run finite tasks and support three trigger types: Manual, Schedule (cron) and Event (a KEDA scaler such as a Service Bus queue) (Jobs in Azure Container Apps). Ledgerly already runs its migrations as a job with triggerType: 'Manual' (📄 sample/infra/modules/migration-job.bicep). A scheduled alert job would change only the trigger:

// illustrative, not in the sample: run the alert check every 5 minutes as a job
    configuration: {
      triggerType: 'Schedule'
      replicaTimeout: 300
      replicaRetryLimit: 0
      scheduleTriggerConfig: { cronExpression: '*/5 * * * *', parallelism: 1, replicaCompletionCount: 1 }
    }

💰 A job that runs for a few seconds every few minutes costs less than a replica that is always on. It also needs its own entry point (a console app that runs one evaluation and exits), which is the price of the split.

🧠 Azure Service Bus queues: peek-lock, retries and the dead-letter queue

💡 A queue doesn't make failures go away. It gives every failure a defined next step: try again, or park the message for a human.

A Service Bus queue is point-to-point: senders put messages in, and competing consumers take them out, so you can add workers to drain it faster. That is load leveling, and it is why a burst of imports doesn't hurt the API.

The important part is how a message is received. In PeekLock mode (the default) the receiver gets the message with a lock, and must then settle it:

Settle action Meaning Ledgerly uses it when
Complete "Done, delete it." The import was applied, or the message was a duplicate
Abandon "I failed, give it to someone else." The delivery count goes up An unexpected exception (database blip, bug)
Dead-letter "This will never work. Park it." The import row doesn't exist: a poison message

(A fourth action, Defer, sets a message aside to fetch later. Ledgerly doesn't use it.)

That gives you at-least-once delivery: a message is only removed when someone completes it. The alternative, ReceiveAndDelete, removes the message on receipt (at-most-once), so a crash loses it. For imports, losing work is worse than doing it twice, as long as doing it twice is harmless.

Three queue settings turn this into a safety net:

  • MaxDeliveryCount (default 10). After that many deliveries, Service Bus moves the message to the dead-letter queue (DLQ), a sub-queue at <entity>/$deadletterqueue, with a reason such as MaxDeliveryCountExceeded. DLQ messages are never deleted automatically, so somebody has to watch them (chapter 16 covers alerting) (Dead-letter queues).
  • Duplicate detection. The queue remembers every MessageId it saw within a time window (default 10 minutes, configurable from 20 seconds to 7 days) and drops repeats. It must be enabled when the queue is created (Duplicate detection).
  • Dead-lettering on expiration. Messages whose time-to-live runs out go to the DLQ instead of disappearing.

💻 In Ledgerly: the queue, as code

📄 sample/infra/modules/servicebus.bicep

// Service Bus Standard (needed for duplicate detection). Local (SAS key) auth is disabled:
// only Entra identities with a data role can send or receive.
// ...
  sku: { name: 'Standard', tier: 'Standard' }
  properties: {
    disableLocalAuth: true
    // ...
resource imports 'Microsoft.ServiceBus/namespaces/queues@2024-01-01' = {
  parent: namespace
  name: 'imports'
  properties: {
    lockDuration: 'PT1M'
    maxDeliveryCount: 10 // after 10 failed attempts the message moves to imports/$deadletterqueue
    requiresDuplicateDetection: true // the same MessageId within the window is dropped
    duplicateDetectionHistoryTimeWindow: 'PT10M'
    deadLetteringOnMessageExpiration: true
  }
}

lockDuration: 'PT1M' is how long the worker holds a message before Service Bus assumes it died and offers the message to someone else. disableLocalAuth: true means no connection-string keys at all: the worker's managed identity gets the Azure Service Bus Data Sender and Data Receiver roles further down the same file (chapter 13).

The tier matters 🔧 💰. Basic has no duplicate detection, sessions or transactions, so Ledgerly uses Standard (shared capacity, pay per operation). Premium adds dedicated Messaging Units, VNet and Private Link, and messages up to 100 MB, at a fixed cost even when idle.

One honest gap: locally, the AppHost declares the queue with messaging.AddServiceBusQueue("imports") and no extra settings, so the emulator queue has no duplicate detection window. The idempotent consumer below is what keeps local runs correct, which is a good argument for never relying on duplicate detection alone.

🗺️ The life of an import message

flowchart TD
    Send["Relay sends message<br/>MessageId = outbox id"]
    Dup{"MessageId seen in<br/>last 10 minutes?"}
    Drop["Dropped by<br/>duplicate detection"]
    Queue["Waiting in<br/>imports queue"]
    Locked["Delivered with a lock<br/>to one worker"]
    Done["Completed<br/>message deleted"]
    Retry{"Delivery count<br/>below 10?"}
    Dlq["Dead-letter queue<br/>waits for a human"]
    Send --> Dup
    Dup -- "yes, in Azure" --> Drop
    Dup -- "no" --> Queue
    Queue --> Locked
    Locked -- "complete" --> Done
    Locked -- "abandon or lock expires" --> Retry
    Retry -- "yes" --> Queue
    Retry -- "no" --> Dlq
    Locked -- "dead-letter: ImportNotFound" --> Dlq

🧠 The transactional outbox: never lose the message

💡 If the database and the queue can't share a transaction, put the message in the database and send it later.

Here is the bug you would write by habit:

// illustrative, not in the sample: the dual-write bug
db.Imports.Add(import);
await db.SaveChangesAsync(ct);                                   // 1. committed
await sender.SendMessageAsync(new ServiceBusMessage(json), ct);  // 2. what if this throws?

If step 2 fails (a network blip, a throttled namespace, a deployment that kills the process), the import row exists but no message does. The import stays Pending forever. Swap the order, and you get the opposite bug: a message about an import that was rolled back. SQL Server and Service Bus can't take part in one transaction, so you need a different shape.

The transactional outbox writes the message as a row in the same database, in the same transaction as the business change. A separate relay reads unsent rows and publishes them. EnqueueOutboxMessage only adds the row: it is saved by the caller's next SaveChangesAsync.

💻 In Ledgerly: the outbox row

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Core/Messaging/Outbox.cs

public sealed class OutboxMessage
{
    public Guid Id { get; init; } = Guid.CreateVersion7();
    public required string Type { get; init; }
    public required string Destination { get; init; }
    public required string Payload { get; init; }
    public DateTimeOffset OccurredAt { get; init; } = DateTimeOffset.UtcNow;
    public DateTimeOffset? ProcessedAt { get; set; }
    public int Attempts { get; set; }
    public string? LastError { get; set; }

    // W3C trace context of the request that created the message, so the trace continues in the worker.
    public string? TraceParent { get; init; }
}
// ...
    public static void EnqueueOutboxMessage<T>(this LedgerlyDbContext db, string destination, T message)
    {
        db.OutboxMessages.Add(new OutboxMessage
        {
            Type = typeof(T).Name,
            Destination = destination,
            Payload = JsonSerializer.Serialize(message),
            TraceParent = System.Diagnostics.Activity.Current?.Id,
        });
    }

Guid.CreateVersion7() gives time-ordered IDs, which index well. TraceParent captures the current trace, so the worker's spans appear under the original HTTP request in the dashboard (chapter 15).

💻 In Ledgerly: the API side, one SaveChanges

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Api/Endpoints/ImportEndpoints.cs

        db.Imports.Add(import);
        db.EnqueueOutboxMessage(Queues.Imports, new ImportRequested(import.Id, id));
        await db.SaveChangesAsync(ct); // one transaction: both rows or neither

        return TypedResults.AcceptedAtRoute(ToResponse(import), "GetImport", new { importId = import.Id });

EF Core wraps one SaveChangesAsync in one transaction, so either both rows exist or neither does. The endpoint returns 202 Accepted with a Location header pointing at GET /api/imports/{importId}, which the client polls. The endpoint is also rate-limited (five uploads per user per minute) and rejects files over 1,000,000 bytes, so the "click Upload three times" problem can't turn into a flood.

💻 In Ledgerly: the relay

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Worker/OutboxRelay.cs

    private const int BatchSize = 20;
    private const int MaxAttempts = 10;
    // ...
        var pending = await db.OutboxMessages
            .Where(m => m.ProcessedAt == null && m.Attempts < MaxAttempts)
            .OrderBy(m => m.OccurredAt)
            .Take(BatchSize)
            .ToListAsync(ct);
        // ...
                var sender = _senders.GetOrAdd(message.Destination, serviceBus.CreateSender);
                await sender.SendMessageAsync(
                    new ServiceBusMessage(message.Payload)
                    {
                        MessageId = message.Id.ToString(),
                        Subject = message.Type,
                        ContentType = "application/json",
                    },
                    ct);

                message.ProcessedAt = clock.GetUtcNow();

Every 2 seconds (PeriodicTimer, again), the relay takes up to 20 unsent rows in order, sends each one, and marks it processed. A failed send increments Attempts and stores LastError. After 10 attempts the row is skipped, so one broken message can't block the rest, and the row stays in the table for someone to investigate.

The class comment names the weak spot: delivery is at-least-once, because if the worker crashes after SendMessageAsync but before SaveChangesAsync, the next tick sends the same row again. The same happens if two worker replicas pick up the same batch. That is why MessageId = message.Id.ToString() matters: the duplicate carries the same ID, so Service Bus duplicate detection drops it in Azure, and the consumer recognizes it everywhere.

🗺️ From upload to processed import

sequenceDiagram
    participant User
    participant Api
    participant Sql as Azure SQL
    participant Relay as OutboxRelay
    participant Bus as Service Bus imports
    participant Proc as ImportProcessor
    User->>Api: POST imports with CSV
    Api->>Sql: insert Import and OutboxMessage in one transaction
    Api-->>User: 202 Accepted with Location
    Relay->>Sql: read unsent outbox rows
    Relay->>Bus: send with MessageId = outbox id
    Relay->>Sql: set ProcessedAt
    Bus->>Proc: deliver with peek-lock
    Proc->>Sql: already in ProcessedMessages?
    Proc->>Sql: trades, import status and ProcessedMessage in one SaveChanges
    Proc->>Bus: complete
    User->>Api: GET import status
    Api-->>User: Completed, rows imported and skipped

🧠 The idempotent consumer: processing twice changes nothing

💡 You can't get exactly-once delivery, but you can get exactly-once effects.

At-least-once delivery means your handler will see some messages twice: relay re-sends, abandoned messages, a lock that expired while a slow import was still running. The handler's job is to make the second time a no-op.

💻 In Ledgerly: the handler

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Worker/ImportProcessor.cs

    private async Task HandleAsync(ProcessMessageEventArgs args)
    {
        var messageId = args.Message.MessageId;
        // ...
        if (await db.ProcessedMessages.AnyAsync(p => p.MessageId == messageId, ct))
        {
            LogDuplicate(logger, messageId);
            await args.CompleteMessageAsync(args.Message, ct);
            return;
        }
        // ...
        if (import is null)
        {
            // Poison message: retrying will never help. Park it in the DLQ for a human to look at.
            await args.DeadLetterMessageAsync(args.Message, "ImportNotFound", $"No import for message {messageId}", ct);
            return;
        }

        try
        {
            var outcome = await ApplyImportAsync(db, import, ct);
            db.ProcessedMessages.Add(new ProcessedMessage { MessageId = messageId, Handler = HandlerName });
            await db.SaveChangesAsync(ct); // trades + import status + idempotency record in ONE transaction
            await args.CompleteMessageAsync(args.Message, ct);
            // ...
        }
        catch (Exception ex) when (ex is not OperationCanceledException)
        {
            // ...
            await args.AbandonMessageAsync(args.Message, cancellationToken: ct); // Service Bus will redeliver
        }
    }

Walk through it the way Maya would:

What happens What the code does Result
A duplicate arrives (relay re-send, emulator without duplicate detection) ProcessedMessages already has this MessageId Complete it, log it, do nothing else
The import row doesn't exist DeadLetterMessageAsync(..., "ImportNotFound", ...) Retrying can't help, so it goes straight to the DLQ
The database blips during SaveChangesAsync Nothing was committed, then AbandonMessageAsync Delivered again, up to 10 times, then the DLQ
The worker crashes after SaveChangesAsync, before Complete The lock expires and the message comes back The ProcessedMessages check finds it: complete, no second import
The file breaks a business rule (selling more than you hold) ApplyImportAsync marks the import Failed and returns "rejected" Saved and completed. A business failure is an outcome, not a retry

Three design choices make this work:

  1. AutoCompleteMessages = false in the ServiceBusProcessorOptions. The processor never completes a message on your behalf. You complete it only after the transaction committed.
  2. The idempotency record is in the same transaction as the work. Trades, import status and the ProcessedMessage row are saved by one SaveChangesAsync. There is no moment where the trades exist but the record doesn't.
  3. The database enforces it, not just the code. ProcessedMessage uses MessageId as its primary key. If two replicas handle the same message at the same moment, both pass the AnyAsync check, but only one insert can succeed. The other gets an exception, abandons, and on redelivery completes as a duplicate.

There is a third layer, in the data model itself. Each broker row's reference is stored as Transaction.ExternalRef, with a filtered unique index on (PortfolioId, ExternalRef) (chapter 5), and ApplyImportAsync skips references the portfolio already has. Re-uploading the same file imports zero new rows and reports them as skipped. That fixes Elena's ticket from the user's side.

The processor also sets MaxConcurrentCalls = 4, so one replica handles four imports in parallel. Each call gets its own DI scope and DbContext, because a DbContext is not thread-safe.

🧠 Graceful shutdown and boring parsing

💡 In the cloud, your process is stopped on purpose many times a day. Make stopping a normal code path.

Container Apps stops replicas when it scales in and when it rolls out a new revision. It sends SIGTERM, and the .NET host then stops accepting new work, signals the CancellationToken passed to ExecuteAsync, and calls StopAsync on every hosted service. HostOptions.ShutdownTimeout caps how long that may take: the default is 30 seconds, and Ledgerly sets 25 so it finishes inside the platform's termination grace period (❓ Container Apps' default is believed to be 30 seconds but this course hasn't re-verified it) (.NET Generic Host).

💻 In Ledgerly: stop taking messages, finish the ones in flight

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Worker/ImportProcessor.cs

    public override async Task StopAsync(CancellationToken cancellationToken)
    {
        // Graceful shutdown: stop taking new messages, let in-flight handlers finish.
        if (_processor is not null)
        {
            await _processor.StopProcessingAsync(cancellationToken);
            await _processor.DisposeAsync();
        }

        await base.StopAsync(cancellationToken);
    }

If an import is still running when the timeout hits, its cancellation token fires, SaveChangesAsync never commits, and the message is never completed. Peek-lock then gives the message to another replica after the lock expires, and the idempotent handler starts from a clean state. A Windows Service killed mid-job left half a batch behind. This one leaves nothing behind, which is the point of putting the whole effect in one transaction.

OutboxRelay.StopAsync does the same in its own way: it lets the loop end, then disposes its cached senders.

💻 In Ledgerly: a parser that doesn't care where it runs

Background work runs on machines you never see, with cultures you didn't choose. BrokerCsvParser parses every number and date with the invariant culture, and it reports bad rows instead of throwing:

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Core/Imports/BrokerCsvParser.cs

/// <summary>
/// Parses the broker export format:
/// <c>date,type,symbol,quantity,price,reference</c> (header required, ISO dates, invariant decimals).
/// Bad rows are reported, not thrown: one typo should not reject a 5 000-row file.
/// </summary>
public static class BrokerCsvParser
{
    // ...
            if (!decimal.TryParse(cells[4], NumberStyles.Number, CultureInfo.InvariantCulture, out var price) || price <= 0)
            {
                errors.Add(new ParseError(lineNumber, $"Invalid price '{cells[4]}'."));
                continue;
            }

A unit test proves it by switching the thread to lt-LT, where the decimal separator is a comma:

📄 sample/tests/Ledgerly.UnitTests/BrokerCsvParserTests.cs

    public void Decimal_parsing_ignores_server_culture()
    {
        // The old IIS box had lt-LT culture and "190,10" broke imports. Invariant culture, always.
        var previous = Thread.CurrentThread.CurrentCulture;
        Thread.CurrentThread.CurrentCulture = new System.Globalization.CultureInfo("lt-LT");
        try
        {
            var result = BrokerCsvParser.Parse($"{Header}\n2026-09-01,buy,AAPL,5,190.10,BRK-1");
            Assert.Equal(190.10m, Assert.Single(result.Trades).Price);
        }
        // ...

"Reported, not thrown" also matters for the queue. A parse error is a business result, saved on the import (completed_with_errors, with the first five errors in Error). If it were an exception, the message would be abandoned and retried 10 times, with the same result every time, and then dead-lettered. Only throw for things a retry can fix.

⚠️ Common traps

Trap What happens Do this instead
Saving to the database, then sending the message A crash between the two loses the message, and the work never happens Use a transactional outbox: both rows in one SaveChanges, and a relay sends later
Leaving AutoCompleteMessages on and doing work after an await The message is completed even if your save failed, so the work is lost Set AutoCompleteMessages = false and complete only after the commit
Checking for duplicates in code only Two replicas pass the check at the same time and both apply the import Back the check with a primary key or unique index in the same transaction
Nobody watches the dead-letter queue Messages pile up silently, because DLQ messages are never auto-deleted Alert on DLQ depth and write a runbook for replaying them (chapters 16 and 19)
A timer job in a worker that scales out Every replica runs the job, so alerts fire twice Keep that worker at one replica, move the job to a scheduled Container Apps job, or make it idempotent
Ignoring the CancellationToken in ExecuteAsync Shutdown waits for the timeout and then the process is killed mid-work Pass stoppingToken everywhere, and keep the shutdown timeout below the platform's grace period

🧰 Helpers

Checklist: a new message type

  1. Define the message as a small record with IDs, not whole entities (like ImportRequested(ImportId, PortfolioId)).
  2. Enqueue it with db.EnqueueOutboxMessage(...) before the one SaveChangesAsync of the request.
  3. In the consumer: check ProcessedMessages, do the work, add the ProcessedMessage, save once, then complete.
  4. Decide, for each failure, which settle action it gets: abandon for transient faults (timeouts, throttling), dead-letter for messages that can never succeed, complete for business outcomes you recorded.
  5. Add a test that delivers the same message twice and asserts that nothing changes the second time (chapter 17).

Make it permanent:

  • Add "What happens when this message is delivered twice?" to your pull request review checklist, for example the toolkit's pr-review-checklist.md (see the toolkit).
  • Keep queue settings (maxDeliveryCount, duplicate detection, dead-lettering) in Bicep next to the code that depends on them, so a reviewer sees both in one pull request (chapter 11).
  • Write down the rule "one SaveChanges per message, complete after commit" in your team's ADRs. Terms such as outbox and idempotency are in the glossary.

🧪 Try it yourself (optional, ~30 min)

  1. Start the system and create a token: bash cd sample aspire run dotnet user-jwts create --project src/Ledgerly.Api
  2. Open src/Ledgerly.Api/Ledgerly.Api.http in your IDE, set @api to the API's URL from the dashboard and @token to your token, and create a portfolio. Copy its id.
  3. Save this as broker.csv: text date,type,symbol,quantity,price,reference 2026-09-01,buy,AAPL,5,190.10,BRK-1001 2026-09-15,buy,MSFT,3,410.00,BRK-1002 2026-09-20,buy,MSFT,abc,410.00,BRK-1003
  4. Upload it (replace the URL, ID and token): bash curl -i -X POST https://localhost:7180/api/portfolios/<id>/imports -H "Authorization: Bearer <token>" -F "file=@broker.csv" You get 202 Accepted and a Location header right away.
  5. Request the Location URL (with the same Authorization header) until the status is Completed. Expect 2 rows imported and 1 skipped, with "Invalid quantity" in error.
  6. Upload the same file again. The second import completes with 0 rows imported and all rows skipped: idempotency by ExternalRef.
  7. In the dashboard's Traces view, find the POST request. The worker's outbox publish imports span appears in the same trace: that is the TraceParent column at work. Then find the import process span for the same import (chapter 15 explains how trace context crosses the queue).
  8. Stop the AppHost and run the unit tests, including the lt-LT parser test: bash dotnet test --solution Ledgerly.slnx

✅ Recap

  1. Slow work leaves the HTTP request: the API returns 202, and hosted services in the worker do the work with PeriodicTimer loops and a Service Bus processor.
  2. The transactional outbox guarantees the message is sent if and only if the data was saved, and the idempotent consumer (one transaction plus a primary key) makes at-least-once delivery safe.
  3. Peek-lock, MaxDeliveryCount, the dead-letter queue and graceful shutdown give every failure a defined next step, so stopping or crashing a worker never leaves half an import behind.

Next → Chapter 8: GitHub as the team's home

Chapter 8: GitHub as the team's home

⏱ About 30 min · You'll learn: how a change travels from a short-lived branch to main through a pull request, how rulesets, required checks and CODEOWNERS turn team rules into settings GitHub enforces, and how Dependabot, CodeQL, secret scanning and Copilot code review keep watch without a meeting.

📖 Story: Your first pull request

The background import from chapter 7 works on your machine. The Service Bus emulator drains the queue, the duplicate test passes, and Elena's support ticket can finally be closed. At Nordbank Insurance, this is where the waiting began. Your work would sit on a branch called release/2019-Q3 for weeks. Someone would zip a diff and email it to two senior developers, who would print it and bring it to the Thursday review meeting. Then came the merge weekend, when four long-lived branches met for the first time and nobody went home before midnight.

You ask Maya who you should email for a review.

"Nobody," she says. "Push your branch and open a pull request. CI will build it, CODEOWNERS will ask the right people, and Copilot will leave comments before any of us look. If main is red or nobody approved, the merge button stays grey. Not because we're strict: because GitHub won't let it."

Tomás adds from the next desk: "And keep it small. If your PR is open longer than two days, it's two PRs."

Jonas Berg forwards you a link. A Dependabot pull request bumped the OpenTelemetry packages overnight, CI is already green, and it's waiting for one approval. "That's the other half of the job," he says. "The robots open PRs too. We review them like anyone else's."

In your notebook, under Habits I brought with me, you write: "Code review is a meeting." Then: "Merging is an event." You suspect both are about to be crossed out.

🏚️ Old way → 🚀 New way

What you did before What you do now Why it changed
Long-lived release branches, merged on a "merge weekend" Short-lived branches off main, merged through a pull request within a day or two (GitHub flow) Small diffs are easy to review and easy to revert, and conflicts stay small
Reviews by email and in a Thursday meeting Reviews in the pull request, with CODEOWNERS requesting the right people automatically The discussion lives next to the code, forever, and nobody has to remember who owns what
"Never push to trunk" as a line in the wiki A ruleset on main: pull request required, checks required, no force pushes A rule GitHub enforces can't be forgotten on a Friday evening
A build server someone checked "now and then" Required status checks from GitHub Actions on every PR (chapter 9) Red means "can't merge", not "someone should look at that"
Library upgrades once a year, as a project Dependabot opens grouped update PRs every week, and CI tests each one Small, frequent upgrades are cheap. A three-year jump is a migration
A security review by another team, just before release CodeQL on every PR, secret scanning and push protection on every push Findings arrive while the code is still fresh in your head
Commit messages like "fix" and "wip 2" Conventional commits and PR titles such as feat(imports): process CSV files in the background History you can scan, and release notes you can generate

🧠 GitHub flow: one main branch, many short ones

💡 main is always releasable. Every change reaches it through a pull request, and nothing else does.

GitHub flow is deliberately simple:

  1. Branch from main with a descriptive name (fix/import-timeout, feat/price-alerts).
  2. Commit small steps and push early. A pushed branch is backed up and visible.
  3. Open a pull request, as a draft if you want feedback before it's finished.
  4. CI runs and reviewers comment. You push fixes to the same branch, and the PR updates.
  5. Merge when the checks are green and the required approvals are in. Delete the branch.
  6. Deploy from main. At Ledgerly that happens automatically after CI passes on main (chapter 12).

There is no develop branch, no release/* branch and no merge weekend. Unfinished work either stays on its branch, or it's merged behind a feature flag (the alerts endpoint already does this, see chapter 19). The flag decides what users see. The branch decides nothing.

💻 In Ledgerly: what "merged to main" sets in motion

The CI workflow runs on every pull request and every push to main that touches the sample:

📄 .github/workflows/ci.yml

# CI: every push and pull request is built and tested. A red build blocks the merge (see branch protection, chapter 8).
name: ci

on:
  push:
    branches: [main]
    paths: ["sample/**", ".github/workflows/ci.yml"]
  pull_request:
    paths: ["sample/**", ".github/workflows/ci.yml"]
  workflow_dispatch:

The deploy workflow doesn't watch branches at all. It waits for CI to finish on main:

📄 .github/workflows/deploy.yml

on:
  workflow_run:
    workflows: [ci]
    types: [completed]
    branches: [main]
  workflow_dispatch:

So the only road to production is: pull request, green CI, merge, green CI on main, deploy. Chapter 9 walks through the CI file line by line, and chapter 12 does the same for deploy.

Issues, projects and commit messages

Work starts as an issue, not as an email. Elena writes the "CSV imports time out" issue with the user's words and a sample file. You link your PR to it, and the issue closes when the PR merges. A project board (Elena's has the columns Todo, In progress, In review and Done) shows the team's work without a status meeting. Leo uses the same board for frontend tasks, so the API change and the UI change sit next to each other.

Ledgerly writes commit messages and PR titles as conventional commits: a type, an optional scope and a short summary.

feat(imports): process CSV files in a background worker
fix(worker): complete duplicate messages instead of abandoning them
docs(readme): replace the setup wiki with aspire run
chore(deps): bump the opentelemetry group

The team squash-merges, so the PR title becomes the one commit on main. Scan git log --oneline and you can tell features from fixes from chores at a glance, and a tool can turn the history into release notes later.

🗺️ The life of a change

flowchart LR
    Issue["Issue<br/>CSV imports time out"] --> Branch["Branch<br/>fix/import-timeout"]
    Branch -- "push" --> PR["Pull request"]
    PR --> CI["CI checks<br/>build-test, containers, infra"]
    PR --> Scan["CodeQL<br/>analyze"]
    PR --> Review["Reviews<br/>Copilot, then CODEOWNERS"]
    CI --> Gate{"Ruleset on main<br/>all green and approved?"}
    Scan --> Gate
    Review --> Gate
    Gate -- "no" --> Branch
    Gate -- "yes, squash merge" --> Main["main"]
    Main -- "CI passes on main" --> Deploy["deploy.yml<br/>production environment"]

🧠 Protecting main: rulesets, required checks and environments

💡 A team rule that lives in a wiki is a suggestion. A team rule that lives in a ruleset is a fact.

GitHub has two ways to protect a branch. Classic branch protection rules are the older one. Rulesets are the newer mechanism, and the one to reach for today (About rulesets):

  • 🔧 Availability: rulesets work on public repositories on GitHub Free, and on public and private repositories on Pro, Team and Enterprise Cloud. Organization-wide rulesets and push rulesets need Team or Enterprise.
  • A repository can have up to 75 rulesets. They layer with each other and with classic branch protection, and when several apply, the most restrictive wins.
  • Each ruleset has an enforcement status: Active, Evaluate (report what would have been blocked, without blocking) or Disabled. Evaluate is how you roll out a stricter rule without surprising the team.
  • A bypass list names who may skip the rules, for example a release bot. Keep it short. Every name on it is a way around review.
  • Anyone with read access can see the rulesets, so "what are the rules here?" has an answer you can look up.

The rules you can turn on include (Available rules for rulesets): require a pull request (with a number of approvals, review from code owners, and dismissing stale approvals when new commits arrive), require status checks to pass, require linear history, block force pushes, require signed commits, require deployments to succeed and require code scanning results.

Ledgerly's ruleset on main is short:

Rule Setting Why
Require a pull request 1 approval, review from code owners, dismiss stale approvals Nobody merges their own unreviewed code, and approving an old version doesn't count
Require status checks The CI and CodeQL jobs (below), branch up to date main stays green
Block force pushes On History on main is never rewritten
Require linear history On Squash merges keep one commit per PR
Bypass list Empty Not even Maya skips review

💻 In Ledgerly: where the check names come from

A required status check must have passed on the latest commit of the PR, and its name must match the job name exactly. The names come straight from the workflow files:

📄 .github/workflows/ci.yml

jobs:
  build-test:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    timeout-minutes: 20
    # ...
  containers:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    needs: build-test
    timeout-minutes: 20
    strategy:
      matrix:
        project: [Ledgerly.Api, Ledgerly.Worker, Ledgerly.MarketData, Ledgerly.MigrationService]
    # ...
  infra:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    timeout-minutes: 10

📄 .github/workflows/codeql.yml

jobs:
  analyze:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    timeout-minutes: 30

None of the jobs has a name:, so the job ID is the check name: build-test, infra and analyze. A matrix job reports one check per matrix value, so containers shows up four times, as containers (Ledgerly.Api), containers (Ledgerly.Worker) and so on. Rename a job, and the required check waits forever for a name that no longer reports. Change the workflow and the ruleset in the same PR.

Environments: the gate before production

Rulesets guard main. Environments guard deployments. Ledgerly's deploy job runs in an environment called production:

📄 .github/workflows/deploy.yml

    environment:
      name: production # protection rules (reviewers, branch filter) live on the environment
      url: ${{ steps.apps.outputs.apiUrl }}

An environment can require reviewers (up to 6 users or teams, and only one of them needs to approve, optionally never the person who triggered the run), a wait timer, deployment branch restrictions and custom protection rules. It also holds its own secrets and variables (Deployments and environments).

🔧 💰 Read the plan small print: on Free, Pro and Team plans, required reviewers and wait timers work only for public repositories. For a private repository you need Enterprise. Ledgerly's beta repo is private on Team, so the team relies on the main ruleset plus a branch filter on the environment, and Priya gets a notification for every deploy.

Tomás has one complaint about all of this: rulesets and environments are settings, not files, so they don't show up in a diff. "If it's not in git, it doesn't exist," he says, and writes the ruleset down as an ADR (the toolkit has adr-template.md) so the next person can see what was decided and why.

🧠 Reviews that find the right people: CODEOWNERS, templates and Copilot

💡 Let GitHub decide who reviews and what the author must say. Spend human attention on the design.

CODEOWNERS

A CODEOWNERS file maps paths to the people or teams who own them. When a PR touches a path, its owners are requested for review automatically. With the "require review from code owners" rule, their approval becomes mandatory (About code owners).

Two details matter:

  • GitHub looks for the file in .github/CODEOWNERS, then CODEOWNERS at the root, then docs/CODEOWNERS, and uses the first one it finds.
  • Inside the file, the last matching pattern wins. Put the broad rule first and the specific ones below it.

The sample repository doesn't contain a CODEOWNERS file. This is what Ledgerly's looks like:

# illustrative, not in the sample: .github/CODEOWNERS
# The last matching pattern wins, so the catch-all goes first.
*                                   @ledgerly/developers

# Infrastructure and pipelines: Tomás's team reviews every change.
/sample/infra/                      @ledgerly/platform
/.github/workflows/                 @ledgerly/platform

# Supply chain: package sources and versions need a security review.
/sample/nuget.config                @ledgerly/security
/sample/Directory.Packages.props    @ledgerly/security

# Schema changes are hard to undo: the tech lead looks at every migration.
/sample/src/Ledgerly.Core/Data/Migrations/   @maya-okafor

Use teams rather than individuals where you can, so a vacation doesn't block a merge. Note what the supply-chain lines mean: every Dependabot NuGet PR touches Directory.Packages.props, so Jonas's team approves each one. That's intended.

The pull request template

A pull request template pre-fills the description of every new PR, so the reviewer always gets the same questions answered. The sample doesn't include one, but the toolkit has pull_request_template.md ready to copy to .github/ (see the toolkit). Its shape:

<!-- illustrative, not in the sample: .github/pull_request_template.md -->
## What and why
Fixes #

## How I tested it
- [ ] Unit or integration tests added or updated
- [ ] Ran it locally with `aspire run`

## What happens when it fails?
<!-- Retries, timeouts, the dead-letter queue, rollback -->

## Checklist
- [ ] Database migration is backward compatible (old code still runs against the new schema)
- [ ] New settings are documented, and no secrets are in code or config
- [ ] Logs, metrics or traces added for the new path

The "What happens when it fails?" heading is Maya's. Every PR answers it before she has to ask.

Copilot code review and the Copilot cloud agent

Copilot code review reviews a pull request the way a teammate would: it leaves comments on the diff, often with a suggested change. You request it like any other reviewer, or make it automatic for a repository or organization through settings or a ruleset. Business and Enterprise organizations can enable it even for members who don't have a Copilot license, billed to the organization 💰 (Copilot on GitHub).

At Ledgerly, Copilot reviews first and humans second. It's good at the things people skim: a missing CancellationToken, a DbContext captured in a singleton, a null path nobody tested. Its comment is not an approval, and it doesn't count toward the ruleset. A person who understands the design still signs off.

🔧 The Copilot cloud agent (GitHub's docs renamed it from "Copilot coding agent") goes one step further. You assign an issue to Copilot, it works in an environment powered by GitHub Actions, and it opens a pull request for review. It's available on paid plans, and for Business and Enterprise an org admin has to enable it. Its PR goes through exactly the same gates as yours: CI, CODEOWNERS, a human approval. Chapter 20 covers how to write issues it can finish, and the .github/copilot-instructions.md file that teaches it Ledgerly's conventions.

🗺️ A pull request meets the gates

sequenceDiagram
    participant You as You
    participant GH as GitHub
    participant CI as Actions ci and codeql
    participant Bot as Copilot code review
    participant Owner as Code owner
    You->>GH: Push branch and open pull request
    GH->>CI: Start build-test, containers, infra, analyze
    GH->>Bot: Request automatic review
    GH->>Owner: Request review from CODEOWNERS
    Bot-->>GH: Comments and suggested changes
    CI-->>GH: build-test failed
    GH-->>You: Merge blocked by required checks
    You->>GH: Push a fix to the same branch
    GH->>CI: Run all checks again
    CI-->>GH: All checks passed
    Owner-->>GH: Approve
    GH-->>You: Ruleset satisfied, merge enabled

🧠 Supply chain on autopilot: Dependabot, CodeQL and secret scanning

💡 The safest dependency update is a small one that CI tested last week, not a big one somebody postponed for a year.

Dependabot version updates

Dependabot reads .github/dependabot.yml and opens pull requests when a newer version of a package or an action exists. Every one of those PRs runs CI like yours does, so "is this upgrade safe?" is answered by your tests, not by a hunch.

💻 In Ledgerly: grouped updates for NuGet and actions

📄 .github/dependabot.yml

# Dependabot opens pull requests for outdated packages and actions. CI tests every one of them.
version: 2
updates:
  - package-ecosystem: nuget
    directory: /sample
    schedule:
      interval: weekly
    groups:
      # One PR per family instead of twenty. Versions inside a family must move together.
      aspire:
        patterns: ["Aspire.*"]
      dotnet-platform:
        patterns: ["Microsoft.AspNetCore.*", "Microsoft.EntityFrameworkCore*", "Microsoft.Extensions.*"]
      opentelemetry:
        patterns: ["OpenTelemetry*", "Azure.Monitor.OpenTelemetry*"]
      testing:
        patterns: ["xunit*", "Microsoft.Testing.*", "Testcontainers*"]

  - package-ecosystem: github-actions
    directory: /
    schedule:
      interval: weekly
    groups:
      actions:
        patterns: ["*"]

Read it top to bottom:

  • Two ecosystems. nuget looks in /sample, where the solution and Directory.Packages.props live. github-actions looks at /, which means the workflows in .github/workflows. Dependabot understands Central Package Management, so it edits the one PackageVersion line instead of every project file.
  • Groups. Without them you'd get one PR per package: six Aspire PRs that must all merge together anyway. With them you get one PR per family. Groups match by patterns, and can also be limited with update-types: [minor, patch] if you want majors to arrive alone.
  • Weekly. schedule.interval also accepts daily, monthly, quarterly, semiannually, yearly and cron. Weekly is a rhythm a small team can keep.
  • 🔧 Cooldown. Version updates have a default cooldown of 3 days: a new release isn't proposed until it's 3 days old. That gives the ecosystem time to discover a broken release before it reaches you.

Two more ecosystems worth knowing: dotnet-sdk updates the SDK version in global.json, and docker updates base images in Dockerfiles (Ledgerly has none, see chapter 9) (Dependabot options reference).

Dependabot is half of the supply-chain story. The other half runs at build time: NuGet audit fails the build when a package has a known vulnerability, which is exactly what happened while building the sample (chapter 9).

CodeQL code scanning

CodeQL is GitHub's static analysis engine. It looks for security bugs (SQL injection, path traversal, unsafe deserialization) and reports them under Security → Code scanning and as comments on the pull request (Configuring code scanning).

💻 In Ledgerly: the CodeQL workflow

📄 .github/workflows/codeql.yml

# Static security analysis (SAST). Findings show up under Security > Code scanning and on pull requests.
name: codeql

on:
  push:
    branches: [main]
    paths: ["sample/**"]
  pull_request:
    paths: ["sample/**"]
  schedule:
    - cron: "17 3 * * 1" # weekly, catches new queries against unchanged code

permissions:
  contents: read
  security-events: write

jobs:
  analyze:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    timeout-minutes: 30
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v7
      - uses: github/codeql-action/init@v4
        with:
          languages: csharp
          build-mode: none # no build needed for C#, analysis reads the source
      - uses: github/codeql-action/analyze@v4

Three choices stand out:

  • build-mode: none. For C#, CodeQL can analyze the source without compiling it (the other modes are autobuild and manual). It's faster, and a broken build can't hide a finding.
  • The weekly schedule. The queries improve over time, so every Monday at 03:17 UTC the same code gets checked against the new queries, even if nobody pushed.
  • security-events: write. That's the one extra permission the workflow needs, to upload its results. Everything else stays read-only.

This is the advanced setup: a workflow file you control. The default setup is a one-click setting with no file at all, and for C# it also uses build-mode: none. It scans pushes to the default branch, pull requests and weekly. 🔧 💰 CodeQL is free for public repositories. Private and internal repositories in an organization need GitHub Code Security, sold separately since 2025.

Secret scanning and push protection

Secret scanning finds credentials committed to the repository, and push protection blocks a push that contains one before it ever reaches GitHub. Both are free and automatic for public repositories, where push protection for users is on by default. 🔧 For private repositories and repo-level push protection you need GitHub Secret Protection (About push protection).

Ledgerly's best defense is having almost nothing to leak: no SAS keys (chapter 7), no deployment secrets thanks to OIDC (chapter 12), and managed identities everywhere (chapter 13). Jonas still turns push protection on, because a developer's personal token in a test script is enough to ruin a week.

⚠️ Common traps

Trap What happens Do this instead
Renaming a CI job without updating the ruleset The required check never reports under the old name, and every PR waits forever Change the job name and the required checks in the same change, and tell the team
Making a path-filtered workflow a required check A PR that doesn't touch sample/** never runs CI, so the required check stays pending Require only checks that run on every PR, or remove the path filter from the required workflow
Expecting environment reviewers on a private repo on Team The production approval gate silently doesn't apply 🔧 Check your plan. Use branch filters and the main ruleset, or move to Enterprise
A broad CODEOWNERS rule at the bottom of the file It matches everything and overrides the specific owners above it Catch-all first, specific paths last, because the last match wins
Treating Copilot's review as the approval Nobody who understands the design looked at the change Copilot reviews first, a human approves. Its comments don't satisfy the ruleset
Ignoring Dependabot PRs for months They pile up, conflict with each other, and the upgrade becomes a project again Review the grouped PRs weekly. Green CI plus a release-notes skim is usually enough
A long bypass list "just in case" Anyone on it can merge without checks, and eventually someone does Keep the bypass list empty or limited to a bot, and use Evaluate mode to trial new rules

🧰 Helpers

The pull request loop from the terminal, with the GitHub CLI:

git switch -c fix/import-timeout          # branch from an up-to-date main
git commit -am "fix(imports): move CSV processing to the worker"
git push -u origin HEAD
gh pr create --fill                       # title and body from your commits
gh pr checks --watch                      # wait for CI and CodeQL
gh pr view --web                          # read the review comments
gh pr merge --squash --delete-branch      # when the ruleset allows it

Reviewing someone else's PR locally:

gh pr list                                # open PRs in this repo
gh pr checkout 42                         # check out PR #42 as a local branch
cd sample
dotnet test --solution Ledgerly.slnx      # run the tests yourself

A conventional commit cheat sheet: feat (a new feature), fix (a bug fix), docs, test, refactor (no behavior change), ci (workflows), chore (dependencies, tooling). Add a scope in parentheses when it helps: fix(worker): ....

Make it permanent:

  • Copy the toolkit's pull_request_template.md to .github/ and dependabot.yml to .github/, then adjust the directory and groups (toolkit).
  • Add a CODEOWNERS file with teams, not individuals, and turn on "require review from code owners" in the main ruleset.
  • Create the ruleset in Evaluate mode first, watch a week of PRs, then switch it to Active. Record it in an ADR.
  • Turn on Copilot code review as automatic for the repository, and add the toolkit's copilot-instructions.md so its comments follow your conventions.
  • Use the toolkit's pr-review-checklist.md when you review. New terms are in the glossary.

🧪 Try it yourself (optional, ~25 min)

  1. Fork the course repository and clone your fork, then make your fork the default for gh: bash gh repo fork vygantas-meskauskis/cloud-native-dotnet --clone cd cloud-native-dotnet gh repo set-default Pick your fork when gh repo set-default asks. On GitHub, open your fork's Actions tab and enable workflows if GitHub asks you to.
  2. Create a branch and make a small change under sample/ so CI runs, for example improve a comment in sample/src/Ledgerly.Worker/AlertEvaluator.cs: bash git switch -c docs/alert-evaluator-comment git commit -am "docs(worker): explain why alerts need a single replica" git push -u origin HEAD
  3. Open the pull request against your fork and watch the checks: bash gh pr create --fill gh pr checks --watch Note the check names: build-test, containers (Ledgerly.Api) and its siblings, infra, analyze.
  4. In your fork, go to Settings → Rules → Rulesets and create a branch ruleset for the default branch. Require a pull request and the build-test status check, block force pushes, and set enforcement to Evaluate. (Your fork is public, so rulesets work on the Free plan 🔧.)
  5. Push another commit to the PR. Then open Settings → Rules → Insights and see what the ruleset would have blocked, without anything being blocked.
  6. If your plan includes Copilot code review, add Copilot as a reviewer on the PR and read its comments. Decide which ones you'd accept.
  7. Add an illustrative .github/CODEOWNERS with yourself as the owner of /sample/infra/, open a second PR that touches a Bicep comment, and watch GitHub request your review automatically.
  8. Merge with gh pr merge --squash --delete-branch and run git log --oneline on main: one commit, with your conventional title.

✅ Recap

  1. GitHub flow keeps main releasable: short branches, pull requests for every change, and deployment only after CI passes on main.
  2. Rulesets, required status checks, CODEOWNERS and environments turn the team's rules into settings GitHub enforces, so nothing depends on someone remembering.
  3. Dependabot, CodeQL, secret scanning and Copilot code review run on every PR, and humans still give the approval.

Next → Chapter 9: CI with GitHub Actions

Chapter 9: CI with GitHub Actions

⏱ About 35 min · You'll learn: how Ledgerly's CI workflow builds, tests, packages and lints every pull request on a fresh machine, why it runs the same commands you run locally, how container images get built without a Dockerfile, and the real failures that shaped the workflow.

📖 Story: Green on my machine, red in CI

Your first week includes a turn on "CI duty", and Tomás starts it by opening the run history. He scrolls back a month to a red cross next to build-test on a pull request from Leo, and opens the log:

error NU1903: Package 'System.Security.Cryptography.Pkcs' 10.0.12 has a known high severity vulnerability

"Leo's laptop was green," Tomás says. "He hadn't touched a package. CI was red anyway, and CI was right. It's the only machine that counts. Your laptop has a warm cache, an SDK you installed in March and a bin folder full of history. The runner starts from nothing, every time."

The cause was a package nobody referenced directly, pulled in by another one, with a newly published vulnerability. NuGet audit flagged it, and because warnings are errors at Ledgerly, the build stopped. One line in Directory.Packages.props pinned the patched version. Then Priya tells you about the week the team moved to the .NET 10 SDK and dotnet test refused to run at all.

At Nordbank Insurance, "the build" was BUILD01, a tower PC under a desk in the server room. Somebody installed SDKs on it by hand, a scheduled batch file ran every night, and when it broke, the person who knew the batch file was on holiday. In your notebook, under Habits I brought with me, you write: "The build server is a pet." You don't need a question mark this time.

🏚️ Old way → 🚀 New way

What you did before What you do now Why it changed
BUILD01 under a desk, with SDKs installed by hand A fresh ubuntu-latest runner per job, with the SDK version taken from global.json Nothing is left over from the last build, so a green build means the repo is enough
A build definition clicked together in a UI A YAML workflow in .github/workflows, reviewed in pull requests The build changes with the code, in the same PR, with the same review
A nightly build A run on every push and pull request, with outdated runs cancelled You find out in minutes which commit broke it, not the next morning
Test results in a shared folder, or nowhere TRX results and coverage uploaded as an artifact, even when tests fail The evidence for a red build is one click away for 14 days
A hand-written Dockerfile one person maintains dotnet publish /t:PublishContainer, no Dockerfile The SDK picks a secure base image, a non-root user and the right port
Infrastructure scripts tested by running them in production az bicep lint and az bicep build in CI Mistakes in infrastructure code fail the PR, not the deployment
A build service account with admin rights permissions: contents: read for the workflow token A compromised step can read the repo, and that's all

🧠 Anatomy of a workflow

💡 A workflow is a file that answers four questions: when does it run, with which permissions, in what order, and where.

GitHub Actions runs workflows (YAML files in .github/workflows). A workflow has triggers (on:), one or more jobs that each run on a fresh runner machine, and steps inside each job that either run a shell command (run:) or call a reusable action (uses:). Jobs run in parallel unless one needs another.

💻 In Ledgerly: the top of ci.yml

📄 .github/workflows/ci.yml

# CI: every push and pull request is built and tested. A red build blocks the merge (see branch protection, chapter 8).
name: ci

on:
  push:
    branches: [main]
    paths: ["sample/**", ".github/workflows/ci.yml"]
  pull_request:
    paths: ["sample/**", ".github/workflows/ci.yml"]
  workflow_dispatch:

# Least privilege: this workflow only reads the repo.
permissions:
  contents: read

# A new push to the same branch cancels the run that is now outdated.
concurrency:
  group: ci-${{ github.ref }}
  cancel-in-progress: true

defaults:
  run:
    working-directory: sample

env:
  DOTNET_NOLOGO: true
  DOTNET_CLI_TELEMETRY_OPTOUT: true

Line by line:

  • on: CI runs on pushes to main, on every pull request, and by hand (workflow_dispatch, which is what gh workflow run ci uses). The paths filter skips CI when a change touches neither the sample nor the workflow itself. The course text in chapters/ changes often, and none of it needs a .NET build. Note that the workflow file is in its own filter: a change to CI must run CI.
  • permissions: Every run gets a short-lived GITHUB_TOKEN. This block says that token can only read the repository. A step that's compromised (a malicious package in a restore, a hijacked action) can't push code, open releases or change settings. Set it at the top of every workflow and widen it per job only when needed, like CodeQL's security-events: write in chapter 8.
  • concurrency: Runs in the same group queue up, and cancel-in-progress: true cancels the older one. Push three fixes in a row, and only the last one keeps a runner busy. github.ref makes the group per branch, so your PR never cancels someone else's (Control workflow concurrency). Deployments use the opposite setting, as you'll see below.
  • defaults.run.working-directory: sample. Every run: step starts in sample/, so commands look exactly like the ones in the README. It applies only to run: steps, which is why paths given to actions (sample/global.json, sample/TestResults/) still include the folder.
  • env: Two variables turn off the .NET welcome banner and CLI telemetry. Less noise in the log.

Compare the deploy workflow's concurrency block:

📄 .github/workflows/deploy.yml

# Never run two deployments at once, and never cancel one halfway.
concurrency:
  group: deploy-production
  cancel-in-progress: false

Cancelling an outdated build is free. Cancelling a deployment halfway leaves production in a state nobody tested. One fixed group name, no cancellation: deployments wait in line (chapter 12).

🧠 The build-test job: the commands you already know

💡 CI should run the same commands you run locally. If the workflow needs a trick your laptop doesn't, one of them is lying.

💻 In Ledgerly: build-test

📄 .github/workflows/ci.yml

  build-test:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    timeout-minutes: 20
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v7

      - uses: actions/setup-dotnet@v6
        with:
          global-json-file: sample/global.json

      - name: Restore
        run: dotnet restore Ledgerly.slnx

      # Warnings are errors, analyzers + code style run, NuGet audit fails on vulnerable packages.
      - name: Build
        run: dotnet build Ledgerly.slnx -c Release --no-restore

      # Integration tests start a throwaway SQL Server container with Testcontainers (Docker is on the runner).
      - name: Test
        run: >
          dotnet test --solution Ledgerly.slnx -c Release --no-build
          --coverage --coverage-output-format cobertura
          --report-xunit-trx

      - name: Upload test results
        if: always()
        uses: actions/upload-artifact@v7
        with:
          name: test-results
          path: sample/TestResults/
          retention-days: 14

The steps, in order:

  1. actions/checkout@v7 clones the commit under test.
  2. actions/setup-dotnet@v6 installs the SDK. Instead of a version number in YAML, it reads global.json, the same file your local dotnet reads. Upgrade the SDK in one place and both follow.
  3. Restore, then Build with --no-restore, then Test with --no-build. Three steps instead of one so the log shows exactly which phase failed, and nothing is done twice.
  4. timeout-minutes: 20. A hung test (a container that never starts) fails after 20 minutes instead of burning runner time for hours.
  5. if: always() on the upload. Without it, a failed test step skips every later step, and you lose the results exactly when you need them.

The > after run: is YAML's folded style: the three lines become one command, which keeps a long command readable.

The SDK pin itself:

📄 sample/global.json

{
  "sdk": {
    "version": "10.0.100",
    "rollForward": "latestFeature"
  },
  "test": {
    "runner": "Microsoft.Testing.Platform"
  }
}

rollForward: latestFeature means "10.0.100 or any newer 10.0 SDK", so a patched SDK on the runner is used without a commit. The test section is the second real lesson below.

Lesson 1: NuGet audit failed the build

The Build step's comment promises that warnings are errors and NuGet audit fails on vulnerable packages. Both come from the repo-wide build settings (chapter 3):

📄 sample/Directory.Build.props

    <!-- Quality gates: warnings fail the build, analyzers and code style run in every build (also in CI). -->
    <TreatWarningsAsErrors>true</TreatWarningsAsErrors>
    <AnalysisLevel>latest-recommended</AnalysisLevel>
    <EnforceCodeStyleInBuild>true</EnforceCodeStyleInBuild>

NuGet audit checks every package in the graph during restore, including the ones you never referenced yourself. While the sample was being built, a transitive dependency, System.Security.Cryptography.Pkcs (versions 9.0.11 and 10.0.12 came in through other packages), had known vulnerabilities: NU1902 (moderate) and NU1903 (high). With TreatWarningsAsErrors, those warnings stopped the build.

The tempting fix is to turn audit off or downgrade the warning. The right fix is to pin the patched version with Central Package Management's transitive pinning:

📄 sample/Directory.Packages.props

  <PropertyGroup>
    <ManagePackageVersionsCentrally>true</ManagePackageVersionsCentrally>
    <!-- Lets a PackageVersion below also override a TRANSITIVE dependency (used for security patches). -->
    <CentralPackageTransitivePinningEnabled>true</CentralPackageTransitivePinningEnabled>
  </PropertyGroup>

  <ItemGroup Label="Security pins for transitive dependencies (NuGet audit, NU1902/NU1903)">
    <PackageVersion Include="System.Security.Cryptography.Pkcs" Version="10.0.14" />
  </ItemGroup>

The label says why the pin exists, so whoever removes it later (when the parent packages ship the fix) knows what to check. This is why a red build with no code change is a feature: the vulnerability was found by a robot during a pull request, not by an attacker.

Lesson 2: VSTest is gone, Microsoft.Testing.Platform is in

The second failure happened when the team moved to the .NET 10 SDK with xUnit v3 4.x. dotnet test stopped with:

Testing with VSTest target is no longer supported by Microsoft.Testing.Platform on .NET 10 SDK

Some background. dotnet test now has two modes. VSTest mode is the old one and still the default. Microsoft.Testing.Platform (MTP) mode is new in the .NET 10 SDK: the test runner is embedded in the test project, which is an executable. xUnit v3 supports MTP natively, and running MTP test projects through the VSTest bridge is now legacy (MTP v2 drops it on the .NET 10 SDK).

The fix had three parts:

  1. Opt in to MTP with "test": { "runner": "Microsoft.Testing.Platform" } in global.json (above).
  2. Use the new command line: dotnet test --solution Ledgerly.slnx. In MTP mode the solution is passed with --solution.
  3. Drop the VSTest packages. Microsoft.NET.Test.Sdk, xunit.runner.visualstudio and coverlet.collector were removed. Coverage now comes from Microsoft.Testing.Extensions.CodeCoverage, which provides --coverage:

📄 sample/tests/Ledgerly.UnitTests/Ledgerly.UnitTests.csproj

  <PropertyGroup>
    <IsPackable>false</IsPackable>
    <IsTestProject>true</IsTestProject>
    <OutputType>Exe</OutputType>
  </PropertyGroup>

  <ItemGroup>
    <PackageReference Include="xunit.v3" />
    <PackageReference Include="Microsoft.Testing.Extensions.CodeCoverage" />
    <PackageReference Include="Microsoft.Extensions.TimeProvider.Testing" />
  </ItemGroup>

In the Test step, --coverage --coverage-output-format cobertura writes a Cobertura coverage file, and --report-xunit-trx writes xUnit's TRX results file. The suite has 23 tests: 15 unit tests and 8 integration tests against a real SQL Server that Testcontainers starts on the runner (Docker is already installed there). Locally, the integration tests skip themselves when no SQL Server is available (chapter 17).

One more MTP detail bit the team: when zero tests run, MTP returns exit code 8, and CI fails. That's usually right, because a filter that matches nothing or a project that isn't discovered should be loud. If a run with no tests is genuinely expected, MTP's --ignore-exit-code 8 says so explicitly.

Test results without a test UI

GitHub Actions has no built-in test results page beyond job summaries and artifacts. You have three options: upload the TRX files with actions/upload-artifact (what Ledgerly does), write a Markdown summary to $GITHUB_STEP_SUMMARY, or use a community action that renders TRX files as a check. The artifact keeps the evidence for retention-days: 14, long enough to investigate a flaky test without paying to store every run forever 💰.

🗺️ What one pull request runs

flowchart LR
    PR["Pull request<br/>touches sample/**"] --> BT["build-test<br/>restore, build, test"]
    PR --> Infra["infra<br/>bicep lint and build"]
    PR --> CQ["analyze<br/>codeql.yml"]
    BT -- "needs" --> C1["containers<br/>Ledgerly.Api"]
    BT -- "needs" --> C2["containers<br/>Ledgerly.Worker"]
    BT -- "needs" --> C3["containers<br/>Ledgerly.MarketData"]
    BT -- "needs" --> C4["containers<br/>Ledgerly.MigrationService"]
    BT -- "always" --> Art["Artifact<br/>test-results, 14 days"]
    C1 --> Checks["Required status checks<br/>ruleset on main"]
    C2 --> Checks
    C3 --> Checks
    C4 --> Checks
    Infra --> Checks
    CQ --> Checks

build-test, infra and analyze start in parallel on separate runners. The four containers jobs wait for build-test, because there's no point packaging code whose tests fail.

🧠 Container images without a Dockerfile

💡 The .NET SDK knows your app better than a Dockerfile does. Let it build the image.

The .NET SDK can build an OCI container image directly: dotnet publish with the PublishContainer target. No Dockerfile, no docker build. Without a registry it loads the image into the local Docker or Podman daemon. With -p:ContainerRegistry=... it pushes straight to a registry such as Azure Container Registry (SDK container publishing). Aspire and azd use the same mechanism for .NET projects.

💻 In Ledgerly: the containers matrix

📄 .github/workflows/ci.yml

  # Proves the deployable images build. Pushing happens only in deploy.yml.
  containers:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    needs: build-test
    timeout-minutes: 20
    strategy:
      matrix:
        project: [Ledgerly.Api, Ledgerly.Worker, Ledgerly.MarketData, Ledgerly.MigrationService]
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v7
      - uses: actions/setup-dotnet@v6
        with:
          global-json-file: sample/global.json
      - name: Build container image (no Dockerfile needed)
        run: dotnet publish src/${{ matrix.project }} -c Release /t:PublishContainer -p:ContainerImageTag=ci-${{ github.sha }}

A matrix turns one job definition into one job per value: four projects, four parallel jobs, four checks. Each image is tagged ci-<commit SHA>, so you can tell exactly which commit produced it. The image name comes from the project file:

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Api/Ledgerly.Api.csproj

  <PropertyGroup>
    <UserSecretsId>ledgerly-api-6f1d2c</UserSecretsId>
