    <!-- SDK container support: `dotnet publish /t:PublishContainer` builds an image without a Dockerfile. -->
    <ContainerRepository>ledgerly-api</ContainerRepository>
  </PropertyGroup>

The other three set ledgerly-worker, ledgerly-marketdata and ledgerly-migrations. Without ContainerRepository, the name would default to the assembly name.

CI builds the images but never pushes them: it has no registry credentials and only contents: read. The deploy workflow runs the same command with a registry, after signing in to Azure with OIDC:

📄 .github/workflows/deploy.yml

          for project in Ledgerly.Api Ledgerly.Worker Ledgerly.MarketData Ledgerly.MigrationService; do
            dotnet publish "src/$project" -c Release /t:PublishContainer \
              -p:ContainerRegistry="$LOGIN_SERVER" -p:ContainerImageTag="$SHA"
          done

What you get for free

The SDK makes choices that a hand-written Dockerfile often gets wrong:

  • A current base image. For .NET 10 the default distro is Ubuntu 24.04 (Noble). The images Ledgerly built this way were roughly 120 MB compressed for the default ASP.NET image (illustrative: yours will differ).
  • A non-root user. Since .NET 8, the Linux images include a non-root user called app. A container escape lands in an unprivileged account.
  • Port 8080, not 80. The default ASP.NET Core port in containers is 8080 (it was 80 before .NET 8), configured through ASPNETCORE_HTTP_PORTS. That's why every probe and ingress in Ledgerly's Container Apps points at 8080:

📄 sample/infra/modules/apps.bicep

var probes = [
  {
    type: 'Startup'
    httpGet: { path: '/alive', port: 8080 }
    // ...
  }
  // ...
]

Going smaller: chiseled images

Chiseled images (Ubuntu's distroless flavor) have no shell, no package manager, run non-root by default and contain only the packages the runtime needs. Less inside the image means fewer CVEs to patch. You choose them with ContainerFamily, which is appended to the tag (noble-chiseled gives mcr.microsoft.com/dotnet/aspnet:10.0-noble-chiseled):

<!-- illustrative, not in the sample: smaller, shell-less base images -->
<PropertyGroup>
  <ContainerFamily>noble-chiseled-extra</ContainerFamily>
</PropertyGroup>

Plain chiseled images have no ICU (globalization) or time zone data, so culture-aware formatting and time zone conversions stop behaving like they do on your laptop. Ledgerly would use the -extra variant, which adds both. The alternative is InvariantGlobalization=true, which is only right for apps that never need culture-specific behavior. 🔧 And no shell means no docker exec ... bash for debugging, which is exactly the point.

One escaping trap: to set several tags, use ContainerImageTags (plural), which takes a semicolon-separated list. In a shell, an unquoted semicolon ends the command, so quote the value carefully (Container publish configuration).

🧠 Infrastructure is code, so CI lints it

💡 Bicep is a compiler. Use it on every pull request, not just on deployment day.

💻 In Ledgerly: the infra job

📄 .github/workflows/ci.yml

  # Infrastructure is code, so it gets linted like code. (Deploying it happens in deploy.yml.)
  infra:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    timeout-minutes: 10
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v7
      - name: Lint and build Bicep
        run: |
          az bicep install
          az bicep lint --file infra/main.bicep
          az bicep build --file infra/main.bicep --stdout > /dev/null
          az bicep build-params --file infra/main.bicepparam --stdout > /dev/null

The job needs no Azure login: linting and compiling are local operations, so it runs with the same read-only token as everything else. lint applies the linter rules, build compiles main.bicep and all its modules to an ARM template (thrown away with > /dev/null, since only success matters), and build-params checks that main.bicepparam matches the template's parameters.

This job exists because of a real lesson. While writing the infrastructure, the Bicep compiler caught two mistakes before Azure did:

  • BCP138: a for expression inside concat() in a property, which Bicep doesn't allow. The fix was to move it to a variable.
  • BCP037: identity wasn't a known property on a KEDA scale rule for API version 2024-03-01. The fix was the newer 2025-01-01 API version.

Neither needed a subscription to find. Chapter 11 goes through the Bicep itself.

🗺️ From push to green

sequenceDiagram
    participant Dev as You
    participant GH as GitHub
    participant R as Runners
    participant RS as Ruleset on main
    Dev->>GH: Push commit A to the PR branch
    GH->>R: Start ci run for commit A
    Dev->>GH: Push commit B a minute later
    GH->>R: Cancel run A, same concurrency group
    GH->>R: Start ci run for commit B
    R->>R: build-test, infra and analyze in parallel
    R->>R: containers matrix after build-test
    R-->>GH: Upload test-results artifact
    R-->>GH: Report checks for commit B
    GH->>RS: Are all required checks green on the latest commit
    RS-->>Dev: Merge allowed after approval

🧠 Keeping CI fast, current and trustworthy

💡 Your workflow runs third-party code with access to your repository. Treat it like a dependency, because it is one.

Action versions and pinning

Every uses: line runs code someone else wrote. Ledgerly uses these versions 🔧:

Action Version in the sample
actions/checkout v7
actions/setup-dotnet v6
actions/upload-artifact v7
github/codeql-action (init and analyze) v4
azure/login (deploy only) v3

These are major version tags: @v7 moves to each new 7.x release. You get fixes without editing the file, and you trust the publisher not to break or compromise the tag. The alternative is pinning to a full commit SHA, which can never change under you, at the price of less readable files and more update PRs. There's no single right answer. A common split is major tags for actions published by GitHub and Microsoft, and SHA pins for smaller third-party actions. Never use a branch ref like @master: for azure/login, branch refs aren't supported at all.

Either way, Dependabot keeps them current. The github-actions ecosystem in .github/dependabot.yml (chapter 8) groups every action update into one weekly PR, and CI tests it like any other change.

Caching and lock files

The sample doesn't cache NuGet packages, and RestorePackagesWithLockFile is false in Directory.Build.props. On a fresh runner, restore downloads everything each time. For a solution this size that's a small part of the run, and it keeps the workflow simple. If restore time grows, measure first, then add a cache. A cache needs a stable key, which is the usual reason teams turn on NuGet lock files (packages.lock.json) at the same moment. Treat it as an optimization you add with numbers, not a default.

The rest of the safety net

  • CodeQL runs as its own workflow on every PR (chapter 8), so CI doesn't need a security step.
  • Required status checks turn this workflow into a gate. Without them, a red CI is just a red icon next to a merged PR (chapter 8 shows the exact check names).
  • Least privilege at the top of every workflow. ci.yml reads, codeql.yml adds security-events: write, and only deploy.yml asks for id-token: write to get an OIDC token (chapter 12).

⚠️ Common traps

Trap What happens Do this instead
Running VSTest-style dotnet test on the .NET 10 SDK with xUnit v3 4.x "Testing with VSTest target is no longer supported by Microsoft.Testing.Platform on .NET 10 SDK" Opt in to MTP in global.json, use dotnet test --solution, and remove Microsoft.NET.Test.Sdk, xunit.runner.visualstudio and coverlet.collector
Silencing NuGet audit when it breaks the build The vulnerable package ships to production Pin the patched version with CPM transitive pinning, with a label that says why
A test filter or project setup that runs zero tests MTP exits with code 8 and CI goes red Fix the filter or discovery. Use --ignore-exit-code 8 only when zero tests is truly expected
Uploading test results without if: always() The upload is skipped when tests fail, the one time you need it Add if: always() to every evidence-gathering step
No permissions: block The token gets the repository's default permissions, possibly write access Start every workflow with contents: read and widen per job
cancel-in-progress: true on a deployment A newer push kills a deployment halfway Cancel outdated builds, queue deployments (cancel-in-progress: false)
Passing several ContainerImageTags unquoted in a shell The shell treats ; as the end of the command Quote the property value, or use a single ContainerImageTag per publish

🧰 Helpers

Run what CI runs, from your machine:

cd sample
dotnet restore Ledgerly.slnx
dotnet build Ledgerly.slnx -c Release --no-restore
dotnet test --solution Ledgerly.slnx -c Release --no-build --coverage --coverage-output-format cobertura --report-xunit-trx
dotnet publish src/Ledgerly.Api -c Release /t:PublishContainer -p:ContainerImageTag=local

Watch and debug runs with the GitHub CLI:

gh workflow run ci                 # start CI by hand (workflow_dispatch)
gh run list --workflow ci          # recent runs
gh run watch                       # follow a run live
gh run view --log-failed           # only the logs of failed steps
gh run download <run-id> -n test-results   # get the TRX and coverage files
gh pr checks                       # check status for the current PR

A job summary, for when the artifact is too far away:

# illustrative, not in the sample: a one-line summary on the run page
      - name: Summary
        if: always()
        run: echo "Tests finished for commit ${{ github.sha }}. Results are in the test-results artifact." >> "$GITHUB_STEP_SUMMARY"

Make it permanent:

  • Start new repositories from the toolkit's ci.yml and dependabot.yml (toolkit). Change the paths, the solution name and the matrix, and keep the permissions, concurrency and if: always() lines.
  • Keep global.json as the single source of the SDK version, for your machine and for setup-dotnet.
  • Make build-test, every containers (...) check, infra and analyze required in the main ruleset (chapter 8).
  • Add a "CI is red" section to your runbook (the toolkit has runbook-template.md): gh run view --log-failed first, then reproduce locally with the commands above. Terms are in the glossary.

🧪 Try it yourself (optional, ~30 min)

  1. Run the CI commands locally, in order: bash cd sample dotnet restore Ledgerly.slnx dotnet build Ledgerly.slnx -c Release --no-restore dotnet test --solution Ledgerly.slnx -c Release --no-build --coverage --coverage-output-format cobertura --report-xunit-trx With Docker running, all 23 tests run. Without it, notice which integration tests skip.
  2. Build a container image without a Dockerfile: bash dotnet publish src/Ledgerly.Api -c Release /t:PublishContainer -p:ContainerImageTag=local docker image inspect ledgerly-api:local In the output, find the user (Config.User) and the exposed port (Config.ExposedPorts). Compare them with the probes in apps.bicep.
  3. In your fork from chapter 8, start CI by hand and follow it: bash gh workflow run ci gh run watch
  4. Break a test on purpose (change an expected value in a unit test), push to a PR branch, and read the failure with gh run view --log-failed. Download the test-results artifact and open the TRX file.
  5. Push two commits quickly to the same branch. In the Actions tab, watch the first run get cancelled by the concurrency group.
  6. Edit only a file under chapters/ in a separate branch and open a PR. CI doesn't start, because of the paths filter. If you made build-test a required check in chapter 8, notice that the PR now waits for a check that will never run.
  7. Revert the broken test and watch the PR turn green.

✅ Recap

  1. Ledgerly's CI runs the same restore, build and test commands you run locally, on a fresh runner, with least-privilege permissions and cancellation of outdated runs.
  2. Real failures shaped it: NuGet audit stopped a vulnerable transitive package, the .NET 10 SDK required Microsoft.Testing.Platform, and Bicep lint catches infrastructure mistakes before Azure does.
  3. The SDK builds secure, non-root container images on port 8080 without a Dockerfile, and CI proves every image builds before deploy ever pushes one.

Next → Chapter 10: Choosing Azure services

Chapter 10: Choosing Azure services

⏱ About 30 min · You'll learn: how to choose compute, data, messaging, caching, secrets and observability services on Azure with decision tables, why Ledgerly runs on Azure Container Apps, and which cost drivers actually move the monthly bill.

📖 Story: The Azure menu

Elena Rossi walks into standup with a spreadsheet open on her laptop. "The beta opens in eight weeks. Finance wants to know what it costs per month. I don't need a number to the cent. I need a range, and I need to know what makes it go up."

Maya Okafor books the small meeting room for an hour and writes AZURE MENU across the top of the whiteboard. "Azure has a couple of hundred services. We need six kinds." She draws six columns: compute, data, messaging, caching, secrets, observability. "For each column we pick one dish, write down why, and write down what makes it expensive. Elena gets cost drivers, not a guess."

You open your notebook. At Nordbank Insurance, choosing infrastructure meant a ticket, a three-week wait and a VM sized for the one busy week in January. Under Habits I brought with me you write: "Size for peak, then forget about it."

The numbers in this chapter's story (weeks, users, percentages) are illustrative. The service limits and free grants are real and marked 🔧.

🏚️ Old way → 🚀 New way

What you did before What you do now Why it changed
Order a VM sized for peak load from the ops team Pick a managed service that bills for use and scales with rules Idle capacity costs money every hour, and a scale rule reacts faster than a purchase order
One IIS site per app on a shared server farm One Container App per service in a shared Container Apps environment Same "shared host" idea, but the platform owns the OS, patching, TLS and scaling
SQL Server on a VM, looked after by a DBA Azure SQL Database, serverless tier Same engine and T-SQL, with backups and patching managed and compute that pauses when idle
MSMQ installed on each server Azure Service Bus queues One durable queue shared by every replica, with dead-lettering and duplicate detection built in
MemoryCache per server, or a self-hosted cache cluster HybridCache in memory, plus Azure Managed Redis only when replicas must share a cache Two levels with stampede protection, and you pay for a shared cache only when you need one
Passwords in web.config, encrypted with aspnet_regiis Managed identities for almost everything, Key Vault (RBAC mode) for the few secrets left A password can leak, an identity can't be pasted into a chat
Perfmon counters and log files on each server Application Insights backed by a Log Analytics workspace One place to query every replica, with costs you can cap

💡 In the cloud, the size of the bill is decided by your architecture choices, not by a hardware order.

🧠 How the menu is organized

Before choosing dishes, learn how the menu is laid out. Four concepts decide where a resource lives, who can touch it and who pays for it.

Concept What it is Old-world equivalent Ledgerly's choice
Subscription A billing and access boundary. Quotas and several free grants are counted per subscription A cost center with its own budget line One subscription for the beta
Resource group A folder of resources that share a lifecycle. You deploy, grant access to and delete them together "All the servers for app X" in the CMDB One resource group per environment, for example rg-ledgerly-prod
Region The datacenter location of a resource. It affects latency, data residency and which SKUs exist Your own datacenter The resource group's region. Everything stays in one region
Tags Key/value labels on resources. Cost analysis can group and filter by them A column in the asset spreadsheet app and managed-by on every resource

Pick a region close to your users and keep every service in it, so calls between them stay short (not every SKU exists in every region, so check before you commit). Put tags in code so they can't be forgotten: cost analysis can group by them (cost management).

💻 In Ledgerly: one region, one name, one set of tags

📄 sample/infra/main.bicep

param location string = resourceGroup().location
// ...
var name = '${appName}-${take(uniqueString(resourceGroup().id), 6)}'
var tags = {
  app: appName
  'managed-by': 'bicep'
}
  • location defaults to the resource group's region, so you choose the region once, when you create the group.
  • uniqueString(resourceGroup().id) is deterministic: the same resource group always gives the same six characters. Names like sql-ledgerly-ab12cd are unique across Azure but stable across deployments.
  • Every module receives tags, so every resource can be found in cost analysis with app = ledgerly. Chapter 11 walks through the whole file.

💡 A resource group is a lifecycle boundary: choose its contents by asking "would I delete these together?"

🧠 Compute: where your code runs

This is the biggest decision on the menu, and the one with the most opinions attached. Microsoft publishes a comparison of its container and compute options (compare options). Here it is as a decision table.

Option What it is Use it when Avoid it when
Azure App Service Fully managed hosting optimized for web apps, from code or a container. The closest thing to your IIS mental model You have one or a few web apps and want "a site" with as little platform thinking as possible You have several cooperating services, background workers scaled by queue length, and run-to-completion jobs
Azure Container Apps (ACA) Serverless containers built on Kubernetes, KEDA, Dapr and Envoy, with no direct Kubernetes API APIs, microservices, event-driven workers and jobs, including scale to zero You need the Kubernetes API itself: custom operators, CRDs or cluster-level control
Azure Kubernetes Service (AKS) Full Kubernetes API control. Offered as Standard or AKS Automatic 🔧 A platform team already runs Kubernetes, or you need what only the Kubernetes API offers A small team without Kubernetes skills that just wants to ship an API
Azure Functions, Flex Consumption 🔧 Functions as a service. Flex Consumption is the recommended serverless plan: Linux, scale to zero, VNet integration and "always ready" instances. It replaces Linux Consumption for new apps Small event handlers and glue code: react to a blob, a queue message or a timer A normal ASP.NET Core API you also run locally with Aspire

Sources: compare options and Flex Consumption plan.

Why Ledgerly chose Container Apps

Maya writes the requirements on the left of the board and the matching ACA feature on the right. The result becomes an ADR (use the ADR template from the toolkit).

Ledgerly needs Container Apps gives
Three long-running services (api, worker, marketdata) and one run-to-completion task (migrations) Container apps for the services and a Container Apps job with a manual trigger for migrations
The worker must scale with the import queue, not with HTTP traffic KEDA scale rules, including a Service Bus queue-length scaler
The beta API is idle at night Scale to zero: with min replicas 0 and no traffic, no usage charges 🔧
The fake market-data vendor must not be reachable from the internet Internal ingress, visible only inside the environment
Deploys without downtime Revisions: in single revision mode the new revision replaces the old one with zero downtime
A small team with no Kubernetes specialists Kubernetes underneath, but no cluster to run or upgrade

The trade-off Maya writes underneath: cold starts. A service scaled to zero takes a moment to wake up. The sample makes it a parameter (apiMinReplicas) so you can change your mind without changing code (overview, scaling).

💻 In Ledgerly: one environment, the serverless profile

📄 sample/infra/modules/environment.bicep

resource environment 'Microsoft.App/managedEnvironments@2025-01-01' = {
  name: 'cae-${name}'
  location: location
  tags: tags
  properties: {
    appLogsConfiguration: {
      destination: 'log-analytics'
      // ...
    }
    workloadProfiles: [
      { name: 'Consumption', workloadProfileType: 'Consumption' } // 💰 serverless, pay per use, free monthly grant
    ]
  }
}

The environment is the shared boundary: one network and one Log Analytics workspace for all apps and jobs inside it. Think of it as the IIS server farm, except that you never see the servers. The Consumption workload profile is the serverless option; Dedicated profiles reserve hardware when you need it.

💡 Choose the platform that matches the shape of your work: one web app, many event handlers, or a handful of services, workers and jobs.

🗺️ Picking compute in four questions

flowchart TB
    Start["A new service"] --> K8s{"Do you need the<br/>Kubernetes API itself?"}
    K8s -- "yes" --> AKS["AKS<br/>Standard or Automatic"]
    K8s -- "no" --> Shape{"What shape<br/>is the work?"}
    Shape -- "one web app,<br/>IIS-like" --> AppSvc["App Service"]
    Shape -- "small event<br/>handlers" --> Func["Functions<br/>Flex Consumption"]
    Shape -- "APIs, workers<br/>and jobs" --> ACA["Container Apps"]
    ACA --> Idle{"Idle for<br/>long periods?"}
    Idle -- "yes" --> Zero["min replicas 0<br/>accept cold starts"]
    Idle -- "no, or must<br/>poll on a timer" --> One["min replicas 1"]

🧠 Data, messaging and caching

Data: Azure SQL Database, serverless

Your SQL Server knowledge transfers directly, which is the main reason Ledgerly didn't look further. The interesting decision is the purchasing model.

Feature What it means 🔧 Why it matters
Serverless compute Compute scales between a min and max vCore count (min can be 0.5 vCore), billed per vCore-second Light traffic costs little, and there's no instance to size
Auto-pause General Purpose serverless databases pause after an idle delay. The default is 60 minutes, the minimum is 15, and -1 disables it 💰 Nights and weekends cost only storage. The first connection after a pause waits for a resume, so the app needs retries
Free offer Per database, per month: 100,000 vCore-seconds, 32 GB of data and 32 GB of backup storage, for the life of the subscription. Up to 10 free databases per subscription 💰 A beta can run inside it. When the monthly allowance is used up, the database is either paused until next month or billed for the overage, depending on a setting
Entra-only authentication Disables SQL logins, so only Microsoft Entra ID principals can sign in No database password exists to leak (chapter 13)

Sources: serverless tier, free offer, Entra-only authentication. Serverless fits low or bursty traffic. A database that is busy around the clock is more predictable on a provisioned tier.

📄 sample/infra/modules/sql.bicep

  sku: {
    name: 'GP_S_Gen5_2'
    tier: 'GeneralPurpose'
    family: 'Gen5'
    capacity: 2
  }
  properties: {
    autoPauseDelay: 60
    minCapacity: json('0.5')
    useFreeLimit: useFreeLimit // 💰 100,000 vCore-seconds per month, then pause until next month
    freeLimitExhaustionBehavior: useFreeLimit ? 'AutoPause' : null
  }

GP_S_Gen5_2 reads as General Purpose, Serverless, Gen5, max 2 vCores. Elena spots the catch: "Pause until next month means the beta is down until next month?" Yes. Fine for learning, bad once real users depend on it, so Maya adds a launch-checklist item: set sqlUseFreeLimit = false before the public beta. The migration service already copes with resume delays: "The execution strategy retries transient failures (e.g. Azure SQL serverless waking up)."

Messaging: Azure Service Bus tiers

Chapter 7 built the import pipeline on a queue. Here is how to pick the tier.

Basic Standard Premium
Queues ✅ ✅ ✅
Topics and subscriptions ❌ ✅ ✅
Sessions, transactions, duplicate detection ❌ ✅ ✅
Capacity Shared Shared, pay per operation Dedicated messaging units
Max message size 🔧 256 KB 256 KB Up to 100 MB
VNet / Private Link, geo-replication ❌ ❌ ✅

Source: Service Bus Premium and tiers.

Ledgerly's outbox relay sets MessageId to the outbox row id, and the queue drops any message with an id it has seen in the last ten minutes (duplicate detection). That one feature rules out Basic. Nothing in the beta needs dedicated capacity, private networking or 100 MB messages, which rules out Premium for now. Standard it is.

📄 sample/infra/modules/servicebus.bicep

// Service Bus Standard (needed for duplicate detection). Local (SAS key) auth is disabled:
// only Entra identities with a data role can send or receive.
// ...
  sku: { name: 'Standard', tier: 'Standard' }
  properties: {
    disableLocalAuth: true
    minimumTlsVersion: '1.2'
  }

Duplicate detection is configured on the entity when it is created, so this is a decision you want right on day one.

Caching: HybridCache first, Azure Managed Redis when you need it

Two facts shape this decision.

First, Azure Cache for Redis is being retired 🔧 (retirement FAQ):

Azure Cache for Redis tiers Retirement date 🔧 Instances disabled from 🔧
Enterprise and Enterprise Flash 2027-03-31 2027-04-01
Basic, Standard and Premium 2028-09-30 2028-10-01

New instance creation is being restricted before those dates. Don't start new work on it, even if a blog post from 2023 tells you to.

Second, the replacement is Azure Managed Redis 🔧 (overview). It is built on Redis Enterprise, comes in Memory Optimized, Balanced, Compute Optimized and Flash Optimized SKUs, is clustered by default (a non-clustered option exists up to 25 GB), is zone-redundant by default and supports Microsoft Entra ID authentication. StackExchange.Redis works with it without special cluster configuration.

But the first question isn't "which Redis?" It's "do we need a shared cache at all?"

Option What it is Use it when Avoid it when
HybridCache, L1 only In-process memory cache with stampede protection (HybridCache) Few replicas, short expirations, and a little staleness between replicas is fine Replicas must agree on cached values, or the cache is expensive to rebuild
HybridCache + Azure Managed Redis as L2 The same API, with a shared distributed cache behind the memory cache Many replicas, expensive values, or cache data that should survive a restart You're adding it "just in case" (💰 it is a paid resource even at its smallest size)
Azure Cache for Redis The retiring service 🔧 Only to keep an existing instance alive while you migrate Any new work

💻 In Ledgerly: Redis is optional by design

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Api/Program.cs

// ── Caching: HybridCache (in-memory L1). Redis becomes the shared L2 when a "cache" connection exists.
if (!string.IsNullOrEmpty(builder.Configuration.GetConnectionString("cache")))
{
    builder.AddRedisDistributedCache("cache");
}

builder.Services.AddHybridCache(options => options.DefaultEntryOptions = new HybridCacheEntryOptions
{
    Expiration = TimeSpan.FromSeconds(30),
    LocalCacheExpiration = TimeSpan.FromSeconds(10),
});

The code doesn't care which mode it runs in. Locally, Aspire starts a Redis container and passes a cache connection string, so you get both levels. In Azure, enableRedis defaults to false in main.bicep: the API uses memory only, and prices are cached for 30 seconds per replica. If the beta grows, you flip one parameter and the infrastructure creates Azure Managed Redis (Balanced_B0, the smallest size) and wires the connection string through Key Vault.

💡 Start with the cheapest option that is correct, and make the expensive option a parameter rather than a rewrite.

🧠 Secrets and observability

Secrets: Azure Key Vault in RBAC mode

The best secret is one that doesn't exist. Chapter 13 shows how managed identities remove passwords from SQL, Service Bus and the container registry entirely. Key Vault is for what's left: in Ledgerly, exactly one secret, the Redis access key, and only when Redis is enabled.

Decision Choice Why 🔧
Permission model Azure RBAC RBAC is the recommended model and vault access policies are legacy. Starting with ARM API version 2026-02-01, new vaults default to RBAC
Who reads secrets Key Vault Secrets User for the app identity only Reads secret values and nothing else
Who manages secrets Key Vault Secrets Officer or Administrator, for people or pipelines that need it Separate "read" from "manage"
Scope The vault, or narrower Never the whole subscription

Sources: RBAC vs access policies, Key Vault RBAC guide.

Observability: Application Insights and Log Analytics

There is only one shape left for Application Insights: workspace-based. Classic Application Insights resources are retired, and all telemetry is stored in a Log Analytics workspace and billed through it (workspace-based resources). Chapters 15 and 16 cover the telemetry itself. The menu decision here is about cost: Log Analytics ingestion is often the surprise on the bill. Sampling, a daily cap and a sensible retention period are the levers (sampling).

💻 In Ledgerly: a hard daily cap

📄 sample/infra/modules/monitoring.bicep

resource workspace 'Microsoft.OperationalInsights/workspaces@2023-09-01' = {
  name: 'log-${name}'
  location: location
  tags: tags
  properties: {
    sku: { name: 'PerGB2018' }
    retentionInDays: 30
    workspaceCapping: { dailyQuotaGb: 1 } // 💰 a hard daily cap keeps a learning subscription safe
  }
}

resource appInsights 'Microsoft.Insights/components@2020-02-02' = {
  // ...
  properties: {
    Application_Type: 'web'
    WorkspaceResourceId: workspace.id
  }
}
  • PerGB2018 is pay per GB ingested, and retentionInDays: 30 keeps a month of data.
  • dailyQuotaGb: 1 stops ingestion for the rest of the day once 1 GB has arrived. Priya objects ("a cap means blind spots during an incident"), so the team records it as a beta setting to revisit with real traffic data.

💡 Telemetry is a product with a price per GB: decide what you keep, for how long and at what sample rate.

🧠 What actually drives the bill

Now you can answer Elena. Not with a number, but with a table of drivers, where each one lives in Ledgerly's code, and the lever that controls it. Prices change and differ by region, so the source of truth is each service's pricing page and the Azure Pricing Calculator 🔧.

Service What you pay for 💰 Where it is in Ledgerly Lever
Container Apps (Consumption) vCPU-seconds, GiB-seconds and requests, after a monthly free grant per subscription of 180,000 vCPU-seconds, 360,000 GiB-seconds and 2 million requests 🔧. Replicas held warm by min replicas > 0 are billed at a reduced idle rate apps.bicep: api min 0, marketdata min 0, worker min 1 Scale to zero where you can accept a cold start (apiMinReplicas)
Azure SQL serverless vCore-seconds while running, plus storage. Free offer covers 100,000 vCore-seconds a month 🔧 sql.bicep: autoPauseDelay: 60, minCapacity: 0.5, useFreeLimit Auto-pause, free offer, max vCores
Service Bus Standard Shared capacity, pay per operation servicebus.bicep Fewer, larger messages, and no polling loops against the queue
Log Analytics + App Insights GB ingested and retention monitoring.bicep: 1 GB daily cap, 30 days Sampling, daily cap, retention, and not logging every probe
Azure Managed Redis A fixed size that runs all month, even when idle redis.bicep, off unless enableRedis = true Leave it off until you need a shared cache
Container Registry, Key Vault Small, mostly fixed or per-operation charges registry.bicep (Basic), keyvault.bicep Check the pricing pages, then move on

Sources: Container Apps billing, Azure SQL free offer.

Two details hide in that table:

  • The worker never scales to zero. Its comment explains why: "the outbox relay and price alerts poll on a timer, so one replica must always run." That replica is your baseline Container Apps cost. A design that triggered the worker purely from the queue could scale to zero, at the price of a different outbox design.
  • The API's health probes don't create telemetry. The service defaults filter /health and /alive out of traces, so probes don't eat the daily cap.

Budgets: an alarm, not a brake

Maya's last item: a budget on the resource group, with alerts on actual or forecasted cost. It's cheap insurance, but budgets don't stop resources: they notify, and stopping things needs automation wired to an action group. Cost data typically lands within 8 to 24 hours and budgets are evaluated every 24 hours, so a runaway loop can run for a day before anyone hears about it (create budgets).

Elena leaves with what she needed: "Most of the beta fits in free grants and serverless pauses. The fixed costs are the worker replica, the registry and Redis if we turn it on. The surprise risk is logs, and that's capped."

💡 You can't predict a cloud bill to the cent, but you can name every driver and put a lever next to it.

🗺️ Ledgerly on Azure, with its cost drivers

flowchart LR
    subgraph RG["Resource group rg-ledgerly-prod, one region"]
        subgraph ENV["Container Apps environment, Consumption profile"]
            Api["api<br/>min 0, max 10"]
            Worker["worker<br/>min 1, max 5"]
            Market["marketdata<br/>internal, min 0, max 3"]
            Job["job-migrations<br/>runs once per deploy"]
        end
        Sql[("Azure SQL serverless<br/>auto-pause, free offer")]
        Bus["Service Bus Standard<br/>pay per operation"]
        Redis[("Azure Managed Redis<br/>optional, off by default")]
        KV["Key Vault<br/>RBAC mode"]
        Logs["Log Analytics + App Insights<br/>1 GB daily cap, 30 days"]
        ACR["Container Registry<br/>Basic"]
    end
    Api -- "EF Core" --> Sql
    Worker -- "EF Core" --> Sql
    Worker -- "imports queue" --> Bus
    Api -. "L2 cache if enabled" .-> Redis
    KV -. "Redis secret reference" .-> Api
    ACR -- "images" --> ENV
    ENV -- "telemetry and logs" --> Logs

⚠️ Common traps

Trap What happens Do this instead
Choosing AKS because "everyone uses Kubernetes" Weeks of cluster plumbing, upgrades and node pools before the first feature ships Start on Container Apps. The concepts (replicas, probes, revisions, KEDA) transfer if you ever need AKS
Creating Azure Cache for Redis for a new project You build on a service with published retirement dates 🔧 and migrate within two years Use Azure Managed Redis, or no shared cache at all until you need one
Picking Service Bus Basic to save money Duplicate detection, topics and sessions are missing, and duplicate detection is set when the entity is created Use Standard when your design depends on duplicate detection or topics
Leaving the SQL free offer on "auto-pause when exhausted" in production The allowance runs out mid-month and the database pauses until next month Use the free offer for learning and early testing, then switch to billed overage before real users arrive
Min replicas 1 on every app "to be safe" Every app is billed at least at the idle rate, all month Use 0 where a cold start is acceptable, 1 only where it isn't or where a timer must run
No daily cap, no sampling, verbose logging in production Log ingestion becomes the largest line on the bill Cap, sample and filter (probes, chatty categories) from day one
Treating a budget as a spending limit The alert email arrives, the resources keep running Wire an action group to automation if you need a brake

🧰 Helpers

A one-page "Azure menu" for any new service. Copy it into the ADR for each choice:

## Service choice: <compute | data | messaging | caching | secrets | observability>
- Requirement in one sentence:
- Options considered: (from the decision table)
- Chosen, and why:
- What makes it expensive, and the lever in Bicep:
- What would make us change our mind:
- 🔧 Volatile facts we relied on, with doc links:

Monthly cost review (15 minutes):

  • [ ] Cost analysis grouped by the app tag: anything grow unexpectedly?
  • [ ] Log Analytics ingestion: close to the daily cap?
  • [ ] Azure SQL: when does the free allowance run out?
  • [ ] Any resource without tags? (It was probably created by hand.)

Make it permanent:

  • Put names, tags and SKUs in Bicep (chapter 11), so a reviewer sees every cost decision in a pull request.
  • Add one ADR per menu decision, using the ADR template in the toolkit.
  • Create a budget with forecast alerts on each environment's resource group as part of the one-time setup (chapter 12).
  • Add "cost driver and lever" as a question in your PR template for infrastructure changes.

🧪 Try it yourself (optional, ~25 min)

No Azure subscription is needed for these steps.

  1. Install the Bicep CLI. If you have the Azure CLI, run az bicep install. Otherwise install the standalone bicep CLI from the Bicep docs.
  2. Find every cost lever in the parameters: bash cd sample grep -n "param " infra/main.bicep Mark the ones that change the bill: enableRedis, sqlUseFreeLimit and apiMinReplicas. Read their @description text.
  3. Find the scale settings for each app: bash grep -n -A2 "scale:" infra/modules/apps.bicep Observe: the API uses apiMinReplicas, the worker is pinned to minReplicas: 1, and marketdata scales from 0 to 3.
  4. Compile the Bicep to the ARM JSON that Azure would receive, and look for every SKU: bash bicep build infra/main.bicep --stdout | grep -n '"sku"' (With the Azure CLI: az bicep build --file infra/main.bicep --stdout.)
  5. Open the Azure Pricing Calculator. Add Container Apps, Azure SQL Database (serverless), Service Bus Standard and Log Analytics. Model a quiet beta month and a busy one. Note which line moves the most between the two.
  6. Write a short ADR: "Why Ledgerly runs on Azure Container Apps", with the cold-start trade-off and the condition that would make you switch apiMinReplicas to 1.

✅ Recap

  1. Pick compute by the shape of the work: App Service for an IIS-like web app, Functions Flex Consumption for event handlers, Container Apps for services, workers and jobs, AKS only when you need the Kubernetes API.
  2. Ledgerly uses Azure SQL serverless, Service Bus Standard (for duplicate detection), HybridCache with optional Azure Managed Redis, Key Vault in RBAC mode and workspace-based Application Insights.
  3. Cost is a design property: scale to zero, free grants, auto-pause and a log ingestion cap are levers in Bicep, and budgets warn but never stop spending.

Next → Chapter 11: Infrastructure as code

Chapter 11: Infrastructure as code

⏱ About 35 min · You'll learn: Bicep basics (parameters, variables, modules, outputs, conditions, loops, existing, .bicepparam), how Ledgerly's main.bicep deploys in three phases, least-privilege role assignments for managed identities, how lint and what-if catch mistakes before Azure does, and how the database user for a managed identity gets created without anyone clicking.

📖 Story: "Show me the pull request"

Maya's Azure menu from chapter 10 is on the wiki. Priya has a free afternoon and offers to "just click it together in the portal, it's maybe twenty resources." Tomás Reyes, who owns the platform, doesn't even look up from his screen. "No."

He turns around. "If I click it, there is no record of what I clicked. Next month someone changes a setting during an incident, nobody remembers, and staging and production quietly drift apart. If it isn't in a pull request, it doesn't exist." He opens an empty file called main.bicep. "We write it down once. Azure reads it. Anyone can review it, rerun it or delete everything and get it back."

You think of the server build document at Nordbank Insurance: a 40-page Word file with screenshots, version 7, last updated by someone who left in 2019. In your Habits I brought with me notebook you write: "The runbook is the documentation." Next to it, Tomás writes in pencil: "The code is the runbook."

🏚️ Old way → 🚀 New way

What you did before What you do now Why it changed
A Word document with screenshots of every setting A .bicep file in the same repo as the app Text can be reviewed, diffed and versioned, and it can't fall out of date with itself
Click through the portal, then hope staging matches production Deploy the same template with a different .bicepparam file Environments differ only where the parameters say so
"Who changed this?" answered by guessing git log infra/ and the deployment history Every change is a commit with an author and a reason
Ask a DBA to create logins and grant rights by hand Managed identities and role assignments declared in Bicep, the database user created by the migration job Permissions are reviewed in a pull request like any other code
Find typos when the deployment fails at 2 a.m. bicep lint and bicep build in CI, what-if before applying The compiler and a preview catch mistakes before Azure does
One big script that only works on a clean server Idempotent, incremental deployments Running the same template twice changes nothing the second time

🧠 Bicep in ten minutes

Bicep is a declarative language for Azure resources. You describe the end state; Azure Resource Manager (ARM) works out what to create or change. bicep build compiles it to an ARM JSON template, and that JSON is what Azure actually receives. For a C# developer, it reads like a typed configuration file with functions.

Concept What it does Ledgerly example
targetScope Where the template deploys: a resource group, subscription, management group or tenant targetScope = 'resourceGroup'
param + decorators An input, with type, default and validation @minLength(3) @maxLength(12) param appName string = 'ledgerly'
var A computed value, like a const var name = '${appName}-${take(uniqueString(resourceGroup().id), 6)}'
resource One Azure resource: symbolic name, type and API version resource registry 'Microsoft.ContainerRegistry/registries@2023-07-01'
module Another Bicep file, called like a function with params module sql 'modules/sql.bicep' = { ... }
output A value returned to the caller or the CLI output loginServer string = registry.properties.loginServer
Condition Deploy only if an expression is true module redis 'modules/redis.bicep' = if (enableRedis) { ... }
Loop Create one item per element of an array [for principalId in pullPrincipalIds: { ... }]
existing Reference a resource you didn't create in this file, to read its properties or nest children resource vault '...' existing = { name: keyVaultName }
parent Declare a child resource without spelling out the full type path The imports queue has parent: namespace
.bicepparam The modern parameter file, type-checked against the template using 'main.bicep' then param appName = 'ledgerly'

Two details trip up every newcomer:

  • Dependencies are inferred. When sql uses identities.outputs.migratorClientId, Bicep knows the identity module must deploy first. You rarely write dependsOn.
  • The API version is part of the type. Microsoft.App/containerApps@2025-01-01 and @2024-03-01 are different schemas. A property that exists in one may not exist in the other, which is exactly how lesson 7 below happened.

💻 In Ledgerly: parameters that explain themselves

📄 sample/infra/main.bicep

@description('Short name used in every resource name.')
@minLength(3)
@maxLength(12)
param appName string = 'ledgerly'

param location string = resourceGroup().location

@description('Container image tag (the git commit SHA). Empty = deploy the platform only.')
param imageTag string = ''

@description('Deploy the long-running apps. The pipeline sets this after the migration job succeeded.')
param deployApps bool = false
// ...
@description('0 = scale to zero (cheapest), 1 = always warm (no cold start).')
@minValue(0)
param apiMinReplicas int = 0

📄 sample/infra/main.bicepparam

// Values for the "production" environment. Secrets never go here: there are none to pass,
// because every service authenticates with a managed identity.
using 'main.bicep'

param appName = 'ledgerly'
param jwtAuthority = 'https://login.microsoftonline.com/<your-tenant-id>/v2.0'
param jwtAudience = 'api://ledgerly-api'
param corsOrigins = []
param enableRedis = false
param apiMinReplicas = 0

// imageTag and deployApps are passed by the pipeline on the command line.

using 'main.bicep' binds the file to the template, so a typo like param apiMinReplica = 0 is a compile error, not a silently ignored value. A second environment is a second file (staging.bicepparam), not a second template. Before your first real deployment, replace <your-tenant-id> with your tenant id (chapter 12).

💡 A good parameter file reads like the environment's spec sheet: everything that differs, nothing that doesn't, and no secrets.

🧠 One template, three phases

A naive template deploys everything at once. That fails on day one (the apps reference an image tag that doesn't exist yet in a registry the same deployment creates), and it's wrong after day one (the new API would start before the migrations ran).

Ledgerly's main.bicep solves both with two parameters and two conditions. The header comment is the design document.

💻 In Ledgerly: phases as conditions

📄 sample/infra/main.bicep

// Ledgerly on Azure. Resource-group scope; the deploy workflow runs it in three phases:
//   1. imageTag = ''                       -> platform only (registry, data, messaging, environment)
//   2. imageTag = <sha>, deployApps = false -> + the migration job with the new image (pipeline runs it)
//   3. imageTag = <sha>, deployApps = true  -> + api, worker, marketdata on the new image
// Deployments are incremental and idempotent: re-running a phase changes only what differs.
targetScope = 'resourceGroup'
// ...
module migrationJob 'modules/migration-job.bicep' = if (!empty(imageTag)) {
  name: 'migration-job'
  params: {
    // ...
    image: '${registry.outputs.loginServer}/ledgerly-migrations:${imageTag}'
    identityId: identities.outputs.migratorId
    // ...
  }
}

module apps 'modules/apps.bicep' = if (deployApps && !empty(imageTag)) {
  name: 'apps'
  params: {
    // ...
    cacheSecretUri: enableRedis ? redis!.outputs.secretUri : ''
    // ...
  }
}
// ...
output apiUrl string = (deployApps && !empty(imageTag)) ? apps!.outputs.apiUrl : ''
Phase Parameters What Azure has afterwards What the pipeline does next
1 imageTag = '' Monitoring, identities, registry, Key Vault, Service Bus, SQL, the Container Apps environment (and Redis if enabled) Builds the four images and pushes them, tagged with the commit SHA
2 imageTag = <sha>, deployApps = false Phase 1, plus job-migrations on the new image Starts the job and waits for success
3 imageTag = <sha>, deployApps = true Everything, with api, worker and marketdata on the new image Smoke-tests the API

Three Bicep details make this work:

  • Incremental mode. A resource-group deployment adds and updates resources, but leaves resources that aren't in the template alone. So phase 1 on the second day doesn't delete the apps that phase 3 created yesterday; it just confirms the platform is unchanged.
  • The ! operator. redis and apps are conditional modules, so their outputs might not exist. redis!.outputs.secretUri tells the compiler "I've checked the condition myself"; it's only evaluated when enableRedis is true. It works like C#'s null-forgiving operator.
  • Deterministic names. Every resource name comes from name (ledgerly- plus six characters derived from the resource group id), so each phase targets the same resources again.

Faster paths, and why the course doesn't take them

You don't have to write Bicep by hand. Know the alternatives:

Tool What it does 🔧 Why Ledgerly hand-writes instead
aspire deploy Provisions Azure resources and deploys containers from the AppHost model. Now the recommended path for Aspire apps. aspire publish emits the generated artifacts, including Bicep. AddAzureContainerAppEnvironment and PublishAsAzureContainerApp customize the output (deploy with Aspire, Container Apps) Great for getting started. The course wants you to see every resource, role and setting, so you can review what a generator gives you
Azure Developer CLI (azd), version 1.37.1 azd up provisions (infra/) and deploys from azure.yaml (host: containerapp). azd pipeline config creates OIDC credentials and repository variables. For Aspire it is "still supported but no longer the recommended default" (azd overview) Same reason. azd pipeline config is still a handy shortcut for the setup in chapter 12
Azure Verified Modules (AVM) Microsoft-maintained modules in the public registry, for example br/public:avm/res/storage/storage-account:0.18.0. Resource modules live under avm/res, patterns under avm/ptn. Non-AVM modules have been retired from the public registry (modules) A good option for a team template. Ledgerly's modules are small on purpose, so every line can be read in a course

A practical path: start with aspire publish to see what it generates, then own the Bicep once you need to change it.

💡 Ordering belongs in the template's design, not in a human's memory: conditions plus a pipeline turn "deploy in the right order" into code.

🗺️ The module graph

flowchart TB
    Main["main.bicep<br/>params and phases"]
    Mon["monitoring<br/>Log Analytics, App Insights"]
    Id["identity<br/>app and migrator"]
    Reg["registry<br/>AcrPull for both"]
    KV["keyvault<br/>Secrets User for app"]
    SB["servicebus<br/>Sender and Receiver for app"]
    Sql["sql<br/>migrator is Entra admin"]
    Redis["redis<br/>if enableRedis"]
    Env["environment<br/>Container Apps"]
    Job["migration-job<br/>if imageTag set"]
    Apps["apps<br/>if deployApps and imageTag"]
    Main --> Mon
    Main --> Id
    Id -- "principal ids" --> Reg
    Id -- "principal id" --> KV
    Id -- "principal id" --> SB
    Id -- "migrator name and client id" --> Sql
    KV -- "vault name" --> Redis
    Mon -- "workspace name" --> Env
    Env -- "environment id" --> Job
    Env -- "environment id" --> Apps
    Redis -. "secret URI" .-> Apps

🧠 Identities and least-privilege role assignments

On your old servers, the app pool ran as a domain account and someone added that account to a SQL login and an MSMQ ACL. In Azure, the equivalent is a managed identity: an identity in Microsoft Entra ID whose credentials Azure manages for you. A user-assigned managed identity is a resource you declare and then attach to apps; it survives redeploys and can be granted rights before any app exists. That's why Ledgerly uses user-assigned identities instead of system-assigned ones, which are created and deleted with each app.

💻 In Ledgerly: two identities, two jobs

📄 sample/infra/modules/identity.bicep

// Two user-assigned managed identities. No passwords or connection-string secrets anywhere:
//  - "app": what api, worker and marketdata run as (least privilege: read/write data, send/receive messages).
//  - "migrator": what the migration job runs as. It is the SQL Entra admin, so it can change the schema.
// ...
resource app 'Microsoft.ManagedIdentity/userAssignedIdentities@2023-01-31' = {
  name: 'id-${name}-app'
  location: location
  tags: tags
}

The identity is a principal. Rights come from role assignments: a principal, a role definition and a scope. Ledgerly grants the smallest role at the narrowest scope:

Principal Role Scope Lets it
app, migrator AcrPull The registry Pull images. Nothing else
app Key Vault Secrets User The vault Read secret values. Not list keys, not manage the vault
app Azure Service Bus Data Sender + Data Receiver The namespace Send and receive messages. Not create queues or change settings
app Azure Service Bus Data Owner 🔧 The imports queue only Lets the KEDA scale rule read the queue length (see below)
migrator SQL Entra admin The SQL server Change the schema and create database users
app db_datareader + db_datawriter The ledgerly database Read and write rows. Not alter tables

💻 In Ledgerly: role assignments with guid() names

📄 sample/infra/modules/registry.bicep

var acrPullRoleId = '7f951dda-4ed3-4680-a7ca-43fe172d538d'
// ...
resource acrPull 'Microsoft.Authorization/roleAssignments@2022-04-01' = [for principalId in pullPrincipalIds: {
  name: guid(registry.id, principalId, acrPullRoleId)
  scope: registry
  properties: {
    roleDefinitionId: subscriptionResourceId('Microsoft.Authorization/roleDefinitions', acrPullRoleId)
    principalId: principalId
    principalType: 'ServicePrincipal'
  }
}]

Read it line by line:

  • Built-in role ids are fixed GUIDs. 7f951dda-... is AcrPull in every tenant. Using the id instead of the display name makes the template independent of language and renames.
  • The name must be a GUID, and stable. guid(registry.id, principalId, acrPullRoleId) hashes its inputs into a deterministic GUID. Same scope, principal and role give the same name, so a rerun finds the existing assignment instead of failing with "role assignment already exists". Use newGuid() here and every deployment tries to create a duplicate.
  • scope: registry puts the assignment on the registry, not the resource group. Least privilege applies to scope as much as to role.
  • principalType: 'ServicePrincipal' tells Azure what kind of principal to expect. A freshly created managed identity can take a moment to replicate in Entra ID, and stating the type avoids a lookup that might fail during that window.

Service Bus needs two roles per principal, so it builds the list with map and flatten:

📄 sample/infra/modules/servicebus.bicep

resource roles 'Microsoft.Authorization/roleAssignments@2022-04-01' = [for item in flatten(map(dataPrincipalIds, p => [
  { principalId: p, roleId: senderRoleId }
  { principalId: p, roleId: receiverRoleId }
])): {
  name: guid(namespace.id, item.principalId, item.roleId)
  scope: namespace
  // ...
}]

If you know LINQ, this is dataPrincipalIds.SelectMany(p => new[] { (p, sender), (p, receiver) }).

The same file has one more assignment, and it's there because of a scaling detail. The worker's KEDA scale rule (chapter 18) reads the imports queue's message count through the Service Bus management API, and KEDA's docs say that needs Manage rights. Sender and Receiver don't include Manage, so without another role the worker would quietly never scale out. The fix grants Data Owner, but scoped to the one queue rather than the namespace:

📄 sample/infra/modules/servicebus.bicep

// 🔧 KEDA reads the queue's message count through the management API, which needs Manage rights.
// Sender and Receiver don't include them, so grant Data Owner on this one queue only, not the namespace.
resource scalerRole 'Microsoft.Authorization/roleAssignments@2022-04-01' = {
  name: guid(imports.id, scalerPrincipalId, dataOwnerRoleId)
  scope: imports
  // ...
}

scope: imports works because a queue is a child resource with its own id, and Azure RBAC can scope a role to any resource. 🔧 Verify the scaler after your first deploy: if the worker stays at one replica while the queue grows, check the scale rule's events in the portal.

Jonas reviews the pull request: the API, worker and marketdata share the app identity, so the API could receive from the imports queue even though it never does. A per-app identity is stricter and costs a few more resources. They keep one identity for the beta and record it in an ADR. Least privilege is a dial; know where yours is set.

💡 A role assignment is code like any other: reviewed in a pull request, named deterministically, scoped as narrowly as the work allows.

🧠 Catch it before Azure does: lint, build and what-if

Bicep has a compiler and a linter, and you should treat their output exactly like C# compiler warnings: fix them, then make CI enforce it.

💻 In Ledgerly: infrastructure gets a CI job

📄 .github/workflows/ci.yml

  infra:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    timeout-minutes: 10
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v7
      - name: Lint and build Bicep
        run: |
          az bicep install
          az bicep lint --file infra/main.bicep
          az bicep build --file infra/main.bicep --stdout > /dev/null
          az bicep build-params --file infra/main.bicepparam --stdout > /dev/null

No Azure login is needed: the linter and compiler run offline. build-params checks the parameter file against the template's types.

Lesson 7: two mistakes Bicep caught first

This really happened while building the sample.

BCP138. The first version of the API's environment variables put the CORS loop directly inside concat():

// illustrative, not in the sample: the first attempt, rejected by bicep build with BCP138
env: concat(commonEnv, [ /* ... */ ], [for (origin, i) in corsOrigins: { name: 'Cors__AllowedOrigins__${i}', value: origin }])

bicep build refused it: a for expression can't be nested inside a function call in that position. The fix was to move the loop into a variable and pass the variable:

📄 sample/infra/modules/apps.bicep

var corsEnv = [for (origin, i) in corsOrigins: { name: 'Cors__AllowedOrigins__${i}', value: origin }]
// ...
          ], empty(cacheSecretUri) ? [] : [
            { name: 'ConnectionStrings__cache', secretRef: 'cache-connection' }
          ], corsEnv)

BCP037. The container apps were first declared with API version 2024-03-01, and the linter warned that identity was not a known property on the worker's KEDA scale rule. On that schema, the managed-identity setting for a custom scaler didn't exist, so Azure would likely have ignored it and the scaler would have had no way to authenticate. Moving to Microsoft.App/containerApps@2025-01-01 fixed it:

📄 sample/infra/modules/apps.bicep

            name: 'imports-queue'
            custom: {
              type: 'azure-servicebus'
              metadata: {
                namespace: serviceBusNamespace
                queueName: 'imports'
                messageCount: '20'
              }
              identity: identityId
            }

The lesson: a warning about an unknown property is not noise. It usually means "this setting will be silently dropped." Since then, lint is a CI job.

What-if: the diff before the deploy

Lint proves the template is valid. What-if shows what it would change in a real resource group: creates, modifies and deletes, without applying anything (what-if).

# 🧪 needs an Azure login and an existing resource group
az deployment group what-if -g <rg> -f infra/main.bicep -p infra/main.bicepparam

🧪 Not run by the author: this step was written from the docs and validated with bicep build only. Expect small differences.

Related options from the docs: az deployment group create ... --confirm-with-what-if (-c) shows the preview and asks before applying, and --no-pretty-print returns JSON, which a PR workflow can post as a comment. Ledgerly doesn't run what-if in CI yet, because that would need Azure credentials on pull requests. It's a good next step once the OIDC setup from chapter 12 exists, with a read-only identity.

💡 Lint answers "is this valid?", what-if answers "what will this change?". You want both before anything touches production.

🗺️ From edit to Azure

flowchart LR
    Edit["Edit a .bicep file"] --> Local["bicep lint<br/>bicep build"]
    Local --> PR["Pull request"]
    PR --> CI["CI infra job<br/>lint, build, build-params"]
    CI -- "fails" --> Edit
    CI -- "passes" --> Review["Review<br/>roles, SKUs, cost"]
    Review --> WhatIf["what-if<br/>optional, needs Azure"]
    WhatIf --> Merge["Merge to main"]
    Merge --> Deploy["deploy.yml<br/>three phases"]

🧠 The database user nobody has to click

There's one permission Bicep can't grant: a database user inside Azure SQL. Role assignments live in Azure Resource Manager. Database users live inside the database, and only a principal that can sign in to SQL can create them.

Ledgerly's design:

  1. The SQL server has Entra-only authentication, and its Entra admin is the migrator identity.
  2. The migration job runs as the migrator, so it can change the schema.
  3. After migrating, the same job creates a database user for the app identity and gives it read and write rights, nothing more.

📄 sample/infra/modules/sql.bicep

    administrators: {
      administratorType: 'ActiveDirectory'
      principalType: 'Application'
      login: adminIdentityName
      sid: adminIdentityClientId // 🔧 for a managed identity, the Learn Bicep sample uses the client id
      tenantId: subscription().tenantId
      azureADOnlyAuthentication: true
    }

Lesson 8: FROM EXTERNAL PROVIDER needs Graph, WITH SID doesn't

The documented way to create a user for an Entra principal is CREATE USER [name] FROM EXTERNAL PROVIDER. To resolve the name, the SQL server looks the principal up in Microsoft Entra ID, which requires the server itself to have permission to read the directory through Microsoft Graph. Granting that is a tenant-level step that many organizations reserve for administrators, and it would have been the one manual step in an otherwise automated setup.

The alternative: tell SQL the identity's id directly. CREATE USER [name] WITH SID = <id>, TYPE = E maps an external (Entra) principal without a directory lookup. For a managed identity, the SID is the client id as bytes.

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.MigrationService/Program.cs

    /// <summary>
    /// CREATE USER ... WITH SID, TYPE = E maps a managed identity (by its client id) without needing Microsoft Graph
    /// permissions on the SQL server. Idempotent, so it is safe on every deployment.
    /// </summary>
    private static async Task EnsureAppUserAsync(LedgerlyDbContext db, string name, Guid clientId, CancellationToken ct)
    {
        var user = name.Replace("]", "]]", StringComparison.Ordinal);
        var sid = "0x" + Convert.ToHexString(clientId.ToByteArray());
        var sql = $"""
            IF NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM sys.database_principals WHERE name = N'{user.Replace("'", "''", StringComparison.Ordinal)}')
                CREATE USER [{user}] WITH SID = {sid}, TYPE = E;
            ALTER ROLE db_datareader ADD MEMBER [{user}];
            ALTER ROLE db_datawriter ADD MEMBER [{user}];
            """;

#pragma warning disable EF1002 // values come from deployment configuration, are escaped above, and DDL cannot be parameterized
        await db.Database.ExecuteSqlRawAsync(sql, ct);
#pragma warning restore EF1002
    }
  • Guid.ToByteArray() produces the byte order SQL expects for the SID, and Convert.ToHexString turns it into a 0x... literal.
  • IF NOT EXISTS makes it idempotent, so it runs safely on every deployment.
  • DDL can't take parameters, so the name is escaped for both [...] and N'...', and the analyzer warning (EF1002) is suppressed with a reason, not blindly.
  • It only runs when Database:AppUser:Name and ClientId are configured. The migration job module sets them from the identity module's outputs, so locally (with Aspire and a SQL container) the step is simply skipped.

Elena asks why this matters for a PM. Tomás: "It means a new environment is git push plus one-time OIDC setup. No ticket to the identity team, no DBA."

💡 When the "last manual step" needs elevated rights, look for a design that removes the step rather than a person who can do it.

⚠️ Common traps

Trap What happens Do this instead
Fixing a production setting in the portal "just this once" The next deployment silently reverts it, or worse, staging and production drift Change the Bicep, open a PR, deploy. Use the portal to look, not to change
newGuid() or a random name for role assignments Every rerun tries to create a duplicate and fails guid(scope.id, principalId, roleId)
Roles at resource-group or subscription scope "to make it work" The identity can do far more than its job Assign at the resource (scope: registry) with the narrowest data role
Ignoring BCP037 "unknown property" warnings The property is dropped and the feature silently doesn't work Check the API version, upgrade it, and keep lint in CI
Deploying apps and schema changes in one step New code runs against the old schema for a few minutes Separate phases: platform, migration job, then apps
Secrets in .bicepparam They end up in git history forever Managed identities first, Key Vault for what's left, never secrets in parameter files
Granting the SQL server Graph permissions to create users A tenant-level permission for one CREATE USER CREATE USER ... WITH SID = <client id>, TYPE = E from the migrator

🧰 Helpers

A minimal module template (copy for each new resource type):

// illustrative, not in the sample: a starting point for a new module
param location string
param name string
param tags object

resource thing '<Provider/type>@<api-version>' = {
  name: '<prefix>-${name}'
  location: location
  tags: tags
  properties: {}
}

output id string = thing.id

Add role assignments by copying the acrPull loop from registry.bicep and changing the scope and role id.

PR review checklist for Bicep:

  • [ ] Every new resource has tags: tags and a name derived from name.
  • [ ] Every role assignment uses guid() and the narrowest scope.
  • [ ] No secret in parameters or outputs, and a 💰 note for any SKU change.

Make it permanent:

  • Keep the CI infra job required on pull requests (chapter 8's branch protection).
  • Add bicep lint to your pre-commit habits: cd sample && bicep lint infra/main.bicep.
  • Copy the toolkit's main.bicep pointer and module layout for new services (toolkit).
  • Once OIDC exists (chapter 12), add a what-if job on PRs that touch sample/infra/**, with a read-only identity.

🧪 Try it yourself (optional, ~30 min)

Steps 1 to 4 run locally without Azure.

  1. Install Bicep: az bicep install if you have the Azure CLI, otherwise the standalone bicep CLI.
  2. Build and lint: bash cd sample bicep lint infra/main.bicep bicep build infra/main.bicep --stdout > main.json bicep build-params infra/main.bicepparam --stdout Open main.json and find the role assignment names: they are guid(...) expressions that ARM evaluates at deploy time. Delete main.json when you're done.
  3. Reproduce lesson 7. In infra/modules/apps.bicep, temporarily replace corsEnv in the API's env with the loop itself: [for (origin, i) in corsOrigins: { name: 'Cors__AllowedOrigins__${i}', value: origin }]. Run bicep build infra/main.bicep and read the error. Then revert with git checkout -- infra/modules/apps.bicep.
  4. Add a tag. In main.bicep, add environment: 'production' to var tags, build again and confirm every resource in main.json picks it up. Revert.
  5. 🧪 With an Azure subscription and a resource group, run what-if: bash az deployment group what-if -g <rg> -f infra/main.bicep -p infra/main.bicepparam

    🧪 Not run by the author: this step was written from the docs and validated with bicep build only. Expect small differences.

On an empty group, everything shows as "create". Run phase 1 for real (chapter 12 does this in the pipeline), then run what-if again: it should show almost no changes. That's idempotency.

✅ Recap

  1. Bicep turns infrastructure into reviewed code: parameters and .bicepparam files per environment, modules for structure, conditions and loops for variation, and lint plus build in CI.
  2. Ledgerly's main.bicep deploys in three phases (platform, migration job, apps), and every identity gets the narrowest role at the narrowest scope, with guid() names so reruns are idempotent.
  3. The migration job creates the app's database user with CREATE USER ... WITH SID, TYPE = E, so no portal clicks and no Graph permissions are needed.

Next → Chapter 12: CD to Azure with OIDC

Chapter 12: CD to Azure with OIDC

⏱ About 40 min · You'll learn: how GitHub Actions signs in to Azure with OIDC and no stored secret, how to set up the federated credential and the deployer's roles, how Ledgerly's deploy.yml builds once and deploys in three phases with a migration job and a smoke test, and how to roll back and change schemas without downtime.

📖 Story: The first real deploy

The Bicep from chapter 11 is merged, the CI infra job is green and Elena has a date for the beta. Tomás asks the question that decides the whole design: "How does GitHub get into our Azure subscription?"

You know the answer from Nordbank Insurance. A service account with a password that never expires, pasted into the build server's credential store in 2017. Everyone knew it was there. Nobody knew who else had copied it. When the auditor asked, the answer was a long silence. In your Habits I brought with me notebook you write: "The deploy account has a password. Rotation is a project."

Jonas shakes his head before you finish describing it. "No password. No client secret. GitHub proves who it is with a token that lives for a few minutes, and Azure decides whether to trust it. If someone steals that token tomorrow, it's already useless." Maya pulls up deploy.yml. "And the deploy is a workflow anyone can read. If it isn't in the file, it doesn't happen in production."

🏚️ Old way → 🚀 New way

What you did before What you do now Why it changed
A deploy account with a long-lived password in the build server OIDC: a short-lived GitHub token exchanged for an Azure token through a federated credential Nothing long-lived to steal, rotate or leak
Right-click, Publish from Visual Studio, or Web Deploy to IIS A workflow that runs after CI passes on main, with an environment approval Every deploy is reviewed, logged and repeatable
Build on the build server, build again for production Build once per commit, tag images with the commit SHA, run that exact image everywhere What you tested is what you run
Run migration scripts by hand before or after the deploy A migration job that must succeed before the apps get the new image The order is code, and a failed migration stops the pipeline
"The deploy succeeded" means the copy finished A smoke test calls the new revision before the pipeline goes green Accepted by Azure isn't the same as working
Rollback means restoring last night's backup of the site folder Redeploy an earlier commit, or point traffic back at an earlier revision Images and revisions are immutable, so the old version still exists
Schema changes need a maintenance window Expand and contract migrations across releases Old and new code can both run against the schema during the switch

🧠 OIDC: signing in without a secret

OpenID Connect (OIDC) lets GitHub Actions prove its identity to Azure with a token that GitHub signs and Azure verifies. Nothing secret is stored in GitHub. The setup has two halves:

  1. In GitHub, the workflow asks for an ID token. That needs permissions: id-token: write.
  2. In Azure, an identity has a federated credential that says: "trust tokens from this issuer, for this audience, with exactly this subject." The identity can be an Entra app registration or a user-assigned managed identity (GitHub OIDC in Azure, Azure Login with OIDC).
Federated credential field Value What it pins down
Issuer https://token.actions.githubusercontent.com Only GitHub Actions tokens
Audience api://AzureADTokenExchange Tokens minted for Azure token exchange
Subject (sub) Must match the token's sub claim exactly Which repository, and which branch, tag or environment

The subject is where the security lives (OIDC reference):

Workflow context Subject format
Job with environment: production repo:ORG/REPO:environment:production
Branch repo:ORG/REPO:ref:refs/heads/main
Tag repo:ORG/REPO:ref:refs/tags/v1
Pull request repo:ORG/REPO:pull_request

If the job declares an environment:, the subject is the environment form, not the branch form. Ledgerly's deploy job uses environment: production, so its federated credential uses repo:<owner>/<repo>:environment:production. That's a strong pin: only jobs that pass the production environment's protection rules can sign in.

🔧 The subject format is changing for new repositories. Repositories created after 2026-07-15 get an immutable subject that includes the owner and repository ids, for example repo:OWNER@OWNER-ID/REPO@REPO-ID:ref:refs/heads/main. Older repositories keep the classic format unless they opt in, and a rename or transfer after that date switches a repository to the new format. A fork is a new repository. The environment form follows the same pattern for the prefix; check the docs and the actual claim before you create the credential, because a single character of difference means "no".

💻 In Ledgerly: permissions and the login step

📄 .github/workflows/deploy.yml

permissions:
  id-token: write # request the OIDC token for azure/login
  contents: read
# ...
      - name: Sign in to Azure (OIDC, no secret)
        uses: azure/login@v3
        with:
          client-id: ${{ vars.AZURE_CLIENT_ID }}
          tenant-id: ${{ vars.AZURE_TENANT_ID }}
          subscription-id: ${{ vars.AZURE_SUBSCRIPTION_ID }}

azure/login@v3 🔧 requests the GitHub token, sends it to Entra ID and gets back an Azure access token for the identity. Every az command after it runs as that identity. There is no creds input with a JSON secret: that's the legacy way.

Why these are variables, not secrets

Ledgerly stores four repository variables: AZURE_CLIENT_ID, AZURE_TENANT_ID, AZURE_SUBSCRIPTION_ID and AZURE_RESOURCE_GROUP. Many examples use secrets for the first three, and that works too. Ledgerly uses variables on purpose:

  • They're identifiers, not credentials. A client id without a matching federated credential is useless, in the same way a username without a password is. The trust lives in Azure, pinned to your repository and environment.
  • Variables can gate jobs. The workflow's if: checks vars.AZURE_CLIENT_ID != '', so the deploy switches itself off in forks and in your clone until you set it up. The secrets context can't be used that way in a job's if:.
  • Variables are readable in logs, which makes "which subscription did this deploy to?" a question with an answer.

What the deployer identity may do

The deployer creates resources and role assignments (chapter 11's guid() assignments), and pushes images. That needs three things on the resource group:

Role Why Note
Contributor Create and update every resource in main.bicep Can't create role assignments
Role Based Access Control Administrator (or User Access Administrator) Create the AcrPull, Key Vault Secrets User and Service Bus data role assignments Prefer the narrower RBAC Administrator role, ideally with a condition limiting which roles it can assign. Check the Azure RBAC docs
AcrPush az acr login plus pushing the images Contributor may not include registry data-plane push, depending on the registry's permission settings, so grant it explicitly

Scope all three to the resource group, never the subscription. The identity can then do anything inside rg-ledgerly-prod and nothing outside it.

💡 OIDC moves trust from "who knows the password" to "which repository, which environment": there's nothing in GitHub worth stealing.

🗺️ The token exchange

sequenceDiagram
    participant Job as "Deploy job in production"
    participant GH as "GitHub OIDC provider"
    participant Entra as "Microsoft Entra ID"
    participant ARM as "Azure Resource Manager"
    Job->>GH: Request an ID token for the Azure token exchange audience
    GH-->>Job: Signed JWT with the environment production subject
    Job->>Entra: Exchange the JWT, client id from repository vars
    Entra->>Entra: Match issuer, audience and subject on the federated credential
    Entra-->>Job: Short-lived Azure access token
    Job->>ARM: az deployment group create as the deployer identity
    ARM-->>Job: Allowed by the roles on the resource group

🧠 One-time setup

This is the only manual part, and you do it once per environment. It's written as Azure CLI commands so you can keep it in your repo as a script. You could also let azd pipeline config create the credential and variables for you 🔧 (azd pipelines).

🧪 Not run by the author: this step was written from the docs and validated with bicep build only. Expect small differences.

# illustrative, not in the sample: one-time setup for the production environment
# Prerequisites: az login (as someone who can assign roles), gh auth login, and the repo cloned.
RG=rg-ledgerly-prod
LOCATION=westeurope            # pick your region (chapter 10)
REPO=<owner>/<repo>            # check the real sub claim format (classic or immutable)

az group create --name "$RG" --location "$LOCATION"

# A user-assigned managed identity for the pipeline (not the app identities from Bicep).
az identity create --name id-github-deployer --resource-group "$RG"
CLIENT_ID=$(az identity show --name id-github-deployer --resource-group "$RG" --query clientId -o tsv)
PRINCIPAL_ID=$(az identity show --name id-github-deployer --resource-group "$RG" --query principalId -o tsv)

# Trust GitHub tokens from the production environment of this repository only.
az identity federated-credential create \
  --name github-production \
  --identity-name id-github-deployer \
  --resource-group "$RG" \
  --issuer https://token.actions.githubusercontent.com \
  --subject "repo:$REPO:environment:production" \
  --audiences api://AzureADTokenExchange

# Roles on the resource group only.
RG_ID=$(az group show --name "$RG" --query id -o tsv)
for role in "Contributor" "Role Based Access Control Administrator" "AcrPush"; do
  az role assignment create \
    --assignee-object-id "$PRINCIPAL_ID" \
    --assignee-principal-type ServicePrincipal \
    --role "$role" \
    --scope "$RG_ID"
done

# Identifiers, not secrets: store them as repository variables.
gh variable set AZURE_CLIENT_ID --body "$CLIENT_ID"
gh variable set AZURE_TENANT_ID --body "$(az account show --query tenantId -o tsv)"
gh variable set AZURE_SUBSCRIPTION_ID --body "$(az account show --query id -o tsv)"
gh variable set AZURE_RESOURCE_GROUP --body "$RG"

Then, in GitHub:

  1. Create the production environment (Settings → Environments). Add required reviewers (up to 6 people or teams, one approval is enough), turn on prevent self-review, and restrict deployment branches to main (environments). 🔧 On Free, Pro and Team plans, required reviewers and wait timers only work for public repositories.
  2. Edit sample/infra/main.bicepparam: replace <your-tenant-id> in jwtAuthority and set jwtAudience to your API's app registration (chapter 13).
  3. Push to main. Watch CI, then the deploy waits for approval.

One caution on the setup itself: the deployer identity lives in the resource group it deploys to. Don't delete that group to "start fresh" unless you're ready to repeat this section. Teams with several environments usually keep pipeline identities in a separate resource group.

💡 Keep the one-time setup as a script in the repo: "manual" should mean "run once", not "remember how".

🧠 The pipeline, step by step

Trigger, gate and concurrency

📄 .github/workflows/deploy.yml

on:
  workflow_run:
    workflows: [ci]
    types: [completed]
    branches: [main]
  workflow_dispatch:
# ...
# Never run two deployments at once, and never cancel one halfway.
concurrency:
  group: deploy-production
  cancel-in-progress: false

env:
  # ...
  RESOURCE_GROUP: ${{ vars.AZURE_RESOURCE_GROUP }}
  # The exact commit CI tested. Every image is tagged with it, so a revision maps back to a commit.
  SHA: ${{ github.event.workflow_run.head_sha || github.sha }}

jobs:
  deploy:
    # workflow_run also fires for pull requests (even from forks whose branch is named main), so only
    # deploy CI runs that came from a push to this repository, never someone else's code.
    if: >
      vars.AZURE_CLIENT_ID != '' &&
      (github.event_name == 'workflow_dispatch' ||
        (github.event.workflow_run.conclusion == 'success' &&
         github.event.workflow_run.event == 'push' &&
         github.event.workflow_run.head_repository.full_name == github.repository))
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    timeout-minutes: 45
    environment:
      name: production # protection rules (reviewers, branch filter) live on the environment
      url: ${{ steps.apps.outputs.apiUrl }}
