  • workflow_run starts this workflow when the ci workflow completes for main. "Completed" includes failures, so the job's if: also requires conclusion == 'success'. workflow_dispatch adds a manual "Run workflow" button.
  • The two extra checks in if: close a hole Jonas found in review. branches: [main] matches the head branch name, and CI also runs on pull requests, so a pull request from a fork whose branch happens to be called main would complete CI and trigger this workflow. Requiring event == 'push' and the same repository means only code that was actually pushed to this repository's main can reach Azure.
  • SHA is the commit CI actually tested (head_sha), not whatever main points to by the time this runs. The checkout step uses ref: ${{ env.SHA }}.
  • concurrency with cancel-in-progress: false: the docs' example cancels superseded runs (concurrency). For a deploy that runs migrations, cancelling halfway is worse than waiting, so a second deploy queues instead.
  • environment: production pauses the job until a reviewer approves, makes the OIDC subject the environment form and shows the API URL on the deployment.

Three phases, built once

Step What it runs Why
Phase 1: platform az deployment group create --name "platform-$SHA" with only the parameter file Creates or confirms the registry, data, messaging and environment. Its outputs (registry name, login server, job name) feed the next steps
Build and push az acr login, then dotnet publish ... /t:PublishContainer -p:ContainerImageTag="$SHA" for four projects No Dockerfile (chapter 9). Each image is tagged with the commit
Phase 2: migration job Same template, imageTag="$SHA" deployApps=false Points job-migrations at the new migrations image
Run migrations and wait Start the job, poll its status The apps must not change until the schema is ready
Phase 3: apps Same template, imageTag="$SHA" deployApps=true New revisions of api, worker and marketdata on the same SHA
Smoke test curl -fsS "$apiUrl/alive" until it answers Proves the new revision runs

📄 .github/workflows/deploy.yml

      - name: Build and push images (no Dockerfile)
        env:
          LOGIN_SERVER: ${{ steps.platform.outputs.loginServer }}
        run: |
          az acr login --name "${{ steps.platform.outputs.registry }}"
          for project in Ledgerly.Api Ledgerly.Worker Ledgerly.MarketData Ledgerly.MigrationService; do
            dotnet publish "src/$project" -c Release /t:PublishContainer \
              -p:ContainerRegistry="$LOGIN_SERVER" -p:ContainerImageTag="$SHA"
          done

Build once, promote the tag. CI builds the images too, but tags them ci-<sha> and never pushes them: it proves they build. The deploy builds once per commit and pushes once. After that, the migration job and all three apps use the same tag, and nothing is rebuilt. When you add a staging environment, keep that rule: build and push in the first environment, then deploy the existing tag to the next one. A revision's image tag always maps back to one commit.

The migration gate

📄 .github/workflows/deploy.yml

      - name: Run migrations and wait
        env:
          JOB: ${{ steps.platform.outputs.job }}
        run: |
          execution=$(az containerapp job start -n "$JOB" -g "$RESOURCE_GROUP" --query name -o tsv)
          echo "Started $execution"
          for i in $(seq 1 60); do
            status=$(az containerapp job execution show -n "$JOB" -g "$RESOURCE_GROUP" \
              --job-execution-name "$execution" --query properties.status -o tsv)
            echo "[$i] $status"
            case "$status" in
              Succeeded) exit 0 ;;
              Failed|Stopped|Degraded) echo "::error::Migration $execution ended as $status. See its logs in Log Analytics."; exit 1 ;;
            esac
            sleep 10
          done
          echo "::error::Migration did not finish within 10 minutes"; exit 1

az containerapp job start returns immediately with an execution name, so the step polls: up to 60 times, every 10 seconds. The job itself (chapter 11) has replicaTimeout: 600 and one retry. The migration service sets Environment.ExitCode = 1 on any failure, so a failed migration becomes a failed execution, the step exits 1 and phase 3 never runs. The old apps keep serving on the old, untouched schema.

The smoke test

📄 .github/workflows/deploy.yml

      # The deployment "succeeding" only means Azure accepted it. This proves the new revision answers.
      - name: Smoke test
        run: |
          for i in $(seq 1 30); do
            if curl -fsS "${{ steps.apps.outputs.apiUrl }}/alive"; then echo; echo "API is up"; exit 0; fi
            sleep 10
          done
          echo "::error::API did not become healthy"; exit 1

With apiMinReplicas = 0, the first call may hit a cold start, so the loop allows up to five minutes. /alive is mapped in every environment by the service defaults (unlike the Aspire template's development-only default), because the Container Apps probes need it too.

💡 A pipeline is a sequence of gates: tests, approval, migrations, smoke test. Each one is a place where a bad change stops before users see it.

🗺️ From merge to running revision

flowchart TB
    Merge["Merge to main"] --> CI["ci workflow<br/>build, test, containers, infra lint"]
    CI -- "success" --> Gate{"AZURE_CLIENT_ID set<br/>and CI succeeded?"}
    Gate -- "no" --> Skip["Job skipped"]
    Gate -- "yes" --> Approve["Environment production<br/>reviewer approves"]
    Approve --> Login["azure/login<br/>OIDC"]
    Login --> P1["Phase 1<br/>platform"]
    P1 --> Build["Build and push<br/>tag = commit SHA"]
    Build --> P2["Phase 2<br/>migration job image"]
    P2 --> Run{"Migration job<br/>succeeded?"}
    Run -- "no" --> Stop["Fail, old apps keep running"]
    Run -- "yes" --> P3["Phase 3<br/>new app revisions"]
    P3 --> Smoke["Smoke test /alive"]

🧠 Revisions, rollback and zero-downtime schema changes

Revisions in single mode

Every change to an app's template (a new image, an environment variable, a scale rule) creates a new revision, an immutable snapshot (revisions). Ledgerly's apps use activeRevisionsMode: 'Single', the default: the new revision replaces the old one with zero downtime, once its startup and readiness probes pass. If the new revision never becomes ready, it doesn't get traffic, and the smoke test fails the pipeline.

Multiple mode keeps several revisions active and lets you split traffic by percentage, for canary or blue/green releases, with labels that give a revision a stable URL for testing (traffic splitting). Ledgerly starts with single mode because it's simpler; Priya has multiple mode on her list for after the beta.

Rollback

Situation What to do
The deploy failed before phase 3 Nothing. The old revisions are still serving
A bad change is live, and you have time Roll forward: revert the commit on main. CI and deploy ship the revert like any change
A bad change is live, and you need it gone now Re-run the earlier deploy run in the Actions tab. It reuses that run's head_sha, so it redeploys the previous images. Migrations have nothing to apply, because the schema is already ahead
You use multiple revision mode Shift traffic back to the previous revision

Break glass, when even a pipeline run is too slow:

# illustrative, not in the sample: point the API back at the previous image (🧪 check the az containerapp docs)
az containerapp update -n api -g rg-ledgerly-prod --image <loginServer>/ledgerly-api:<previous-sha>

That creates a new revision outside the pipeline. The next deploy will overwrite it, so follow up with a revert on main.

The rollback rows have one hidden requirement: the previous code must still work against the current schema. That's what expand and contract guarantees.

Expand and contract

Suppose you rename Portfolio.Name to Portfolio.DisplayName. A single migration that renames the column breaks the old revision for the seconds (or minutes) between phase 2 and phase 3, and it makes rollback impossible. Split it across releases:

Release Migration (phase 2) Code (phase 3) Old code still works?
1. Expand Add DisplayName, nullable. Copy existing values Write both columns, read DisplayName with a fallback ✅ It ignores the new column
2. Switch Make DisplayName required Read and write only DisplayName ✅ Release 1 code still writes both
3. Contract Drop Name No change ✅ Release 2 code doesn't use Name

Each release is safe to roll back by one step. It's slower than one big migration, and that's the price of no maintenance window.

💡 Rollback is only real if the previous version can run against today's schema: design migrations so it always can.

🗺️ Expand and contract as states

stateDiagram-v2
    [*] --> OldOnly: schema has Name
    OldOnly --> Expanded: release 1 adds DisplayName
    Expanded --> Switched: release 2 code uses DisplayName only
    Switched --> Contracted: release 3 drops Name
    Contracted --> [*]
    Expanded --> OldOnly: rollback is safe, Name still exists
    Switched --> Expanded: rollback is safe, both columns exist

⚠️ Common traps

Trap What happens Do this instead
A client secret in AZURE_CREDENTIALS "because the tutorial did it" A long-lived secret that can leak and must be rotated OIDC with a federated credential, no secret at all
Federated subject ...:ref:refs/heads/main while the job uses environment: Login fails: the token's subject is the environment form Match the subject to the job, and check the real claim (classic or immutable 🔧)
Deployer with Owner on the subscription A compromised workflow can do anything, anywhere Contributor + RBAC Administrator + AcrPush, on the resource group only
cancel-in-progress: true on a deploy with migrations A newer push cancels a run halfway through a migration cancel-in-progress: false: queue, never cancel
A workflow_run gate that only checks conclusion branches: [main] matches the head branch name, so a pull request from a fork whose branch is called main can also trigger it after CI Also require github.event.workflow_run.event == 'push' and github.event.workflow_run.head_repository.full_name == github.repository
Renaming or dropping a column in the same release as the code change Old revision errors during the switch, and rollback breaks Expand and contract across releases
"Deploy succeeded" as the definition of done A revision that crashes on startup ships green A smoke test against the real URL

🧰 Helpers

Hardened gate for any workflow_run deploy (the same condition the sample uses, ready to paste into your own workflows):

    if: >
      vars.AZURE_CLIENT_ID != '' &&
      (github.event_name == 'workflow_dispatch' ||
       (github.event.workflow_run.conclusion == 'success' &&
        github.event.workflow_run.event == 'push' &&
        github.event.workflow_run.head_repository.full_name == github.repository))

Pre-flight checklist for the first deploy:

  • [ ] The real sub claim matches the federated credential (environment form, classic or immutable).
  • [ ] Repository variables set: AZURE_CLIENT_ID, AZURE_TENANT_ID, AZURE_SUBSCRIPTION_ID, AZURE_RESOURCE_GROUP.
  • [ ] The production environment has reviewers and a main-only branch rule.
  • [ ] jwtAuthority and jwtAudience in main.bicepparam are real values.
  • [ ] A budget exists on the resource group (chapter 10).

Make it permanent:

  • Commit the one-time setup script (as docs/setup-production.sh or similar) so the next environment is a copy and a rename.
  • Copy deploy.yml from the toolkit and adjust paths, projects and resource names.
  • Add "Is this migration expand-only?" to your PR template for any change under Data/Migrations.
  • Practice a rollback once, on a quiet day, before you need it.

🧪 Try it yourself (optional, ~45 min)

  1. Locally, no Azure: read .github/workflows/deploy.yml and trace one commit SHA through it: where is it set, which steps use it, and which image tags and deployment names it ends up in? Then check that bicep build-params infra/main.bicepparam still passes from cd sample.
  2. Locally: run the migration service against your Aspire SQL with aspire run and find its "Database migrated" log line in the dashboard. That line is what the job execution logs show in Azure.
  3. 🧪 With Azure: run the one-time setup script for your fork, create the production environment and push a commit to main. Approve the deployment and watch the three phases.

    🧪 Not run by the author: this step was written from the docs and validated with bicep build only. Expect small differences.

  4. 🧪 With Azure: make a visible change (for example, a new field in an endpoint response), deploy it, then roll back by re-running the previous deploy run. Confirm with az containerapp revision list -n api -g <rg> -o table that a new revision appeared.

✅ Recap

  1. OIDC replaces deploy secrets: GitHub issues a short-lived token, and Azure trusts it through a federated credential pinned to issuer, audience and an exact subject (the environment form for Ledgerly, and the immutable format for new repos 🔧).
  2. The deployer needs Contributor, a role-assignment role and AcrPush on the resource group only, and its identifiers live in repository variables because they aren't secrets.
  3. deploy.yml builds once per SHA and deploys in three gated phases (platform, migration job, apps), then smoke-tests, while revisions plus expand-and-contract migrations make rollback safe.

Next → Chapter 13: Identity and secrets

Chapter 13: Identity and secrets

⏱ About 40 min · You'll learn: how Ledgerly reaches SQL, Service Bus, Key Vault and the registry with managed identities instead of passwords, when to use DefaultAzureCredential and when to use ManagedIdentityCredential, how the API authenticates users with JWT bearer tokens and keeps each user's data private, and which few secrets remain and where they live.

📖 Story: The connection string in Slack

It's Tuesday morning. Jonas, the security engineer, sends you a screenshot without a greeting. It's a message from the team's Slack channel, eight weeks old, from the time before you joined. Someone was debugging a prototype and pasted a full connection string: Endpoint=sb://...;SharedAccessKeyName=RootManageSharedAccessKey;SharedAccessKey=....

"Is that ours?" he asks.

You check. It belongs to a prototype namespace that nobody deleted. The key still works. Jonas rotates it, deletes the namespace, and then asks the question that matters: "Could the same thing happen with Ledgerly?"

You open servicebus.bicep together. One line answers him: disableLocalAuth: true. Ledgerly's namespace doesn't accept keys at all. Even if someone pasted the root key in a channel, it would be rejected. The worker connects with a managed identity that has two data roles, and there's nothing to paste.

"Good," Jonas says. "Now show me everything else."

That becomes the afternoon. Maya joins, and the three of you walk through every arrow in the architecture. For each one, Jonas asks the same two questions: "Who is calling?" and "What would an attacker need to steal to pretend to be them?"

In your notebook, under Habits I brought with me, you write: "Encrypt the connection strings section of web.config." Then you cross out the word "encrypt" and write "delete".

🏚️ Old way → 🚀 New way

What you did before What you do now Why it changed
SQL logins with passwords in web.config, sometimes encrypted with aspnet_regiis Authentication=Active Directory Managed Identity and Entra-only auth on the server There's no password to leak, rotate or forget in a backup
One shared domain service account for every app User-assigned managed identities with narrow data-plane roles Each workload gets only the access it needs, and you can see it in Bicep
Service Bus or storage keys in config files Namespace name only, local (key) auth disabled A leaked key is worthless when the service refuses keys
Secrets typed into release variables by an admin Key Vault references resolved by the platform with the app's identity The value never appears in the pipeline, the logs or the template outputs
Windows auth or forms-auth cookies JWT bearer tokens from Microsoft Entra ID, dotnet user-jwts locally The API is stateless and serves a SPA and other clients the same way
A security review the week before release Dependabot, CodeQL and push protection on every pull request Problems surface while the change is small and the author still remembers it

🧠 Managed identity everywhere

A managed identity is a Microsoft Entra ID identity for a workload. Azure creates and stores its credentials, and the code asks the platform for a token when it needs one. You never see a password, so you can't paste it anywhere.

Container Apps supports two kinds (facts from the Container Apps managed identity docs):

  • System-assigned: one per app, deleted with the app.
  • User-assigned: a standalone resource that several apps can share.

Ledgerly uses two user-assigned identities, created before the apps exist so that role assignments and the SQL admin can refer to them (chapter 11 explains why the order matters).

💻 In Ledgerly: two identities, two jobs

📄 sample/infra/modules/identity.bicep

// Two user-assigned managed identities. No passwords or connection-string secrets anywhere:
//  - "app": what api, worker and marketdata run as (least privilege: read/write data, send/receive messages).
//  - "migrator": what the migration job runs as. It is the SQL Entra admin, so it can change the schema.
// ...
resource app 'Microsoft.ManagedIdentity/userAssignedIdentities@2023-01-31' = {
  name: 'id-${name}-app'
  location: location
  tags: tags
}

resource migrator 'Microsoft.ManagedIdentity/userAssignedIdentities@2023-01-31' = {
  name: 'id-${name}-migrator'
  location: location
  tags: tags
}

Splitting the identities is the important design choice. The apps can read and write rows, but they can't drop a table. Only the short-lived migration job can change the schema.

Now follow each arrow Jonas asked about:

Arrow How the app proves who it is Permission it gets Where it's set
Container Apps → Container Registry registries entry with the identity, admin user disabled AcrPull registry.bicep, apps.bicep
api, worker → Azure SQL Authentication=Active Directory Managed Identity;User Id=<client id> db_datareader and db_datawriter apps.bicep, MigrationService
migration job → Azure SQL Same keyword, the migrator's client id Entra admin of the server sql.bicep
worker → Service Bus Namespace FQDN, no key Azure Service Bus Data Sender and Data Receiver servicebus.bicep
KEDA scaler → Service Bus identity: identityId on the scale rule Reads the queue length (see the traps) apps.bicep
api → Key Vault Key Vault reference with the identity Key Vault Secrets User keyvault.bicep

💻 In Ledgerly: SQL without a password

📄 sample/infra/modules/apps.bicep

var sqlConnection = 'Server=tcp:${sqlServerFqdn},1433;Database=${databaseName};Authentication=Active Directory Managed Identity;User Id=${identityClientId};Encrypt=True;Connect Timeout=60'

var commonEnv = [
  { name: 'AZURE_CLIENT_ID', value: identityClientId } // makes Azure SDK credentials pick the user-assigned identity
  { name: 'APPLICATIONINSIGHTS_CONNECTION_STRING', value: appInsightsConnectionString }
]

Read the connection string closely. There's a server, a database, an authentication mode and a User Id that is the identity's client id, not a user name. Microsoft.Data.SqlClient asks the platform for a token for that identity and sends it to SQL. The SqlClient Entra authentication docs describe the options:

  • Authentication=Active Directory Default walks the whole DefaultAzureCredential chain. It's convenient on a laptop, but the docs say it can be slow and isn't recommended for production.
  • Authentication=Active Directory Managed Identity with User Id=<client id> is the production form for a user-assigned identity. That's what Ledgerly uses.

On the server side, sql.bicep sets azureADOnlyAuthentication: true, which disables SQL authentication entirely (Entra-only authentication). The database user for the app identity is created by the migration service with CREATE USER ... WITH SID = ..., TYPE = E, so the SQL server doesn't need Microsoft Graph permissions to look the identity up. Chapter 11 tells the story of how the team found that out.

💻 In Ledgerly: Service Bus without a key

📄 sample/infra/modules/servicebus.bicep

// Service Bus Standard (needed for duplicate detection). Local (SAS key) auth is disabled:
// only Entra identities with a data role can send or receive.
// ...
  properties: {
    disableLocalAuth: true
    minimumTlsVersion: '1.2'
  }

📄 sample/infra/modules/apps.bicep

            { name: 'ConnectionStrings__messaging', value: serviceBusFqns } // a namespace, not a key: Entra auth
// ...
            custom: {
              type: 'azure-servicebus'
              metadata: {
                namespace: serviceBusNamespace
                queueName: 'imports'
                messageCount: '20'
              }
              identity: identityId
            }

The worker's code doesn't change between your laptop and Azure: it calls builder.AddAzureServiceBusClient("messaging"). Locally, Aspire hands it an emulator connection string. In Azure, it gets only the namespace name, and the client authenticates with a token credential. The KEDA scale rule uses the same identity, so the scaler doesn't need a key either.

The registry follows the same pattern: adminUserEnabled: false in registry.bicep, and var registries = [ { server: registryServer, identity: identityId } ] in apps.bicep. Container Apps pulls images with the identity and the AcrPull role, so no registry password exists.

💡 For every arrow in your architecture, ask "what would an attacker need to steal?" With managed identities the answer becomes "control of the running workload", which is a much higher bar than "one line from a Slack message".

🗺️ Who can reach what

flowchart LR
    API["api"] -- "runs as" --> APPID["app identity<br/>user-assigned"]
    WORKER["worker"] -- "runs as" --> APPID
    MD["marketdata"] -- "runs as" --> APPID
    JOB["migrations job"] -- "runs as" --> MIGID["migrator identity<br/>user-assigned"]
    APPID -- "AcrPull" --> ACR["Container Registry<br/>admin user off"]
    MIGID -- "AcrPull" --> ACR
    APPID -- "Data Sender and Receiver" --> SB["Service Bus<br/>local auth off"]
    APPID -- "Key Vault Secrets User" --> KV["Key Vault<br/>RBAC mode"]
    APPID -- "db_datareader and db_datawriter" --> SQL["Azure SQL<br/>Entra-only auth"]
    MIGID -- "Entra admin, changes schema" --> SQL

Every arrow on the right is a role assignment or a database role in the Bicep or the migration service. If it isn't in the code, the access doesn't exist.

🧠 DefaultAzureCredential or ManagedIdentityCredential?

When Azure SDK clients need a token, they take a TokenCredential. The one you see in every quick start is DefaultAzureCredential. It tries a chain of credentials in order and uses the first that works. In Azure.Identity 1.21.0 🔧 the order is (credential chains):

  1. Environment
  2. Workload Identity
  3. Managed Identity
  4. Visual Studio
  5. Visual Studio Code
  6. Azure CLI
  7. Azure PowerShell
  8. Azure Developer CLI
  9. Interactive browser (excluded by default)
  10. Broker

That chain is why the same code works on your laptop (Visual Studio or Azure CLI sign-in) and in Azure (managed identity). It's also why it can surprise you. The SDK best practices are direct about it:

  • Use deterministic credentials in production. Replace DefaultAzureCredential with a specific credential such as ManagedIdentityCredential, and pass the client id for a user-assigned identity.
  • Or compose a ChainedTokenCredential, for example ManagedIdentityCredential and then AzureCliCredential, for a production-safe chain with a developer fallback.
  • The reasons: probing adds latency and unpredictable behavior, and environment-dependent fallbacks are hard to debug.
  • Reuse credential instances. Tokens are cached per instance, so creating a new credential per request throws the cache away.
  • 🔧 The environment variable AZURE_TOKEN_CREDENTIALS narrows the default chain: prod keeps Environment, Workload Identity and Managed Identity, dev keeps the developer tools, or you can name one credential.

💻 In Ledgerly: no credential code at all

Ledgerly has no new DefaultAzureCredential() anywhere in its application code. Two things make that possible:

  • SQL picks its credential from the connection string keyword. Active Directory Managed Identity with a User Id is already deterministic.
  • Service Bus goes through the Aspire client integration, which builds a credential for you when the connection string is just a namespace. Check the Aspire Service Bus integration docs for the current default and for the settings that let you supply your own credential.

The AZURE_CLIENT_ID environment variable in commonEnv ties it together. A container app can have several user-assigned identities, and the variable tells Azure SDK credentials which one to use. Forget it, and authentication fails in Azure even though everything worked locally.

If you write your own SDK client registration, follow the best-practice pattern:

// illustrative, not in the sample
TokenCredential credential = builder.Environment.IsDevelopment()
    ? new DefaultAzureCredential()
    : new ManagedIdentityCredential(ManagedIdentityId.FromUserAssignedClientId(builder.Configuration["AZURE_CLIENT_ID"]));

builder.Services.AddSingleton(credential); // one instance, so its token cache is shared

🔧 The ManagedIdentityId factory is in recent Azure.Identity versions; check the API reference for the version you use.

💡 DefaultAzureCredential is a great developer experience and a poor production contract. In Azure, say exactly which identity you mean.

🧠 Users: JWT bearer and per-user data

Managed identities answer "which workload is calling Azure?". Users are a different question: "which person is calling the API, and which portfolios are theirs?"

The API uses JWT bearer authentication. A client gets an access token from an identity provider and sends it in the Authorization header. The API checks the signature, issuer, audience and lifetime, and then trusts the claims inside. No session, no cookie, no server-side state.

💻 In Ledgerly: two lines and a config section

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Api/Program.cs

// ── Security: JWT bearer. Settings come from "Authentication:Schemes:Bearer" ───────────────────
// Locally: `dotnet user-jwts create`. In Azure: Authority = your Entra ID tenant, audience = the API app.
builder.Services.AddAuthentication().AddJwtBearer();
builder.Services.AddAuthorization();

AddJwtBearer() with no arguments reads its settings from the Authentication:Schemes:Bearer configuration section (configure JWT bearer authentication). That's what lets the same code accept two kinds of tokens:

  • Locally, dotnet user-jwts create --project src/Ledgerly.Api issues a development token. The tool keeps its signing key in your user secrets and writes the issuer and audience it needs into the development configuration. --name sets the user name, which is handy for testing two users.
  • In Azure, Bicep sets the authority and audience as environment variables:

📄 sample/infra/modules/apps.bicep

            { name: 'Authentication__Schemes__Bearer__Authority', value: jwtAuthority }
            { name: 'Authentication__Schemes__Bearer__ValidAudiences__0', value: jwtAudience }

The authority is your Entra ID tenant, and the audience is the API's app registration. The handler downloads the tenant's signing keys from the authority's metadata, so you never configure a key.

Microsoft.Identity.Web (4.16.1 🔧) is the other option. AddMicrosoftIdentityWebApi reads an AzureAd section and adds Entra-specific validation and helpers (protected web API configuration). Delegated scopes arrive in the scp claim and app roles in the roles claim (scopes and app roles). Ledgerly keeps plain AddJwtBearer so local and Entra tokens share one code path. When Leo builds the React SPA, it signs users in with MSAL.js and the authorization code flow with PKCE; the implicit flow isn't supported.

💻 In Ledgerly: a stable user id

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Api/ApiConventions.cs

    /// <summary>
    /// Stable user id. Entra ID puts the object id in "oid"; dev tokens from `dotnet user-jwts` use "sub"
    /// (mapped to NameIdentifier by the JWT handler).
    /// </summary>
    public static string GetOwnerId(this ClaimsPrincipal user) =>
        user.FindFirstValue("oid")
        ?? user.FindFirstValue("http://schemas.microsoft.com/identity/claims/objectidentifier")
        ?? user.FindFirstValue(ClaimTypes.NameIdentifier)
        ?? user.FindFirstValue("sub")
        ?? throw new InvalidOperationException("Authenticated user has no id claim.");

Each portfolio stores this id as OwnerId. Not the email, not the display name: those change, and an id that changes orphans data. The fallbacks exist because the JWT handler can map claim names, so oid may arrive under its long URI form and sub as NameIdentifier.

💻 In Ledgerly: authorization is a WHERE clause

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Api/Endpoints/PortfolioEndpoints.cs

        var group = app.MapGroup("/api/portfolios")
            .WithTags("Portfolios")
            .RequireAuthorization();
// ...
        var ownerId = user.GetOwnerId();
        var portfolio = await db.Portfolios.AsNoTracking()
            .Include(p => p.Transactions)
            .SingleOrDefaultAsync(p => p.Id == id && p.OwnerId == ownerId, ct);

        if (portfolio is null)
        {
            return TypedResults.NotFound();
        }

RequireAuthorization() on the group means "you must be signed in", and an anonymous call gets 401. The second check is per row: the query filters on both the id and the owner. If you ask for someone else's portfolio, the database returns nothing, and you get 404, not 403. A 403 would confirm that the id exists. Jonas calls this "don't answer questions nobody should be asking".

💡 Authentication is configuration. Authorization is code, and in a multi-user API most of it is a WHERE OwnerId = @me that you never forget.

🗺️ One request, two kinds of identity

sequenceDiagram
    participant SPA as "React SPA"
    participant Entra as "Microsoft Entra ID"
    participant Api as "Ledgerly.Api"
    participant Sql as "Azure SQL"
    SPA->>Entra: Sign in with authorization code and PKCE
    Entra-->>SPA: Access token for the API audience
    SPA->>Api: GET /api/portfolios/id with the bearer token
    Api->>Api: Validate signature, issuer, audience and lifetime
    Api->>Api: GetOwnerId reads the oid claim
    Api->>Entra: Token request as the app managed identity
    Entra-->>Api: Token for Azure SQL
    Api->>Sql: Select where Id and OwnerId match
    Sql-->>Api: No row, the portfolio belongs to someone else
    Api-->>SPA: 404 Not Found

Two identities take part in every request: the user, proven by the bearer token, and the workload, proven by the managed identity. The user never talks to SQL, and the API never sees the user's password.

🧠 The secrets that remain: Key Vault, CORS and Data Protection

After the identity work, Jonas asks for the list of real secrets in Ledgerly. It's short:

  • The Redis access key, only when enableRedis is on. Redis is optional in Ledgerly (chapter 10).
  • Nothing else that grants access. The Application Insights connection string is passed as a plain environment variable in the sample; it tells the SDK where to send telemetry.

💻 In Ledgerly: a Key Vault reference

The Redis connection string is built inside Bicep, written straight into Key Vault and never returned as an output. The API gets it through a Key Vault reference:

📄 sample/infra/modules/apps.bicep

      // A Key Vault reference: Container Apps fetches the value with the managed identity.
      secrets: empty(cacheSecretUri) ? [] : [
        { name: 'cache-connection', keyVaultUrl: cacheSecretUri, identity: identityId }
      ]
// ...
            { name: 'ConnectionStrings__cache', secretRef: 'cache-connection' }

Container Apps reads the secret with the app identity, which needs Key Vault Secrets User (Container Apps secrets). The value never passes through the GitHub workflow or the deployment outputs.

📄 sample/infra/modules/keyvault.bicep

// Key Vault in RBAC mode (access policies are legacy). Apps read secrets via Key Vault references
// in Container Apps, so the secret value never appears in the pipeline or in app settings.
// ...
    enableRbacAuthorization: true
    enableSoftDelete: true
    softDeleteRetentionInDays: 7

Key Vault facts worth knowing (Key Vault RBAC guide):

  • Azure RBAC is the recommended permission model; vault access policies are legacy. 🔧 Starting with ARM API version 2026-02-01, new vaults default to RBAC.
  • The data-plane roles are Key Vault Secrets User (read), Key Vault Secrets Officer (manage) and Key Vault Administrator. Apps get Secrets User, at vault scope or narrower.
  • If an app reads Key Vault directly, use Azure.Extensions.AspNetCore.Configuration.Secrets (1.5.2 🔧) with AddAzureKeyVault; ConnectionStrings--Db becomes ConnectionStrings:Db (Key Vault configuration provider). Ledgerly doesn't need it.

The header of redis.bicep admits the gap: "Entra auth is the stronger option, see chapter 13." Azure Managed Redis supports Microsoft Entra ID authentication (Azure Managed Redis overview), and Aspire's Aspire.Microsoft.Azure.StackExchangeRedis package is the client side of it. With that in place you could turn off accessKeysAuthentication and delete the last secret. Redis is optional, so the team keeps the key for now, and Jonas puts the switch on the backlog with a date.

CORS for the SPA

Chapter 4 added the CORS policy. Here's how it reaches production: the allowed origins come from corsOrigins in main.bicepparam, and Bicep turns them into indexed environment variables.

📄 sample/infra/modules/apps.bicep

var corsEnv = [for (origin, i) in corsOrigins: { name: 'Cors__AllowedOrigins__${i}', value: origin }]

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Api/Program.cs

app.UseExceptionHandler();
app.UseStatusCodePages();
app.UseCors(ApiPolicies.Spa);
app.UseAuthentication();
app.UseAuthorization();
app.UseRateLimiter();

UseCors runs before authentication, because a browser's preflight OPTIONS request carries no token and must still get CORS headers. Remember what CORS is: a browser rule about which sites may call your API from JavaScript. It isn't authentication. A script or curl ignores it, which is why the bearer token and the owner check do the real work.

Data Protection

ASP.NET Core Data Protection encrypts cookies and antiforgery tokens. Ledgerly's API uses bearer tokens with no cookies, so nothing depends on the key ring today. If you add cookie authentication or a backend-for-frontend later, all replicas must share one key ring, or a cookie written by one replica can't be read by another. Check the ASP.NET Core Data Protection docs for key storage options before you add cookies.

💡 Count your secrets. The goal isn't a better vault; it's a shorter list. Every secret you can replace with an identity is one less thing to rotate, scan for and explain.

🧠 The supply chain is part of security

Jonas's last stop isn't Azure at all. "Half of your code is other people's packages," he says. "Who checks them?"

💻 In Ledgerly: one package source

📄 sample/nuget.config

  <!-- Only nuget.org, no machine-wide feeds leaking in. Package source mapping blocks dependency-confusion attacks. -->
  <packageSources>
    <clear />
    <add key="nuget.org" value="https://api.nuget.org/v3/index.json" />
  </packageSources>
  <packageSourceMapping>
    <packageSource key="nuget.org">
      <package pattern="*" />
    </packageSource>
  </packageSourceMapping>

<clear /> ignores feeds configured on the machine, and source mapping says which feed each package may come from. When you add a private feed, map your company's prefix to it, so a public package with the same name can't sneak in. NuGet audit proved its worth in chapter 3: it failed the build on a vulnerable transitive System.Security.Cryptography.Pkcs, fixed with transitive pinning.

💻 In Ledgerly: Dependabot and CodeQL

📄 .github/dependabot.yml

  - package-ecosystem: nuget
    directory: /sample
    schedule:
      interval: weekly
    groups:
      # One PR per family instead of twenty. Versions inside a family must move together.
      aspire:
        patterns: ["Aspire.*"]
# ...
  - package-ecosystem: github-actions
    directory: /

Dependabot opens pull requests for outdated NuGet packages and GitHub Actions, grouped by family so Aspire packages move together (Dependabot options). 🔧 Version updates have a default cooldown of 3 days, so brand-new releases aren't proposed until they've been out for a few days. CI tests every Dependabot PR like any other.

📄 .github/workflows/codeql.yml

  schedule:
    - cron: "17 3 * * 1" # weekly, catches new queries against unchanged code
# ...
      - uses: github/codeql-action/init@v4
        with:
          languages: csharp
          build-mode: none # no build needed for C#, analysis reads the source
      - uses: github/codeql-action/analyze@v4

CodeQL is static analysis. build-mode: none analyzes C# without a build, which keeps the workflow fast (configure code scanning). The weekly run matters: new queries find problems in code that hasn't changed. 🔧 Code scanning is free for public repositories; private and internal ones need GitHub Code Security.

Secret scanning and push protection

Secret scanning looks for known secret formats in the repository, and push protection blocks a push that contains one. Both are free and automatic for public repositories, and push protection for users is on by default there. 🔧 Repository-level push protection and private repositories need GitHub Secret Protection.

Jonas points out the limit: push protection guards Git, not Slack. The only complete fix for the story at the top of this chapter is the one you already made: have nothing worth pasting.

💡 You own every package and action in your build. Pin the sources, let a bot propose updates, let CI test them, and let a scanner read the code every week.

⚠️ Common traps

Trap What happens Do this instead
Authentication=Active Directory Default in production The full credential chain is probed on connect: slow, and the docs advise against it Active Directory Managed Identity;User Id=<client id>
A user-assigned identity without AZURE_CLIENT_ID It works locally, then fails in Azure because the SDK can't tell which identity to use Set AZURE_CLIENT_ID per app, as commonEnv does
Leaving local auth on "just in case" Any leaked key still works, as the Slack story shows disableLocalAuth: true on Service Bus, admin user off on the registry
Giving apps Contributor or Key Vault Administrator A compromised app can change infrastructure or read every secret Data-plane roles only: Secrets User, Data Sender, Data Receiver, AcrPull
Returning 403 for another user's portfolio Attackers learn which ids exist Filter by owner in the query and return 404
Keying data on email or display name Data is orphaned when a user's email changes Store the oid claim through GetOwnerId
The worker never scales out The KEDA scaler's identity can't read queue metrics: that needs Manage rights, which Sender and Receiver don't include Grant Azure Service Bus Data Owner on the queue only, as servicebus.bicep does (chapter 11), and watch the scale events after deploying 🔧
Treating CORS as security curl and scripts ignore it Rely on the bearer token and per-user checks
A secret pasted in chat Push protection can't see it Rotate it immediately, then remove the need for it

🧰 Helpers

The "no secrets" review for a new arrow (copy into your design notes):

  • [ ] Who calls whom? Name the identity (app or migrator) and the target resource.
  • [ ] Can the target use Microsoft Entra ID auth? If yes, disable keys or local users.
  • [ ] Which data-plane role is the narrowest that works? Assign it in Bicep with a guid() name.
  • [ ] If a secret is unavoidable, does it go from Bicep straight into Key Vault, with no output?
  • [ ] Is AZURE_CLIENT_ID set on the app that uses the identity?

See what an identity can do 🧪 (illustrative, not in the sample):

# illustrative, not in the sample
# principal id of the app identity, from the Bicep output appPrincipalId or the portal
az role assignment list --assignee <app-principal-id> --all -o table
az servicebus namespace show -g <rg> -n <namespace> --query disableLocalAuth

🧪 Not run by the author: this step was written from the docs and validated with bicep build only. Expect small differences.

Make it permanent:

  • Add "No new secrets, or a Key Vault reference with a reason" to your PR checklist. The toolkit has a PR review checklist and a production readiness checklist to extend.
  • Turn on Dependabot, CodeQL and push protection on day one of a repository, not after the first incident.
  • Look up any unfamiliar term in the glossary.

🧪 Try it yourself (optional, ~30 min)

  1. Locally, two users: start everything with cd sample and aspire run. In a second terminal, create two tokens: bash cd sample dotnet user-jwts create --project src/Ledgerly.Api --name alice dotnet user-jwts create --project src/Ledgerly.Api --name bob Open src/Ledgerly.Api/Ledgerly.Api.http, set @api to the API URL from the dashboard and @token to Alice's token. Create a portfolio and copy its id from the Location header.
  2. Switch @token to Bob's token and request GET {{api}}/api/portfolios/<alice's id>. You get 404. List portfolios as Bob: the list is empty.
  3. Remove the Authorization header and send the list request again. You get 401.
  4. Locally, no Azure: from cd sample, run bicep build infra/main.bicep --stdout and search the output for password and Active Directory. The only password= sits inside the Redis expression that writes straight into Key Vault. Active Directory Managed Identity shows up in both SQL connection strings: the apps' and the migration job's.
  5. 🧪 With Azure: after a deployment, run the two commands from the helpers. Match each role assignment to an arrow in the "who can reach what" diagram, and confirm disableLocalAuth is true.

    🧪 Not run by the author: this step was written from the docs and validated with bicep build only. Expect small differences.

✅ Recap

  1. Managed identities replace passwords and keys for every Ledgerly arrow into Azure (SQL, Service Bus, KEDA, the registry and Key Vault), and disabling local auth makes leaked keys worthless.
  2. Use a deterministic credential in production (ManagedIdentityCredential with the client id, or the SQL Active Directory Managed Identity keyword) and keep DefaultAzureCredential for developer machines.
  3. Users authenticate with JWT bearer tokens (user-jwts locally, Entra ID in Azure), every query filters by the owner's oid, and the few remaining secrets live in Key Vault while Dependabot, CodeQL and push protection guard the supply chain.

Next → Chapter 14: Reliability patterns

Chapter 14: Reliability patterns

⏱ About 30 min · You'll learn: what the standard resilience handler really does with its defaults, how to degrade gracefully and wire health probes without restarting healthy replicas, and how idempotency, EF retries, rate limits and bulkheads stop one failure from becoming many.

📖 Story: Priya flips the switch

It is demo day. Elena has invited two advisors to see the beta build, and you are driving. You open a portfolio, click Valuation, and the numbers appear in a few hundred milliseconds. Elena nods. Then Priya, sitting at the back with her laptop, says "one second" and types something. You refresh. The spinner turns, and keeps turning. After about fifteen seconds the page fills in: holdings and cost basis are there, but every price says "unavailable" and the total market value is empty.

"I set the market-data failure rate to 100%," Priya says. "That's what happens when the vendor goes down at 9 a.m. on a Monday." Elena asks the question she always asks: "What does the user see?" Priya answers honestly: "A spinner for fifteen seconds, then half a page. That's better than a 500. It's not good enough."

Afterwards Maya pulls you into a room. "At Nordbank, how did you handle the flaky SOAP vendor?" You admit it: a for loop, Thread.Sleep(1000), three attempts, and a WCF sendTimeout nobody remembered setting. She smiles. "Same instinct, better tools. Let's see what's already protecting us, and what those fifteen seconds are made of." You open your notebook to Habits I brought with me and write: "Retry in a loop with Thread.Sleep."

Numbers in the Ledgerly story (latency, request counts, timings) are illustrative. The resilience defaults quoted from the docs are real and marked 🔧.

🏚️ Old way → 🚀 New way

What you did before What you do now Why it changed
A for loop with Thread.Sleep around a SOAP or HTTP call AddStandardResilienceHandler(): retries with exponential backoff and jitter, bounded by timeouts Fixed delays from many callers arrive in waves and hammer a recovering dependency; jitter spreads them out
WCF binding timeouts in web.config, set once and forgotten Per-attempt and total timeouts in the pipeline, plus a CancellationToken that flows from the HTTP request to EF and HttpClient A slow dependency must not hold your threads and your user longer than the user is willing to wait
Catch the exception and show an error page Graceful degradation: return what you have and say it is partial (IsComplete = false) Most screens have parts that don't depend on the failing service
The load balancer pings default.aspx or checks "is the app pool running" Startup, liveness and readiness probes mapped to /alive and /health The platform restarts or removes replicas for you, so the checks must mean the right thing
MSDTC and the dream of exactly-once delivery At-least-once delivery plus idempotent consumers (ProcessedMessages, Service Bus duplicate detection) Distributed transactions don't exist across cloud services; duplicates must be harmless instead
Deadlock-retry code copied into every repository class The EF Core execution strategy retries transient SQL errors in one place Azure SQL has transient faults (failovers, serverless resume) that are normal, not exceptional
One slow dependency starved the IIS thread pool Bulkheads: concurrency limits, MaxConcurrentCalls, rate limiting with 429 Isolating a failure keeps the rest of the system responsive

💡 Reliability is not about preventing failure. It is about deciding, in advance, what happens when each dependency fails.

🧠 The failures you design for

Before choosing patterns, name the failures. Maya keeps this table on the team wiki, and each row is a question for every new dependency.

Failure mode What it looks like Ledgerly example Pattern
Transient error A single request fails, the next one works MarketData returns 503 for one call Retry with backoff and jitter
Slow dependency Requests succeed, but take seconds MarketData adds 3 s of latency Timeouts per attempt and in total, cancellation
Hard outage Every call fails for minutes The vendor is down Circuit breaker, then graceful degradation
Overload Too many requests for the capacity A user scripts 200 CSV uploads Rate limiting, bulkheads, queue-based load leveling
Duplicate delivery The same message arrives twice The outbox relay crashes after Send but before SaveChanges Idempotent consumer, duplicate detection
Poison message A message fails every time An import message for an import row that no longer exists Dead-letter queue, alert a human
Process death A replica is killed mid-request Scale-in or a new revision sends SIGTERM Graceful shutdown, probes, durable queues

The first four are about calls, the last three are about work. Chapter 7 covered the work side in depth (outbox, dead-letter, graceful shutdown). This chapter connects both sides and adds the HTTP pipeline, probes and limits.

💡 A retry is a bet that the next attempt will succeed. It is a good bet for transient errors and a terrible bet for outages and overload.

🧠 The standard resilience handler, defaults and all

The package Microsoft.Extensions.Http.Resilience 🔧 is built on Polly v8. One call, AddStandardResilienceHandler(), wraps every outgoing HttpClient request in a pipeline of five strategies. The older Microsoft.Extensions.Http.Polly (AddPolicyHandler, Polly v7) is deprecated, so if you find it in old code, migrate.

Order (outer → inner) Strategy Default 🔧 What it protects
1 Rate limiter Concurrency limiter, permit limit 1,000, queue 0 Your own process: a cap on concurrent outbound calls
2 Total request timeout 30 s The caller: no request waits longer than this, retries included
3 Retry 3 retries, exponential backoff with jitter, base delay 2 s. Handles 5xx, 408, 429 and transient exceptions Transient errors
4 Circuit breaker Opens at a 10% failure ratio, minimum throughput 100, sampling duration 30 s, break duration 5 s The dependency: stop calling it while it's down, and fail fast
5 Attempt timeout 10 s per attempt One hung attempt can't eat the whole budget

Source: Build resilient HTTP apps.

💻 In Ledgerly: one line for every HttpClient

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.ServiceDefaults/Extensions.cs

        builder.Services.ConfigureHttpClientDefaults(http =>
        {
            // Retries with jittered back-off, per-attempt + total timeouts, circuit breaker, rate limiter.
            http.AddStandardResilienceHandler();

            // Resolves "https+http://marketdata" to a real address (Aspire locally, config or DNS in Azure).
            http.AddServiceDiscovery();
        });

ConfigureHttpClientDefaults applies to every client created by IHttpClientFactory in that service, including the typed MarketDataClient. The client itself stays boring on purpose:

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Core/Market/MarketDataClient.cs

/// <summary>
/// Typed HttpClient for the market-data service. Base address, retries, timeouts and the circuit breaker
/// are configured at registration time (see ServiceDefaults), not here. This class just speaks HTTP.
/// </summary>
public sealed class MarketDataClient(HttpClient http) : IMarketDataClient
{
    // ...
        var quotes = await http.GetFromJsonAsync<Quote[]>($"/prices?symbols={query}", cancellationToken) ?? [];
    // ...
}

No retry loop, no timeout constant, no try/catch. Policy lives in one place, code lives in another. That separation is the biggest change from the Thread.Sleep habit.

What the defaults did during the demo

Now you can explain Priya's fifteen seconds:

  1. Every call to /prices got a 503 from the chaos switch.
  2. The retry strategy treated 503 as transient and tried again three times, waiting a little longer each time (starting around 2 s, growing exponentially, with jitter). Those waits add up to roughly the fifteen seconds you saw.
  3. The circuit breaker never opened. It needs at least 100 calls in its 30-second sampling window before it judges the failure ratio. A demo with three people clicking produces a handful of calls.
  4. After the last retry, the handler returned the final 503, GetFromJsonAsync threw HttpRequestException, and the valuation degraded (next section).

Point 3 is the one that surprises people. The defaults are tuned for services with real traffic. At beta volume, the breaker protects you only when many users hit the outage at once. In production with real load, the breaker opens after enough failures, every call fails immediately with BrokenCircuitException for 5 seconds, and the page degrades in milliseconds instead of seconds.

flowchart LR
    App["ValuationService<br/>calls MarketDataClient"] --> RL["1 Rate limiter<br/>1,000 concurrent"]
    RL --> TT["2 Total timeout<br/>30 s"]
    TT --> RT["3 Retry<br/>3 times, backoff + jitter"]
    RT --> CB["4 Circuit breaker<br/>10% of at least 100 calls"]
    CB --> AT["5 Attempt timeout<br/>10 s"]
    AT --> MD["MarketData<br/>/prices"]
    CB -- "open: fail fast" --> X["BrokenCircuitException"]
    TT -- "budget spent" --> Y["TimeoutRejectedException"]

Tuning it for a user-facing call

For a valuation screen, fifteen seconds is too long. You can tune the standard handler with an options callback:

// illustrative, not in the sample
http.AddStandardResilienceHandler(options =>
{
    options.Retry.MaxRetryAttempts = 2;
    options.AttemptTimeout.Timeout = TimeSpan.FromSeconds(3);
    options.TotalRequestTimeout.Timeout = TimeSpan.FromSeconds(8);
});

Two rules keep tuning safe. Keep the total timeout shorter than your caller's patience (the browser, Leo's SPA, or an upstream service). And change the pipeline in one place: adding a second AddStandardResilienceHandler() on a typed client that already gets one from ConfigureHttpClientDefaults stacks two pipelines, and the retries multiply.

Use retries when Avoid retries when
The operation is a read (GET) or is idempotent It is a POST that creates something and has no idempotency key. Use o.Retry.DisableForUnsafeHttpMethods()
Errors are transient (503, 408, timeouts, connection resets) Errors are permanent (400, 401, 404): retrying can't help
The dependency is shared and recovers on its own You are already inside a retry loop higher up (queue redelivery, the caller's retry)

For non-HTTP work (a database call, a file share, an SDK without built-in retries), Microsoft.Extensions.Resilience gives you the same building blocks with AddResiliencePipeline("name", ...).

💡 The defaults are a good starting point, not a design. Read them, do the math for your traffic, and tune the call paths that users wait on.

🧠 Graceful degradation, timeouts and cancellation

Retries and breakers decide when to give up. Degradation decides what to return when you do. Ledgerly's valuation has two parts: the holdings and cost basis come from your own database, the prices come from the vendor. If the vendor is down, half the answer is still true.

💻 In Ledgerly: a partial valuation instead of a 500

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Api/Services/ValuationService.cs

    private async Task<decimal?> GetPriceAsync(string symbol, CancellationToken ct)
    {
        try
        {
            return await cache.GetOrCreateAsync(
                $"quote:{symbol}",
                async token =>
                {
                    var quotes = await marketData.GetQuotesAsync([symbol], token);
                    return quotes.TryGetValue(symbol, out var q) ? q.Price : (decimal?)null;
                },
                tags: ["quotes"],
                cancellationToken: ct);
        }
        catch (Exception ex) when (ex is HttpRequestException or BrokenCircuitException or TimeoutRejectedException)
        {
            LogPriceUnavailable(logger, symbol, ex.GetType().Name);
            return null;
        }
    }

And in GetAsync:

        var complete = lines.All(l => l.Price is not null);
        return new PortfolioValuation(
            portfolio.Id,
            portfolio.BaseCurrency,
            lines,
            lines.Sum(l => l.CostBasis),
            complete ? lines.Sum(l => l.MarketValue!.Value) : null,
            complete,
            clock.GetUtcNow());

What matters here:

Line Why
The catch names three exception types These are the three ways the resilience pipeline gives up: final HTTP failure, open circuit, spent timeout. Anything else (a bug) still fails loudly
OperationCanceledException is not caught If the user closes the tab, the request is aborted and the work should stop, not degrade
HybridCache.GetOrCreateAsync A price cached a few seconds ago is still served during an outage, and stampede protection means 50 concurrent requests for AAPL make one vendor call (chapter 18)
IsComplete and a null TotalMarketValue The API is honest. Leo's SPA can show "prices delayed" instead of a wrong total

When you showed Elena the JSON, she asked for one change in the SPA backlog: a banner that says "Live prices are temporarily unavailable" when isComplete is false. That is graceful degradation done properly. It is a product decision, not only a technical one.

Cancellation is part of the timeout story

In minimal APIs, a CancellationToken parameter is bound to the request's abort signal. Ledgerly passes ct from the endpoint into ValuationService, into EF Core and into HttpClient. When the user gives up, everything downstream stops. At Nordbank most of your call stacks had no tokens at all, so an abandoned request kept a thread busy until the WCF timeout fired.

sequenceDiagram
    participant U as "Browser"
    participant A as "Api ValuationService"
    participant C as "HybridCache"
    participant P as "Resilience pipeline"
    participant M as "MarketData"
    U->>A: GET valuation
    A->>C: GetOrCreate quote AAPL
    C->>P: cache miss, fetch price
    P->>M: attempt 1
    M-->>P: 503 chaos
    P->>M: retries with backoff and jitter
    M-->>P: 503 again
    P-->>C: HttpRequestException
    C-->>A: exception
    A-->>U: 200 with IsComplete false

💡 Every dependency needs a written answer to "what does the user see when this is down?" Even "a 500" is a decision, so make it on purpose.

🧠 Health probes: liveness, readiness and startup

In IIS, health was "is the app pool running?". In Azure Container Apps, the platform asks your replica three different questions, and it takes different actions for each answer. Getting them confused is one of the most common outages in container platforms.

Probe Question Action on failure Ledgerly path
Startup Has the process finished starting? Keep waiting, other probes are paused. After too many failures, restart /alive
Liveness Is the process responsive at all? Restart the container /alive
Readiness Can this replica serve traffic right now? Remove it from load balancing until it recovers. No restart /health

💻 In Ledgerly: two endpoints with different jobs

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.ServiceDefaults/Extensions.cs

    public static TBuilder AddDefaultHealthChecks<TBuilder>(this TBuilder builder) where TBuilder : IHostApplicationBuilder
    {
        builder.Services.AddHealthChecks()
            // Liveness: "the process is responsive". Never check dependencies here, or a DB blip restarts every replica.
            .AddCheck("self", () => HealthCheckResult.Healthy(), ["live"]);

        return builder;
    }

    public static WebApplication MapDefaultEndpoints(this WebApplication app)
    {
        // Readiness: all checks (including DB, Redis, Service Bus registered by Aspire integrations).
        app.MapHealthChecks(ReadinessPath).DisableHttpMetrics();

        // Liveness: only checks tagged "live".
        app.MapHealthChecks(LivenessPath, new HealthCheckOptions
        {
            Predicate = r => r.Tags.Contains("live"),
        }).DisableHttpMetrics();

        return app;
    }

Read the comment on the self check twice. If liveness checked the database, a 30-second Azure SQL failover would fail liveness on every replica at once, the platform would restart all of them, and you would turn a short database blip into a full outage with cold starts on top. Liveness answers only "is this process stuck?".

Readiness (/health) runs all registered checks. You didn't write a database check: Aspire's AddSqlServerDbContext registers one, and the other Aspire client integrations do the same for their resources. By default, Healthy and Degraded return HTTP 200 and Unhealthy returns 503.

The Aspire service defaults template maps these endpoints only in Development. Ledgerly maps them in every environment because Container Apps probes need them, and the default response is a single word ("Healthy") that reveals no details.

📄 sample/infra/modules/apps.bicep

var probes = [
  {
    type: 'Startup'
    httpGet: { path: '/alive', port: 8080 }
    initialDelaySeconds: 2
    periodSeconds: 3
    failureThreshold: 20
  }
  {
    type: 'Liveness'
    httpGet: { path: '/alive', port: 8080 }
    periodSeconds: 10
    failureThreshold: 3
  }
  {
    type: 'Readiness'
    httpGet: { path: '/health', port: 8080 }
    periodSeconds: 10
    failureThreshold: 3
  }
]

The startup probe gives a replica about a minute (20 checks, 3 seconds apart) to start before anything else is judged. After that, three failed liveness checks in a row (about 30 seconds) trigger a restart, and three failed readiness checks take the replica out of rotation.

flowchart TB
    Start["Replica starts"] --> SP{"Startup probe<br/>/alive ok?"}
    SP -- "not yet" --> SP
    SP -- "20 failures" --> Restart["Container restarted"]
    SP -- "yes" --> Run["Running"]
    Run --> LP{"Liveness<br/>/alive ok?"}
    LP -- "3 failures" --> Restart
    Run --> RP{"Readiness<br/>/health ok?"}
    RP -- "yes" --> Traffic["Receives traffic"]
    RP -- "3 failures" --> Out["Out of rotation<br/>no restart"]
    Out -- "checks pass again" --> Traffic

A trade-off worth discussing

Readiness that checks a shared dependency has its own risk: if the database is down, every API replica becomes unready at once, and callers get an error from the platform instead of your nicely shaped ProblemDetails. Maya's position, which the team wrote down in an ADR: readiness checks only dependencies without which this replica can do nothing useful. For the API, that is SQL. MarketData is not a readiness dependency, because valuation degrades without it. Never add the vendor to a health check.

💡 Liveness asks "should I be restarted?", readiness asks "should I get traffic?". A dependency failure is almost never a reason to restart.

🧠 Idempotency, EF retries, rate limits and bulkheads

Retries create duplicates. Every layer that retries (the HTTP pipeline, Service Bus redelivery, the outbox relay, a user clicking twice) needs a partner that makes the duplicate harmless.

💻 In Ledgerly: an idempotent consumer

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Worker/ImportProcessor.cs

        if (await db.ProcessedMessages.AnyAsync(p => p.MessageId == messageId, ct))
        {
            LogDuplicate(logger, messageId);
            await args.CompleteMessageAsync(args.Message, ct);
            return;
        }
        // ...
        try
        {
            var outcome = await ApplyImportAsync(db, import, ct);
            db.ProcessedMessages.Add(new ProcessedMessage { MessageId = messageId, Handler = HandlerName });
            await db.SaveChangesAsync(ct); // trades + import status + idempotency record in ONE transaction
            await args.CompleteMessageAsync(args.Message, ct);
            // ...
        }
        catch (Exception ex) when (ex is not OperationCanceledException)
        {
            // ...
            await args.AbandonMessageAsync(args.Message, cancellationToken: ct); // Service Bus will redeliver
        }

The trades, the import status and the "I processed this message" record are saved in one SaveChangesAsync, so they commit together or not at all. MessageId is the primary key of ProcessedMessages, so even two replicas racing on the same message can't both commit. There are three layers of protection:

Layer Mechanism Catches
Service Bus Duplicate detection: the same MessageId within the window is dropped. Ledgerly sets MessageId = outbox row id The relay re-sending after a crash, within 10 minutes
Database ProcessedMessages keyed by message id, written in the same transaction Redelivery after the window, or after an abandon
Domain ExternalRef from the broker CSV is unique per portfolio (filtered unique index, chapter 5) The user uploading the same file twice

📄 sample/infra/modules/servicebus.bicep

  properties: {
    lockDuration: 'PT1M'
    maxDeliveryCount: 10 // after 10 failed attempts the message moves to imports/$deadletterqueue
    requiresDuplicateDetection: true // the same MessageId within the window is dropped
    duplicateDetectionHistoryTimeWindow: 'PT10M'
    deadLetteringOnMessageExpiration: true
  }

Duplicate detection needs the Standard or Premium tier 🔧 and is configured when the queue is created. The 10-minute window is also the default (range 20 s to 7 days) 🔧.

EF Core: the execution strategy

Azure SQL has transient faults that are part of normal operation: a failover, a connection reset, a serverless database resuming from auto-pause. EF Core's SQL Server provider can retry them through an execution strategy (EnableRetryOnFailure). Aspire's AddSqlServerDbContext turns retries on for you. The catch: once retries are on, a transaction you start must run inside the strategy, so the whole unit can be replayed.

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.MigrationService/Program.cs

            // The execution strategy retries transient failures (e.g. Azure SQL serverless waking up).
            var strategy = db.Database.CreateExecutionStrategy();
            await strategy.ExecuteAsync(async () => await db.Database.MigrateAsync(stoppingToken));

A single SaveChangesAsync is already one transaction, which is why the import handler doesn't need this wrapper. Reach for CreateExecutionStrategy().ExecuteAsync(...) when you call BeginTransactionAsync yourself or group several saves.

Rate limiting and bulkheads

A bulkhead (named after ship compartments) limits how much of your capacity one kind of work can take, so a flood in one place can't sink everything. Ledgerly has several, some of which you might not have noticed:

Bulkhead Where Limit Protects
Per-user rate limit on imports Program.cs 5 uploads per minute per user (fixed window) API and worker from one noisy user
Outbound concurrency limiter Standard resilience handler 1,000 concurrent calls, queue 0 🔧 The process from unbounded outbound calls
MaxConcurrentCalls = 4 ImportProcessor 4 imports per worker replica SQL from too many parallel imports
maxReplicas: 5 on the worker apps.bicep KEDA adds replicas, up to 5 SQL again: scale is bounded on purpose
CPU and memory per container apps.bicep 0.5 vCPU, 1 GiB for api and worker Neighbors in the same environment

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Api/Program.cs

builder.Services.AddRateLimiter(options =>
{
    options.RejectionStatusCode = StatusCodes.Status429TooManyRequests;
    options.AddPolicy(ApiPolicies.Imports, http => RateLimitPartition.GetFixedWindowLimiter(
        partitionKey: http.User.Identity?.Name ?? http.Connection.RemoteIpAddress?.ToString() ?? "anonymous",
        factory: _ => new FixedWindowRateLimiterOptions { PermitLimit = 5, Window = TimeSpan.FromMinutes(1) }));
});

Note the first line inside the lambda. The rate limiting middleware's default RejectionStatusCode is 503, not 429. A 503 tells clients and monitoring "the server is broken", while 429 says "you are sending too much". Leo's SPA should back off on 429; your alerts should not page Priya for it. Set it explicitly, as the rate limiting docs samples do.

💡 Every retry needs an idempotency partner, and every queue or scale rule needs an upper bound that your database can survive.

🗺️ How a duplicate becomes harmless

flowchart LR
    API["Api<br/>import + outbox row<br/>one transaction"] --> OB[("OutboxMessages")]
    OB --> Relay["OutboxRelay<br/>MessageId = outbox id"]
    Relay -- "send, maybe twice" --> SB["Service Bus queue imports<br/>duplicate detection 10 min"]
    SB -- "deliver, at least once" --> W["ImportProcessor"]
    W --> Q{"MessageId in<br/>ProcessedMessages?"}
    Q -- "yes" --> Done["Complete, do nothing"]
    Q -- "no" --> Apply["Apply trades + record id<br/>one SaveChanges"]
    Apply -- "crash or error" --> Ab["Abandon, redelivered"]
    Ab --> SB
    SB -- "after 10 deliveries" --> DLQ["imports dead-letter queue"]

⚠️ Common traps

Trap What happens Do this instead
Liveness probe checks the database A short SQL failover fails liveness everywhere, every replica restarts, and the outage gets longer Liveness checks only the process (self). Dependencies go in readiness, and only the essential ones
Retrying a non-idempotent POST A timeout after the server committed leads to a second order, a second trade, a second import o.Retry.DisableForUnsafeHttpMethods(), or an idempotency key the server checks
Stacking two resilience handlers ConfigureHttpClientDefaults adds one, a typed client adds another, and 3 retries become many Configure the pipeline in one place and read the effective defaults
Catching Exception around a call, including cancellation Aborted requests "degrade" instead of stopping, and real bugs return 200 with missing data Catch the specific resilience outcomes, as ValuationService does, and let OperationCanceledException flow
Trusting the circuit breaker at low traffic With a minimum throughput of 100 in 30 s 🔧, a quiet service never trips it, and every user pays the full retry time Do the math for your traffic and tune attempt and total timeouts for user-facing calls
Leaving the rate limiter on its default status Rejections show up as 503s, alerts fire, and clients retry harder Set RejectionStatusCode = 429 and let clients back off
Unbounded scale-out on a queue KEDA adds 50 workers, each opens SQL connections, and the database becomes the outage Set maxReplicas and MaxConcurrentCalls to what the database can take

🧰 Helpers

The "new dependency" checklist (paste into your ADR or PR description):

- [ ] What does the user see when this dependency is down? (degrade, queue, or fail)
- [ ] Timeouts: attempt and total, shorter than the caller's patience
- [ ] Retries: only for idempotent operations, with backoff and jitter
- [ ] Circuit breaker: does our traffic reach the minimum throughput?
- [ ] Health: readiness only if this replica is useless without it, never liveness
- [ ] Duplicates: what makes a second delivery harmless?
- [ ] Limits: concurrency, rate and scale bounds the dependency can survive

Run chaos against your local system (MarketData's /chaos endpoint exists only in Development):

# 50% failures and 2 s of latency on every /prices call
curl -X POST "$MARKETDATA_URL/chaos" -H "Content-Type: application/json" -d '{"failureRate":0.5,"latencyMs":2000}'
# back to normal
curl -X POST "$MARKETDATA_URL/chaos" -H "Content-Type: application/json" -d '{"failureRate":0,"latencyMs":0}'

Make it permanent:

  • Keep resilience and health checks in the shared service defaults, so new services get them with one line. The toolkit has a copy as ServiceDefaults.Extensions.cs; see the toolkit.
  • Add the checklist above to production-readiness-checklist.md and the probe rule ("no dependencies in liveness") to pr-review-checklist.md.
  • Turn each degradation decision into a test. Ledgerly has Valuation_degrades_gracefully_when_market_data_is_down, which flips FakeMarketData.IsDown and asserts IsComplete is false (Chapter 17).
  • Never ship a "break me" endpoint: the chaos routes are mapped inside if (app.Environment.IsDevelopment()).

🧪 Try it yourself (optional, ~25 min)

You need a container runtime for Aspire.

  1. Start the system and open the Aspire dashboard link from the console: bash cd sample aspire run
  2. Create a dev token and use src/Ledgerly.Api/Ledgerly.Api.http (or curl) to create a portfolio, record a buy, and call the valuation endpoint. Note the response time and "isComplete": true. bash dotnet user-jwts create --project src/Ledgerly.Api
  3. In the dashboard, find the marketdata resource and copy its URL into MARKETDATA_URL. Turn on full failure with the curl command from Helpers, using "failureRate":1.0. Wait about 30 seconds so the cached quote expires (HybridCache expiration is 30 s in Program.cs).
  4. Call the valuation again. Observe: it takes many seconds, returns 200, and isComplete is false. In the dashboard Traces view, open the trace and count the HTTP client attempts to marketdata. In Structured logs, find "Price for ... unavailable (HttpRequestException)".
  5. Now set "failureRate":0,"latencyMs":12000. Call the valuation again. Observe: each attempt hits the 10 s attempt timeout, the 30 s total timeout ends the retries, and the log reason becomes TimeoutRejectedException.
  6. Reset chaos to zero. If the /chaos route isn't reachable on your machine, set FailureRate and LatencyMs in the Chaos section of src/Ledgerly.MarketData/appsettings.json instead and restart.

✅ Recap

  1. The standard resilience handler gives every HttpClient a rate limiter, total timeout, jittered retries, a circuit breaker and attempt timeouts, but you must check its defaults against your traffic and your users' patience.
  2. Degrade on purpose (IsComplete = false), flow CancellationToken everywhere, keep dependencies out of liveness, and keep only essential ones in readiness.
  3. Retries create duplicates and load: pair them with idempotent consumers, the EF execution strategy, a 429 rate limiter and bounded concurrency and scale.

Next → Chapter 15: Observability I: logging and OpenTelemetry

Chapter 15: Observability I: logging and OpenTelemetry

⏱ About 30 min · You'll learn: structured logging with message templates and [LoggerMessage], the OpenTelemetry model of traces, metrics and logs, and how Ledgerly follows one request from the browser through the outbox and Service Bus into the worker.

📖 Story: the log file that doesn't exist

The first real bug report from a beta tester arrives on a Tuesday. "I uploaded my broker CSV. It said the import finished, but three trades are missing." Elena forwards it with one line: "Can we tell her what happened today, not next week?"

Your hands know what to do. At Nordbank you would RDP into the three IIS servers, open D:\Logs\Policy\, and grep each day's log4net file for the customer number. So you ask Tomás, half joking: "Where's the log file for the worker?" He doesn't laugh. "Which replica? The one KEDA started at 10:02 and removed at 10:09? Its file system is gone. There are no files, and there's no box to log in to."

Maya slides her laptop over. The Aspire dashboard is open on a local run, and she types the import id into the structured logs filter. One line appears: Import 0193... completed_with_errors: 47 imported, 3 skipped. She clicks the trace id next to it, and the whole story unfolds on one screen: the upload request in the API, the outbox publish in the worker, the Service Bus processing and every SQL call. "Same thing in Application Insights for the beta environment," she says. "Logs aren't files anymore. They're events with properties, and they belong to a trace." In your notebook under Habits I brought with me: "Grep log files on each server."

The ids, row counts and timings in this chapter are illustrative. Package versions and defaults from the docs are marked 🔧.

🏚️ Old way → 🚀 New way

What you did before What you do now Why it changed
log4net or NLog files on each server, RDP and grep Logs exported as structured events (OTLP) to the Aspire dashboard locally and Application Insights in Azure Containers are short-lived and have no persistent disk; there is no server to log in to
logger.Info("Import " + id + " done") Message templates: "Import {ImportId} {Outcome}: ..." with named properties, generated by [LoggerMessage] Properties are queryable fields, not text you parse with regex
A correlation id copied by hand into WCF headers W3C trace context (traceparent) propagated by OpenTelemetry, and carried through the outbox in a TraceParent column One trace id links every span and log line across services and queues
Performance counters and perfmon on the box Meter counters and histograms, plus built-in ASP.NET Core, HttpClient and runtime metrics Metrics are cheap aggregates that survive sampling and replica churn
The Application Insights 2.x SDK with TelemetryClient.TrackEvent everywhere Vendor-neutral APIs (ILogger, ActivitySource, Meter) and an exporter chosen at startup Code doesn't change when the backend changes, and the classic SDK is now built on OpenTelemetry anyway
"Turn on Debug in production" when something breaks Traces show the path; logs at Information and above carry the facts; sampling keeps the cost bounded Debug-level volume in the cloud is a cost and a privacy problem

💡 You don't search logs to find out what happened anymore. You find one event, then follow its trace.

🧠 Structured logging that you can query

The single most important habit to drop is building log messages with string concatenation or interpolation. A message template keeps the text and the values separate. The text stays constant (so you can group by it), and each {Placeholder} becomes a named property on the log record. The official guidance is direct about it: use templates, never interpolation.

Style Example What the backend stores
Interpolation ❌ logger.LogInformation($"Import {id} done") One unique string per import. Nothing to filter on, and the string is built even when the level is off
Template ✅ logger.LogInformation("Import {ImportId} done", id) A constant template plus a property ImportId you can query
Source-generated ✅✅ [LoggerMessage(...)] static partial void LogImportDone(...) Same as the template, with no boxing, no template parsing at runtime, and no allocation when the level is disabled

💻 In Ledgerly: every log call is generated

Ledgerly uses [LoggerMessage] everywhere except one deliberate line in the chaos service. The worker's import processor declares its messages at the bottom of the class:

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Worker/ImportProcessor.cs

    [LoggerMessage(Level = LogLevel.Information, Message = "Message {MessageId} already processed, completing duplicate")]
    private static partial void LogDuplicate(ILogger logger, string messageId);

    [LoggerMessage(Level = LogLevel.Information, Message = "Import {ImportId} {Outcome}: {RowsImported} imported, {RowsSkipped} skipped")]
    private static partial void LogImportDone(ILogger logger, Guid importId, string outcome, int rowsImported, int rowsSkipped);

    [LoggerMessage(Level = LogLevel.Error, Message = "Import {ImportId} failed on delivery {DeliveryCount}; abandoning for retry")]
    private static partial void LogImportCrashed(ILogger logger, Guid importId, int deliveryCount, Exception ex);

Three details are worth copying:

  1. The class is sealed partial class ImportProcessor. The generator writes the method bodies into the other half of the partial class, which is why the methods must be partial.
  2. The Exception parameter isn't in the template. The generator recognizes it and attaches it to the log record as the exception, with its stack trace.
  3. If you add a parameter and forget to use it in the template, the build warns with SYSLIB1015. With warnings as errors (chapter 3), that is a build break, which is exactly what you want.

Compare it with the one hand-written call, in the market-data simulator:

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.MarketData/Program.cs

        logger.LogWarning("Chaos: failing /prices request for {Symbols}", symbols);

It still uses a template, so Symbols is a property. For a throwaway simulator that's fine. In code that runs on every request, prefer the generator.

Scopes: properties for a whole block

IncludeScopes = true (set in the service defaults) means properties from ILogger.BeginScope are attached to every log record written inside the scope. Ledgerly doesn't use scopes, because spans already group the work, but they're handy when several log lines need the same business key:

// illustrative, not in the sample
using (logger.BeginScope(new Dictionary<string, object?>
{
    ["ImportId"] = import.Id,
    ["PortfolioId"] = import.PortfolioId,
}))
{
    // every log written here carries ImportId and PortfolioId
}

💡 Write log messages for the query you'll run at 2 a.m.: a constant template, the business ids as properties, and the exception as an exception.

🧠 OpenTelemetry: one model for traces, metrics and logs
