OpenTelemetry (OTel) is a vendor-neutral standard for telemetry. In .NET, the beautiful part is that the APIs are already in the runtime. Your code doesn't reference OpenTelemetry at all; only the composition root does.

Signal .NET API Question it answers Ledgerly example
Traces (spans) ActivitySource and Activity in System.Diagnostics "What happened in this one request, and where did the time go?" outbox publish imports, import process
Metrics Meter, Counter<T>, Histogram<T> in System.Diagnostics.Metrics "How much, how often, how fast, in aggregate?" ledgerly.imports.processed, http.server.request.duration
Logs ILogger "What fact did the code want to record?" Import {ImportId} {Outcome}: ...

A span in OTel is an Activity in .NET (the names differ for historical reasons). Each span has a trace id shared by the whole operation, its own span id and a parent span id. Log records written while a span is active automatically get its trace id and span id. That's how Maya jumped from one log line to the whole trace.

Three more concepts finish the model:

  • Resource attributes describe who is sending: service.name, service.version, the host. OTEL_SERVICE_NAME sets service.name, and OTEL_RESOURCE_ATTRIBUTES adds others. In Azure, apps.bicep sets OTEL_SERVICE_NAME to api, worker or marketdata for each container app. Application Insights shows it as the cloud role name.
  • OTLP (OpenTelemetry Protocol) is the wire format. An app exports to an OTLP endpoint over gRPC (default port 4317) or HTTP/protobuf (default port 4318) 🔧, configured with OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT, OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_PROTOCOL and OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_HEADERS (OTLP exporter spec). Aspire injects these variables for every project it runs.
  • Semantic conventions are agreed attribute names such as http.request.method, http.response.status_code, url.full and server.address. ASP.NET Core 8 and later emits built-in metrics like http.server.request.duration that follow them. Use the conventions where they fit and a reverse-DNS-style prefix (ledgerly.) for your own names.
flowchart LR
    subgraph Code["Your code: no OTel references"]
        L["ILogger<br/>LoggerMessage"]
        A["ActivitySource<br/>Ledgerly"]
        M["Meter<br/>Ledgerly"]
    end
    subgraph SDK["ServiceDefaults: OTel SDK"]
        I["Instrumentation<br/>AspNetCore, HttpClient, Runtime"]
        P["Pipeline<br/>sources, meters, filters"]
        E{"Which exporter?"}
    end
    L --> P
    A --> P
    M --> P
    I --> P
    P --> E
    E -- "OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT set" --> D["Aspire dashboard<br/>or any OTLP backend"]
    E -- "APPLICATIONINSIGHTS_CONNECTION_STRING set" --> AI["Application Insights<br/>Log Analytics workspace"]

💡 Instrument with the .NET APIs, export with OpenTelemetry. The backend becomes a startup decision, not a code change.

🧠 How ServiceDefaults wires it

Every Ledgerly service calls builder.AddServiceDefaults(). That one line is where all the OTel decisions live.

💻 In Ledgerly: signals, sources and filters

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.ServiceDefaults/Extensions.cs

    public static TBuilder ConfigureOpenTelemetry<TBuilder>(this TBuilder builder) where TBuilder : IHostApplicationBuilder
    {
        builder.Logging.AddOpenTelemetry(logging =>
        {
            logging.IncludeFormattedMessage = true;
            logging.IncludeScopes = true;
        });

        builder.Services.AddOpenTelemetry()
            .WithMetrics(metrics => metrics
                .AddAspNetCoreInstrumentation()
                .AddHttpClientInstrumentation()
                .AddRuntimeInstrumentation()
                .AddMeter(TelemetryName))
            .WithTracing(tracing => tracing
                .AddSource(builder.Environment.ApplicationName)
                .AddSource(TelemetryName)
                .AddSource("Azure.*")
                .AddAspNetCoreInstrumentation(options =>
                    // Probes hit these every few seconds: keep them out of traces.
                    options.Filter = context =>
                        !context.Request.Path.StartsWithSegments(ReadinessPath)
                        && !context.Request.Path.StartsWithSegments(LivenessPath))
                .AddHttpClientInstrumentation());

        builder.AddOpenTelemetryExporters();
        return builder;
    }

Line by line:

Line What it does
IncludeFormattedMessage = true Sends the rendered text ("Import 0193... completed: 47 imported") alongside the template and properties
AddMeter(TelemetryName) / AddSource(TelemetryName) Subscribes to Ledgerly's own meter and activity source, both named "Ledgerly". Without these lines, custom telemetry is silently dropped
AddSource("Azure.*") Collects spans from Azure SDK clients such as Service Bus
The Filter on ASP.NET Core instrumentation Drops /health and /alive requests. Container Apps probes call them every few seconds on every replica, and you don't want them in traces or in your bill
.DisableHttpMetrics() on the health endpoints (in MapDefaultEndpoints) The same idea for metrics: probes don't skew request-duration percentiles

Choosing the exporter at startup

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.ServiceDefaults/Extensions.cs

    private static TBuilder AddOpenTelemetryExporters<TBuilder>(this TBuilder builder) where TBuilder : IHostApplicationBuilder
    {
        // Locally Aspire sets this and the dashboard receives OTLP. Any OTLP backend works the same way.
        if (!string.IsNullOrWhiteSpace(builder.Configuration["OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT"]))
        {
            builder.Services.AddOpenTelemetry().UseOtlpExporter();
        }

        // In Azure the Container App gets this setting and telemetry goes to Application Insights.
        if (!string.IsNullOrWhiteSpace(builder.Configuration["APPLICATIONINSIGHTS_CONNECTION_STRING"]))
        {
            builder.Services.AddOpenTelemetry().UseAzureMonitor();
        }

        return builder;
    }

The code never asks "am I in Azure?". It asks "where am I told to send telemetry?". Locally, Aspire sets the OTLP endpoint and the dashboard receives everything. In Azure, apps.bicep puts APPLICATIONINSIGHTS_CONNECTION_STRING into commonEnv for every container app (chapter 16 follows it from there). With neither variable, as in integration tests, nothing is exported.

Two distros, pick one 🔧

UseAzureMonitor() comes from the Azure Monitor OpenTelemetry Distro (Azure.Monitor.OpenTelemetry.AspNetCore, 1.6.0 in the sample 🔧). The current docs recommend a newer option for new apps, the Microsoft OpenTelemetry Distro (package Microsoft.OpenTelemetry, 1.2.0 🔧), which can export to Azure Monitor, any OTLP endpoint or Agent 365:

// illustrative, not in the sample
using Microsoft.OpenTelemetry;

builder.UseMicrosoftOpenTelemetry(o => o.Exporters = ExportTarget.AzureMonitor);

The docs say existing users of the Azure Monitor distro need to take no action, which is why Ledgerly stays on UseAzureMonitor() for now. The one hard rule: don't initialize both distros in the same app. And if you inherit an older app on the classic Application Insights SDK, know that SDK 3.x (Microsoft.ApplicationInsights.AspNetCore 3.2.0 🔧) is itself built on OpenTelemetry, that many 2.x packages were removed, and that 2.x and 3.x packages must not be mixed. See Enable Azure Monitor OpenTelemetry and the migration guide.

The Aspire dashboard

Locally you don't need any cloud resource to see all three signals. aspire run starts the dashboard with an OTLP receiver, and it shows resources, console output, structured logs (filterable by property), traces (a waterfall per trace) and metrics. It is the fastest feedback loop you'll have: add a span, run, look. Chapter 6 covers the dashboard's other views.

💡 Configure telemetry in one shared place, choose the exporter from configuration, and filter out probe noise before it costs you anything.

🧠 Custom spans, custom metrics and one trace through the queue

Built-in instrumentation covers HTTP in and out, and the Azure SDKs. Your business operations need their own names.

💻 In Ledgerly: one source, one meter

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Core/Diagnostics/LedgerlyTelemetry.cs

public static class LedgerlyTelemetry
{
    public const string SourceName = "Ledgerly";

    public static readonly ActivitySource ActivitySource = new(SourceName);

    private static readonly Meter Meter = new(SourceName);

    public static readonly Counter<long> TransactionsRecorded =
        Meter.CreateCounter<long>("ledgerly.transactions.recorded", unit: "{transaction}", description: "Trades recorded via API or import.");

    public static readonly Counter<long> ImportsProcessed =
        Meter.CreateCounter<long>("ledgerly.imports.processed", unit: "{import}", description: "Broker imports processed, tagged by outcome.");

    public static readonly Histogram<double> ImportDuration =
        Meter.CreateHistogram<double>("ledgerly.imports.duration", unit: "s", description: "Time to process one broker import.");
    // ...
}

Ledgerly.Core references no OpenTelemetry package. ActivitySource and Meter are plain .NET types, and the service defaults subscribe to the name "Ledgerly". Two notes on choices you might make differently:

  • Static vs IMeterFactory. Since .NET 8, IMeterFactory is registered in DI and is the recommended way to create meters in ASP.NET Core apps (the container owns the meter's lifetime). Ledgerly uses a static class for simplicity, because the meter is shared by the API and the worker. For a new library, consider the factory (metrics instrumentation).
  • Counters vs histograms. A counter answers "how many"; a histogram answers "how long" and gives you percentiles. Units follow the OTel convention: s for seconds, {import} for a count of things.

Using them is one line each, with tags as key/value pairs:

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Worker/ImportProcessor.cs

        var request = args.Message.Body.ToObjectFromJson<ImportRequested>();
        using var activity = LedgerlyTelemetry.ActivitySource.StartActivity("import process");
        activity?.SetTag("ledgerly.import.id", request?.ImportId);
        // ...
            LedgerlyTelemetry.ImportsProcessed.Add(1, new KeyValuePair<string, object?>("outcome", outcome));
            LedgerlyTelemetry.ImportDuration.Record(Stopwatch.GetElapsedTime(started).TotalSeconds);

Note activity?.: StartActivity returns null when nothing is listening (in unit tests, for example), so always use the null-conditional operator.

Carrying the trace across the outbox

HTTP calls propagate trace context automatically: HttpClient adds a traceparent header and ASP.NET Core reads it. A database row doesn't. The outbox is a gap where the trace would normally break: the API commits a row, and seconds later a different process publishes it. Ledgerly closes the gap by storing the W3C trace context with the row.

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Core/Messaging/Outbox.cs

    // W3C trace context of the request that created the message, so the trace continues in the worker.
    public string? TraceParent { get; init; }
    // ...
        db.OutboxMessages.Add(new OutboxMessage
        {
            Type = typeof(T).Name,
            Destination = destination,
            Payload = JsonSerializer.Serialize(message),
            TraceParent = System.Diagnostics.Activity.Current?.Id,
        });

Activity.Current?.Id is the W3C traceparent string of the current span (version, trace id, span id, flags). The relay then starts its span as a child of that id:

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Worker/OutboxRelay.cs

            // Continue the trace of the HTTP request that created the message.
            using var activity = LedgerlyTelemetry.ActivitySource.StartActivity(
                $"outbox publish {message.Destination}", ActivityKind.Producer, message.TraceParent);

ActivityKind.Producer tells backends this span hands work to a queue. Inside it, the Service Bus SDK sends the message, and its own spans (collected through AddSource("Azure.*")) belong to the same trace. On the consumer side, the processor callback runs inside the SDK's processing span, and import process becomes a child of whatever Activity.Current is at that moment.

Ledgerly relies on the Azure SDK to carry context across the broker. The SDK's exact propagation behavior isn't something this course verified, so if you see the trace split in two at the queue, carry it yourself. The pattern is the same as the outbox:

// illustrative, not in the sample
// Producer: copy the current trace context onto the message.
var outgoing = new ServiceBusMessage(payload) { MessageId = id };
if (Activity.Current?.Id is { } traceParent)
{
    outgoing.ApplicationProperties["traceparent"] = traceParent;
}

// Consumer: continue that trace explicitly.
var parentId = args.Message.ApplicationProperties.TryGetValue("traceparent", out var value) ? value as string : null;
using var activity = LedgerlyTelemetry.ActivitySource.StartActivity("import process", ActivityKind.Consumer, parentId);

💡 Anywhere work crosses a boundary that isn't HTTP (a table, a queue, a file), the trace breaks unless you carry traceparent across it yourself.

🗺️ One import, one trace

This is the trace Maya opened in the story. Every arrow below is a span or a log record with the same trace id, as long as the context is carried across the outbox row and the queue.

sequenceDiagram
    participant B as "Browser"
    participant A as "Api"
    participant D as "SQL"
    participant R as "OutboxRelay"
    participant Q as "Service Bus"
    participant W as "ImportProcessor"
    B->>A: POST import with traceparent
    A->>D: Import row plus outbox row with TraceParent
    A-->>B: 202 Accepted
    R->>D: read pending outbox rows
    R->>R: span outbox publish as child of TraceParent
    R->>Q: send with MessageId equal to outbox id
    Q->>W: deliver message
    W->>W: span import process
    W->>D: trades plus ProcessedMessages in one SaveChanges

When you search Application Insights for the beta tester's import id, you land on the last log line ("Import ... completed_with_errors: 47 imported, 3 skipped"). From there, the trace id leads back to the upload request, and the import's Error field (returned by GET /api/imports/{importId}) lists the first rows that failed to parse. That's the answer Elena wanted the same day.

🧠 What not to send: PII, secrets and cardinality

Observability data is data. It is stored, retained, queried by many people and billed by volume. Three rules keep it safe and affordable.

1. No secrets, no unnecessary personal data. Log ids, not content. Ledgerly logs ImportId, MessageId, Symbol and counts. It never logs the CSV content, the JWT, connection strings or the user's name. Jonas has a short list for PR reviews:

Never log Log instead
Tokens, keys, connection strings, passwords Nothing. If you must, a fixed marker like "token present: true"
File contents and request bodies Ids, sizes, row counts, line numbers of errors
Names, emails, account numbers The internal owner id, or nothing
Full exception messages from third parties that echo input Your own message plus the exception object (and review what the exception contains)

Remember that spans carry attributes too. url.full on an outgoing request includes the query string, so never put secrets in URLs.

2. Mind metric cardinality. Every unique combination of tag values on a metric becomes a separate time series. outcome on ledgerly.imports.processed has three values ("completed", "completed_with_errors", "rejected"), which is perfect. AlertsTriggered is tagged by symbol, which is fine while the simulator knows a short fixed list of symbols, and worth revisiting if users can enter any ticker. A tag like userId or importId on a metric is a mistake: put high-cardinality values on spans and logs, where each event is stored individually, and keep metric tags to small, bounded sets.

Value On a span or log? As a metric tag?
ImportId, MessageId, PortfolioId ✅ Yes ❌ No, unbounded
outcome, HTTP status code, operation name ✅ Yes ✅ Yes, a small set
symbol ✅ Yes ⚠️ Only while the list is bounded
User id or email ⚠️ Internal id only ❌ Never

3. Choose levels on purpose. Information for business facts you want in production ("Import ... completed"). Warning for degraded but handled situations ("Price for AAPL unavailable"). Error for failures that need attention ("Import ... failed on delivery 3"). Debug and Trace for local investigation only. Generated [LoggerMessage] methods cost almost nothing when a level is disabled, so you can leave debug messages in the code without paying for them.

💡 Ids on spans, small enums on metrics, nothing secret anywhere. Cardinality and PII mistakes are both cheap to make and expensive to clean up.

⚠️ Common traps

Trap What happens Do this instead
String interpolation in log calls Every message is unique, nothing is queryable, and the string is built even when the level is off Message templates, ideally [LoggerMessage]
Forgetting AddSource / AddMeter for your own names Custom spans and metrics are silently dropped, and you think the code doesn't run Subscribe to your names in one shared place ("Ledgerly" in the service defaults)
Calling activity.SetTag without ?. NullReferenceException whenever no listener is attached, typically in tests activity?.SetTag(...)
Initializing both UseAzureMonitor() and UseMicrosoftOpenTelemetry() 🔧 Duplicate or conflicting pipelines, per the docs Pick one distro per app
Health probes in traces Thousands of meaningless spans per hour per replica, and an inflated bill Filter /health and /alive out, as ConfigureOpenTelemetry does
User ids or import ids as metric tags Cardinality explosion, slow queries, cost High-cardinality values go on spans and logs only
Assuming the trace survives a queue or a table The worker's span starts a new trace, and you can't connect cause and effect Store traceparent (outbox TraceParent column) and start the next span from it

🧰 Helpers

A [LoggerMessage] starter you can paste into any class:

// illustrative, not in the sample
public sealed partial class SomethingHandler(ILogger<SomethingHandler> logger)
{
    [LoggerMessage(Level = LogLevel.Information, Message = "Order {OrderId} placed with {LineCount} lines")]
    private static partial void LogOrderPlaced(ILogger logger, Guid orderId, int lineCount);

    [LoggerMessage(Level = LogLevel.Error, Message = "Order {OrderId} failed")]
    private static partial void LogOrderFailed(ILogger logger, Guid orderId, Exception ex);
}

OTLP variables worth knowing (Aspire sets these for you locally):

Variable Purpose
OTEL_SERVICE_NAME service.name, shown as the role name in Application Insights
OTEL_RESOURCE_ATTRIBUTES Extra resource attributes, for example deployment.environment=beta
OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT Where to send OTLP
OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_PROTOCOL grpc or http/protobuf
OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_HEADERS Auth headers for a hosted OTLP backend

Make it permanent:

  • Keep telemetry setup only in the shared service defaults (ServiceDefaults.Extensions.cs in the toolkit), so every new service is observable on day one.
  • Treat warnings as errors so SYSLIB1015 (an unused [LoggerMessage] parameter) fails the build.
  • Add "no interpolated log messages, no PII, no high-cardinality metric tags, new spans use activity?." to pr-review-checklist.md, and ask Copilot to check it via copilot-instructions.md (both in the toolkit).
  • Put a ledgerly. prefix on every custom span attribute and metric name, and record the naming rule in an ADR.

🧪 Try it yourself (optional, ~25 min)

You need a container runtime for Aspire.

  1. Start everything and open the dashboard link printed in the console: bash cd sample aspire run
  2. Create a token, then use src/Ledgerly.Api/Ledgerly.Api.http to create a portfolio and upload a small broker CSV to the imports endpoint. bash dotnet user-jwts create --project src/Ledgerly.Api
  3. In Structured logs, filter by the worker resource and find "Import ... completed". Expand it and look at the properties: ImportId, Outcome, RowsImported, RowsSkipped, plus the trace id.
  4. Click the trace id. In the waterfall, find the API's POST span, the outbox publish imports span, and the import process span. Check whether they share one trace id or the trace splits at Service Bus. Either answer is a finding worth writing down.
  5. Open Metrics, choose the worker, and find ledgerly.imports.processed. Look at the outcome dimension. Then find http.server.request.duration on the api and check that no /health or /alive requests appear in Traces.
  6. Break something on purpose: in src/Ledgerly.Api/Services/ValuationService.cs, remove ({Reason}) from the LogPriceUnavailable template so the reason parameter is unused. Build with dotnet build Ledgerly.slnx and look for SYSLIB1015; with warnings as errors it fails the build. Revert the change.

✅ Recap

  1. Logs are structured events, not lines in a file: use message templates and [LoggerMessage], and put business ids in properties so you can query them.
  2. OpenTelemetry unifies traces (Activity), metrics (Meter) and logs (ILogger). The service defaults subscribe to "Ledgerly", filter probe noise, and pick OTLP or Azure Monitor from configuration (one distro, never two).
  3. Traces cross HTTP automatically but not tables or queues: Ledgerly stores TraceParent in the outbox and continues it in the relay, and you keep secrets, PII and high-cardinality tags out of telemetry.

Next → Chapter 16: Observability II: Azure Monitor, KQL and SLOs

Chapter 16: Observability II: Azure Monitor, KQL and SLOs

⏱ About 35 min · You'll learn: where Ledgerly's telemetry lands in Azure, how to answer real questions with KQL, and how to turn "is it healthy?" into SLIs, SLOs, error budgets and alerts that don't wake people up for nothing.

📖 Story: prove it with a number

Elena wants to open the beta to five hundred more users next month. In the planning meeting she asks the obvious question: "Is the app healthy enough for that?" You say yes. It feels healthy: the dashboard was green when you last looked, and nobody has complained since the import fix.

Priya puts her coffee down. "Is the app healthy? Prove it with a number." She isn't being difficult. She's the one who will carry the pager when five hundred more people arrive, and she has been burned before by "it feels fine". At her last job, an alert fired on every single exception, and after two weeks everyone had a mail rule that moved those alerts to a folder nobody opened. "I don't want more alerts," she says. "I want fewer, better ones. Tell me what 'healthy' means for Ledgerly, measure it, and page me only when users are actually hurting."

You think back to Nordbank. Health there was SCOM turning a server icon red, and a monthly uptime number someone typed into a slide. You open your notebook under Habits I brought with me and write: "Healthy = the server is up." Then you open the Application Insights resource that monitoring.bicep created and start learning KQL.

All targets, percentages, request counts and budgets in this chapter are illustrative. Product behavior and dates from the docs are marked 🔧.

🏚️ Old way → 🚀 New way

What you did before What you do now Why it changed
SCOM or perfmon watching CPU and disk on named servers Application Insights and a Log Analytics workspace receiving OpenTelemetry from every replica There are no named servers, and CPU doesn't tell you whether users are happy
Grep and Excel to answer a question about last week KQL queries over AppRequests, AppDependencies, AppTraces and friends Telemetry is a queryable dataset, not a pile of text
An email for every exception Alerts on symptoms users feel (error-budget burn rate), routed by action groups Noisy alerts get ignored, and ignored alerts are worse than none
"Uptime 99.9%" typed into a monthly slide SLIs computed from real requests, SLOs agreed with the product owner, error budgets that drive priorities A target you don't measure is a wish
A ping to default.aspx from a monitoring box Availability standard tests from several Azure regions URL ping tests are deprecated 🔧, and one location can't tell you about the internet
Log everything at Debug "just in case" Sampling, log levels, a daily cap and retention chosen on purpose Ingestion is billed by volume 💰

💡 Healthy isn't a feeling or a green icon. It's a number your users would agree with, measured continuously.

🧠 Where the telemetry lands

In chapter 15 you saw the switch in the service defaults: when APPLICATIONINSIGHTS_CONNECTION_STRING is set, UseAzureMonitor() exports traces, metrics and logs to Application Insights. In Azure, Bicep sets that variable for every container app, and the resource it points to is created by one small module.

💻 In Ledgerly: one workspace, one Application Insights

📄 sample/infra/modules/monitoring.bicep

// Observability backbone: one Log Analytics workspace (logs of the platform + apps) and
// one workspace-based Application Insights resource (OpenTelemetry traces, metrics, logs).
// ...
resource workspace 'Microsoft.OperationalInsights/workspaces@2023-09-01' = {
  name: 'log-${name}'
  location: location
  tags: tags
  properties: {
    sku: { name: 'PerGB2018' }
    retentionInDays: 30
    workspaceCapping: { dailyQuotaGb: 1 } // 💰 a hard daily cap keeps a learning subscription safe
  }
}

resource appInsights 'Microsoft.Insights/components@2020-02-02' = {
  name: 'appi-${name}'
  location: location
  tags: tags
  kind: 'web'
  properties: {
    Application_Type: 'web'
    WorkspaceResourceId: workspace.id
  }
}

📄 sample/infra/modules/apps.bicep

var commonEnv = [
  { name: 'AZURE_CLIENT_ID', value: identityClientId } // makes Azure SDK credentials pick the user-assigned identity
  { name: 'APPLICATIONINSIGHTS_CONNECTION_STRING', value: appInsightsConnectionString }
]

WorkspaceResourceId is the important line. Classic Application Insights resources are retired; every Application Insights resource is now workspace-based, storing its data in a Log Analytics workspace and billed through Log Analytics (create a workspace-based resource). The Container Apps environment (environment.bicep) also sends its platform and console logs to the same workspace, so one place holds everything.

Two names for the same data

You'll query the same telemetry from two places, and the table names differ. The Application Insights Logs blade shows the classic names; the Log Analytics workspace shows the App* names. This course uses the workspace names, because that's where the data really lives and where cross-resource queries work.

Application Insights name Workspace table What lands there from Ledgerly
requests AppRequests Incoming HTTP requests (server spans)
dependencies AppDependencies Outgoing calls: HttpClient to marketdata, SQL, Service Bus
traces AppTraces ILogger records, including every [LoggerMessage]
exceptions AppExceptions Exceptions recorded on logs and spans
customMetrics AppMetrics Meter instruments, for example ledgerly.imports.processed
availabilityResults AppAvailabilityResults Availability test results

Columns are renamed too: timestamp becomes TimeGenerated and cloud_RoleName becomes AppRoleName (data model). AppRoleName comes from OTEL_SERVICE_NAME, which is why apps.bicep sets it to api, worker and marketdata. Your custom spans also appear as requests or dependencies depending on their kind; check the data model page when a span isn't where you expect it.

The portal experiences sit on top of the same data. Application Map draws api, worker, marketdata, SQL and Service Bus as a graph keyed by role name, with call volume and failure rates (Application Map). Transaction search and the end-to-end transaction view follow one W3C trace across services, which is the cloud version of what you did in the Aspire dashboard. Failures, Performance and Live Metrics cover the rest.

flowchart LR
    subgraph ACA["Container Apps environment"]
        API["api"]
        WRK["worker"]
        MD["marketdata"]
        ENV["Environment<br/>platform and console logs"]
    end
    API -- "OTel via UseAzureMonitor" --> AI["Application Insights<br/>appi-name"]
    WRK -- "OTel via UseAzureMonitor" --> AI
    MD -- "OTel via UseAzureMonitor" --> AI
    ENV -- "appLogsConfiguration" --> LAW["Log Analytics workspace<br/>log-name, 30 days, 1 GB per day cap"]
    AI -- "stores in" --> LAW
    LAW --> KQL["KQL queries"]
    KQL --> WB["Workbooks and Grafana"]
    KQL --> AL["Log search alerts"]
    AL --> AG["Action group"]

💡 One workspace per environment, every service tagged with its role name: that's what makes "show me everything about this request" a single query.

🧠 KQL: the queries you'll actually run

Kusto Query Language (KQL) reads top to bottom: start with a table, then pipe through filters and aggregations. If you know SQL and LINQ, you'll be productive in an hour. The pattern from the docs is the one you'll use most:

AppRequests
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1h)
| summarize count(), p95=percentile(DurationMs, 95) by Name, bin(TimeGenerated, 5m)

The eight queries below are starting points, written for Ledgerly's names. TimeGenerated, AppRoleName, Name and DurationMs come straight from the docs; other columns (Success, Target, Properties, Message, Sum, ItemCount) come from the workspace schema, so check them in the Logs view's schema pane before you build alerts on them 🔧. The full set, with comments, is in kql-queries.md in the toolkit.

1. Failure rate per service, hourly

AppRequests
| where TimeGenerated > ago(24h)
| summarize total = count(), failed = countif(Success == false) by AppRoleName, bin(TimeGenerated, 1h)
| extend failurePct = round(100.0 * failed / total, 2)
| order by TimeGenerated desc

2. p95 latency per operation

AppRequests
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1h)
| where AppRoleName == "api"
| summarize requests = count(), p95 = percentile(DurationMs, 95) by Name
| order by p95 desc

Operation names come from route templates (for example the valuation route with {id:guid}), so all portfolios group into one row. That's the low-cardinality naming from chapter 15 paying off.

3. Market data dependency health

AppDependencies
| where TimeGenerated > ago(6h)
| where Target has "marketdata"
| summarize calls = count(), failed = countif(Success == false), p95 = percentile(DurationMs, 95) by bin(TimeGenerated, 5m)
| render timechart

During Priya's chaos demo in chapter 14, this chart would show a wall of failures and a p95 near the attempt timeout.

4. What users saw: partial valuations

AppTraces
| where TimeGenerated > ago(6h)
| where Message has "partial valuation"
| summarize partialPrices = count() by bin(TimeGenerated, 5m)

This counts LogPriceUnavailable from ValuationService. Query 3 tells you the vendor is failing; query 4 tells you how many prices users didn't get. Elena cares about the second one.

5. Import throughput by outcome

AppMetrics
| where TimeGenerated > ago(24h)
| where Name == "ledgerly.imports.processed"
| extend outcome = tostring(Properties["outcome"])
| summarize imports = sum(Sum) by outcome, bin(TimeGenerated, 1h)
| render columnchart

Metrics arrive pre-aggregated, so you sum the Sum column rather than counting rows. The outcome tag is one of "completed", "completed_with_errors" or "rejected".

6. Imports heading for the dead-letter queue

union AppTraces, AppExceptions
| where TimeGenerated > ago(24h)
| where isnotempty(Properties["DeliveryCount"])
| extend importId = tostring(Properties["ImportId"]), delivery = toint(Properties["DeliveryCount"])
| summarize lastDelivery = max(delivery), lastSeen = max(TimeGenerated) by importId
| where lastDelivery >= 8

LogImportCrashed logs "Import {ImportId} failed on delivery {DeliveryCount}; abandoning for retry". With maxDeliveryCount: 10, an import that reaches delivery 10 and fails is in the dead-letter queue next. The query unions both tables because a log with an exception attached may be stored as an exception. Note the gap Priya found: the ImportNotFound path dead-letters without logging, so this query can't see it. Service Bus also exposes namespace metrics in Azure Monitor (dead-lettered message counts among them, check the docs), and an alert on that metric covers every path.

7. Cold starts per service

AppTraces
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where Message has "Application started"
| summarize starts = count() by AppRoleName, bin(TimeGenerated, 1d)
| order by TimeGenerated desc

ASP.NET Core logs "Application started" once per process at Information level, and Ledgerly's appsettings.json keeps that category at Information. With apiMinReplicas = 0 (the default in main.bicepparam), every idle period ends with a cold start for the first user. If this number is high and users complain about the first request, that's the evidence for setting it to 1 💰.

8. Top exceptions

AppExceptions
| where TimeGenerated > ago(24h)
| summarize occurrences = count() by AppRoleName, ExceptionType, OuterMessage
| top 10 by occurrences

💡 Write queries in terms of what users experience (requests, partial results, stuck imports), then add the cause queries (dependencies, exceptions) you'll use to explain it.

🧠 SLIs, SLOs and error budgets

This is the answer to Priya's question. The vocabulary comes from the Google SRE Workbook (implementing SLOs):

Term Meaning Ledgerly example (illustrative)
SLI (indicator) A measured ratio of good events to all events Valuation requests that return 2xx in under 1 s, divided by all valuation requests
SLO (objective) A target for the SLI over a window 99.5% over 28 days
Error budget 1 − SLO: how much badness you're allowed 0.5% of valuation requests per 28 days
Burn rate How fast you're spending the budget compared to "exactly on target" A burn rate of 1 spends the budget in exactly 28 days. A burn rate of 10 spends it in 2.8 days

You and Elena wrote three SLOs, using slo-worksheet.md from the toolkit:

User journey SLI: good events SLO
View portfolio and valuation api requests on portfolio routes that succeed in under 1 s 99.5% over 28 days
Record a trade POST transaction requests that don't return 5xx 99.9% over 28 days
Import a broker file Imports that reach a final status within 5 minutes of upload 99% over 28 days

Two discussions came up that you'll have too:

  • Is a partial valuation "good"? The request returns 200, so a request-based SLI counts it as good. Elena disagreed: users see "prices delayed". The team kept the request SLI simple and added query 4 as a separate quality indicator with its own looser target. Be explicit about such choices rather than letting the query decide.
  • Why not 100%? Because nothing is, and because a 100% target means you can never deploy, experiment or take a dependency on a vendor. The budget is permission to take risks. When it's healthy, ship features. When it's spent, the next sprint goes to reliability. That trade-off is the real value of an SLO.

The math, with illustrative traffic: if the API serves 200,000 valuation requests in 28 days, a 99.5% SLO allows 1,000 bad ones. If a bad deploy causes 2% of requests to fail, the burn rate is 2% ÷ 0.5% = 4, and the whole month's budget is gone in a week.

An SLI and burn rate in KQL

// illustrative SLO: 99.5% of valuation requests succeed in under 1 s
let slo = 0.995;
AppRequests
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1h)
| where AppRoleName == "api" and Name has "valuation"
| summarize total = count(), good = countif(Success == true and DurationMs < 1000)
| extend sli = todouble(good) / total
| extend burnRate = (1.0 - sli) / (1.0 - slo)

Run it over 1 hour and over 6 hours. A high burn rate over a short window means something broke now; a moderate burn rate over a long window means a slow leak. The SRE Workbook recommends alerting on burn rate instead of raw thresholds, and checking a short and a long window together keeps alerts both fast and quiet.

💡 An SLO is a product decision with a number on it. Agree on it with the product owner, publish it, and let the error budget settle the "features or reliability?" argument.

🧠 Dashboards and alerts that don't cry wolf

Workbooks and Grafana

A Workbook is an interactive report in the portal that combines KQL queries, metrics and parameters (a time range, an environment). Ledgerly's "Service health" workbook has four tiles: SLI per journey (the burn-rate query), failure rate per service (query 1), market data health (queries 3 and 4) and import outcomes (query 5). If your team already lives in Grafana, Azure Managed Grafana and the built-in Dashboards with Grafana in Application Insights 🔧 read the same data (Grafana dashboards).

Alert types

Alert type Evaluates Use it for in Ledgerly
Metric alert A platform or custom metric against a threshold, evaluated frequently Service Bus dead-lettered messages above zero (check the metric name in the Service Bus docs)
Log search alert A KQL query on a schedule SLO burn rate, imports at delivery 8 or more
Activity log alert Control-plane events in the subscription Someone deleted or reconfigured a resource
Smart detection Anomalies Application Insights finds by itself A free extra signal, not a paging source

Action groups define who gets told and how: email, SMS, voice, push, webhooks, Logic Apps, Functions and more. They're reused across alerts. Email receivers now require one-time-passcode verification 🔧, so new recipients have a step to complete. Alert processing rules suppress notifications during planned maintenance (alerts overview, action groups).

Priya's alerting policy, which the team adopted:

Severity Condition (illustrative) Goes to
Page Burn rate above 10 over 1 hour and above 10 over 5 minutes On-call phone
Ticket Burn rate above 2 over 6 hours Team channel, handled next working day
Ticket Dead-lettered imports above zero Team channel, with the runbook link
No alert A single exception, a single failed request, high CPU without user impact Dashboards only

The second, short window on the page condition makes the alert stop firing quickly once the problem is fixed. Every alert that pages must link a runbook (runbook-template.md in the toolkit); chapter 19 covers on-call and postmortems.

Availability tests

Burn-rate alerts need traffic. At 3 a.m. there may be none, so an outage can go unnoticed until the first user arrives. An availability test sends synthetic requests from several Azure regions. Use standard tests: they support SSL certificate checks, HTTP verbs and custom headers. URL ping tests are deprecated and retire on 2028-09-30 🔧 (the date was extended, so older articles say 2026). See availability tests. Results land in AppAvailabilityResults.

For Ledgerly, point the test at the API's /health endpoint. It checks that the replica can reach its essential dependencies, it is mapped in every environment, and it reveals nothing beyond one word. If apiMinReplicas is 0, remember that the test itself keeps waking the API, which removes cold starts for users but also costs replica time 💰.

💡 Page on symptoms that burn the error budget, ticket on causes, and put everything else on a dashboard. Fewer alerts, each one meaning "a human must act now".

🗺️ From bad requests to a page

flowchart TB
    R["AppRequests<br/>valuation requests"] --> SLI["SLI query<br/>good divided by total"]
    SLI --> BR{"Burn rate"}
    BR -- "above 10 over 1 h and 5 min" --> PG["Page on-call<br/>action group"]
    BR -- "above 2 over 6 h" --> TK["Ticket<br/>team channel"]
    BR -- "below 2" --> OK["Dashboard only"]
    PG --> RB["Runbook"]
    TK --> RB
    AV["Availability standard test<br/>/health from regions"] -- "fails" --> PG
    DLQ["Dead-lettered imports"] -- "above zero" --> TK

🧠 Sampling and cost 💰

Log Analytics ingestion is often the surprise cost of a cloud-native system. Every request, dependency call, log line and metric point is billed by volume, and a chatty service at Debug level can cost more than the service itself. The levers, from most to least effective:

Lever Where in Ledgerly Trade-off
Don't produce noise Probes filtered out of traces; health endpoints excluded from HTTP metrics; Microsoft.EntityFrameworkCore.Database.Command and Azure.Messaging.ServiceBus at Warning in appsettings.json None, really. Do this first
Sampling The distro's default sampling 🔧 You keep representative traces, not every one
Retention retentionInDays: 30 in monitoring.bicep Longer retention for audits costs more
Daily cap dailyQuotaGb: 1 in monitoring.bicep A hard cap: once it's hit, you're blind until the next day

Sampling. Per the sampling docs, the Microsoft distro defaults to rate-limited sampling of 5 traces per second 🔧, fixed-percentage sampling is the alternative, and trace-based log sampling is on by default in .NET: logs that belong to a trace that wasn't sampled are dropped too. That keeps traces complete (you never get half a trace), but it means a rare log line can disappear at high volume. Two consequences for your queries:

  • Ratios stay correct, raw counts don't. Failure rates and SLIs (ratios) are fine on sampled data. For absolute counts, the workspace tables carry an ItemCount column that says how many original items each stored row represents; sum(ItemCount) estimates the real total 🔧.
  • Metrics are aggregated, not sampled per trace. Meter counters are summed in the process before export, so ledgerly.imports.processed stays a reliable count even when traces are sampled. Use metrics for counts you must trust.

The daily cap is a safety net, not a budget. One GB a day is right for a learning subscription: a logging bug can't produce a shocking bill. For the production environment, the team raised it and set a Cost Management budget with alerts instead (chapter 19), because going blind at 4 p.m. during an incident is worse than paying for a few extra gigabytes.

To see what's filling the workspace, count rows by table and service:

union withsource = TableName AppRequests, AppDependencies, AppTraces, AppExceptions, AppMetrics
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| summarize rows = count() by TableName, AppRoleName
| order by rows desc

For prices, use the Azure Pricing Calculator; per-GB ingestion and retention prices change and vary by region 🔧.

💡 Cut noise at the source first, sample second, cap last. Metrics are your exact counters; traces and logs are your representative evidence.

⚠️ Common traps

Trap What happens Do this instead
Querying classic names in one place and App* tables in another Queries "don't work" when copied between the Application Insights blade and the workspace Pick the workspace names (AppRequests, TimeGenerated, AppRoleName) and keep a shared query file
Alerting on every exception or a fixed error count Alert fatigue, mail rules, and the one alert that matters gets missed Alert on SLO burn rate over two windows; send causes to dashboards and tickets
Counting rows on sampled data Request totals look far lower than reality Use ratios, sum(ItemCount) 🔧, or metrics for exact counts
Setting a 100% SLO, or none Either nothing can ever ship, or "healthy" stays a feeling Pick a target users would accept, and an error budget policy agreed with the product owner
Relying only on request-based alerts At night there's no traffic, so an outage stays silent until the first user hits it Add an availability standard test (not URL ping, which retires 🔧)
A low daily cap in production Telemetry stops mid-incident, exactly when you need it Use the cap for dev and learning, and budgets plus sampling for production 💰
Assuming every failure path logs ImportNotFound dead-letters silently, and log queries miss it Pair log alerts with platform metrics (dead-lettered message counts)

🧰 Helpers

The SLO one-pager (the full version is slo-worksheet.md in the toolkit):

Journey:            View portfolio valuation
SLI (good events):  api requests on valuation route, Success == true and DurationMs < 1000
SLI (all events):   api requests on valuation route
SLO:                99.5% over 28 days
Error budget:       0.5% of requests
Page:               burn rate > 10 over 1 h and 5 min
Ticket:             burn rate > 2 over 6 h
Budget policy:      when spent, the next sprint prioritizes reliability work
Owner:              Priya (SRE) and Elena (product)

Make it permanent:

  • Keep the queries in version control: kql-queries.md in the toolkit is the team's shared file, and every alert references a query in it.
  • Define workbooks and alert rules as Bicep next to monitoring.bicep once they stabilize, so a new environment gets them on day one. Tomás's rule applies: if it's not in git, it doesn't exist.
  • Add "which SLO does this change affect?" and "does every new failure path log or emit a metric?" to pr-review-checklist.md.
  • Review SLOs and the error budget in a monthly meeting with Elena, and record target changes in an ADR (adr-template.md).

🧪 Try it yourself (optional, ~40 min)

These steps need the Azure environment from chapter 12. If you don't have it, run the KQL in your head against the tables above, and do step 1 only.

  1. Locally, start the system with cd sample and aspire run, create some traffic with src/Ledgerly.Api/Ledgerly.Api.http, and look at the Traces and Metrics views in the dashboard. Every concept in this chapter has a local twin there.

🧪 Not run by the author: this step was written from the docs and validated with bicep build only. Expect small differences.

  1. In the Azure portal, open the Application Insights resource appi-<name>, then Application Map. Check that api, worker and marketdata appear with their dependencies.
  2. Open the Log Analytics workspace log-<name>, then Logs. Run queries 1, 2 and 7 from this chapter. Note which columns exist in the schema pane and adjust the queries if your schema differs.
  3. Save the burn-rate query, then create a log search alert from it that fires when burnRate is above 10. Create an action group with your email (complete the verification step 🔧).
  4. Create an availability standard test against https://<api-fqdn>/health. Wait for results, then query AppAvailabilityResults.
  5. Optional: if your environment allows it, temporarily raise market data failures (in Azure, only through configuration, because /chaos exists only in Development) and watch queries 3 and 4 react. Reset it afterwards.
  6. Fill in slo-worksheet.md for one Ledgerly journey you care about.

✅ Recap

  1. Ledgerly's telemetry lands in one workspace-based Application Insights resource backed by Log Analytics; query it with KQL using the App* tables, TimeGenerated and AppRoleName.
  2. "Healthy" is an SLI with an SLO and an error budget agreed with the product owner, and alerts page only when the burn rate says users are hurting, with availability tests covering quiet hours.
  3. Ingestion is the surprise cost 💰: cut noise at the source, rely on sampling and exact metrics, and treat the daily cap as a learning-environment safety net.

Next → Chapter 17: Testing strategy

Chapter 17: Testing strategy

⏱ About 30 min · You'll learn: how to shape a test pyramid for a small cloud-native system, how to run integration tests against a real SQL Server with WebApplicationFactory and Testcontainers, why the .NET 10 SDK pushed Ledgerly onto Microsoft.Testing.Platform, and where end-to-end and contract tests fit.

📖 Story: The 409 on every trade

It is Tuesday morning and Elena forwards a message from a beta tester: "I can't record any trades. Every time I press Save it says the portfolio changed and I should retry. I retried eleven times." You try it on the beta environment and get the same answer, a 409 Conflict with "Portfolio changed while recording the trade. Retry the request."

The unit tests are green. HoldingCalculator is correct, the CSV parser is correct, and the price generator is correct. The bug isn't in any of them. It lives in the seam between your code and the database: a small refactor added the new Transaction through portfolio.Transactions instead of through the DbContext, and EF Core decided the row already existed. Nothing in the test suite sent a real trade to a real database.

Maya pulls up a chair. "At Nordbank, how did you test the data layer?" You think about it. A test database on a shared server that someone restored every few weeks, a few NUnit tests that only ran on the build machine, and a lot of manual clicking before each release. "So mostly in production," she says, kindly. You open your notebook to Habits I brought with me and write: "Unit tests for logic, manual testing for everything else."

"Let's write the test that would have caught it," Maya says. "Then let's make sure it can't be skipped by accident."

Numbers in the Ledgerly story (test durations, tester counts) are illustrative. The 23 tests in the sample (15 unit, 8 integration against real SQL Server) are real.

🏚️ Old way → 🚀 New way

What you did before What you do now Why it changed
MSTest or NUnit tests run by vstest.console on the build server xUnit v3 test projects that are executables, run by Microsoft.Testing.Platform through dotnet test The .NET 10 SDK supports MTP mode natively, and xUnit v3 is built for it
A shared test database restored "every few weeks" A throwaway SQL Server per test run (Testcontainers) or a unique LocalDB database per run Tests that share state fail randomly, and nobody trusts them
The EF in-memory provider, or mocked repositories The real API in memory (WebApplicationFactory<Program>) against a real SQL Server The bugs that hurt are in the seams: SQL translation, keys, concurrency tokens, migrations
DateTime.Now in domain code and Thread.Sleep in tests TimeProvider in code, FakeTimeProvider in tests Time becomes a dependency you control, so tests are fast and deterministic
Manual regression clicking before each release End-to-end smoke tests of the whole system, and contract checks for the SPA Releases are frequent now, so humans can't be the regression suite
Coverage from a separate tool with its own runner dotnet test --coverage with Microsoft.Testing.Extensions.CodeCoverage One runner, one command

💡 A test suite is a portfolio. You spend most on cheap, fast tests and buy a few expensive ones only where the cheap ones can't see.

🧠 Concept 1: A test pyramid for this system

The classic pyramid says: many unit tests, fewer integration tests, very few end-to-end tests. The shape depends on where your bugs live. Ledgerly has a small domain and a lot of plumbing (HTTP, auth, EF Core, SQL, Service Bus, a second service). So the useful layers are:

Layer What it proves in Ledgerly Speed Where it runs
Unit Holding math, alert rules, CSV parsing, price generation Milliseconds Every build, every PR
Integration (in-process API plus real SQL) Endpoints, auth, validation, EF mappings, concurrency, degradation Seconds Every PR (CI has Docker)
Contract The OpenAPI document Leo's SPA depends on hasn't changed by accident Seconds Every PR
End-to-end (whole AppHost) Services find each other, health checks pass, messages flow Minutes Nightly, or before a release

Unit tests in Ledgerly follow two habits that pay off quickly. First, time is injected. PriceGenerator takes a TimeProvider, so the test controls the clock instead of sleeping:

💻 In Ledgerly: a clock you can move

📄 sample/tests/Ledgerly.UnitTests/PriceTests.cs

    [Fact]
    public void Prices_are_stable_within_a_minute_and_move_over_time()
    {
        // TimeProvider makes "time" a dependency you can control in tests. No more DateTime.Now in domain code.
        var clock = new FakeTimeProvider(new DateTimeOffset(2026, 10, 28, 12, 0, 0, TimeSpan.Zero));
        var generator = new PriceGenerator(clock);

        var first = generator.GetQuote("aapl");
        clock.Advance(TimeSpan.FromSeconds(20));
        var sameMinute = generator.GetQuote("AAPL");
        clock.Advance(TimeSpan.FromHours(3));
        var later = generator.GetQuote("AAPL");
        // ...
    }

Three hours pass in a microsecond. FakeTimeProvider comes from the Microsoft.Extensions.TimeProvider.Testing package, and production code gets TimeProvider.System instead.

Second, every production bug becomes a test. The old IIS server had a Lithuanian culture, and "190,10" broke imports. The fix was invariant-culture parsing. The test pins the culture to lt-LT and proves the fix holds:

💻 In Ledgerly: the culture regression test

📄 sample/tests/Ledgerly.UnitTests/BrokerCsvParserTests.cs

    [Fact]
    public void Decimal_parsing_ignores_server_culture()
    {
        // The old IIS box had lt-LT culture and "190,10" broke imports. Invariant culture, always.
        var previous = Thread.CurrentThread.CurrentCulture;
        Thread.CurrentThread.CurrentCulture = new System.Globalization.CultureInfo("lt-LT");
        try
        {
            var result = BrokerCsvParser.Parse($"{Header}\n2026-09-01,buy,AAPL,5,190.10,BRK-1");
            Assert.Equal(190.10m, Assert.Single(result.Trades).Price);
        }
        finally
        {
            Thread.CurrentThread.CurrentCulture = previous;
        }
    }

Note the try/finally. A test that changes shared state (culture, environment variables, a static flag) must put it back, or the next test inherits it and fails for no visible reason.

Ledgerly uses xUnit v3 (xunit.v3 4.0.2 🔧). xUnit v2 is in maintenance mode: critical fixes only, with all new features going to v3. v3 test projects are executables (OutputType Exe), which is what lets them run on Microsoft.Testing.Platform (Concept 3). If your team prefers another framework, MSTest and NUnit also support MTP, and TUnit (1.76.28 🔧) is a newer, MTP-only, source-generated framework that runs tests in parallel by default.

💡 Unit tests are for logic that has no I/O. If a test needs a mock of DbContext to say anything, it probably belongs one layer up.

🧠 Concept 2: Integration tests against a real database

WebApplicationFactory<TEntryPoint> (package Microsoft.AspNetCore.Mvc.Testing, 10.0.14 🔧) starts your real API in memory on a TestServer (integration tests docs). Requests go through the real middleware, routing, authentication, validation and EF Core code. Only the network socket is missing. Because Program.cs uses top-level statements, it ends with public partial class Program; so the test project can see it.

The question is what to put behind it. Ledgerly answers: a real SQL Server, never the EF in-memory provider. The factory looks for one in two places.

💻 In Ledgerly: where the database comes from

📄 sample/tests/Ledgerly.IntegrationTests/LedgerlyApiFactory.cs

public sealed class LedgerlyApiFactory : WebApplicationFactory<Program>, IAsyncLifetime
{
    // ...
    public async ValueTask InitializeAsync()
    {
        var external = Environment.GetEnvironmentVariable("LEDGERLY_TEST_SQL");
        if (!string.IsNullOrWhiteSpace(external))
        {
            var csb = new Microsoft.Data.SqlClient.SqlConnectionStringBuilder(external)
            {
                InitialCatalog = $"LedgerlyTests_{Guid.NewGuid():N}",
            };
            _connectionString = csb.ConnectionString;
        }
        else
        {
            try
            {
                _container = new MsSqlBuilder("mcr.microsoft.com/mssql/server:2022-latest").Build();
                await _container.StartAsync();
                _connectionString = _container.GetConnectionString();
            }
            catch (Exception ex) when (ex is not OutOfMemoryException)
            {
                SkipReason = $"No SQL Server available (set LEDGERLY_TEST_SQL or start Docker): {ex.GetType().Name}";
                return;
            }
        }

        await using var scope = Services.CreateAsyncScope();
        await scope.ServiceProvider.GetRequiredService<LedgerlyDbContext>().Database.MigrateAsync();
    }

There are three ideas packed in here:

  • A fresh database per run. With LEDGERLY_TEST_SQL (LocalDB on a Windows laptop), the factory creates LedgerlyTests_<guid> and deletes it in DisposeAsync. Without it, Testcontainers (Testcontainers.MsSql 4.16.0 🔧) starts a throwaway SQL Server container, which needs Docker. Either way, no test ever shares data with another run.
  • The real migrations run. MigrateAsync applies the same migrations production uses, so a broken migration fails a test, not a deployment.
  • No SQL, no failure. If neither source works, the factory records a SkipReason, and each test calls Assert.SkipWhen(...). A laptop without Docker still gets a green unit-test run. Keep that convenience in mind, because Concept 4 comes back to its cost.

The rest of the factory swaps the parts you don't want to be real:

    protected override void ConfigureWebHost(IWebHostBuilder builder)
    {
        builder.UseEnvironment("Testing");

        // UseSetting is applied before Program.cs reads configuration, so Aspire's client integration sees it.
        builder.UseSetting("ConnectionStrings:ledgerlydb", _connectionString ?? "Server=unused;Database=unused");

        builder.ConfigureLogging(logging => logging.SetMinimumLevel(LogLevel.Warning));

        builder.ConfigureTestServices(services =>
        {
            services.RemoveAll<IMarketDataClient>();
            services.AddSingleton<IMarketDataClient>(MarketData);

            services.AddAuthentication(TestAuthHandler.SchemeName)
                .AddScheme<AuthenticationSchemeOptions, TestAuthHandler>(TestAuthHandler.SchemeName, _ => { });
        });
    }
  • Market data is replaced by FakeMarketData, a dictionary of prices plus an IsDown switch that throws HttpRequestException. That's how Valuation_degrades_gracefully_when_market_data_is_down proves the IsComplete = false path from Chapter 14 without a second service.
  • Authentication is replaced by TestAuthHandler, which signs in whoever is named in an X-Test-User header. factory.CreateClientFor("alice") returns an HttpClient that is "logged in" as alice, so Users_cannot_see_each_others_portfolios can test ownership with two clients in three lines. The handler is in the test project only, and its doc comment says so: "Test code only!"
  • The connection string goes in through UseSetting, because Aspire's SQL client integration reads configuration while Program.cs builds the host, and later overrides would arrive too late.

💻 In Ledgerly: the bug that started this chapter

With that factory in place, you write a test that buys 5 shares and checks for 201 Created. It fails with 409. Now you can see the trap, and the fix sits in the endpoint with a comment so nobody "simplifies" it back:

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Api/Endpoints/PortfolioEndpoints.cs

        // EF trap: the Id is already set (Guid v7, generated in C#). Adding via portfolio.Transactions would make EF
        // assume the row EXISTS and send an UPDATE (0 rows -> DbUpdateConcurrencyException). Add it explicitly.
        db.Transactions.Add(transaction);
        portfolio.UpdatedAt = DateTimeOffset.UtcNow; // bumps rowversion: two parallel "sell all" requests cannot both win

        try
        {
            await db.SaveChangesAsync(ct);
        }
        catch (DbUpdateConcurrencyException)
        {
            return TypedResults.Problem("Portfolio changed while recording the trade. Retry the request.", statusCode: StatusCodes.Status409Conflict);
        }

The entity key is a client-generated Guid.CreateVersion7(). When EF discovers a new object in a tracked navigation collection and its key is already set, it assumes the row exists and sends an UPDATE. Zero rows are affected, and EF reports that as a concurrency conflict. The endpoint's honest 409 handler then made the bug look like a user problem. Chapter 5 covers the EF side. The testing lesson is simpler: no unit test could see this, because it only happens when EF talks to a real database.

💡 Mock what you don't own and can't run (a vendor API). Run what you can (your database, your API, your migrations).

🗺️ How the pieces fit

The first diagram is Ledgerly's test portfolio. Width is roughly "how many tests", and the label says when each layer runs.

flowchart TB
    E2E["End-to-end<br/>Aspire testing builder, whole AppHost<br/>nightly"]
    CON["Contract<br/>OpenAPI document diff for Leo's SPA<br/>every PR"]
    INT["Integration<br/>WebApplicationFactory plus real SQL<br/>8 tests, every PR"]
    UNIT["Unit<br/>holdings, alerts, CSV, prices<br/>15 tests, every PR"]
    E2E -- "fewer, slower, broader" --> CON
    CON --> INT
    INT -- "more, faster, narrower" --> UNIT

The second diagram is one run of the integration test class. The factory is created once per test class (IClassFixture<LedgerlyApiFactory>), so the database and the in-memory server are shared by the eight tests, and each test uses its own user and its own portfolio.

sequenceDiagram
    participant X as "xUnit v3 on MTP"
    participant F as "LedgerlyApiFactory"
    participant S as "SQL Server"
    participant A as "Api in TestServer"
    participant M as "FakeMarketData"
    X->>F: InitializeAsync
    F->>S: LEDGERLY_TEST_SQL or Testcontainers
    F->>S: MigrateAsync on a fresh database
    X->>F: CreateClientFor carol
    X->>A: POST transaction with X-Test-User header
    A->>S: INSERT transaction and bump rowversion
    A-->>X: 201 Created
    X->>A: GET valuation
    A->>M: GetQuotesAsync
    M-->>A: prices or HttpRequestException
    A-->>X: 200 with IsComplete true or false
    X->>F: DisposeAsync drops database or container

🧠 Concept 3: Microsoft.Testing.Platform on the .NET 10 SDK

The first time you ran the new suite with the .NET 10 SDK, dotnet test didn't run a single test. It stopped with:

Testing with VSTest target is no longer supported by Microsoft.Testing.Platform on .NET 10 SDK

Here's what happened. dotnet test has two modes. VSTest mode is the default and was the only mode before .NET 10. MTP mode is new in the .NET 10 SDK 🔧. Microsoft.Testing.Platform is a lightweight runner embedded in the test project itself, so a v3 test project is just an executable you can also start with dotnet run. Running MTP projects through VSTest mode is now legacy, and MTP v2 removes that support when you use the .NET 10 SDK. That's the wall Ledgerly hit with xUnit v3 4.x: the old path was closed.

The fix is one setting at the repository root:

💻 In Ledgerly: opting in to MTP

📄 sample/global.json

{
  "sdk": {
    "version": "10.0.100",
    "rollForward": "latestFeature"
  },
  "test": {
    "runner": "Microsoft.Testing.Platform"
  }
}

MTP mode requires MTP 1.7 or later 🔧 (dotnet test docs). After the switch, you name the solution with --solution, and MTP options go directly on the command line (in VSTest mode they came after --).

Then you clean up the test projects. The VSTest-era packages (Microsoft.NET.Test.Sdk, xunit.runner.visualstudio, coverlet.collector) are gone. Coverage now comes from an MTP extension:

📄 sample/tests/Ledgerly.IntegrationTests/Ledgerly.IntegrationTests.csproj

  <PropertyGroup>
    <IsPackable>false</IsPackable>
    <IsTestProject>true</IsTestProject>
    <OutputType>Exe</OutputType>
  </PropertyGroup>

  <ItemGroup>
    <PackageReference Include="xunit.v3" />
    <PackageReference Include="Microsoft.Testing.Extensions.CodeCoverage" />
    <PackageReference Include="Microsoft.AspNetCore.Mvc.Testing" />
    <PackageReference Include="Testcontainers.MsSql" />
  </ItemGroup>

There are no versions here because Central Package Management keeps them in Directory.Packages.props (Chapter 3). The CI step uses the same command you run locally, plus coverage and a TRX report:

📄 .github/workflows/ci.yml

      # Integration tests start a throwaway SQL Server container with Testcontainers (Docker is on the runner).
      - name: Test
        run: >
          dotnet test --solution Ledgerly.slnx -c Release --no-build
          --coverage --coverage-output-format cobertura
          --report-xunit-trx

The workflow then uploads sample/TestResults/ as an artifact even when tests fail, so a red build always comes with evidence (Chapter 9).

One MTP behavior surprises people coming from VSTest: when zero tests run, MTP returns exit code 8, and dotnet test fails. Tomás hit it on the first nightly job, where a test filter had a typo and matched nothing. Under the old runner, that job would have stayed green forever. "I ran nothing" is not "everything passed". If a project legitimately runs no tests in some configuration, MTP lets you ignore specific exit codes; check the MTP docs for the current option 🔧, and scope it to that one job.

💡 A runner change is a good moment to delete packages. Every test package you no longer need is one less Dependabot PR and one less audit warning.

🧠 Concept 4: Tests that lie, and tests that hide

A passing test is a claim: "this behavior works". Two things can make the claim false, and Ledgerly met both.

A test that passes for the wrong reason. The first version of the oversell test bought 5 shares, sold 6, and expected 400 Bad Request. It passed. Weeks later, during the 409 investigation, you noticed something odd: the setup buy had been failing too (with the 409), so the portfolio held nothing, and selling 6 of nothing is also a validation error. The test proved a sentence that was true for an unrelated reason. The fix is to assert the setup, not just the outcome:

💻 In Ledgerly: asserting the arrange step

📄 sample/tests/Ledgerly.IntegrationTests/PortfolioApiTests.cs

    [Fact]
    public async Task Overselling_is_a_validation_error()
    {
        var client = ClientFor("carol");
        var portfolio = await CreatePortfolioAsync(client);

        await BuyAsync(client, portfolio.Id, "AAPL", 5);
        var oversell = await PostTradeAsync(client, portfolio.Id, "AAPL", TransactionType.Sell, 6);

        Assert.Equal(HttpStatusCode.BadRequest, oversell.StatusCode);
    }
    // ...
    private static async Task BuyAsync(HttpClient client, Guid portfolioId, string symbol, decimal quantity)
    {
        var response = await PostTradeAsync(client, portfolioId, symbol, TransactionType.Buy, quantity);
        Assert.Equal(HttpStatusCode.Created, response.StatusCode);
    }

BuyAsync now fails loudly if the buy doesn't return 201 Created, and CreatePortfolioAsync calls EnsureSuccessStatusCode(). A helper that hides a failed precondition is a test that lies. Whenever a test expects an error, ask "could this error come from the setup?" And watch every new test fail once before you trust it.

A test that hides. The factory skips integration tests when there's no SQL Server, which is friendly on a laptop. In CI it's a risk: if Docker were ever missing on the runner, eight tests would report "skipped" and the build would stay green, which is exactly how a regression slips through. Two cheap defenses:

  • Read the TRX report or the console summary in CI, and treat a non-zero skipped count on the integration project as a problem.
  • Make "SQL is required" explicit for CI. Ledgerly doesn't do this yet; here's one way to add it:
// illustrative, not in the sample
catch (Exception ex) when (ex is not OutOfMemoryException)
{
    // Set LEDGERLY_REQUIRE_SQL=true in the CI workflow so a missing database fails the run instead of skipping it.
    if (string.Equals(Environment.GetEnvironmentVariable("LEDGERLY_REQUIRE_SQL"), "true", StringComparison.OrdinalIgnoreCase))
    {
        throw;
    }

    SkipReason = $"No SQL Server available (set LEDGERLY_TEST_SQL or start Docker): {ex.GetType().Name}";
    return;
}

Flaky tests are the third kind of liar: sometimes green, sometimes red, with no code change. In a cloud-native system, the usual causes are shared state, real time and real networks. Ledgerly's tests avoid all three on purpose:

Cause How Ledgerly avoids it
Two tests share data A fresh database per run, a different user per test (alice, bob, carol...), unique portfolio names (P-{Guid})
Shared switches leak between tests FakeMarketData.IsDown is reset in finally, and the culture test restores the culture
Real time FakeTimeProvider instead of Thread.Sleep or DateTime.Now
Real network to a vendor FakeMarketData replaces IMarketDataClient in integration tests
Hanging tests Every call passes TestContext.Current.CancellationToken, so the runner can cancel cleanly

When a test flakes, don't add a retry. Open an issue, skip the test with a reason that links to it, and fix it within the sprint. A suite people re-run "until it's green" has stopped being a test suite.

💡 Distrust a test you've never seen fail. Distrust a green build that skipped things even more.

🧠 Concept 5: The whole system, and the contract with Leo

Integration tests run one service in one process. They can't tell you whether the API can find marketdata through service discovery, whether the worker's health checks pass with a real Service Bus emulator, or whether the migration service finishes before the API starts. The AppHost from Chapter 6 already describes all of that, and the Aspire testing builder (package Aspire.Hosting.Testing) can run it inside a test, with real containers. Ledgerly doesn't have this test yet. A first smoke test could look like this:

// illustrative, not in the sample
// Test project references Aspire.Hosting.Testing and the Ledgerly.AppHost project.
public sealed class SystemSmokeTests
{
    [Fact]
    public async Task Api_is_healthy_when_the_whole_system_runs()
    {
        var builder = await DistributedApplicationTestingBuilder.CreateAsync<Projects.Ledgerly_AppHost>();
        await using var app = await builder.BuildAsync();
        await app.StartAsync();

        // Wait until the "api" resource reports healthy before calling it (see the Aspire testing docs).
        var http = app.CreateHttpClient("api");
        var response = await http.GetAsync(new Uri("/health", UriKind.Relative), TestContext.Current.CancellationToken);

        Assert.Equal(HttpStatusCode.OK, response.StatusCode);
    }
}

This test starts SQL Server, Redis, the Service Bus emulator and four .NET projects. That's real coverage, but it's slow and needs a container runtime, so it belongs in a nightly job. Keep end-to-end tests few and broad ("the system starts and is healthy", "an import gets processed"), never one per business rule.

Contract tests protect the boundary with Leo's React SPA. Leo generates TypeScript types from the API's OpenAPI document (served at /openapi/v1.json, and only in Development in Ledgerly's Program.cs). When you renamed totalMarketValue in a branch last month, his build broke a day later, after the merge. The fix is to make the contract a reviewed file. ASP.NET Core can generate the OpenAPI document at build time with Microsoft.Extensions.ApiDescription.Server. Commit the generated document, and let CI fail when the build produces a different one that nobody committed:

# illustrative, not in the sample
# After adding Microsoft.Extensions.ApiDescription.Server to Ledgerly.Api and pointing its output at sample/contracts/
dotnet build Ledgerly.slnx -c Release
git diff --exit-code -- contracts/

Now a contract change shows up as a diff in the PR, and a CODEOWNERS rule puts Leo on the review. Additive changes (a new optional field) are usually safe. Renames, removals and type changes are breaking. Note that .NET 10 generates OpenAPI 3.1 by default 🔧, which matters if Leo's code generator only understands 3.0. For snapshots of other outputs (JSON responses, generated SQL), Verify (Verify.XunitV3 33.3.4 🔧) does approval testing for xUnit v3.

So where does each test run?

Trigger What runs Why
Every PR Build with warnings as errors, unit, integration (Testcontainers), contract diff, coverage Fast enough to block a merge, and it covers the seams
Nightly Aspire end-to-end smoke tests, longer chaos scenarios from Chapter 14 Slow or needs the whole system, so it shouldn't hold up a PR
After each deploy A smoke call to /health and one read-only endpoint in the target environment Proves this deployment, not just this code (Chapter 12)
Before big launches Load tests (Chapter 18) Answers "how much", which no functional test does

💡 Each layer answers a different question. Unit: "is the rule right?" Integration: "does it work with the real database?" End-to-end: "does the system start and talk?" Contract: "will Leo's app still work?"

⚠️ Common traps

Trap What happens Do this instead
Testing data code with the EF in-memory provider It doesn't translate SQL or behave like SQL Server, so bugs in keys, concurrency or queries pass the tests Run against real SQL Server: Testcontainers in CI, LocalDB or a container locally
Keeping VSTest settings on the .NET 10 SDK with xUnit v3 4.x "Testing with VSTest target is no longer supported..." and nothing runs Opt in to MTP in global.json, then drop Microsoft.NET.Test.Sdk, xunit.runner.visualstudio and coverlet.collector
Helpers that ignore the setup result A test passes because the setup failed (Ledgerly's oversell test) Assert every arrange step, and watch each new test fail once
Skipped tests that nobody reads Integration tests silently stop running and regressions slip through a green build Check skip counts in CI, or make the database required there
One shared database for all test runs Tests depend on each other's data and fail randomly A fresh database per run, a different user per test
Thread.Sleep and DateTime.Now in tests Slow, timing-dependent, flaky tests TimeProvider in code and FakeTimeProvider in tests
End-to-end tests for every rule A slow suite that people skip A few broad end-to-end smoke tests, nightly. Push rules down to unit tests
Silencing exit code 8 globally A typo in a filter means nothing runs, and CI stays green Treat exit code 8 as a failure, except in the one job that really expects no tests

🧰 Helpers

A test checklist for every PR (paste into the PR description or pr-review-checklist.md):

- [ ] New logic has unit tests (no I/O, no mocks of DbContext)
- [ ] New or changed endpoints have an integration test against real SQL
- [ ] Every new test was seen failing once before it passed
- [ ] Setup steps are asserted, not assumed
- [ ] No Thread.Sleep, DateTime.Now or shared static state in tests
- [ ] A bug fix comes with the test that reproduces the bug
- [ ] OpenAPI changes are intended, and Leo is on the review

Run the suite locally:

cd sample
dotnet build Ledgerly.slnx
dotnet test --solution Ledgerly.slnx

# No Docker on this laptop? Point the integration tests at LocalDB instead.
$env:LEDGERLY_TEST_SQL='Server=(localdb)\MSSQLLocalDB;Integrated Security=true;TrustServerCertificate=true'
dotnet test --solution Ledgerly.slnx

Make it permanent:

  • Copy Directory.Packages.props and ci.yml from the toolkit: they already carry the MTP-era test packages and the dotnet test --solution ... --coverage step.
  • Put the "test": { "runner": "Microsoft.Testing.Platform" } block in every new repository's global.json on day one.
  • Add "a bug fix includes a regression test" and "assert the setup" to pr-review-checklist.md, and add a "Tests" section to pull_request_template.md.
  • copilot-instructions.md already tells Copilot that tests run on MTP and need SQL Server. Add your own rules (FakeTimeProvider, assert the setup), so generated tests follow the house style (Chapter 20).
  • In your notebook, cross out "Unit tests for logic, manual testing for everything else" and write "Every bug becomes a test at the lowest layer that can see it."

🧪 Try it yourself (optional, ~25 min)

You need Docker (for Testcontainers) or LocalDB (with LEDGERLY_TEST_SQL) for the integration tests. The unit tests run anywhere.

  1. Run the whole suite and confirm 23 tests pass (15 unit, 8 integration): bash cd sample dotnet build Ledgerly.slnx dotnet test --solution Ledgerly.slnx If you have neither Docker nor LocalDB, notice how the eight integration tests are reported as skipped with the reason "No SQL Server available...".
  2. Reproduce the EF trap. In src/Ledgerly.Api/Endpoints/PortfolioEndpoints.cs, replace db.Transactions.Add(transaction); with portfolio.Transactions.Add(transaction);. Run the tests again. Observe which tests fail and with which status code. The unit tests stay green. Revert the change.
  3. Make a test lie. In tests/Ledgerly.IntegrationTests/PortfolioApiTests.cs, replace the two lines inside BuyAsync with _ = await PostTradeAsync(client, portfolioId, symbol, TransactionType.Buy, quantity); and repeat step 2. Observe that Overselling_is_a_validation_error now passes even though buying is broken. Restore both changes.
  4. Break the culture fix. In src/Ledgerly.Core/Imports/BrokerCsvParser.cs, find the line that parses the price with CultureInfo.InvariantCulture and change it to CultureInfo.CurrentCulture. Run the tests and watch Decimal_parsing_ignores_server_culture catch it. Revert.
  5. See the coverage. Run dotnet test --solution Ledgerly.slnx --coverage --coverage-output-format cobertura and find the .cobertura.xml files in the TestResults output. Which endpoint has the lowest coverage?
  6. Read the runner. Remove the test block from global.json, run dotnet test again, and read the error. Put it back.

✅ Recap

  1. Shape the pyramid around where your bugs live: fast unit tests for rules, integration tests with WebApplicationFactory<Program> against a real SQL Server for the seams, and a few nightly end-to-end tests with the Aspire testing builder.
  2. On the .NET 10 SDK, xUnit v3 runs on Microsoft.Testing.Platform: opt in through global.json, drop the VSTest packages, use --coverage, and treat exit code 8 ("zero tests ran") as a real failure.
  3. Make tests honest: assert the setup, watch new tests fail once, keep state isolated, notice skipped tests, and turn Leo's API contract into a reviewed file.

Next → Chapter 18: Performance and scale

Chapter 18: Performance and scale

⏱ About 35 min · You'll learn: how to find the real bottleneck before you touch code, when to use HybridCache and when to use output caching, and how Container Apps scale rules, EF Core habits and load tests fit together.

📖 Story: The newsletter

On Tuesday morning a popular personal-finance newsletter mentions Ledgerly's public beta. By ten o'clock signups are running at roughly eight times the normal rate (all numbers in this chapter are illustrative). Elena posts a screenshot of the signup graph in the team channel with three rocket emojis. Ten minutes later Priya posts a different graph: the p95 latency of GET /api/portfolios/{id}/valuation has gone from about 180 ms to over 2 seconds.

Your reflex comes straight from Nordbank Insurance: "Let's add a server." Back there, when the quote screen got slow before the annual renewal season, someone raised a ticket for two more VMs and the problem went away for a year. Maya shakes her head. "We can add ten replicas with one parameter. That's the easy part. The question is whether replicas fix it, or whether they just make the real bottleneck hurt faster. Measure first."

You open your notebook to the page titled Habits I brought with me and write: "Slow? Buy a bigger box." Then, under it: "Find out where the time goes."

🏚️ Old way → 🚀 New way

What you did before What you do now Why it changed
Buy a bigger VM or add a server when it's slow Measure with traces, counters and KQL, fix the bottleneck, then scale out with rules Replicas are cheap to add, so adding them without measuring just moves the problem (often to the database)
MemoryCache or HttpRuntime.Cache on each server, with hand-written locking HybridCache: in-process L1, optional Redis L2, stampede protection and tags One API covers both levels, and only one caller rebuilds an expired entry
.Result and .Wait() in controllers "because it's simpler" Async all the way, with CancellationToken passed through Blocking calls starve the thread pool under load, long before CPU is busy
