Load test once before go-live with a big vendor tool Small load tests with pass/fail criteria, run on demand or from a pipeline Performance regressions are bugs, so they need a repeatable test
A Stopwatch loop in a console app to compare two methods BenchmarkDotNet in Release mode, with memory diagnostics JIT warm-up, tiering and GC make hand-made timing loops misleading
A fixed number of servers sized for the yearly peak minReplicas, maxReplicas and KEDA scale rules for HTTP traffic and queue length Capacity follows demand, and 💰 you stop paying for the peak all year
"The ORM is slow" EF Core with no-tracking reads, projections, indexes and pooling EF Core sends the SQL you let it send. Your SQL instincts still apply

💡 Performance work is a loop: measure, change one thing, measure again. Skip the first step and you are guessing with production traffic.

🧠 Measure first: counters, traces, load tests and benchmarks

Priya's dashboard told you that the valuation endpoint is slow. It didn't tell you why. Each of these tools answers a different question:

Question Tool What you look at
Which endpoints are slow, and since when? KQL over AppRequests in Application Insights (chapter 16) p95 by operation name in 5-minute bins
Where does one slow request spend its time? A trace in the Aspire dashboard locally, or end-to-end transaction details in Application Insights Long spans: SQL, the market-data HTTP call, the cache
Is the process healthy right now? dotnet-counters Thread-pool queue length, thread count, GC, exceptions, request rate
What is the CPU doing? dotnet-trace A .nettrace file you open in Visual Studio or PerfView
How does the system behave at 10× traffic? A load test (Azure Load Testing, k6) p95 and error rate under load, plus server-side metrics
Is method A faster than method B? BenchmarkDotNet Mean time and allocations per call

Start at the top and go down only as far as you need. Most problems are found in the first two rows. Priya's first query is the one from chapter 16:

AppRequests
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1h)
| summarize count(), p95=percentile(DurationMs, 95) by Name, bin(TimeGenerated, 5m)

The trace for a slow valuation shows the answer. The SQL is fast. The time goes to the market-data vendor, which is slow because every valuation asks for every symbol, and many users hold the same handful of ETFs.

The diagnostic CLI tools

The dotnet-* diagnostic tools attach to a running .NET process and need no profiler installed:

Tool Use it for
dotnet-counters monitor -p <pid> --counters System.Runtime,Microsoft.AspNetCore.Hosting Live metrics: thread pool, GC, request rate and duration
dotnet-trace collect -p <pid> A CPU and events trace (.nettrace) for Visual Studio or PerfView

New in .NET 10, dnx runs a tool without installing it first: dnx dotnet-counters ... 🔧. Sources: dotnet-counters and dotnet-trace.

Load tests and micro-benchmarks

These two get confused, but they answer different questions:

Load test Micro-benchmark
Question "How does the deployed system behave with N users?" "Which of these two methods is faster, and how much does it allocate?"
Scope The whole system: ingress, replicas, database, vendor One method, in-process
Tools Azure Load Testing 🔧 (URL quick tests, JMeter or Locust scripts, fail criteria such as p95 or error %, GitHub Actions integration), or Grafana k6 🔧 (JavaScript scripts with thresholds) BenchmarkDotNet 🔧 ([Benchmark], [MemoryDiagnoser], always in Release)
Where it runs Against a test environment, never against real users On a quiet machine
Cost 💰 Azure Load Testing is billed per virtual-user-hour 🔧, and the target environment scales up during the test Free

Azure Load Testing runs JMeter and Locust scripts, not k6 scripts natively 🔧 (overview). k6 2.x is a new major line 🔧 (k6 releases); see also BenchmarkDotNet releases. The Helpers section has a k6 script and a benchmark class for Ledgerly.

💡 A trace tells you where one request spent its time. A load test tells you which resource gives up first. You need both, in that order.

🧠 Caching: HybridCache for data, output caching for responses

The trace showed the real problem: the same quote is fetched from the vendor hundreds of times a minute. A price that is 30 seconds old is fine for a portfolio overview, so the answer is a cache. Before you write new MemoryCache(...) like you did at Nordbank, look at the two caches ASP.NET Core gives you:

HybridCache (data caching) Output caching (response caching)
What is cached A value your code computes: a quote, a DTO, a list The whole HTTP response for an endpoint
Setup AddHybridCache(), then GetOrCreateAsync(key, factory) AddOutputCache(), UseOutputCache(), then .CacheOutput() on an endpoint
Levels In-process L1, plus an optional IDistributedCache L2 such as Redis In-memory by default; a Redis store is available (Microsoft.AspNetCore.OutputCaching.StackExchangeRedis)
Concurrency Stampede protection: concurrent callers for the same key wait for one factory call Handled by the middleware
Invalidation Expiry, remove by key, or by tag Expiry, or IOutputCacheStore.EvictByTagAsync
Use it when Different responses share expensive building blocks (the same quote appears in many portfolios) Many callers get exactly the same response (a public list of symbols)
Avoid it when The value must be exact, like a balance after a trade The response depends on the user, unless you vary the cache key by user

Microsoft.Extensions.Caching.Hybrid is GA (10.11.0 🔧). Docs: HybridCache and output caching.

💻 In Ledgerly: HybridCache with an optional Redis L2

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Api/Program.cs

// ── Caching: HybridCache (in-memory L1). Redis becomes the shared L2 when a "cache" connection exists.
if (!string.IsNullOrEmpty(builder.Configuration.GetConnectionString("cache")))
{
    builder.AddRedisDistributedCache("cache");
}

builder.Services.AddHybridCache(options => options.DefaultEntryOptions = new HybridCacheEntryOptions
{
    Expiration = TimeSpan.FromSeconds(30),
    LocalCacheExpiration = TimeSpan.FromSeconds(10),
});

What matters here:

  • Redis is optional. AddRedisDistributedCache registers an IDistributedCache, and HybridCache picks it up as L2. Locally, the AppHost provides a Redis container called cache, so you get both levels. In Azure, enableRedis defaults to false in main.bicep 💰, so the API runs with L1 only until you decide the extra service is worth paying for.
  • Two expiry times. An entry lives 10 seconds in each replica's memory (LocalCacheExpiration) and 30 seconds in Redis (Expiration). The short L1 time limits how far two replicas can disagree.

💻 In Ledgerly: one vendor call per symbol, not per request

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Api/Services/ValuationService.cs

    private async Task<decimal?> GetPriceAsync(string symbol, CancellationToken ct)
    {
        try
        {
            return await cache.GetOrCreateAsync(
                $"quote:{symbol}",
                async token =>
                {
                    var quotes = await marketData.GetQuotesAsync([symbol], token);
                    return quotes.TryGetValue(symbol, out var q) ? q.Price : (decimal?)null;
                },
                tags: ["quotes"],
                cancellationToken: ct);
        }
        catch (Exception ex) when (ex is HttpRequestException or BrokenCircuitException or TimeoutRejectedException)
        {
            LogPriceUnavailable(logger, symbol, ex.GetType().Name);
            return null;
        }
    }

Read it the way Maya would, asking "what happens when it fails?":

Situation What happens
300 valuations ask for quote:VWCE in the same second, and the entry has expired Stampede protection: one factory call goes to the vendor, the other callers wait for its result
The vendor knows the symbol The price is stored in L1 and L2, and the next callers never leave the process (or Redis)
The vendor doesn't know the symbol The factory returns null, and null is cached too. Unknown symbols don't hit the vendor on every request
The vendor is down The factory throws, so there is nothing to store. The catch sits outside GetOrCreateAsync, so the null it returns is not cached, and the next request tries again
You need to drop all prices at once Every entry carries the quotes tag, so one remove-by-tag call (RemoveByTagAsync) clears them

💻 In Ledgerly: output caching for a public list

The fake vendor uses the other kind of cache for a response that is the same for everyone:

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.MarketData/Program.cs

builder.Services.AddOutputCache();
// ...
app.UseOutputCache();

app.MapGet("/symbols", () => PriceGenerator.KnownSymbols)
    .CacheOutput(policy => policy.Expire(TimeSpan.FromMinutes(10)));

The list of known symbols changes rarely and contains nothing personal, so the whole response can be reused for 10 minutes. A valuation is the opposite: it is different for every user, so caching the response would be wrong. Caching its building blocks (quotes) is right.

💡 Cache the expensive, shared parts with HybridCache. Use output caching only for responses that really are identical for every caller.

🗺️ How a valuation finds its prices

flowchart LR
    Req["GET valuation<br/>for one portfolio"] --> Svc["ValuationService<br/>one task per holding"]
    Svc -- "quote:VWCE" --> L1["L1 memory<br/>this replica, 10 s"]
    L1 -- "miss" --> L2["L2 Redis<br/>shared, 30 s, optional"]
    L2 -- "miss" --> Gate["Stampede protection<br/>one factory call per key"]
    Gate -- "HTTP with resilience" --> Vendor["MarketData vendor"]
    Vendor -- "price or unknown" --> Gate
    Gate -- "store in L2 and L1" --> Svc
    Svc -- "vendor down after retries" --> Partial["Partial valuation<br/>IsComplete = false"]

🧠 Async all the way, and why .Result hurts more here

At Nordbank, a .Result in an ASP.NET MVC controller could deadlock, because the classic SynchronizationContext tried to resume on the thread you had just blocked. ASP.NET Core has no such context, so the deadlock is gone. The habit is still harmful, because the failure mode changed: thread-pool starvation.

Each blocked call holds a thread-pool thread while it waits for I/O. Under light load you never notice. Under the newsletter spike, every request blocks a thread for the full duration of the vendor call. The pool injects new threads slowly, requests queue up, and latency explodes while the CPU sits mostly idle. In dotnet-counters this shows up as a growing thread-pool queue length and a climbing thread count.

// illustrative, not in the sample: the old habit
app.MapGet("/api/quotes/{symbol}", (string symbol, IMarketDataClient client) =>
{
    var quotes = client.GetQuotesAsync([symbol], CancellationToken.None).Result; // blocks a pool thread
    return quotes[symbol].Price;
});

// illustrative, not in the sample: the same endpoint, async all the way
app.MapGet("/api/quotes/{symbol}", async (string symbol, IMarketDataClient client, CancellationToken ct) =>
{
    var quotes = await client.GetQuotesAsync([symbol], ct);
    return quotes[symbol].Price;
});
Do Avoid
async Task all the way from the endpoint to the I/O call .Result, .Wait(), GetAwaiter().GetResult() in request paths
Pass the request's CancellationToken down CancellationToken.None in request code
Run independent I/O calls concurrently with Task.WhenAll Concurrent queries on one DbContext (it isn't thread-safe)
Move CPU-heavy work to the worker Task.Run in an endpoint to "make it async"

💻 In Ledgerly: concurrent price lookups, one database query

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Api/Services/ValuationService.cs

    public async Task<PortfolioValuation?> GetAsync(Guid portfolioId, string ownerId, CancellationToken ct)
    {
        var portfolio = await db.Portfolios.AsNoTracking()
            .Include(p => p.Transactions)
            .SingleOrDefaultAsync(p => p.Id == portfolioId && p.OwnerId == ownerId, ct);

        if (portfolio is null)
        {
            return null;
        }

        var holdings = HoldingCalculator.Calculate(portfolio.Transactions);
        var prices = await Task.WhenAll(holdings.Select(h => GetPriceAsync(h.Symbol, ct)));
        // ...
    }

The database is queried once, then the price lookups run concurrently. That is safe because GetPriceAsync never touches the DbContext. Eight holdings cost one round trip for the slowest price, not eight in a row.

Don't expect the build to catch every blocking call for you. Analyzers flag some async mistakes, but a .Result buried in a helper method usually survives until a load test or a trace finds it. That is why "is every I/O call awaited?" belongs in code review.

💡 In ASP.NET Core, .Result rarely deadlocks. It does something worse: it works fine in testing and falls over under the first real spike.

🧠 EF Core performance: your SQL instincts still apply

The trace showed the SQL was fast today. It won't stay that way by accident. EF Core sends the SQL you let it send, so the habits you built tuning stored procedures at Nordbank translate directly:

Habit EF Core version In Ledgerly
Read-only queries shouldn't pay for change tracking AsNoTracking() on reads Every read endpoint and ValuationService
Select only the columns you need Project with .Select(...) into a DTO ListNotifications
Avoid N+1 round trips Load related data in the same query with Include, or project it ValuationService.GetAsync loads transactions with the portfolio
Index the columns you filter on HasIndex(...) in OnModelCreating, shipped as a migration Owner, alert and outbox indexes
Don't open a new connection per call Connection pooling plus DbContext pooling The Aspire client integration enables DbContext pooling
Cap the result size Take(n) with a stable OrderBy Notifications are capped at 50
Update many rows without loading them ExecuteUpdateAsync / ExecuteDeleteAsync DeleteAlert uses ExecuteDeleteAsync

💻 In Ledgerly: a no-tracking projection with a cap

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Api/Endpoints/AlertEndpoints.cs

    private static async Task<Ok<NotificationResponse[]>> ListNotifications(ClaimsPrincipal user, LedgerlyDbContext db, CancellationToken ct)
    {
        var ownerId = user.GetOwnerId();
        var items = await db.Notifications.AsNoTracking()
            .Where(n => n.OwnerId == ownerId)
            .OrderByDescending(n => n.CreatedAt)
            .Take(50)
            .Select(n => new NotificationResponse(n.Id, n.Message, n.CreatedAt))
            .ToArrayAsync(ct);
        return TypedResults.Ok(items);
    }

The generated SQL selects three columns, filters by owner, sorts and stops at 50 rows. The filter is backed by an index:

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Core/Data/LedgerlyDbContext.cs

        modelBuilder.Entity<PriceAlert>(e =>
        {
            // ...
            e.HasIndex(a => new { a.IsActive, a.Symbol });
        });

        modelBuilder.Entity<Notification>(e =>
        {
            // ...
            e.HasIndex(n => n.OwnerId);
        });

An honest note: ValuationService loads every transaction of a portfolio. For 40 trades that is nothing. For ten years of broker history, it is the next thing a trace will show you, and the fix (store calculated holdings) is a design decision for an ADR.

Compiled queries help only for very hot, simple queries where LINQ translation shows up in a CPU trace:

// illustrative, not in the sample
private static readonly Func<LedgerlyDbContext, string, IAsyncEnumerable<Portfolio>> PortfoliosByOwner =
    EF.CompileAsyncQuery((LedgerlyDbContext db, string ownerId) =>
        db.Portfolios.AsNoTracking().Where(p => p.OwnerId == ownerId));

// usage: await foreach (var portfolio in PortfoliosByOwner(db, ownerId)) { ... }

For what is new in EF Core 10, see What's new in EF Core 10.

💡 EF Core doesn't make SQL slow. Unbounded, tracked, unindexed queries do, in any data-access technology.

🧠 Scaling out in Container Apps

Once the vendor calls were cached, Priya's p95 dropped back below 300 ms. The next question was Elena's: "If the next newsletter is twice as big, are we fine?" Now scaling matters.

Container Apps scales with KEDA-based scale rules: HTTP concurrency, TCP, and custom KEDA scalers such as Service Bus queue length. The defaults and limits 🔧:

Setting Default Notes
minReplicas 0 0 means scale to zero: no usage charges when idle, but a cold start on the first request 💰
maxReplicas 10 Configurable up to 1,000 🔧
HTTP scale rule 10 concurrent requests per replica Ledgerly raises it to 50 for the API

Idle replicas (min above 0, not processing) are billed at a reduced idle rate 💰. Docs: scaling and billing.

💻 In Ledgerly: the API scales on HTTP concurrency

📄 sample/infra/modules/apps.bicep

      scale: {
        minReplicas: apiMinReplicas // 0 = no cost when idle but a cold start; 1 = always warm
        maxReplicas: 10
        rules: [
          { name: 'http', http: { metadata: { concurrentRequests: '50' } } }
        ]
      }

When replicas are handling more than about 50 concurrent requests each, Container Apps adds replicas, up to 10. When traffic drops, it removes them again, down to apiMinReplicas.

💻 In Ledgerly: the cold-start decision is a parameter

📄 sample/infra/main.bicep

@description('0 = scale to zero (cheapest), 1 = always warm (no cold start).')
@minValue(0)
param apiMinReplicas int = 0

This one number is a product decision, so Elena should be in the conversation:

apiMinReplicas User experience Cost 💰
0 (sample default) The first request after a quiet period waits for a replica to start No usage charges while idle, and the monthly free grant 🔧 covers a lot of learning traffic
1 Always warm, no cold start One replica billed at the idle rate around the clock

For a learning subscription, 0 is right. For the public beta, the team sets 1 in the parameter file, through a pull request, because "if it's not in git, it doesn't exist."

💻 In Ledgerly: the worker scales on queue length

📄 sample/infra/modules/apps.bicep

      scale: {
        // 1, not 0: the outbox relay and price alerts poll on a timer, so one replica must always run.
        minReplicas: 1
        maxReplicas: 5
        rules: [
          {
            // KEDA watches the queue length and adds replicas when imports pile up.
            name: 'imports-queue'
            custom: {
              type: 'azure-servicebus'
              metadata: {
                namespace: serviceBusNamespace
                queueName: 'imports'
                messageCount: '20'
              }
              identity: identityId
            }
          }
        ]
      }

KEDA reads the length of the imports queue (using the managed identity, no connection string) and adds worker replicas as messages pile up, aiming at roughly 20 waiting messages per replica, up to 5. Check the KEDA scaler details in the scaling docs.

Scaling moves the bottleneck

Adding replicas is never free of side effects. In Ledgerly, three show up right away:

Side effect Why What to do
The database becomes the limit The serverless Azure SQL database in sql.bicep scales up to 2 vCores. Ten API replicas can send more work than that Watch SQL CPU during load tests. Cache reads, fix queries, then raise the max vCores 💰
The import rate limit grows The rate limiter in Program.cs is in memory, per replica: 5 imports per minute per user per replica Accept it as "roughly 5", or move the limit to a shared store if it must be exact
Price alerts are evaluated more than once AlertEvaluator runs inside every worker replica, and its own comment says to run one replica or move it to a scheduled Container Apps job Move the timer work to a scheduled job, or keep the worker at one replica (chapter 19 shows what this costs at 2 a.m.)

💡 Scale rules are easy to add. The hard part is knowing which shared resource the extra replicas will hit next.

🗺️ Who scales on what

flowchart TB
    Users["Beta users"] -- "HTTPS" --> Ingress["Ingress"]
    subgraph Env["Container Apps environment"]
        Api["api<br/>min apiMinReplicas, max 10"]
        Worker["worker<br/>min 1, max 5"]
        MD["marketdata<br/>min 0, max 3"]
    end
    Ingress -- "HTTP rule: about 50 concurrent per replica" --> Api
    Api -- "cached quotes" --> MD
    Api -- "reads and outbox rows" --> Sql["Azure SQL serverless<br/>up to 2 vCores"]
    Worker -- "outbox relay sends" --> Queue["Service Bus queue<br/>imports"]
    Queue -- "messages" --> Worker
    Keda["KEDA scaler"] -- "watches queue length" --> Queue
    Keda -- "about 20 messages per replica" --> Worker
    Worker -- "imports and alerts" --> Sql

⚠️ Common traps

Trap What happens Do this instead
Scaling out before measuring Ten replicas hammer the same slow vendor or a 2-vCore database, and latency gets worse Read one slow trace and the counters first, then decide between caching, fixing and scaling
.Result or .Wait() in a request path Fine in testing. Under load the thread pool starves and latency jumps while CPU looks idle Async all the way, with the request's CancellationToken passed down
Output-caching a per-user response One user can see another user's data, or a stale balance Output-cache only public, identical responses. Use HybridCache with user-scoped keys for the rest
Assuming L1 is shared Each replica has its own memory cache, so replicas can disagree for up to LocalCacheExpiration Keep L1 short, add Redis as L2 when consistency matters, and evict by tag on writes
Load testing a scaled-to-zero app and blaming the code The first minute is dominated by cold starts and scale-out, not by your code Warm up first, or set min replicas for the test, and read p95 after the ramp

🧰 Helpers

Watch a local process (after aspire run): dnx dotnet-counters ps to find the PID, then dnx dotnet-trace collect -p <pid> for a CPU trace.

Find the slow endpoints in Application Insights (the toolkit's kql-queries.md has more):

AppRequests
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1h)
| summarize count(), p95=percentile(DurationMs, 95) by Name, bin(TimeGenerated, 5m)

A k6 load test for the valuation endpoint:

// illustrative, not in the sample (k6 2.x 🔧: check the current script API)
import http from 'k6/http';
import { check, sleep } from 'k6';

export const options = {
  vus: 50,
  duration: '2m',
  thresholds: {
    http_req_duration: ['p(95)<500'], // fail the run if p95 is 500 ms or more
    http_req_failed: ['rate<0.01'],   // or if more than 1% of requests fail
  },
};

export default function () {
  const res = http.get(`${__ENV.API}/api/portfolios/${__ENV.PORTFOLIO}/valuation`, {
    headers: { Authorization: `Bearer ${__ENV.TOKEN}` },
  });
  check(res, { 'status is 200': (r) => r.status === 200 });
  sleep(1);
}

Run it with k6 run -e API=https://... -e PORTFOLIO=<id> -e TOKEN=<token> valuation.js against a test environment, never against production users.

A micro-benchmark for the holdings calculation:

// illustrative, not in the sample: a separate console project referencing Ledgerly.Core
// run with: dotnet run -c Release
using BenchmarkDotNet.Attributes;
using BenchmarkDotNet.Running;
using Ledgerly.Core.Domain;

BenchmarkRunner.Run<HoldingBenchmarks>();

[MemoryDiagnoser]
public class HoldingBenchmarks
{
    private Transaction[] _trades = [];

    [Params(100, 10_000)]
    public int Count { get; set; }

    [GlobalSetup]
    public void Setup() => _trades = [.. Enumerable.Range(0, Count).Select(i => new Transaction
    {
        Symbol = i % 2 == 0 ? "AAPL" : "MSFT",
        Type = TransactionType.Buy,
        Quantity = 1,
        Price = 100 + i,
        TradeDate = new DateOnly(2026, 1, 1).AddDays(i % 300),
    })];

    [Benchmark]
    public int Calculate() => HoldingCalculator.Calculate(_trades).Count;
}

Make it permanent:

  • Add a load test with fail criteria (p95 and error %) to a pipeline that runs against a test environment. Azure Load Testing integrates with GitHub Actions 🔧.
  • Keep the scale settings (apiMinReplicas, max replicas, scale rules) in Bicep, so every change goes through a pull request.
  • Add performance questions to your PR checklist: is every I/O call awaited, are reads bounded and indexed, is any cache key missing the user scope, is there a vendor or database call per item in a loop? The toolkit's pr-review-checklist.md is a starting point; see the toolkit.
  • Record the decision behind each cache (what, how long, why it's safe to be stale) in a short ADR, so the next person doesn't "fix" a deliberate 30-second staleness.

🧪 Try it yourself (optional, ~30 min)

  1. Start the system (you need Docker Desktop or Podman): bash cd sample aspire run
  2. Create a token with dotnet user-jwts create --project src/Ledgerly.Api. Use src/Ledgerly.Api/Ledgerly.Api.http to create a portfolio and record a few trades in different symbols (for example VWCE, AAPL and MSFT).
  3. Call the valuation request twice within a few seconds. In the Aspire dashboard, open Traces. Observe: the first trace has HTTP spans to marketdata, the second has none, because the quotes came from the cache.
  4. Make the vendor slow. Find the marketdata URL in the dashboard, then: bash curl -X POST <marketdata-url>/chaos -H "Content-Type: application/json" -d '{"failureRate": 0, "latencyMs": 2000}' In PowerShell: Invoke-RestMethod -Method Post -Uri <marketdata-url>/chaos -ContentType 'application/json' -Body '{"failureRate": 0, "latencyMs": 2000}'. Call the valuation again a few times over a minute. Observe: one slow call, then fast calls while the cache holds, then slow again after the entries expire.
  5. While the chaos is on, watch the API with dnx dotnet-counters monitor -p <pid> --counters System.Runtime,Microsoft.AspNetCore.Hosting (find the PID with dnx dotnet-counters ps).
  6. Reset the vendor with the same request and {"failureRate": 0, "latencyMs": 0}.
  7. Read sample/infra/modules/apps.bicep and answer: with 10 API replicas, what is the real import limit per user? Which worker component should not run on 5 replicas, and why?

✅ Recap

  1. Measure first: KQL for which endpoint, a trace for where the time goes, counters for process health, load tests for the system and BenchmarkDotNet for single methods.
  2. Cache shared building blocks with HybridCache (stampede protection, L1 plus optional Redis L2, tags), keep requests async all the way, and give EF Core the same care you gave your SQL.
  3. Container Apps scales the API on HTTP concurrency and the worker on queue length with KEDA. Every new replica moves pressure to a shared resource, and apiMinReplicas trades cold starts against 💰.

Next → Chapter 19: Running production

Chapter 19: Running production

⏱ About 35 min · You'll learn: how to run on-call with actionable alerts and runbooks, how to use feature flags, revisions and dead-letter replay to recover safely, and how to learn from an incident with a blameless postmortem, ADRs and a cost review.

📖 Story: The 2 a.m. page

It is your first week on the on-call rotation. Priya is your secondary. At 01:52 your phone buzzes: "Sev 2: imports dead-letter count above 0 and queue length rising for 10 minutes." You open the laptop on the kitchen table. The market-data vendor has been returning errors since about 01:38 (all times and numbers in this chapter are illustrative). Valuations are degrading gracefully, exactly as designed in chapter 14. Imports are not.

Two weeks ago, a pull request added "value at import time" to CSV imports: the worker now asks the vendor for a closing price for every imported trade. (This change is part of the story; it is not in the sample.) It had no fallback and no flag. Now every import message waits for the resilience timeout or hits an open circuit breaker, gets abandoned, comes back, fails again, and after ten attempts lands in the dead-letter queue. KEDA sees the queue growing and scales the worker to five replicas, which doesn't help at all.

At Nordbank, this would have been the moment you opened RDP to three servers and started reading log files. Here there is a runbook link in the alert, a dashboard, and a calm message from Priya: "Mitigate first. Debug in the morning."

🏚️ Old way → 🚀 New way

What you did before What you do now Why it changed
Every error email goes to the whole team, and everybody ignores most of them An on-call rotation with a primary and a secondary, paged only by alerts that need a human now Noisy alerts train people to ignore the one that matters
A wiki page "how to restart the service", last updated years ago A runbook in the repo, linked from the alert, updated after every incident The runbook is read at 2 a.m. by a tired person, so it must be short, current and findable
A hotfix copied to the servers in the middle of the night Turn a feature flag off, or revert the change and let the pipeline roll forward The pipeline is the tested path. A manual change at 3 a.m. is the untested path
A rollback plan of "restore last week's VM snapshot" Immutable revisions, a smoke test after each deploy, and small changes that are easy to revert Small deploys are easy to undo. Big ones are not
Failed messages in a poison table nobody looked at A dead-letter queue with an alert, a known cause per reason, and a replay tool Dead-lettered messages are never deleted automatically, so someone has to own them
"Root cause: human error" A blameless postmortem with a timeline, contributing factors and owned actions Blame hides the system problem. The next person will make the same mistake
The monthly invoice as the first cost signal Tags, budgets with alerts, and a cost review after incidents and every month 💰 Autoscaling means cost follows load, including the load an incident creates

💡 Running production is a practiced skill, not a heroic one. The goal is a boring 2 a.m.: a clear alert, a short runbook and a safe switch.

🧠 On-call that people can live with: alerts and runbooks

Priya's rule for alerts is simple: "If nobody needs to do anything right now, it's not a page." Everything else is a ticket, a dashboard or a weekly report.

A good page A noisy page
Fires on a symptom users feel: failed imports, error rate, SLO burn rate Fires on a cause that may not matter: one CPU spike, one exception
Has a clear owner and a severity Goes to a shared mailbox
Links to a runbook with the first three steps Says "Error in worker" and nothing else
Has a threshold and a time window that ignore one-off blips Fires and resolves five times an hour
Can be silenced during planned maintenance Wakes people during a deploy everyone knew about

Azure Monitor gives you the building blocks:

Building block What it does in Ledgerly
Metric alerts Dead-letter count and queue length on the imports queue
Log search alerts (KQL) Failed requests or dependency failures above a threshold, or SLO burn rate (chapter 16)
Action groups Who gets notified and how: email (🔧 email receivers now need OTP verification), SMS, voice, push, webhook, Logic App and more. Reused across alerts
Alert processing rules Suppress notifications during a maintenance window

Alert on burn rate for SLOs rather than raw thresholds: the error budget is 1 − SLO, and the question that matters is "how fast are we spending it?" See alerts overview, action groups and the SRE Workbook on SLOs.

The runbook

A runbook is not documentation of the system. It is a checklist for a specific alert. The toolkit's runbook-template.md has this shape:

Section What goes in it Example for "imports dead-lettering"
Symptom What the alert means for users Imports stay "Pending". Users don't see new trades
Impact check One query or dashboard that sizes the problem Queue length, DLQ count and import failures in the last hour
Likely causes The usual suspects, most common first Vendor down, SQL unavailable, a bad deploy
Mitigation Safe, reversible steps, in order Check vendor dependency failures, flip the flag, revert the last deploy
Escalation When and whom to wake up No improvement in 20 minutes: page the secondary, then Maya
Recovery What to do after mitigation Replay the DLQ, check for duplicates, post an update

The first query in that runbook looks at dependencies, because the trace context from chapter 15 tells you which service is failing:

AppDependencies
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1h)
| summarize calls = count(), failed = countif(Success == false) by Target, AppRoleName, bin(TimeGenerated, 5m)
| order by TimeGenerated desc

💡 An alert without a runbook is a riddle. Write the runbook on the day you create the alert, not the morning after the first page.

🧠 Feature flags as kill switches

At 02:10, Priya asks the obvious question: "Can we just turn it off?" For the import enrichment, the answer was no. The pull request shipped without a flag. For price alerts, there is a flag, and that is where you learn the most important lesson of the night.

💻 In Ledgerly: the PriceAlerts flag

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Api/appsettings.json

  "FeatureManagement": {
    "PriceAlerts": true
  },

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Api/Endpoints/AlertEndpoints.cs

        var alerts = app.MapGroup("/api/alerts")
            .WithTags("Alerts")
            .RequireAuthorization()
            .AddEndpointFilter(RequireFeature(Features.PriceAlerts));
        // ...

    /// <summary>Feature flag gate: when the flag is off the endpoints behave as if they do not exist.</summary>
    private static Func<EndpointFilterInvocationContext, EndpointFilterDelegate, ValueTask<object?>> RequireFeature(string feature) =>
        async (context, next) =>
        {
            var features = context.HttpContext.RequestServices.GetRequiredService<IFeatureManager>();
            return await features.IsEnabledAsync(feature) ? await next(context) : TypedResults.NotFound();
        };

The flag is read with IFeatureManager.IsEnabledAsync, registered by builder.Services.AddFeatureManagement() in the API's Program.cs. When it is off, every alert endpoint returns 404, the same behavior [FeatureGate] has for controllers.

Now look at what it does not do. AlertEvaluator in the worker keeps polling the vendor every 30 seconds, on every worker replica, because the worker never registers feature management and never checks the flag. Turning PriceAlerts off would have hidden the alert screens from users and changed nothing about the load on the vendor. A kill switch only kills the code that checks it.

To make the flag a real kill switch, the worker needs the same check:

// illustrative, not in the sample: the worker honoring the same flag
// Program.cs: builder.Services.AddFeatureManagement();
// AlertEvaluator: inject IFeatureManager features, then at the top of EvaluateAsync:
if (!await features.IsEnabledAsync("PriceAlerts"))
{
    return;
}

How fast can you flip it?

Today the flag lives in configuration. Changing it in Azure means setting the environment variable FeatureManagement__PriceAlerts on the container app. An environment variable change creates a new revision, so flipping the flag is effectively a deploy. That is the comment in Program.cs coming due: "from configuration now, Azure App Configuration later".

Where the flag lives How you flip it How long it takes
appsettings.json / environment variables (Ledgerly today) Change the value through Bicep and the pipeline A full deploy and a new revision
Azure App Configuration with refresh Toggle it in App Configuration About the refresh interval (30 seconds by default) 🔧, no redeploy

The App Configuration version, from the docs (Microsoft.Azure.AppConfiguration.AspNetCore 8.6.1 🔧, with the App Configuration Data Reader role for the managed identity):

// illustrative, not in the sample
builder.Configuration.AddAzureAppConfiguration(o =>
    o.Connect(new Uri(builder.Configuration["AppConfig:Endpoint"]!), new DefaultAzureCredential())
     .UseFeatureFlags());          // loads flags with no label, refreshed every 30 s by default
builder.Services.AddAzureAppConfiguration();
builder.Services.AddFeatureManagement();
// ...
app.UseAzureAppConfiguration();    // enables dynamic refresh

Microsoft.FeatureManagement.AspNetCore (4.8.0 🔧) also has percentage, time-window and targeting filters, which turn the same mechanism into gradual rollouts. Docs: feature management reference and FeatureManagement-Dotnet.

Flag type Use it for Remove it when
Kill switch Any feature that calls a dependency you don't control Rarely: it is permanent operational tooling
Release flag Shipping code dark and turning it on later The feature is fully on and stable
Rollout flag (percentage, targeting) Trying a change on a few users first The rollout reaches 100%

💡 Every new call to a dependency you don't control should ship behind a flag, and every service that makes that call must check it.

🧠 Safe deploys, rollback and roll forward

At 02:20 the team chose the safest fix available: revert the enrichment pull request and let the normal pipeline ship the revert. Nobody touched Azure by hand.

💻 In Ledgerly: one deploy at a time, and proof it answers

📄 .github/workflows/deploy.yml

# Never run two deployments at once, and never cancel one halfway.
concurrency:
  group: deploy-production
  cancel-in-progress: false
# ...
      # The deployment "succeeding" only means Azure accepted it. This proves the new revision answers.
      - name: Smoke test
        run: |
          for i in $(seq 1 30); do
            if curl -fsS "${{ steps.apps.outputs.apiUrl }}/alive"; then echo; echo "API is up"; exit 0; fi
            sleep 10
          done
          echo "::error::API did not become healthy"; exit 1

Three properties made the revert safe at 2 a.m.:

Property Where it comes from Why it matters during an incident
Deploys never overlap or stop halfway concurrency with cancel-in-progress: false A second urgent merge waits instead of corrupting the first deploy
Every image is tagged with the commit SHA SHA in deploy.yml You know exactly which commit each revision runs
Migrations run before the apps change Phases 2 and 3 of the deploy Old code never runs against a schema it doesn't understand, as long as migrations stay backward compatible

Notice the limit of the smoke test: it proves the API answers /alive, nothing about the worker or imports. After the incident, "check a worker signal after deploy" went on the production readiness checklist.

Revisions: single mode today, traffic splitting next

Every app in apps.bicep uses activeRevisionsMode: 'Single'. A revision is an immutable snapshot (image, environment variables, scale rules), and in single mode the new revision replaces the old one with zero downtime.

Option How it works Use it when
Revert and roll forward (Ledgerly today) git revert, merge, and the pipeline deploys a new revision Always available, always tested, and it keeps git as the truth
Multiple revision mode Several revisions stay active, and you split traffic by percentage You want canary releases, or an instant switch back to the previous revision
Labels A revision gets a stable URL You want to smoke-test a new revision before it receives real traffic

Docs: revisions and traffic splitting.

A database never "rolls back" with the code, so a revert only works if the schema change was additive. The team's rule: migrations add, later releases remove (if ever).

💡 The fastest safe rollback is the one your pipeline already does every day. Practice it before you need it.

🗺️ The night, as a sequence

sequenceDiagram
    participant V as "Vendor"
    participant W as "Worker replicas"
    participant Q as "imports queue"
    participant D as "Dead-letter queue"
    participant M as "Azure Monitor"
    participant Y as "You on call"
    V->>W: 503 on every price request
    W->>Q: Abandon message, delivery N fails
    Q->>W: Redeliver up to MaxDeliveryCount 10
    Q->>D: Move to DLQ after the 10th failure
    M->>Y: Page with runbook link
    Y->>Y: Check dependency failures and decide to mitigate
    Y->>W: Revert merged, pipeline deploys new revision
    W->>Q: Imports complete, queue drains
    Y->>D: Replay dead letters in the morning

🧠 Dead letters: handle them, then replay them

By 02:50 the queue was draining, but 37 messages sat in the dead-letter queue. Those are 37 users whose imports say "Pending". Dead-lettered messages are never deleted automatically, so they are your job.

💻 In Ledgerly: how a message ends up in the DLQ

📄 sample/infra/modules/servicebus.bicep

  properties: {
    lockDuration: 'PT1M'
    maxDeliveryCount: 10 // after 10 failed attempts the message moves to imports/$deadletterqueue
    requiresDuplicateDetection: true // the same MessageId within the window is dropped
    duplicateDetectionHistoryTimeWindow: 'PT10M'
    deadLetteringOnMessageExpiration: true
  }

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Worker/ImportProcessor.cs

        if (import is null)
        {
            // Poison message: retrying will never help. Park it in the DLQ for a human to look at.
            await args.DeadLetterMessageAsync(args.Message, "ImportNotFound", $"No import for message {messageId}", ct);
            return;
        }
        // ...
        catch (Exception ex) when (ex is not OperationCanceledException)
        {
            activity?.AddException(ex);
            activity?.SetStatus(ActivityStatusCode.Error);
            LogImportCrashed(logger, import.Id, args.Message.DeliveryCount, ex);
            await args.AbandonMessageAsync(args.Message, cancellationToken: ct); // Service Bus will redeliver
        }

There are two kinds of dead letters, and they need different handling:

Dead-letter reason Meaning What to do
ImportNotFound (set by Ledgerly) Poison: the message points at an import that doesn't exist. Retrying will never help Investigate, then delete or fix by hand
MaxDeliveryCountExceeded (set by Service Bus) Transient: it failed 10 times, here because the vendor was down Fix the cause, then replay

Replaying safely

Replay means: receive from imports/$deadletterqueue, send a copy to imports, complete the dead-lettered original. Two details make it safe in Ledgerly:

  • Idempotency. ImportProcessor records each processed MessageId in ProcessedMessages in the same transaction as the trades. A replayed message that was somehow processed already is completed as a duplicate.
  • Duplicate detection. The queue drops a MessageId it has seen in the last 10 minutes. A copy of a received message keeps its MessageId, so a replay within 10 minutes of the original send is silently dropped. Hours later, as here, it goes through.
// illustrative, not in the sample: replay transient dead letters after the cause is fixed
await using var client = new ServiceBusClient(fullyQualifiedNamespace, new DefaultAzureCredential());
await using var receiver = client.CreateReceiver("imports", new ServiceBusReceiverOptions { SubQueue = SubQueue.DeadLetter });
await using var sender = client.CreateSender("imports");

while (true)
{
    var batch = await receiver.ReceiveMessagesAsync(maxMessages: 50, maxWaitTime: TimeSpan.FromSeconds(5));
    if (batch.Count == 0)
    {
        break;
    }

    foreach (var message in batch)
    {
        if (message.DeadLetterReason != "MaxDeliveryCountExceeded")
        {
            continue; // poison: leave it, it returns to the DLQ when its lock expires
        }

        await sender.SendMessageAsync(new ServiceBusMessage(message)); // keeps body, properties and MessageId
        await receiver.CompleteMessageAsync(message);
    }
}

The identity running this needs both the Data Sender and Data Receiver roles on the namespace. Docs: dead-letter queues and duplicate detection.

💡 A dead-letter queue is an inbox with your name on it. Alert on it, know what each reason means, and keep a tested replay tool.

🗺️ The life of an import message

stateDiagram-v2
    [*] --> Active: "Outbox relay sends"
    Active --> Locked: "Worker receives"
    Locked --> Completed: "Trades saved and message completed"
    Locked --> Active: "Abandoned, delivery count plus 1"
    Locked --> DeadLettered: "ImportNotFound or 10 deliveries"
    DeadLettered --> Active: "Replay after the fix"
    DeadLettered --> Deleted: "Poison, removed by a human"
    Completed --> [*]
    Deleted --> [*]

🧠 After the incident: postmortem, ADRs and the cost review

At 03:05 the vendor recovered. Then one more thing went wrong: several users got two notifications for the same price alert. The worker was still at three replicas after the scale-out, each running AlertEvaluator, and each found the same active alerts in the same 30-second tick. The class comment had warned about this ("Run the worker with ONE replica (or move this to a scheduled Container Apps job)"), and the scale rule had quietly broken that assumption.

The blameless postmortem

The next afternoon Priya runs the review. The rule: describe what the system allowed, not who made a mistake. The person who merged the enrichment PR is in the room and explains their reasoning, because that reasoning is the most useful data in the review. The toolkit's postmortem-template.md gives the structure: summary, impact, timeline, contributing factors, what went well, actions with owners.

The timeline (illustrative):

Time What happened Signal
01:38 Vendor starts returning 503 for all price requests Dependency failures in AppDependencies
01:40 Valuations return IsComplete = false. No page, by design Partial-valuation warnings in logs
01:52 DLQ count above 0 and queue length rising for 10 minutes. You are paged Metric alert, action group
01:58 You acknowledge and open the runbook. Dependency query shows the vendor at 100% failures KQL from the runbook
02:05 KEDA has scaled the worker to 5 replicas. It doesn't help: every import calls the vendor Replica count
02:10 You page Priya. The team finds that PriceAlerts doesn't stop the worker's vendor calls Code reading
02:20 Revert of the enrichment PR merged with an expedited review Pull request
02:34 Deploy finished, smoke test passed, imports complete again Deploy run, queue length falling
02:50 Queue back to normal. 37 messages in the DLQ Metric
03:05 Vendor recovers. Duplicate alert notifications go out from 3 worker replicas User reports in the morning
09:30 DLQ replayed. All 37 imports complete, no duplicate trades Import status, idempotency log lines

Contributing factors, not causes:

Factor Action Owner
A new vendor call shipped without a flag or fallback PR checklist item: "new dependency call ships behind a kill switch with a fallback" You
The PriceAlerts flag didn't reach the worker Add feature management to the worker, and check the flag in AlertEvaluator You
Timer work ran on every scaled-out replica Move alert evaluation to a scheduled Container Apps job (record it as an ADR) Maya
Flipping a flag requires a deploy Evaluate Azure App Configuration for flags Tomás
The smoke test only proves the API is alive Add a worker check to the production readiness checklist Priya

The ADR

Moving alert evaluation into a scheduled job changes the architecture, so Maya writes it down with the toolkit's adr-template.md: context (timer work in a scaled worker), decision (a Container Apps job with a Schedule trigger, see jobs), alternatives (keep the worker at one replica, or add a distributed lock), and consequences (one more deployable, but scaling the worker becomes safe). The next person who asks "why is this a job?" finds the answer in git.

The cost review 💰

Elena asks her usual question: "What did the night cost?" Autoscaling means an incident has a price, so the team looks at three things:

Cost driver What happened that night Lever
Worker replicas 5 replicas for over an hour instead of 1 Max replicas in Bicep; the scale rule now only scales import work
Log ingestion Five replicas logging failures every few seconds Log Analytics ingestion is often the surprise cost. Sampling, log levels and a daily cap
Vendor calls Retries multiplied by replicas Caching, circuit breakers, and the kill switch

📄 sample/infra/modules/monitoring.bicep

  properties: {
    sku: { name: 'PerGB2018' }
    retentionInDays: 30
    workspaceCapping: { dailyQuotaGb: 1 } // 💰 a hard daily cap keeps a learning subscription safe
  }

A hard cap is right for a learning subscription. In production, a cap reached during an incident stops the telemetry you need most, so many teams use a budget alert instead.

📄 sample/infra/main.bicep

var tags = {
  app: appName
  'managed-by': 'bicep'
}

Every resource gets these tags, so cost analysis can group by app. Budgets add the alarm:

Budget fact Detail
Scopes and resets Subscription, resource group, management group or billing scope, reset monthly, quarterly or annually
Thresholds On actual or forecasted cost, notifying by email or an action group
What budgets don't do Stop resources. They only notify, unless you wire automation to the action group
Delay Cost data lands within 8 to 24 hours, and budgets are evaluated every 24 hours

So a budget will not catch a runaway incident within the hour. That is the job of maxReplicas and your alerts. See create a budget and the pricing calculator.

The production readiness checklist

The last action has the longest life. The toolkit's production-readiness-checklist.md lists what a service needs before real users: health probes, alerts with runbooks, kill switches, a DLQ owner, scale limits, budgets and tags, and a tested rollback. After this night it gained two lines.

💡 A postmortem is worth exactly as much as its actions. Give every action an owner and put the permanent ones in a checklist.

⚠️ Common traps

Trap What happens Do this instead
Paging on causes instead of symptoms People get woken for one CPU spike, then start ignoring pages Page on what users feel (errors, SLO burn, stuck imports). Everything else is a ticket
A flag that only one service checks You flip it at 2 a.m. and the load doesn't change Every service that runs the feature checks the same flag. Test the "off" path
Fixing production by hand at night The fix is lost on the next deploy, or breaks something untested Revert and roll forward through the pipeline. Change Bicep, not the portal
Replaying the DLQ without idempotency Replayed messages create duplicate trades Keep the ProcessedMessages check, and replay only reasons you understand
Scaling out a worker that runs timers Duplicate notifications when the dependency recovers Timer work runs once: a scheduled job, or a single instance
A postmortem that names a person People hide mistakes, and the system problem stays Blameless format, contributing factors and actions with owners
Treating a budget as a safety net The alert arrives a day after the money is spent Hard limits (maxReplicas, caps) for safety, budgets for trends

🧰 Helpers

Incident first steps (paste into your runbook):

  1. Acknowledge the page and post "Investigating" in the incident channel.
  2. Size the impact: which users, which feature, since when.
  3. Check the last deploy and the dependency failures query.
  4. Mitigate with the safest reversible step: flag off, revert, or scale limit.
  5. Escalate if there is no improvement in 20 minutes.
  6. When stable, write down the timeline while you still remember it.

Exceptions by service (KQL, next to the dependency query from the runbook section):

AppExceptions
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1h)
| summarize count() by AppRoleName, ExceptionType, bin(TimeGenerated, 5m)

Templates in the toolkit: runbook-template.md, postmortem-template.md, adr-template.md, production-readiness-checklist.md and kql-queries.md. See the toolkit.

Make it permanent:

  • Create every alert in Bicep together with its action group, and put the runbook link in the alert description.
  • Add "new external call ships behind a kill switch with a fallback" to the PR template and the review checklist.
  • Add "DLQ owner and replay tool", "timer work runs once" and "post-deploy check for every app" to the production readiness checklist.
  • Schedule a monthly 30-minute cost review using tags and budget alerts, and do a quick one after every incident.
  • Practice: once a quarter, run a game day with the chaos switch in a test environment and follow the runbook as written.

🧪 Try it yourself (optional, ~30 min)

  1. Start the system: bash cd sample aspire run
  2. Create a token with dotnet user-jwts create --project src/Ledgerly.Api, then use src/Ledgerly.Api/Ledgerly.Api.http to create a portfolio, a trade and a price alert.
  3. Break the vendor. Find the marketdata URL in the Aspire dashboard, then: bash curl -X POST <marketdata-url>/chaos -H "Content-Type: application/json" -d '{"failureRate": 1, "latencyMs": 0}' In PowerShell: Invoke-RestMethod -Method Post -Uri <marketdata-url>/chaos -ContentType 'application/json' -Body '{"failureRate": 1, "latencyMs": 0}'.
  4. In the dashboard, open Structured logs for the worker. Observe: "Alert evaluation failed; will retry on next tick" every 30 seconds. The loop survives. Call the valuation request and observe isComplete: false.
  5. Turn the flag off for the API: stop the AppHost, set "PriceAlerts": false in src/Ledgerly.Api/appsettings.json, and run aspire run again. Call GET /api/alerts. Observe: 404. Then check the worker logs. Observe: it still evaluates alerts. That is the kill-switch lesson.
  6. Restore the flag and reset the vendor with {"failureRate": 0, "latencyMs": 0}.
  7. Write a five-line runbook for "vendor down" using the shape in this chapter, and one action you would add to the production readiness checklist.

✅ Recap

  1. Page only on symptoms users feel, and give every alert a runbook with safe, reversible first steps.
  2. Recover through tested paths: feature flags that every affected service checks, revert and roll forward through the pipeline, and an idempotent dead-letter replay.
  3. Learn without blame: a timeline, contributing factors, owned actions, an ADR for design changes, and a cost review, all ending up in your checklists.

Next → Chapter 20: AI-assisted .NET development

Chapter 20: AI-assisted .NET development

⏱ About 30 min · You'll learn: which GitHub Copilot surface to use for which job, how to give AI the context it needs with custom instructions, prompt files and MCP, and how to review AI output with the same bar and guardrails as any pull request.

📖 Story: Use AI like a senior

On Monday you find two pull requests from the weekend. The first one, from a junior contractor, "adds retry logic" to the worker: 400 lines, a hand-written retry loop around every Service Bus call, a new package nobody asked for, and tests that assert the mocks were called. The description says "generated with AI, all tests pass". The second one, from Priya, is 60 lines: a new KQL alert in Bicep and a runbook update. Her description says: "Copilot drafted the query, I checked it against a week of real data and fixed the time window."

Maya reviews both in the stand-up. "Same tool, different results. The tool didn't write the first PR badly. Nobody owned it." Then she says the sentence that ends up on the first page of your notebook's last section: "Use AI like a senior, not like a junior." A junior accepts what the tool produces. A senior decides what to ask for, gives it the right context, and checks the result against the bar they would apply to any colleague.

You have 15 years of .NET judgment. That turns out to be exactly the skill that makes these tools useful. At Nordbank you never had them. Here, the question is not whether to use AI, but where your judgment has to stay in the loop.

🏚️ Old way → 🚀 New way

What you did before What you do now Why it changed
Searching forums for an API example, then adapting it by hand Asking Copilot in the IDE, with your code as context, then verifying against the docs The draft is faster. Verification is still yours
Writing boilerplate (DTOs, mappings, test setup) by hand Letting agent mode draft it, then reviewing it like a PR Boilerplate is where AI is strongest and the risk is lowest
Tribal knowledge about "how we do things here" A short .github/copilot-instructions.md that the tools read The model can't guess your conventions. Write the ones that matter down
Reviewing code by trusting the author One bar for every change, human or AI: tests, analyzers, warnings as errors, human review "The AI wrote it" is not a reason to lower the bar, or to raise it unfairly
The upgrade project nobody wanted to start An agent drafting the mechanical parts of an upgrade, step by step, behind a green build The boring 80% gets cheaper. The tricky 20% still needs you
Copying production data into a test to reproduce a bug Synthetic data, and no secrets or customer data in prompts Prompts are data leaving your machine. Treat them like any other data flow

💡 AI changes how fast you produce a draft. It doesn't change who is responsible for what gets merged.

🧠 The Copilot surfaces, and which one to use when

GitHub Copilot is not one tool. It shows up in several places, and each is good at a different size of job. Everything in this table is volatile 🔧: names, plans and features change often, so check Copilot plans and Copilot on GitHub before you rely on a detail.

Surface 🔧 What it does Use it for
Completions and chat in the IDE Inline suggestions and a chat that sees your open files Small, local edits: a method, a test case, "explain this LINQ query"
Agent mode (VS Code and Visual Studio) An autonomous loop in the IDE that edits several files, builds and runs tests A contained multi-file change you watch while it happens
Copilot CLI A terminal agent (it needs Node.js 22 or later) Work that lives in the terminal: scripts, git, build failures, running commands
Copilot cloud agent (formerly "Copilot coding agent") You assign an issue to Copilot, or start a task from the agents panel. It works in an ephemeral GitHub Actions environment and opens a draft pull request Well-specified issues you would give to a new teammate. Paid plans only
Copilot code review Copilot as a reviewer on a PR, requested by hand or automatic through repo or org settings or rulesets A first pass on every PR, before a human looks

The plans are Free, Student, Pro, Pro+, Max, Business and Enterprise 🔧. Premium usage is billed in "GitHub AI Credits" 💰, and each plan includes a monthly allowance. For Business and Enterprise, an org admin must enable the cloud agent. Elena's question applies here too: before you turn on automatic agents and reviews for a whole org, look at what the usage will cost.

Picking the right size

The surfaces differ in one thing that matters more than features: how long you are out of the loop.

Surface How long it runs without you What you must provide up front
Completions A line or a block Nothing: your code is the context
Chat and agent mode Minutes, and you watch it A clear goal and the files that matter
Copilot CLI Minutes, with your approval for commands A goal and permission boundaries
Cloud agent Until it opens a PR A written issue with acceptance criteria, as for a new colleague

The longer it runs alone, the better your specification must be. An issue that says "fix imports" produces a guess. An issue that says "when the CSV has a header row with a byte-order mark, the parser skips the first column; add a failing test in BrokerCsvParserTests, then fix the parser" produces a reviewable PR.

💡 Match the surface to the size of the job, and the size of the job to how good your specification is.

🧠 Giving AI context: custom instructions, prompt files and MCP

The model sees your code, but it doesn't know your rules. It can't tell that Ledgerly uses central package management, TimeProvider, [LoggerMessage] and the outbox pattern unless something tells it. Three mechanisms provide that context.

Custom instructions

A file called .github/copilot-instructions.md in the repository (or AGENTS.md) is read as extra context for Copilot requests in that repo 🔧. It is the team's "how we do things here", written for a reader that is fast, literal and new every time.

💻 In Ledgerly: the instructions template

The sample repo doesn't ship one in .github/, but the toolkit has a template written for this codebase. An excerpt:

📄 toolkit/copilot-instructions.md

## C# style

- C# 14 with nullable reference types on. File-scoped namespaces, primary constructors and records where they fit.
- `async` all the way with `CancellationToken` passed through. Never `.Result`, `.Wait()` or `async void`.
- Inject `TimeProvider` instead of using `DateTime.Now` or `DateTime.UtcNow`.
<!-- ... -->

## Messaging

- Never publish to Service Bus directly from a request. Write an outbox message in the same `SaveChanges` call; the worker relays it.
- Consumers must be idempotent: check `ProcessedMessages` before doing work.
<!-- ... -->

## When you're not sure

- Say so, and point to the file or doc you would check. Don't invent APIs or package versions.
- Prefer small, reviewable changes with a test that fails without the change.

What makes a good instructions file:

Do Don't
Write rules the model can't infer from the code ("never publish from a request, use the outbox") Restate C# basics the model already knows
Name exact commands (dotnet test --solution Ledgerly.slnx) Write a 20-page style guide nobody maintains
Say what is forbidden and why, in one line Paste secrets, URLs of internal systems or customer names
Review changes to it in PRs, like code Let each developer keep a private version

The weekend contractor's PR would have looked very different with this file in place: "consumers must be idempotent" and the resilience conventions are exactly what the hand-written retry loop ignored.

Prompt files

Prompt files are reusable, task-shaped prompts stored in the repository, such as "add an endpoint following our conventions" or "write a migration that is backward compatible". 🔧 The file location, format and which surfaces support them change, so check the current GitHub Copilot docs before you set them up. The idea is what matters: if you type the same careful prompt three times, save it in git, where the team can review and improve it.

MCP servers

The Model Context Protocol (MCP) lets an AI tool call other tools: read your issues, query a database schema, look at a running system. Copilot supports MCP servers 🔧. One you already have: aspire mcp is an MCP server for AI agents built into the Aspire CLI, so an agent can see the resources, logs and traces of the app you started with aspire run, instead of guessing from source code.

Jonas has one rule for MCP: every MCP server is a new integration with access to something. Ask the same questions as for any dependency: who wrote it, what can it read, what can it change, and where do its credentials come from? Prefer read-only access, and never point an agent at production data with write permissions.

💡 Context is the difference between a generic answer and a Ledgerly answer. Put the rules in git, and treat tool access as a security decision.

🗺️ The senior loop

flowchart LR
    A["You write the goal<br/>and acceptance criteria"] --> B["Context:<br/>instructions, files, MCP"]
    B --> C["Copilot drafts<br/>code and tests"]
    C --> D["Build: analyzers<br/>warnings as errors"]
    D -- "fails" --> C
    D -- "passes" --> E["Tests: do they test<br/>behavior or mocks?"]
    E -- "weak" --> A
    E -- "good" --> F["Human review<br/>same bar as any PR"]
    F -- "changes requested" --> C
    F -- "approved" --> G["Merge and deploy<br/>through the pipeline"]

🧠 Reviewing AI output: one bar, enforced by machines first

Maya's review rule: an AI-written PR gets exactly the same review as a human-written one. Not lower, because the tool sounds confident. Not higher out of suspicion, because then people stop saying which PRs used AI. What changes is where you look harder.

Tests first, and read the tests

The single best guardrail is a test you wrote (or at least read carefully) before accepting the implementation. A test that encodes the business rule tells you whether the draft is right. A test generated together with the code mostly tells you the code does what the code does.

💻 In Ledgerly: a test that encodes a rule

📄 sample/tests/Ledgerly.UnitTests/HoldingCalculatorTests.cs

    [Fact]
    public void Selling_keeps_the_average_cost_of_the_rest()
    {
        var holdings = HoldingCalculator.Calculate(
        [
            Trade("AAPL", TransactionType.Buy, 10, 100m, 1),
            Trade("AAPL", TransactionType.Sell, 4, 150m, 2),
        ]);

        var aapl = Assert.Single(holdings);
        Assert.Equal(6, aapl.Quantity);
        Assert.Equal(100m, aapl.AverageCost);

This test states a real rule of average-cost accounting: selling doesn't change the average cost of what's left. If an AI "refactor" of HoldingCalculator breaks that rule, this test fails, no matter how plausible the new code looks. Compare it with the contractor's tests, which verified that a mock was called three times. Those would pass for almost any implementation.

Let the build say no

The second guardrail is the build itself. Ledgerly's build is strict, and that strictness applies to every author:

📄 sample/Directory.Build.props

    <!-- Quality gates: warnings fail the build, analyzers and code style run in every build (also in CI). -->
    <TreatWarningsAsErrors>true</TreatWarningsAsErrors>
    <AnalysisLevel>latest-recommended</AnalysisLevel>
    <EnforceCodeStyleInBuild>true</EnforceCodeStyleInBuild>

📄 .github/workflows/ci.yml

      # Warnings are errors, analyzers + code style run, NuGet audit fails on vulnerable packages.
      - name: Build
        run: dotnet build Ledgerly.slnx -c Release --no-restore

Nullable warnings, unused usings, code style and vulnerable packages all fail the build before a human spends a minute on review. Agent mode runs the build in its loop, so with these settings it fixes many problems itself. One thing to watch: a tool under pressure to make the build green may add a #pragma warning disable or a NoWarn entry. The template instructions say "never suppress them without a comment explaining why", and your review should check for it.

Where to look harder in an AI-written PR

Look for Why AI gets it wrong Ledgerly example
Invented APIs or package versions The model fills gaps with plausible names A HybridCache method that doesn't exist
New dependencies A package is the easy answer A retry library next to the standard resilience handler
Ignored conventions The model doesn't know your rules Publishing to Service Bus from an endpoint instead of the outbox
Missing failure paths Happy paths dominate training data No CancellationToken, no fallback when market data is down
Security shortcuts Examples online often use keys A connection string with a key instead of managed identity
Tests that test mocks Generated together with the code Asserting call counts instead of results

The toolkit's pr-review-checklist.md has the full list, and it applies to every PR, whoever wrote it. Copilot code review can take a first pass, but a human approves.

💡 Make the machines say no first (tests, analyzers, warnings as errors), so human review can spend its time on design and failure paths.

🧠 Where AI shines for you, where it doesn't, and what never goes in a prompt

You are a senior .NET developer moving from .NET Framework to cloud native. Here is an honest map, based on the work in this course:

Task How well AI helps Your job
Upgrading .NET Framework code (the changes from chapter 2) Strong on mechanical changes: project files, System.Web replacements, async signatures Plan the order, keep the build green at each step, and decide what to redesign instead of port
Writing tests Strong at setup and test data. Weak at deciding what to test Write the test names (the rules) first, then let it fill in bodies
Drafting KQL Good first drafts of summarize and join queries Run it against real data, and check time windows and bins
Drafting Bicep Good at resource shapes and properties az bicep lint, az deployment group what-if, and check identities and roles
Explaining unfamiliar code or errors Very good, especially stack traces and config Verify against the docs before acting
Architecture decisions It produces confident options. It doesn't know your constraints Decide, and write the ADR yourself
Security-sensitive code (auth, crypto, permissions) Plausible and sometimes subtly wrong Use the framework's built-in features, and get Jonas to review
Production incidents Can draft queries and summaries Mitigate with the runbook first. Don't experiment on production

For Bicep, the what-if step deserves its own line. It previews create, modify and delete operations before anything changes:

az deployment group what-if -g <rg> -f main.bicep -p main.bicepparam

An AI draft that would delete and recreate a database shows up there as a delete, in plain text. See Bicep what-if.

What never goes into a prompt

Jonas's rules, which apply to every AI tool, including approved ones:

Never Because Instead
Secrets: keys, connection strings, tokens Prompts and logs are data that leaves your machine Managed identity, and placeholder values in examples
Customer data or production records Privacy and compliance obligations follow the data Synthetic test data
Code from a project your license doesn't allow you to share The license applies to the code, not to the tool Check with your organization first
Accepting large generated blocks you don't understand You can't maintain or license-check what you don't understand Ask for smaller pieces, read each one

Your organization's Copilot policies (which features are enabled, what data handling applies, how suggestions that match public code are treated) are set by admins 🔧. Know what yours are before you rely on a feature.

💡 AI is strongest where the result is easy to check. Invest your attention where checking is hard: design, security and failure paths.

🗺️ The cloud agent's pull request

sequenceDiagram
    participant Y as "You"
    participant I as "GitHub issue"
    participant A as "Copilot cloud agent"
    participant CI as "CI workflow"
    participant R as "Reviewers"
    Y->>I: Write goal, acceptance criteria and a failing test idea
    Y->>A: Assign the issue to Copilot
    A->>A: Work in an ephemeral Actions environment
    A->>R: Open a draft pull request
    CI->>R: Build with warnings as errors, tests, audit
    R->>A: Copilot code review and your comments
    A->>R: Push fixes to the same PR
    R->>Y: Human approval required to merge

🧠 Building AI features into Ledgerly: a short pointer

Using AI to write code is one thing. Putting AI inside your app is another, and .NET has standard building blocks for it.

Library What it is When you'd use it
Microsoft.Extensions.AI (GA, 10.11.0 🔧) Common abstractions IChatClient and IEmbeddingGenerator<TInput,TEmbedding>, implemented by provider libraries (OpenAI, Azure OpenAI, Ollama, Foundry and others), plus middleware Any AI call from a .NET app. Apps reference Microsoft.Extensions.AI, libraries only Microsoft.Extensions.AI.Abstractions
Microsoft Agent Framework (Microsoft.Agents.AI, GA, 1.26.0 🔧) Agents and multi-agent workflows for .NET and Python, built on IChatClient, with graph-based workflows, checkpointing, human-in-the-loop and a DevUI. It succeeds Semantic Kernel and AutoGen When one prompt isn't enough: multi-step agents with tools and approvals

The middleware is the part a cloud-native developer will like: UseOpenTelemetry() puts AI calls into the same traces as everything else (chapter 15), UseLogging() logs them, UseDistributedCache() caches identical requests, and UseFunctionInvocation() lets the model call your C# functions.

A small, realistic first feature: explain a failed CSV import in plain language.

// illustrative, not in the sample
// Program.cs: the inner client comes from a provider package (OpenAI, Azure OpenAI, Ollama, Foundry, ...)
builder.Services.AddChatClient(innerClient)
    .UseOpenTelemetry()
    .UseLogging();

public sealed class ImportErrorExplainer(IChatClient chat)
{
    public async Task<string> ExplainAsync(string errorSummary, CancellationToken ct)
    {
        ChatResponse response = await chat.GetResponseAsync(
            $"Explain this CSV import error to a non-technical user in two sentences: {errorSummary}",
            cancellationToken: ct);
        return response.Text;
    }
}

Everything from earlier chapters still applies, and Maya's question comes first: "What happens when it fails?" An AI provider is an external dependency like the market-data vendor. It needs a timeout, a fallback (show the original error message), a kill-switch flag (chapter 19), cost monitoring 💰 and a decision about what user data may be sent. Docs: Microsoft.Extensions.AI and Microsoft Agent Framework.

💡 An AI model in your app is just another unreliable, metered dependency. Wrap it in the same resilience, flags and telemetry as the rest.

⚠️ Common traps

Trap What happens Do this instead
Accepting a large AI diff because "the tests pass" The tests were generated with the code and assert mocks, so the bug ships Write or read the tests first. Ask for small PRs
No instructions file Every suggestion ignores your conventions, and reviewers fix the same things every week A short .github/copilot-instructions.md, reviewed in PRs
Assigning vague issues to the cloud agent The draft PR solves a different problem Acceptance criteria and the files involved, as for a new colleague
Letting AI silence warnings #pragma warning disable hides the problem the build found Treat any suppression as a review finding that needs a reason
Pasting a production error with customer data into chat Personal data leaves your control Redact, or reproduce with synthetic data
Installing MCP servers without review An agent gets access you never approved Review MCP servers like dependencies, read-only first
Trusting AI on versions and APIs Invented packages, wrong method names Check NuGet and the docs. The build and NuGet audit catch some, not all

🧰 Helpers

A good issue for the cloud agent (template):

## Goal
One sentence: what should be true when this is done.

## Acceptance criteria
- [ ] A failing test in <test file> that shows the problem
- [ ] The fix, with the test passing
- [ ] No new packages, no suppressed warnings

## Context
Files involved: <paths>. Conventions: see .github/copilot-instructions.md.

## Out of scope
What not to change.

Prompts that work like a senior:

  • "Before writing code, list the edge cases and failure modes for this method."
  • "Write only the test names for this business rule. I'll review them before you write bodies."
  • "Which part of this change are you least sure about, and what should I check in the docs?"
  • "Rewrite this using our outbox pattern instead of publishing directly. See ImportEndpoints.cs."

Verifying a Bicep draft:

az bicep lint --file main.bicep
az deployment group what-if -g <rg> -f main.bicep -p main.bicepparam

In the toolkit: copilot-instructions.md (copy it to .github/ and edit) and pr-review-checklist.md. See the toolkit.

Make it permanent:

  • Commit .github/copilot-instructions.md and review changes to it like code.
  • Keep TreatWarningsAsErrors, analyzers and NuGet audit on in CI, so the bar is the same for every author.
  • Add "AI-assisted? Which parts did you verify, and how?" to the PR template.
  • Require a human approval to merge, even with automatic Copilot code review enabled.
  • Write down which AI tools, MCP servers and data are allowed in a short ADR, and revisit it twice a year 🔧.

🧪 Try it yourself (optional, ~30 min)

  1. In your own fork of the course repo, copy toolkit/copilot-instructions.md to .github/copilot-instructions.md.
  2. Open sample/tests/Ledgerly.UnitTests/HoldingCalculatorTests.cs. Write only the name of a new test for a rule you know, for example "selling everything removes the holding". Then ask Copilot chat or agent mode to write the body.
  3. Run the tests: bash cd sample dotnet test --solution Ledgerly.slnx Read the generated test. Does it test the rule, or does it just repeat the implementation?
  4. Ask Copilot to "add retry logic to the market data client". Compare its answer with what the sample already does in src/Ledgerly.Api/Program.cs (the standard resilience handler from chapter 14). Did it notice? Would your instructions file have helped?
  5. Ask it to draft a KQL query for "failed imports per hour in the last day". Read every line and decide which table and columns you would check before trusting it.
  6. Build to see the guardrail: dotnet build Ledgerly.slnx. Add an unused using to any file and build again. Observe: the build fails. Remove it.

✅ Recap

  1. Pick the Copilot surface by how long it runs without you, and give it context through committed instructions, prompt files and carefully reviewed MCP servers.
  2. Review AI output with the same bar as any PR: tests that encode rules, analyzers and warnings as errors first, then human judgment on design, security and failure paths.
  3. Use AI where results are easy to check, keep secrets and customer data out of prompts, and treat AI inside your app as one more unreliable, metered dependency.

Next → Chapter 21: Your toolkit

Chapter 21: Your toolkit

⏱ About 25 min · You'll learn: what is in the toolkit and when to reach for each file, the whole habits notebook in one table, a day-one checklist for a new service, and what to learn next (and when you would actually need it).

📖 Story: Closing the notebook

It is a Friday afternoon, a few months after your first day. Ledgerly's beta is stable, the 2 a.m. page has a runbook, and the last three deploys were so boring that nobody mentioned them at stand-up. Maya asks you to lead the next service: a small "statements" worker that builds monthly PDF statements for users. Elena wants it in the beta next quarter. Tomás has already created the repository, and the first thing he put in it was a link to the toolkit.

You open your "Habits I brought with me" notebook one last time. The first page still has your notes from day one: "No servers to RDP into? Logs are queries?" Every habit since then has a line crossed out and a new practice written next to it. Some crossings-out were easy (DateTime.Now). Some took an incident (the flag that didn't reach the worker). You realize the notebook is no longer a list of things you used to do wrong. It is a checklist for the next service.

So this chapter is the handover: from the course to your own work. Here is everything you can copy, the whole notebook in one table, and an honest map of what to learn next.

🏚️ Old way → 🚀 New way

What you did before What you do now Why it changed
Starting a new service by copying the last project and deleting what you don't need Starting from a known-good toolkit: build settings, service defaults, CI, deploy and checklists The defaults carry every lesson you already paid for
Knowledge lived in senior people's heads Knowledge lives in git: instructions, ADRs, runbooks, checklists People change teams. The repository stays
Learning the next big technology because it's popular Learning what the next problem needs, when you hit it Every new platform has a running cost in complexity
"We'll add monitoring and alerts before go-live" Telemetry, health probes, alerts and a runbook from day one Retrofitting observability is slower and always incomplete
A big upgrade project every few years Small, regular updates: Dependabot, NuGet audit, an LTS plan Small steps are cheap. Big jumps are risky and expensive
A personal notebook of lessons A shared checklist the whole team uses and improves A lesson that only you know protects only your code

💡 The toolkit isn't the end of the course. It's the starting point for every service you build after it.

🧠 The toolkit, file by file

Everything below is in the toolkit/ folder. The toolkit README explains how to copy each file into a new repository. Treat the files as starting points: edit them for your team, then keep them under review like code.

File What it is When you use it
