README.md The index of the toolkit, with copy instructions First, when you start a new repository
cheat-sheet-old-to-new.md The .NET Framework habit next to its modern replacement, on one page When you catch yourself typing an old pattern, or when you onboard a colleague with the same background
production-readiness-checklist.md What a service must have before real users touch it Before the first production deploy, and after every incident that adds a line
pr-review-checklist.md The review bar for every PR, human or AI-written Every review, especially large or AI-assisted ones
kql-queries.md Ready-made KQL for requests, dependencies, exceptions and SLOs During an incident, and when you build alerts and workbooks
slo-worksheet.md A worksheet to choose SLIs, SLO targets and error budgets When a service gets its first users, and when Elena asks "is it healthy?"
runbook-template.md The structure of a short runbook for one alert The same day you create an alert
postmortem-template.md A blameless postmortem: timeline, factors, owned actions After every incident that hurt users, within a few days
adr-template.md An architecture decision record: context, decision, consequences When you make a choice that someone will later ask "why?" about
copilot-instructions.md A template for repository custom instructions for GitHub Copilot Copy it to .github/copilot-instructions.md in every new repo and edit it
pull_request_template.md A PR description template with the questions reviewers need Copy it to .github/ so every PR starts with it
Directory.Build.props Shared build settings: net10.0, nullable, warnings as errors, analyzers, code style At the root of every new solution, on day one
Directory.Packages.props Central package management: one place for all package versions At the root of every new solution, before the first package is added
.editorconfig Code style and analyzer severities Next to the props files, so the IDE and CI agree
ServiceDefaults.Extensions.cs AddServiceDefaults(): OpenTelemetry, health checks, service discovery, resilience In every service, as the first line after the builder is created
ci.yml The CI workflow: build with warnings as errors, tests, infrastructure lint In .github/workflows/ of every new repo
deploy.yml The CD workflow: OIDC login, images tagged with the commit, migrations job, smoke test When the service gets its first Azure environment
dependabot.yml Dependabot updates for NuGet packages and GitHub Actions In .github/ on day one, so updates stay small
main.bicep (pointer) A pointer to the sample's Bicep entry point and modules When you write the infrastructure for a new service

💻 In Ledgerly: one line that carries ten chapters

The toolkit's ServiceDefaults.Extensions.cs comes from this file. Look at how much one method gives every service:

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.ServiceDefaults/Extensions.cs

    public static TBuilder AddServiceDefaults<TBuilder>(this TBuilder builder) where TBuilder : IHostApplicationBuilder
    {
        builder.ConfigureOpenTelemetry();
        builder.AddDefaultHealthChecks();

        builder.Services.AddServiceDiscovery();

        builder.Services.ConfigureHttpClientDefaults(http =>
        {
            // Retries with jittered back-off, per-attempt + total timeouts, circuit breaker, rate limiter.
            http.AddStandardResilienceHandler();

            // Resolves "https+http://marketdata" to a real address (Aspire locally, config or DNS in Azure).
            http.AddServiceDiscovery();
        });

        return builder;
    }

Telemetry (chapters 15 and 16), health checks for Container Apps probes (chapter 14), service discovery (chapter 6) and resilience for every HttpClient (chapter 14). A new service that calls builder.AddServiceDefaults() starts where Ledgerly ended, not where it began.

💡 Copy the toolkit on day one, not "when we have time". Defaults are cheapest before the first line of business code.

🧠 The habits notebook, closed

Here is the whole notebook in one table. Each row is a habit you brought from Nordbank, the practice that replaced it, and where it happened.

Old habit New practice Chapter
Servers you log in to, logs you open as files Disposable containers, logs as queries, boring deploys 1
C# 5 style: DateTime.Now, sync I/O, null checks everywhere Modern C#: records, nullable types, TimeProvider, async all the way 2
"Works on my machine" builds with versions in every project One-command builds, central package management, warnings as errors, NuGet audit 3
Controllers returning whatever, errors as strings Minimal APIs, validation, ProblemDetails, OpenAPI, rate limits 4
Hand-run SQL scripts and "last write wins" EF Core migrations as a deploy step, concurrency tokens 5
A wiki page of local setup steps An Aspire AppHost: F5 starts the whole system 6
Windows Services and SQL Agent jobs Queues, the outbox, idempotent consumers, dead-letter handling 7
Long-lived branches and email reviews GitHub flow, rulesets, CODEOWNERS, small PRs 8
A build server someone configured by hand CI as code in GitHub Actions, Dependabot, CodeQL 9
"We'll get a bigger VM" Choosing managed services by need and cost 💰 10
Clicking resources together in the portal Bicep in git, what-if before every change 11
Publish profiles and a deploy password in a secret OIDC, environments with approvals, build once and promote 12
Connection strings with passwords in config Managed identity everywhere, JWT for users 13
"The vendor is never down" Timeouts, retries, circuit breakers, graceful degradation 14
Console.WriteLine and log files Structured logs, OpenTelemetry traces and metrics 15
"It seems fine" KQL, SLOs, error budgets and alerts with numbers 16
Manual testing before release A test pyramid with real dependencies in containers 17
Guessing at performance, caching "everything" Measure first, HybridCache, async, scale rules with limits 18
Heroic nights and "human error" Runbooks, kill switches, roll forward, blameless postmortems 19
Either ignoring AI or trusting it blindly AI as a drafting tool, held to the same review bar 20

A pattern runs through the right-hand column: almost every new practice moves something from a person's memory into a file in git. The build settings, the infrastructure, the pipeline, the alert, the runbook, the instructions for the AI. That is what Tomás meant on your first week: "If it's not in git, it doesn't exist."

The toolkit's cheat-sheet-old-to-new.md is the code-level version of this table: the specific API or pattern you used to write, and what you write now.

💡 You didn't stop being a senior .NET developer. You moved your judgment into files the whole team can use.

🧠 The day-one checklist for a new service

Maya hands you a single page for the statements service. It is the production readiness checklist turned around: what to do on day one, so the list before go-live is short.

💻 In Ledgerly: a whole service on one screen

The worker is a good model for a new service. Everything it needs is wired in a few lines:

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.Worker/Program.cs

var builder = WebApplication.CreateBuilder(args);

builder.AddServiceDefaults();
builder.AddSqlServerDbContext<LedgerlyDbContext>("ledgerlydb");
builder.AddAzureServiceBusClient("messaging");
// ...
builder.Services.AddSingleton(TimeProvider.System);
// ...
// Give in-flight messages time to finish when Container Apps stops a replica (SIGTERM).
builder.Services.Configure<HostOptions>(o => o.ShutdownTimeout = TimeSpan.FromSeconds(25));

var app = builder.Build();
app.MapDefaultEndpoints();
app.Run();

And it joins the local system with one registration in the AppHost:

📄 sample/src/Ledgerly.AppHost/AppHost.cs

builder.AddProject<Projects.Ledgerly_Worker>("worker")
    .WithReference(db)
    .WithReference(messaging)
    .WithReference(marketData)
    .WaitFor(messaging)
    .WaitForCompletion(migrations)
    .WithHttpHealthCheck("/health");

The checklist

Repository (hour one)

  • [ ] Directory.Build.props, Directory.Packages.props and .editorconfig from the toolkit. The build is green with warnings as errors.
  • [ ] ci.yml, dependabot.yml, pull_request_template.md and .github/copilot-instructions.md committed.
  • [ ] Branch protection or a ruleset on main: PRs, reviews and a green CI run required.

Code (day one)

  • [ ] builder.AddServiceDefaults() and app.MapDefaultEndpoints(): telemetry, health probes and resilience from the first commit.
  • [ ] Registered in the AppHost, so aspire run starts it with its dependencies.
  • [ ] TimeProvider, CancellationToken everywhere, [LoggerMessage] for logs.
  • [ ] If it consumes messages: idempotent handler, explicit complete, abandon and dead-letter, graceful shutdown.
  • [ ] One unit test for the core rule and one integration test through the real entry point.

Azure (before the first deploy)

  • [ ] Bicep module with tags, minReplicas and maxReplicas set on purpose 💰, and health probes.
  • [ ] A managed identity with only the roles it needs. No connection strings with secrets.
  • [ ] The deploy workflow with OIDC, an environment and a smoke test that proves the new revision answers.

Running it (before real users)

  • [ ] One SLO with an alert on burn rate, and a runbook linked from the alert.
  • [ ] A kill-switch flag for every external dependency, checked by every service that calls it.
  • [ ] An owner for the dead-letter queue, and a budget alert on the resource group 💰.
  • [ ] An ADR for every decision someone will question later (like "why a scheduled job and not a timer?").

💡 Most of this list is copying files and adding one line each. That's the point: the expensive lessons are already encoded.

🗺️ A new service, from empty repo to first user

flowchart TB
    A["Empty repository"] --> B["Toolkit files:<br/>props, editorconfig, CI, Dependabot"]
    B --> C["Service with AddServiceDefaults<br/>and MapDefaultEndpoints"]
    C --> D["Registered in the AppHost<br/>aspire run works"]
    D --> E["Tests: one rule,<br/>one integration path"]
    E --> F["Bicep module<br/>identity, probes, scale limits"]
    F --> G["Deploy workflow<br/>OIDC and smoke test"]
    G --> H["SLO, alert and runbook"]
    H --> I["Production readiness<br/>checklist passes?"]
    I -- "no" --> J["Fix the gap,<br/>write an ADR if it's a decision"]
    J --> I
    I -- "yes" --> K["First real users"]

🧠 What to learn next, and when you'd need it

The cloud-native world has far more technology than any one team needs. Here is an honest map. "When you'd need it" matters more than "what it is": learn each one when a real problem asks for it.

Topic What it is When you'd need it When you wouldn't
Kubernetes and AKS Full Kubernetes API control on Azure, as AKS Standard or AKS Automatic. Container Apps runs on Kubernetes but doesn't expose its API You need custom operators, special networking or scheduling, or a platform team already runs Kubernetes Your services fit Container Apps. Ledgerly does
Dapr A set of building blocks (state, pub/sub, service invocation) as a sidecar. Optional per app in Container Apps Many services in different languages need the same building blocks A .NET-only system using Aspire integrations and the Azure SDKs directly. Nothing in this course needs it
Event-driven architecture Service Bus topics and subscriptions: publish once, each subscription gets a copy, with SQL or correlation filters A second consumer wants the same events, like the statements service listening for completed imports One producer, one consumer: a queue is simpler
Platform engineering A team that builds paved roads (templates, pipelines, environments) for other teams Several teams build services the same way and keep solving the same setup problems One team, a few services. The toolkit is your paved road
.NET 11 🔧 The next release, STS (2 years of support). At RC3 as of late October 2026, GA expected at .NET Conf in November 2026 You want a specific new feature and can upgrade again within two years You prefer the 3-year LTS cycle. .NET 10 is supported until 2028-11-14
aspire deploy 🔧 The AppHost provisions Azure resources and deploys containers, now the recommended Aspire deploy path New projects or prototypes where the AppHost model is enough You need full Bicep control and review, as Ledgerly's infra/ does
Azure App Configuration Central configuration and feature flags with refresh, no redeploy Flags must flip in seconds, or many services share settings One service with a few settings in environment variables
Microsoft Agent Framework 🔧 Agents and multi-agent workflows on IChatClient, GA Your product needs multi-step AI with tools and approvals One prompt with IChatClient is enough

Release dates and support windows change, so check the .NET support policy and what's new in .NET 11. For compute choices, Microsoft's container options comparison is the place to start. For events, see queues, topics and subscriptions.

How to learn the next thing

The method that worked for this course works for the next topic too:

  1. Start from a problem, not a technology: "two services need the same import events" leads to topics, not the other way round.
  2. Build the smallest version in the sample, locally with aspire run, before it goes near production.
  3. Write the ADR: what you chose, what you didn't, what it costs to run 💰 and to operate.
  4. Add the lesson to the toolkit, so the next service gets it for free.

💡 The best next technology is the one your next real problem needs. Everything else can wait in a bookmark.

⚠️ Common traps

Trap What happens Do this instead
Copying the toolkit once and never updating it Each repository drifts, and lessons from new incidents never reach the old services Treat the toolkit as a repository with PRs, and update it after postmortems
Leaving the readiness checklist until go-live week Alerts, runbooks and identities get rushed or skipped Use the day-one checklist, so go-live is a short final check
Adopting Kubernetes or Dapr "to be ready" Weeks of platform work and a new on-call surface, with no user benefit Wait for a problem that needs it, and write an ADR when it comes
Upgrading to every new STS release without a plan Frequent upgrade work, or running an unsupported version Choose LTS or STS on purpose, and put the end-of-support date in your calendar
Keeping the notebook private Your team repeats the mistakes you already learned from Turn lessons into checklist lines, instructions and templates in git
Treating checklists as bureaucracy People tick boxes without thinking Keep them short, explain each line, delete lines that never catch anything

🧰 Helpers

Starting a new service, in order:

  1. Read the toolkit README and copy the build, style and workflow files.
  2. Add ServiceDefaults and register the service in the AppHost.
  3. Write the first rule as a test, then the code.
  4. Write the Bicep module and the deploy workflow before the first user story is finished.
  5. Walk through production-readiness-checklist.md with Priya before real users arrive.

Questions to ask in any design review (from the whole team):

  • Maya: "What happens when it fails?"
  • Tomás: "Is it in git?"
  • Priya: "How will we know it's healthy, and who gets paged?"
  • Jonas: "Which identity does this use, and what can it access?"
  • Elena: "What does the user see, and what does it cost?" 💰
  • Leo: "What's the contract, and what does an error look like?"

Make it permanent:

  • Put the toolkit in its own repository (or a template repository), owned by the team, with PRs for changes.
  • Link production-readiness-checklist.md and pr-review-checklist.md from your PR template.
  • After every postmortem, ask: "Which toolkit file should change so this can't happen in the next service?"
  • Review the "what to learn next" table every six months against the problems you actually hit 🔧.

🧪 Try it yourself (optional, ~30 min)

  1. Run the finished sample one more time, and walk through the dashboard as if you were showing it to a colleague from your old team: bash cd sample aspire run
  2. Build and test it the way CI does, and notice how little you have to remember: bash dotnet build Ledgerly.slnx dotnet test --solution Ledgerly.slnx
  3. Open the toolkit README. For each file, decide whether you would copy it unchanged, edit it, or skip it for your next service. Write down why.
  4. Take the day-one checklist above and fill it in for a service you know from your own work. Which lines would have prevented an incident you remember?
  5. Open your own notebook (or start one) and add one habit this course didn't cover, with its new practice. Then turn it into a line in a checklist.

✅ Recap

  1. The toolkit turns every lesson of this course into files you copy on day one: build settings, service defaults, workflows, templates and checklists.
  2. Almost every new habit moved knowledge from someone's memory into git, where the whole team can use and improve it.
  3. Learn the next technology when a real problem needs it, write the decision down, and feed the lesson back into the toolkit.

Back to → Start

ADR template

An architecture decision record (ADR) captures one significant decision: the context, the options, the choice and its consequences. Six months later, nobody remembers why you picked Container Apps over App Service. The ADR does.

Write one when a decision is hard to reverse, affects more than one team, or has been argued about twice.

Copy everything below the line into docs/adr/NNNN-short-title.md (number them in order and never reuse a number). Delete the hints in italics. To change a decision, write a new ADR that supersedes the old one; don't edit history.

ADR NNNN: decision title, as a short statement
Status Proposed / Accepted / Superseded by ADR NNNN / Deprecated
Date
Deciders
Consulted

Context

What problem are we solving? What constraints apply (team skills, budget, deadlines, compliance)? What forces are in tension? Keep it factual.

Options considered

Option Pros Cons
A
B
C: do nothing

Decision

We will ... because ...

Consequences

  • Good: what gets easier
  • Bad: what gets harder, and the debt we accept
  • Follow-up: work this creates, with issue links

When to revisit

The signal that would make us reconsider, e.g. "if we need more than 30 services" or "if the monthly bill passes X".

Example (illustrative): ADR 0003, host the services on Azure Container Apps
Status Accepted
Deciders Maya Okafor, Tomás Varga
Consulted Priya Raman, Jonas Berg, Elena Rossi

Context. Four small services (API, worker, market data, migrations), a team of six, a beta in 12 weeks. Nobody on the team runs Kubernetes today. The worker must scale on queue length. Traffic during the beta is low and spiky.

Options considered.

Option Pros Cons
App Service Closest to the IIS model, mature Queue-based scaling of the worker is harder, no scale to zero
Container Apps Scale to zero, KEDA rules for Service Bus, jobs for migrations, no cluster to run Cold starts at zero replicas, fewer knobs than Kubernetes
AKS Full control, huge ecosystem We'd spend the beta learning to operate a cluster

Decision. We will run all services on Azure Container Apps in one environment, because it gives us queue-based scaling and run-to-completion jobs without operating a cluster.

Consequences. Good: low idle cost, one deploy model for every service. Bad: cold starts on the API unless we keep one replica (a cost decision for Elena). Follow-up: the migrations job (chapter 12), scale rules (chapter 18).

When to revisit. If we need service mesh features, or more than about 20 services.

Cheat sheet: old to new

Each row maps an old .NET Framework or IIS habit to its modern replacement, with the chapter that covers it.

Runtime and code

You used to Now you Chapter
Target .NET Framework 4.8, installed on the server Target net10.0 (LTS). The runtime ships inside your container image. 2
Global.asax, Startup, OWIN WebApplication.CreateBuilder(args) in Program.cs. One composition root. 2
Unity, Autofac or Ninject for DI Built-in IServiceCollection. Add a container only if you really need its extra features. 2
ConfigurationManager.AppSettings["x"] IConfiguration plus the options pattern (IOptions<T>), with environment variables overriding JSON 2
web.config transforms per environment appsettings.{Environment}.json, environment variables and Key Vault 2, 13
new HttpClient() per call, or one static client IHttpClientFactory with typed clients and a resilience handler 2, 14
.Result and .Wait() on tasks async all the way, passing CancellationToken 2, 18
DateTime.Now TimeProvider (inject it, fake it in tests) 2, 17
Guid.NewGuid() for clustered keys Guid.CreateVersion7() (time-ordered) 2, 5
Classes with 30 lines of properties record types, primary constructors and required members 2
string.Format log messages Message templates plus [LoggerMessage] source generation 15

You used to Now you Chapter

.sln files that merge-conflict .slnx (XML solution format) 3

Package sources configured per machine nuget.config in the repo with <clear/> and package source mapping 3, 13

Last write wins Optimistic concurrency with rowversion, returning 409 4, 5

You used to Now you Chapter

MSMQ Azure Service Bus queues with peek-lock and dead-lettering 7

Copilot instructions

This repository contains a .NET 10 (LTS) backend: ASP.NET Core minimal APIs, EF Core 10 on Azure SQL, Azure Service Bus and Aspire for local orchestration. It deploys to Azure Container Apps with Bicep and GitHub Actions.

Build and test

  • Build: dotnet build Ledgerly.slnx from sample/. Warnings are errors; fix them, never suppress them without a comment explaining why.
  • Test: dotnet test --solution Ledgerly.slnx. Tests run on Microsoft.Testing.Platform, not VSTest. Don't add Microsoft.NET.Test.Sdk, xunit.runner.visualstudio or coverlet.collector.
  • Integration tests need SQL Server: Docker (Testcontainers) or the LEDGERLY_TEST_SQL environment variable.
  • Run the whole system locally with aspire run.

Packages

  • Versions live only in Directory.Packages.props (central package management). Never put Version= on a PackageReference.
  • Don't add a package when the base class library or an existing package already does the job. Ask first for new dependencies.

C# style

  • C# 14 with nullable reference types on. File-scoped namespaces, primary constructors and records where they fit.
  • async all the way with CancellationToken passed through. Never .Result, .Wait() or async void.
  • Inject TimeProvider instead of using DateTime.Now or DateTime.UtcNow.
  • Money and quantities are decimal. Parse and format with CultureInfo.InvariantCulture for machine data.
  • Log with [LoggerMessage] source-generated methods and message templates. Never log secrets, tokens or personal data.

API conventions

  • Minimal APIs in Endpoints/*.cs, one static class per area, using route groups and TypedResults.
  • Errors are ProblemDetails. Validation errors return 400, concurrency conflicts 409, missing or not-owned resources 404.
  • Every endpoint that touches user data requires authorization and filters by the current user's id.

Data

  • Add new entities through their DbSet (db.Transactions.Add(...)), not through a navigation collection: keys are client-generated with Guid.CreateVersion7().
  • Schema changes need a migration: dotnet ef migrations add <Name> --project src/Ledgerly.Core --output-dir Data/Migrations. Migrations must stay compatible with the previous app version (expand, then contract).
  • Read-only queries use AsNoTracking() or projections.

Messaging

  • Never publish to Service Bus directly from a request. Write an outbox message in the same SaveChanges call; the worker relays it.
  • Consumers must be idempotent: check ProcessedMessages before doing work.

Infrastructure and workflows

  • Azure resources are defined only in sample/infra/*.bicep. Run bicep lint and bicep build after changes.
  • Use managed identities and RBAC role assignments. Never add connection strings with keys or passwords.
  • GitHub Actions use least-privilege permissions: and OIDC for Azure. Never add cloud credentials as secrets.

When you're not sure

  • Say so, and point to the file or doc you would check. Don't invent APIs or package versions.
  • Prefer small, reviewable changes with a test that fails without the change.

KQL queries

Starting-point queries for a Ledgerly-style system in Application Insights (workspace-based). Adapt the names, thresholds and time ranges, then save the good ones as workbook tiles or log search alerts.

🔧 These use the Log Analytics table names (AppRequests, AppDependencies, AppTraces, AppExceptions, AppMetrics) and their columns (TimeGenerated, AppRoleName, DurationMs). If you run them from the Application Insights Logs blade, the classic names also work there: requests, dependencies, traces, exceptions, customMetrics, with timestamp and cloud_RoleName. Check the column names in your workspace schema before you build alerts on them.

AppRoleName is the OpenTelemetry service.name. In the sample it comes from OTEL_SERVICE_NAME: api, worker and marketdata.

1. Failure rate per service (5-minute buckets)

AppRequests
| where TimeGenerated > ago(6h)
| summarize total = count(), failed = countif(Success == false) by AppRoleName, bin(TimeGenerated, 5m)
| extend failureRatePct = round(100.0 * failed / total, 2)
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Use it for: the "is it broken right now?" tile. 🔧 How Success treats 4xx responses depends on the SDK and exporter, so check a few rows first. If needed, define failure yourself with toint(ResultCode) >= 500: a user's validation error is not your outage.

2. p50, p95 and p99 latency per endpoint

AppRequests
| where TimeGenerated > ago(24h) and AppRoleName == "api"
| summarize requests = count(),
            p50 = percentile(DurationMs, 50),
            p95 = percentile(DurationMs, 95),
            p99 = percentile(DurationMs, 99) by Name
| order by p95 desc

Use it for: finding the slow endpoints. Name is the route template (for example GET /api/portfolios/{id:guid}/valuation), so IDs don't explode the result.

3. Calls to the market-data vendor: failures and latency

AppDependencies
| where TimeGenerated > ago(6h)
| where AppRoleName == "api" and Target has "marketdata"
| summarize calls = count(),
            failed = countif(Success == false),
            p95 = percentile(DurationMs, 95) by bin(TimeGenerated, 5m)
| extend failureRatePct = round(100.0 * failed / calls, 2)
| render timechart

Use it for: telling "our API is slow" apart from "the vendor is slow". Retries from the resilience handler appear as separate dependency calls under the same operation.

4. Partial valuations (graceful degradation in action)

AppTraces
| where TimeGenerated > ago(24h)
| where Message has "returning partial valuation"
| extend Symbol = tostring(Properties.Symbol), Reason = tostring(Properties.Reason)
| summarize count() by Symbol, Reason, bin(TimeGenerated, 15m)
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Use it for: seeing what users experienced while the vendor was failing. The message comes from the [LoggerMessage] in ValuationService. Template placeholders become properties, so you can group by them. 🔧 The exact property names depend on the exporter. Run AppTraces | take 10 and look at Properties first.

5. Import throughput and outcomes (custom metric)

AppMetrics
| where TimeGenerated > ago(24h)
| where Name == "ledgerly.imports.processed"
| extend outcome = tostring(Properties.outcome)
| summarize imports = sum(Sum) by outcome, bin(TimeGenerated, 1h)
| render columnchart

Use it for: Elena's question "how many imports ran today, and how many had errors?" The counter is tagged with outcome (completed or completed_with_errors). Metrics arrive pre-aggregated, so you sum(Sum) instead of count().

6. Import duration (custom histogram)

AppMetrics
| where TimeGenerated > ago(24h)
| where Name == "ledgerly.imports.duration"
| summarize totalSeconds = sum(Sum), imports = sum(ItemCount), slowest = max(Max) by bin(TimeGenerated, 1h)
| extend avgSeconds = round(totalSeconds / imports, 2)

Use it for: spotting imports getting slower as files grow. Histograms are exported as aggregates (sum, count, min, max), so you get an average and a maximum, not exact percentiles.

7. Messages being retried or dead-lettered

AppTraces
| where TimeGenerated > ago(24h) and AppRoleName == "worker"
| where Message has "abandoning for retry" or Message has "already processed"
| extend kind = iff(Message has "abandoning", "retry", "duplicate")
| summarize count() by kind, bin(TimeGenerated, 15m)
| render timechart

Use it for: an early warning before messages reach the dead-letter queue. Repeated retries for the same import mean a poison message. The dead-letter count itself is a Service Bus metric on the queue: alert on it with a metric alert, not with KQL.

8. Exceptions grouped by problem

AppExceptions
| where TimeGenerated > ago(24h)
| summarize count(), firstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), lastSeen = max(TimeGenerated),
            sample = take_any(OuterMessage) by ProblemId, AppRoleName
| order by count_ desc

Use it for: the morning check. A new ProblemId that appeared after the last deploy is your first suspect.

9. Follow one request end to end

let op = "<paste an OperationId here>";
union AppRequests, AppDependencies, AppTraces, AppExceptions
| where OperationId == op
| project TimeGenerated, itemType = Type, AppRoleName, Name, Message, DurationMs, Success
| order by TimeGenerated asc

Use it for: the bug report with a timestamp. The OperationId is the W3C trace ID, so this includes the worker's processing of an import that the API accepted, because the sample carries trace context through the outbox and Service Bus. The portal's end-to-end transaction view shows the same data as a timeline.

10. Replica starts (scale-outs, restarts, cold starts)

AppTraces
| where TimeGenerated > ago(24h)
| where Message has "Application started"
| summarize starts = count() by AppRoleName, bin(TimeGenerated, 1h)
| render columnchart

Use it for: correlating latency spikes with replicas starting. Many starts for api with minReplicas 0 means users are hitting cold starts. Unexpected starts for worker (min 1) mean crashes or failed liveness probes.

11. SLI: share of good valuation requests (for the SLO worksheet)

AppRequests
| where TimeGenerated > ago(28d) and AppRoleName == "api"
| where Name has "valuation"
| summarize total = count(), good = countif(Success == true and DurationMs < 500)
| extend sliPct = round(100.0 * good / total, 3)

Use it for: the monthly SLO report. The 500 ms threshold is illustrative; use the one from your SLO worksheet.

Turning a query into an alert

Step What to decide
1 Is it a symptom users feel (failure rate, latency) or a cause (CPU)? Alert on symptoms.
2 Window and frequency: a 5-minute window evaluated every 5 minutes is a sane start.
3 Threshold: start from the SLO, not from a gut feeling.
4 Action group: who gets paged, who only gets an email.
5 Runbook: put the link in the alert description (see the runbook template).

💰 Log ingestion is often the surprise cost of observability, and alert rules have their own charges (check the Azure Monitor pricing page). Prefer a few well-chosen alerts over many noisy ones, and set sampling and a daily cap on purpose.

Postmortem template

A postmortem is blameless: it asks how the system and the process allowed the failure, not who made the mistake. People who fear blame hide information, and then the same incident happens again.

Copy everything below the line into docs/postmortems/YYYY-MM-DD-short-title.md. Write it within 5 working days of the incident while memories are fresh. Delete the hints in italics.

Postmortem: short title
Date of incident
Authors
Status Draft / In review / Final
Severity
Duration from first impact to full recovery
Error budget spent e.g. 40% of the monthly budget for the valuation SLO

Summary

Three sentences: what happened, what users experienced, how it was fixed.

Impact
Users affected number or share
What they saw errors, slowness, wrong data, nothing
Data lost or wrong yes/no, details
Money or contractual impact

Timeline

All times in UTC. Include detection, decisions and dead ends, not only the fix.

Time (UTC) What happened
First impact (found afterwards in the data)
Alert fired / customer reported
On-call acknowledged
Hypothesis 1 tried, ruled out because...
Mitigation applied
Full recovery confirmed

Detection

  • How did we find out? Alert, customer, colleague, luck?
  • How long from first impact to detection? Could an SLO alert have caught it earlier?

Root causes and contributing factors

Ask "why" until you reach something you can change in the system or process. There is usually more than one cause.

  1. Trigger: the change or event that started it.
  2. Why it caused impact: missing timeout, no idempotency, alert not actionable...
  3. Why it took long to fix: runbook missing, rollback unclear, access problems...

What went well

What went badly

Where we got lucky

Action items

Each item has an owner, a due date and an issue link. Prefer items that prevent a whole class of failures over items that fix only this one.

Action Type Owner Due Issue
Prevent / Detect / Mitigate / Process

Lessons for other teams

One or two sentences worth sharing outside the team.

PR review checklist

Use it for every pull request, including ones written with AI help. The bar is the same. CI already checks formatting, warnings, tests and known vulnerabilities, so spend your attention on what tools can't see.

Before you read the code

  • [ ] The description says what changed and why, and links the issue.
  • [ ] The PR is small enough to review in 30 minutes. If not, ask for a split.
  • [ ] CI is green. Don't review red builds.

Correctness

  • [ ] The change does what the issue asked, and no more.
  • [ ] Edge cases are handled: empty input, duplicates, zero, negative numbers, very large input, other cultures (decimal separators, dates).
  • [ ] Concurrency: what happens if two requests do this at the same time?
  • [ ] Money and quantities use decimal, with explicit rounding rules.
  • [ ] Time comes from TimeProvider, not DateTime.Now.

Failure

  • [ ] Every outbound call has a timeout and a CancellationToken.
  • [ ] Failures return the right status code with ProblemDetails, not a stack trace.
  • [ ] Message handlers are idempotent.
  • [ ] Nothing blocks on async code (.Result, .Wait()).

Data

  • [ ] Migrations are backward compatible with the version currently running.
  • [ ] Queries are bounded (paging, Take) and use indexes that exist.
  • [ ] Read-only queries use AsNoTracking() or projections.
  • [ ] New entities are added through the DbSet, not through a navigation, when keys are client-generated.

Security

  • [ ] No secrets, connection strings with passwords, or tokens in code, config or tests.
  • [ ] Endpoints that need authentication require it. Data access checks the current user.
  • [ ] Input is validated at the edge.
  • [ ] New packages are needed, maintained and from the allowed source.
  • [ ] Logs don't contain personal data or secrets.

Observability

  • [ ] New code paths log with message templates ([LoggerMessage] for hot paths), not string concatenation.
  • [ ] New outbound calls or background jobs show up in traces.
  • [ ] If the product manager will ask "how many?", there is a metric.
  • [ ] Metric tags have low cardinality (no user IDs, no free text).

Tests

  • [ ] New behavior has tests at the right level (unit for rules, integration for HTTP + SQL).
  • [ ] Tests assert their setup succeeded, not just the final result.
  • [ ] A bug fix comes with a test that fails without the fix.

AI-assisted changes

  • [ ] The author can explain every line.
  • [ ] APIs used actually exist in the versions we target (check the docs, not the model's memory).
  • [ ] No silent scope creep: unrelated "improvements" are removed or split out.
  • [ ] Generated tests check real behavior, not the implementation copied back.

Review etiquette

  • Prefix optional comments with "nit:".
  • Ask questions instead of giving orders: "What happens if the vendor times out here?"
  • Approve when it's better than main and safe, not when it's how you would have written it.

Production readiness checklist

Go through this before a service takes real traffic, and again once a quarter. Every "no" is either fixed or written down as an accepted risk, with an owner.

Copy this into an issue (one checkbox per line) and work through it with the team. It takes about an hour for a small service.

1. Build and supply chain

  • [ ] The build is reproducible from a clean clone: global.json pins the SDK, nuget.config lists the package sources.
  • [ ] Warnings are errors. Analyzers and code style run in the build.
  • [ ] Central package management is on, with transitive pinning for anything NuGet audit flags.
  • [ ] Dependabot (or similar) opens update PRs for packages and GitHub Actions.
  • [ ] CodeQL or another SAST tool runs on pull requests.
  • [ ] Secret scanning and push protection are on for the repository.
  • [ ] main is protected: pull request required, CI required, no force pushes.

2. Tests

  • [ ] Unit tests cover the domain rules that lose money when wrong.
  • [ ] Integration tests run against the real database engine, not an in-memory fake.
  • [ ] Tests assert their own setup (no tests that pass for the wrong reason).
  • [ ] CI fails when zero tests run.
  • [ ] Flaky tests are fixed or quarantined with an issue, not retried silently.

3. Deploy and rollback

  • [ ] Deploys run from CI only. Nobody has standing write access to production "just in case".
  • [ ] CI authenticates to Azure with OIDC. No cloud credentials are stored in GitHub secrets.
  • [ ] One image is built per commit and tagged with the commit SHA. The same image goes to every environment.
  • [ ] Database migrations run as a separate step before the new app version starts.
  • [ ] Schema changes are backward compatible (expand, deploy, contract).
  • [ ] A smoke test runs after each deploy and fails the workflow.
  • [ ] Rollback is written down and has been practiced once.

4. Identity and secrets

  • [ ] Services use managed identities for Azure SQL, Service Bus, Key Vault and the container registry.
  • [ ] Local (key or password) authentication is disabled where the service allows it.
  • [ ] The few remaining secrets live in Key Vault and reach the app as secret references.
  • [ ] Each identity has only the roles it needs, scoped to the resource, not the subscription.
  • [ ] User-facing endpoints require authentication. Authorization checks ownership (return 404 for other users' data).
  • [ ] CORS allows only known origins.

5. Reliability

  • [ ] Every outbound HTTP call has a timeout, retries with jitter and a circuit breaker.
  • [ ] The service degrades gracefully when a dependency is down. You know what the user sees.
  • [ ] The liveness probe checks only the process. The readiness probe checks dependencies.
  • [ ] Message consumers are idempotent. Duplicate deliveries are harmless.
  • [ ] Poison messages go to the dead-letter queue. Someone is alerted, and there is a way to replay them.
  • [ ] Graceful shutdown finishes in-flight work within the platform's grace period.
  • [ ] Rate limits protect expensive endpoints and return 429.
  • [ ] Min and max replicas are set on purpose. Cold starts are acceptable or avoided.

6. Observability

  • [ ] Logs are structured, exported with OpenTelemetry, and contain no secrets or personal data.
  • [ ] Traces flow end to end, including across queues.
  • [ ] Each service has a clear service.name.
  • [ ] Business metrics exist for the things the product manager asks about.
  • [ ] SLIs and SLOs are defined for the main user journeys (see the SLO worksheet).
  • [ ] Every alert is actionable and links to a runbook.
  • [ ] Sampling and the log daily cap are set on purpose, and you know what gets dropped.

7. Data

  • [ ] Backups and point-in-time restore are enabled, and the retention period is known.
  • [ ] A restore has been tested at least once.
  • [ ] Concurrency conflicts are handled (409), not silently overwritten.
  • [ ] Personal data is identified. You know where it lives and how to delete it.

8. Cost

  • [ ] Every resource has tags (owner, environment, cost center).
  • [ ] A budget with alerts exists for the resource group or subscription. Remember that budgets notify, they don't stop spending.
  • [ ] The main cost drivers are known: replicas, database compute, log ingestion.

9. People

  • [ ] An owner and an on-call rotation exist.
  • [ ] Runbooks exist for the top alerts.
  • [ ] Architecture decisions are recorded as ADRs.
  • [ ] The postmortem process is agreed before the first incident, not after.

What and why

How to verify

Risk

  • [ ] Database migration (backward compatible with the running version?)
  • [ ] Configuration or infrastructure change (Bicep, app settings, roles)
  • [ ] New package or version bump
  • [ ] Behind a feature flag: FlagName
  • [ ] None of the above

Observability

Checklist

  • [ ] Tests added or updated, and they fail without this change
  • [ ] No secrets, personal data or connection strings with passwords
  • [ ] API changes reflected in the OpenAPI document (and the frontend team told)
  • [ ] I can explain every line, including the AI-assisted ones

Runbook template

One runbook per alert. Link it from the alert description so that the person woken at 2 a.m. lands here in one click. Write it for someone who is tired and has never seen this service.

Copy everything below the line into docs/runbooks/<alert-name>.md. Delete the hints in italics.

Runbook: alert name, exactly as it appears in the alert
Service e.g. Ledgerly worker
Owner team and channel
Severity Sev 1 (page now) / Sev 2 (page in working hours) / Sev 3 (ticket)
Last reviewed date, by whom

What this alert means

One or two sentences in plain language. What is the user impact? Example: "Imports are not being processed. Users who upload a CSV see 'pending' forever. Nothing is lost: messages wait in the queue."

Is it real? (first 5 minutes)

  1. Open this dashboard: link.
  2. Run this query: paste the KQL here.
  3. Check the dependency status page: link.
  4. If X is normal, it's a false alarm: resolve the alert and note it in the alert review.

Likely causes

Signal Likely cause Go to
Dependency failures to marketdata above 50% Vendor outage Fix A
Worker replicas restarting Crash loop after deploy Fix B
Messages in the dead-letter queue Poison message Fix C

Fixes

Fix A: dependency outage

  1. Turn off the feature with the kill switch: exact flag name and where to change it.
  2. Confirm users see the degraded experience, not errors.
  3. Post in the status channel using the template below.

Fix B: bad deploy

  1. Find the last good image tag: where to look.
  2. Roll back: exact command or workflow to run.
  3. Confirm the smoke test passes.

Fix C: poison message

  1. How to read the dead-letter queue: tool and permissions.
  2. How to decide: fix data and replay, or discard.
  3. How to replay: exact steps.

Escalation

When Who How
Not mitigated in 30 minutes Tech lead Phone
Data loss suspected Security and product Incident channel

Status message template

We are seeing [symptom] since [time]. [What users experience]. We are [action]. Next update in 30 minutes.

After the incident

  • Open a postmortem if it was Sev 1 or 2, or if it will happen again.
  • Update this runbook with anything that was wrong or missing.

SLO worksheet

Fill this in with your product manager and your SRE, not alone. The goal is a few numbers everybody agrees on: what "healthy" means, and when reliability work beats feature work.

The Ledgerly values below are illustrative examples. Replace them with your own.

Step 1: List the user journeys that matter

Pick 3 to 5. If a journey breaks and nobody notices for a day, it doesn't belong here.

# Journey Who notices when it breaks Ledgerly example
1 See my portfolio valuation
2 Record a trade
3 Import a broker CSV
4 Get a price alert

Step 2: Choose an SLI for each journey

An SLI (service level indicator) is a ratio: good events / valid events, measured where the user would notice.

Journey SLI type Good event Valid event Where it's measured Ledgerly example
Availability Valuation requests that return non-5xx
Latency Valuation requests faster than 500 ms
Freshness Imports finished within 5 minutes of upload
Correctness Valuations with isComplete = true

Rules of thumb:

  • Measure at the edge (API requests), not on internal components.
  • Exclude events the user caused (4xx validation errors) from "valid".
  • Prefer one good SLI over five mediocre ones.

Step 3: Set the SLO

An SLO (service level objective) is a target for the SLI over a window. Start with what you achieve today, minus a little margin, not with "five nines".

SLI SLO target Window Ledgerly example
28 days 99.5% of valuation requests succeed
28 days 95% of valuation requests under 500 ms
28 days 99% of imports finish within 5 minutes

Step 4: Calculate the error budget

Error budget = 1 − SLO. It is the amount of unreliability you are allowed to spend on deploys, experiments and bad luck.

SLO Error budget Over 28 days, that is
99% 1% about 6.7 hours of total failure, or 1 in 100 requests
99.5% 0.5% about 3.4 hours, or 1 in 200 requests
99.9% 0.1% about 40 minutes, or 1 in 1,000 requests

Your numbers:

SLO Valid events per 28 days (estimate) Allowed bad events

Step 5: Agree what happens when the budget runs out

Write this down before you need it. Example policy:

Budget left What the team does
More than 50% Ship normally. Experiments are fine.
25% to 50% Review the top error sources in the weekly meeting.
Less than 25% Reliability work goes first in the next sprint. Risky deploys need a second reviewer.
Exhausted Feature freeze for the service until the budget recovers, except fixes.

Signed off by (product, engineering, SRE): ____________________

Step 6: Alert on burn rate, not on single errors

Burn rate = how fast you are spending the budget compared to "evenly over the window". A burn rate of 1 uses exactly the whole budget in 28 days.

Alert Burn rate Long window (short window) Action
Fast burn 14.4 1 hour (5 minutes) Page on-call
Medium burn 6 6 hours (30 minutes) Page on-call
Slow burn 1 3 days (6 hours) Ticket for the next working day

These values come from the multiwindow, multi-burn-rate example in the Google SRE Workbook, written for a 30-day window. The alert fires only when both the long and the short window exceed the rate, so it stops quickly after recovery. Tune them for your traffic: low-traffic services need longer windows, or a single failed request will page someone.

Step 7: Wire it up

Task Done
SLI query written and tested (see the KQL queries file, query 11)
Workbook tile showing SLI vs SLO for the window
Fast-burn alert with action group and runbook link
Slow-burn alert as a ticket or email
Monthly SLO review in the team calendar

Review questions (every month)

  1. Did we meet the SLO? If we beat it by a lot, is it too loose?
  2. What spent the budget: deploys, a dependency, load, or bugs?
  3. Did any outage happen that the SLI didn't see? Then the SLI is wrong.
  4. Did any alert fire that nobody needed to act on? Then the alert is wrong.

Glossary

Terms used in the course, in plain language, with the chapter that explains them. 🔧 marks names that change often.

Term Meaning Chapter
ACA Azure Container Apps: a serverless platform that runs containers with scale rules, revisions and jobs, without you running a Kubernetes cluster. 10
ACR Azure Container Registry: a private registry for your container images. 11, 12
Activity / ActivitySource .NET's API for OpenTelemetry spans. An ActivitySource creates Activity objects; each one is a span in a trace. 15
ADR Architecture decision record: a short document capturing one significant decision, its context and consequences. 1, 19
AKS Azure Kubernetes Service: managed Kubernetes. Full control, more to operate. 10
Analyzer A Roslyn component that checks code during the build and reports diagnostics, such as IDE0005 or CA2007. 3
AppHost The Aspire project that describes the whole system in C# and starts it locally. 6
Application Insights The Azure Monitor feature for application telemetry: requests, dependencies, traces, exceptions and metrics. Stores data in a Log Analytics workspace. 16
Aspire 🔧 Microsoft's stack for building and running distributed apps: the AppHost, service defaults, integrations and the dashboard. Formerly ".NET Aspire". 6
Aspire dashboard A local web UI that shows resources, console logs, structured logs, traces and metrics from everything the AppHost runs. 6, 15
Availability (SLI) The share of valid requests that succeed. 16
Azure Managed Redis 🔧 The current managed Redis offering in Azure. Use it for new work instead of Azure Cache for Redis, which is being retired. 10
azd The Azure Developer CLI: provisions and deploys an app from a template with a few commands. 11
Bicep A declarative language for Azure resources that compiles to ARM templates. 11
Blameless postmortem An incident review that looks for system and process causes, not people to blame. 19
Build once, deploy many Build one immutable image per commit and promote the same image through every environment. 12
Burn rate How fast an error budget is being spent, relative to spending it evenly over the SLO window. 16
Central package management (CPM) One Directory.Packages.props file holds the versions of every NuGet package in the repo. 3
Circuit breaker A resilience strategy that stops calling a failing dependency for a while so it can recover and your threads aren't wasted. 14
CODEOWNERS A GitHub file that maps paths to the people or teams who must review changes to them. 8
CodeQL GitHub's static analysis engine for finding security bugs. 9
Cold start The delay when a new replica starts to serve a request, for example after scaling from zero. 18
Container Apps environment The boundary around a group of container apps: shared network, logging and scale. 10, 11
Container Apps job A container that runs to completion, started manually, on a schedule or by an event. 7, 12
Correlation / trace context IDs (W3C traceparent) passed between services so their telemetry joins into one trace. 15
CORS Cross-origin resource sharing: browser rules that decide which other origins (like a React SPA) may call your API. 4, 13
Dead-letter queue (DLQ) A sub-queue where Service Bus moves messages that can't be processed, for inspection and replay. 7, 19
DefaultAzureCredential An Azure.Identity credential that tries several sources in order (environment, managed identity, developer tools). Convenient locally; prefer ManagedIdentityCredential in production. 13
Dependabot GitHub's bot that opens pull requests to update dependencies and GitHub Actions. 8, 9
Duplicate detection A Service Bus feature that drops messages with a MessageId already seen within a time window. 7
EF Core Entity Framework Core, the modern .NET ORM. Not the same codebase as EF6. 5
Entra ID Microsoft Entra ID, formerly Azure Active Directory: Microsoft's identity platform for users and workloads. 13
Environment (GitHub) A named deploy target in GitHub with its own protection rules, variables and secrets. 12
Error budget 1 minus the SLO: how much unreliability is allowed in the window. 16
Execution strategy EF Core's retry policy for transient database errors. 5, 14
Expand and contract A migration approach where you add new schema first, deploy code that uses it, then remove the old schema later. Keeps deploys zero-downtime. 12
Feature flag A runtime switch that turns behavior on or off without a deploy. Used as a kill switch during incidents. 4, 19
Federated credential A trust in Entra ID that accepts tokens from an external issuer (like GitHub Actions) for a specific subject, instead of a secret. 12
GitHub Actions GitHub's CI/CD system: YAML workflows in .github/workflows. 9
GitHub flow Branch from main, open a pull request, review, merge, deploy. 8
Graceful degradation Returning a reduced but useful answer when a dependency fails, instead of an error. 14
Graceful shutdown Finishing in-flight work when the platform sends a stop signal, within the grace period. 7, 14
Health probe A platform check of your app. Startup: has it started? Liveness: is the process alive (restart if not)? Readiness: can it take traffic? 14
HybridCache A .NET caching API that combines an in-memory cache with an optional distributed cache and protects against cache stampedes. 18
Idempotency Doing the same operation twice has the same effect as doing it once. Required for message consumers. 7, 14
IHttpClientFactory The .NET factory that manages HttpClient lifetimes and lets you add handlers like resilience. 2, 14
KEDA Kubernetes Event-driven Autoscaling. Container Apps uses KEDA scalers, for example to scale on Service Bus queue length. 18
Kestrel The cross-platform web server built into ASP.NET Core. Replaces IIS as the process that serves requests. 2
Key Vault Azure's service for secrets, keys and certificates. 13
KQL Kusto Query Language, used to query logs in Azure Monitor and Log Analytics. 16
Least privilege Every identity gets only the permissions it needs, on the smallest scope. 11, 13
Log Analytics workspace The Azure Monitor store for logs and Application Insights data, queried with KQL. 16
Managed identity An identity for an Azure resource, managed by Entra ID. Your code gets tokens without any secret. User-assigned identities are separate resources you can share and grant roles before the app exists. 13
Message template A log message with named placeholders ("Import {ImportId} done") whose values are stored as structured properties. 15
Meter .NET's API for OpenTelemetry metrics: counters, histograms and gauges. 15
Microsoft.Testing.Platform (MTP) The modern .NET test runner that replaces VSTest. Opt in through global.json on the .NET 10 SDK. 17
Minimal APIs ASP.NET Core's lightweight way to define HTTP endpoints with lambdas or static methods instead of controllers. 4
Migration (EF) Generated code that changes the database schema from one model version to the next. 5
OIDC OpenID Connect. In CI/CD, GitHub issues a short-lived token that Azure trusts through a federated credential, so no cloud secret is stored. 12
OpenAPI A standard machine-readable description of an HTTP API. ASP.NET Core 10 generates OpenAPI 3.1 by default. 4
OpenTelemetry (OTel) The vendor-neutral standard and SDKs for traces, metrics and logs. 15
OTLP The OpenTelemetry protocol used to send telemetry to a collector or backend, such as the Aspire dashboard. 15
Optimistic concurrency Detecting conflicting updates with a version column (rowversion) and rejecting the second writer (409) instead of locking. 5
Outbox (transactional) Writing the message to publish into the same database transaction as the business change, then relaying it to the broker. No lost or phantom messages. 7
Peek-lock The Service Bus receive mode where a message is locked, not removed, until the consumer completes, abandons or dead-letters it. 7
ProblemDetails The standard JSON shape for HTTP API errors (RFC 9457). 4
Rate limiting Limiting how many requests a client can make in a window. Should return 429 Too Many Requests. 4, 14
RBAC Role-based access control. In Azure, role assignments grant an identity a role on a scope. 11, 13
Replica One running instance of a container app. 10, 18
Revision An immutable snapshot of a container app version in Azure Container Apps. 12
Ruleset GitHub's newer, layered way to protect branches and tags, which can apply across repositories. 8
Run-to-completion A process that does one job and exits, like the migration service. 5, 12
Sampling Keeping only a share of traces to control volume and cost. 15, 16
Scale rule A Container Apps rule that adds or removes replicas based on HTTP concurrency, queue length or other signals. 18
Scalar An interactive UI for browsing and calling an OpenAPI document. 4
Service Bus Azure's enterprise message broker: queues and topics with sessions, dead-lettering and duplicate detection. 7
Service defaults A shared Aspire project that wires OpenTelemetry, health checks, resilience and service discovery into every service with one call. 6
Service discovery Resolving a logical name like https+http://marketdata to a real address from configuration. 6
SLI Service level indicator: a measured ratio of good events to valid events. 16
SLO Service level objective: a target for an SLI over a time window. 16
Span One timed operation in a trace, with attributes and a parent. 15
Standard resilience handler AddStandardResilienceHandler(): a ready-made pipeline of rate limiter, timeouts, retries and circuit breaker for HttpClient. 14
Structured logging Logs as events with named properties, not just text lines, so you can query them. 15
Testcontainers A library that starts real dependencies (like SQL Server) in throwaway containers for tests. 17
TimeProvider The .NET abstraction for the current time and timers. Fake it in tests with FakeTimeProvider. 2, 17
Trace The tree of spans for one request as it flows through every service. 15
Transitive pinning A CPM setting that lets you force the version of a package you don't reference directly, for example to fix a vulnerability. 3
TypedResults ASP.NET Core helpers that return strongly typed HTTP results, which also feed the OpenAPI document. 4
What-if An Azure deployment preview that shows which resources would change, before you deploy. 11
Workload profile The compute type of a Container Apps environment, such as Consumption. 10
xUnit v3 The current major version of the xUnit test framework, running on Microsoft.Testing.Platform. 17
